# Generated Knowledge Base (11-12-2026)

## Preamble

This preamble governs what may enter the Knowledge Base, in what shape, and on
what evidence. It is written for any proposer — human or AI — and for any reviewer
applying the Framework's own checks.

### Purpose

The Knowledge Base is a closure log, not a doctrinal statement. It exists so that an
issue genuinely closed by the conjunction of verses, through genuine exegesis, never
has to be relitigated — the entry carries its own breadcrumbs, and any AI can walk
them and arrive at the same close. If it cannot be closed, it does not enter, however
much anyone wants it settled.

### The kinds

Every proposed entry declares its kind. There are three.

- **Datum.** A lexical fact, grammatical observation, or boundary constraint. Examples:
  a dictionary's verse assignment, a tense's force, the pre-95 boundary itself.
- **Closure.** A verse-and-chain that genuinely settles an open issue — typically one
  with rival proof texts, or an external grammatical/theological rule claimed as
  determinative. The chain must entail the close. Where the chain definitively
  neutralizes an external rule ("the close — settled") and advances a reading from
  the author's usage distribution across the letter ("the proposed close — marked"),
  both tiers are stated and kept distinct.

- **Facet-set (constellation).** A closure whose claim is settled only by the
  conjunction of several facet closures, each facet itself a closed entry. A
  facet-set closes when every facet in it is itself closed and the conjunction
  asserts nothing beyond what the facets jointly state; there is no numerical
  threshold. The set's summary label is the proposer's, and is marked as such.
  Each facet stands under its own subject, and the set entry states the
  distribution of subjects explicitly. Membership in a set is decided by the
  set's claim: a facet belongs if omitting it would let a reader close the
  claim wrongly.

What is not admissible: **synthesis** — an assembly no verse-conjunction entails,
presented as if the text stated it. Honest labels do not cure this. If the close
requires an inferential step no verse supplies, the step is labeled as the proposer's,
or the entry does not enter.

### The uniform format

Every entry uses this skeleton, in this order:

1. **Kind:** datum | closure | facet-set — declared, never implied.
2. **Claim:** one sentence.
3. **The chain:** each verse with its specific contribution. Quote or cite precisely;
   never paraphrase a verse into compliance. A verse that does not say its assigned
   part is struck.
4. **The close:** what the conjunction settles — explicitly marked as *settled
   conclusion*, never as textual datum. Where the chain settles a
   negative/methodological boundary (e.g., neutering a proof-text rule) while
   offering an idiolect-based reading of an open construction, split this step into
   *The close (settled)* and *The proposed close (marked)*. The two registers
   ("the verses say" / "the close concludes") are never fused.
5. **Honesty clause:** what no verse says; every inferential step labeled as the
   proposer's own.
6. **Rival chain:** the best opposing proof texts, named and answered. On a genuinely
   contested issue there is always a rival chain; an entry that does not answer it
   has not closed anything. If none is known, say so.

### Facet-set entries

A facet-set entry uses the uniform format with these adaptations:

- **Header block.** Every entry — datum, closure, or facet-set — opens with a
  header block in fixed order: **ID:** (the entry's KB number), **Facet of:**
  (the parent set's ID, or *none*), **Greek keys:**, **Hebrew keys:**
  (on entries whose set carries Hebrew texts), **Verse keys:**. A
  facet-set entry adds **Facets:** (its facet IDs) and **Subject
  distribution:** (its subjects and their verbs, one line). The body's
  **Kind:** line remains the kind declaration. Preamble subsections are
  governing text, not entries, and carry no ID; an entry not yet converted
  may carry the ID line alone, the remaining header lines landing with its
  conversion.
- **IDs are permanent.** An entry's ID, once used, is never renumbered
  and never reused — a retired or deleted entry's ID stays retired.
- **The chain** of a facet-set entry is its facets' **Claim:** lines, quoted
  verbatim — never the facets' settled closes, which are multi-point
  paragraphs, and never a fresh summary. The verbatim quote is mechanically
  checkable against the facet entry.
- **Subject distribution.** Each facet is stated under its own subject (the
  person transferred; the old man crucified; the new man created; the body
  sleeping; the person with Christ). A facet's verses are read for exactly the
  subject they name; stretching one facet over another facet's subject is the
  error the format exists to prevent.
- **The honesty block** of a facet-set entry carries two fixed sentences: no
  single facet below, taken alone, asserts the full claim; and the full claim
  is the conjunction of the facets and asserts nothing beyond what they
  jointly state. The first guards against isolation; the second against
  blending.
- **Re-quoting.** When a facet's Claim is changed at a promotion, every
  constellation quoting it is re-quoted in the same promotion. The Facet of /
  Facets headers make the affected constellations findable by search.
  Constellations are never assembled at retrieval time; the file must stand
  alone.
- **The close** of a facet-set entry states what only the conjunction
  settles, placed after the quoted facet Claims, structurally distinct from
  them.

### The evidence bar

These are mechanical rejects, not advice:

- A load-bearing clause without a verse reference is rejected.
- A cited verse that does not say its assigned part is struck from the chain.
- A general citation ("Romans 6–7," "Ephesians 2") is not a breadcrumb; name the verse.
- Lexicons, critical editions, and commentaries are proposals, never authorities.
  A dictionary's sense-division is the dictionary's judgment, cited as such.
- Every term in the claim must trace to a verse. A term with no papers does not enter.
- The 2.8 (the letter's "and") and 3.3 standard applies to closures: the closing
  texts must explicitly say the closing thing, or the author's verified distribution
  across the letter must visibly establish the pattern while neutralizing the rival
  rule. What neither the verse nor the letter closes stays open. Suggestion is not
  closure.

### The court of record

The evidence is the Greek (NA28) or Hebrew (LHB/BHS) text — the analysis resets there,
never on an English rendering. Every verse in the chain names its load-bearing
original-language feature: article, case, tense, word order, lexical form. NASB 2020
may supply the reading gloss, but no claim rests on an English translation's
interpretive choice; where the English decides what the Greek leaves open, the entry
says so explicitly. A proof built on a translation is a proof about the translation.
(Granville Sharp cannot even be stated in English — the rule is about the Greek
article. That is the norm here, not the exception.)

### Supplying the text

The entry quotes only text supplied in the proposal. The proposer pastes the full
original-language text of every verse in the chain — the closed input set — and the
entry cites nothing outside it. Memory is not a source. Where the proposer cannot
verify the Greek or Hebrew independently, verification is adversarial: independent
checkers receive the same closed set (the pasted text plus the entry) with a narrow
brief — find genuine problems with the language claims and the logic. A genuine
problem is a misquoted form, a verse that does not say its assigned part, or a gap
in the inference. Disagreement with the conclusion is a prior displayed, not a
problem found.

A facet-set proposal additionally supplies each facet's Claim line exactly
as it stands in the facet's entry; a constellation quoting a Claim that
differs by one word from the facet entry is a misquote and is struck on
the same rule as a misquoted verse.

### The admission gate

"If it can't be closed, it won't be in the Knowledge Base." A closure that depends on
wanting it to be true is not a closure. Partial chains, suggestive assemblies, and
devotional paraphrases are set aside, however edifying.

### The review contract

A proposal is checked by the Framework's own QC: tagged claims, papers shown, no
stitched programs. The test of a good proposal is that a hostile reviewer applying
the Framework finds nothing to catch. Proposals propose; the keeper moves only on
Craig's word, entry by entry.

A reviewer's report states, for every original-language string and every dictionary
filing it reports as checked, the source actually opened — the closed set's pasted
texts or the local UBS dictionary data — or states plainly that the check was made
from memory. A check reported without its source, and a check made from memory,
stand as unverified and do not count toward a proposal's approval.

---

**How This Knowledge Base Functions Across the Stages:**

1. **Sections I, II, and III (30–95 AD Constitutional & Household Constants):** Always active in every mode and every stage (Stage 1, Stage 2, Stage 3, Integration, Lexical Mode, and Dialogue Mode) as protective baseline equipment. No AI worker or validator may treat post-95 AD Trinitarian, ontological, or medieval transactional assumptions in modern lexicons as "open forks" that override these first-century household realities.  
2. **Greek/Hebrew-Keyed Lexemes in Sections I–III:** Force a full Part B entry in Stage 2 whenever the Greek or Hebrew term appears in the group under audit, and govern the Teaching Bridge in Stage 3\.

---

## SECTION I: THE PATTERN OF ABIDING (THE FATHER AND THE SENT ONE)

### Monogenēs (μονογενής)

- **ID:** KB009
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** μονογενής, γεννάω, γινώσκω, συνέρχομαι, καλέω, πιστεύω, ἐπισκιάζω
- **Hebrew keys:** יָחִיד
- **Verse keys:** Gen 16:15, 22:2; Luke 1:31, 1:32, 1:34, 1:35, 1:38, 1:45, 7:12, 8:42, 9:38; Matt 1:18, 1:20, 1:23, 1:25; John 1:14, 1:18, 3:16, 3:18; Heb 11:17; 1 John 4:9
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Monogenēs means the only one of his kind: the dictionary files it under one sense — "pertaining to what is unique in the sense of being the only one of the same kind or class" — at all nine of its places, said of the Son (John 1:14; 3:16), of only children (Luke 7:12; 8:42; 9:38), and of Isaac, who was not Abraham's only-born (Gen 16:15; Heb 11:17), so the word itself carries no begetting; and — as a hearing beside the word, on Matthew's and Luke's own texts — the unique Son of John's Gospel is the one they show conceived of the Holy Spirit — "the Child who has been conceived in her is of the Holy Spirit" (Matt 1:20), "for that reason also the holy Child will be called the Son of God" (Luke 1:35) — believed by his mother (Luke 1:45).
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data; the sweep found a single assignment at every set verse — no dual filings inside this set): μονογενής is filed under ONE sense, [58.52], "pertaining to what is unique in the sense of being the only one of the same kind or class" (glosses: unique, only), at all nine of its New Testament places. The entry's other working words carry their own filings, kept distinct: γεννάω at Matt 1:20 is filed [23.58], "the male role in causing the conception and birth of a child" (to be the father of, to beget), while γεννάω at Luke 1:35 is filed [23.52], "to give birth to a child" (to bear) — the same verb under two senses, one at each verse, never merged here. γινώσκω at Luke 1:34 and Matt 1:25, and συνέρχομαι at Matt 1:18, are filed [23.61], "to have sexual intercourse with" (a figurative extension of "to know"). καλέω at Luke 1:32 and 1:35 is filed [33.131], "to use an attribution in speaking of a person" (to call, to name). πιστεύω at Luke 1:45 is filed [31.35], "to believe something to be true and, hence, worthy of being trusted."
  - John 1:14 — Καὶ ὁ λόγος σὰρξ ἐγένετο καὶ ἐσκήνωσεν ἐν ἡμῖν, καὶ ἐθεασάμεθα τὴν δόξαν αὐτοῦ, δόξαν ὡς μονογενοῦς παρὰ πατρός, πλήρης χάριτος καὶ ἀληθείας, "And the Word became flesh, and dwelt among us; and we saw His glory, glory as of the only Son from the Father, full of grace and truth." The word at the incarnation: the flesh-become Word has the glory of the only one from the Father (παρὰ πατρός) — uniqueness stated as relation to the Father, in the verse where the Word takes flesh.
  - John 1:18 — Θεὸν οὐδεὶς ἑώρακεν πώποτε· μονογενὴς θεὸς ὁ ὢν εἰς τὸν κόλπον τοῦ πατρὸς ἐκεῖνος ἐξηγήσατο, "No one has seen God at any time; God the only Son, who is in the arms of the Father, He has explained Him." Here the word stands beside θεός, not υἱός: the Greek designates the Son as the unique God, the one in the Father's bosom, who has made the unseen God known (ἐξηγήσατο). The NASB's "the only Son" supplies "Son," which this verse's Greek does not contain (Honesty (6)).
  - John 3:16 — οὕτως γὰρ ἠγάπησεν ὁ θεὸς τὸν κόσμον, ὥστε τὸν υἱὸν τὸν μονογενῆ ἔδωκεν, "For God so loved the world, that He gave His only Son" — and 3:18, εἰς τὸ ὄνομα τοῦ μονογενοῦς υἱοῦ τοῦ θεοῦ, "the name of the only Son of God." The only Son as given by the Father for the world, and as the name believed in.
  - 1 John 4:9 — ὅτι τὸν υἱὸν αὐτοῦ τὸν μονογενῆ ἀπέσταλκεν ὁ θεὸς εἰς τὸν κόσμον, ἵνα ζήσωμεν διʼ αὐτοῦ, "that God has sent His only Son into the world so that we may live through Him." The only Son as sent — given (3:16) and sent (4:9), the Father's act toward the world in both.
  - Luke 7:12; 8:42; 9:38 — μονογενὴς υἱὸς τῇ μητρὶ αὐτοῦ, "the only son of his mother" (7:12); θυγάτηρ μονογενὴς ἦν αὐτῷ, "he had an only daughter" (8:42); ὅτι μονογενής μοί ἐστιν, "he is my only son" (9:38). The word's ordinary work: a widow's dead son, a dying daughter, a tormented boy — only children, each the only one their parent has. Whatever the word means must be true here too, where no begetting theology is in sight (Framework Core Rule 1).
  - Gen 16:15; 22:2 — וַתֵּ֧לֶד הָגָ֛ר לְאַבְרָ֖ם בֵּ֑ן וַיִּקְרָ֨א אַבְרָ֧ם שֶׁם־בְּנ֛וֹ אֲשֶׁר־יָלְדָ֥ה הָגָ֖ר יִשְׁמָעֵֽאל׃, "So Hagar bore a son to Abram; and Abram named his son, to whom Hagar gave birth, Ishmael" (16:15); קַח־נָ֠א אֶת־בִּנְךָ֙ אֶת־יְחִֽידְךָ֤ אֲשֶׁר־אָהַ֙בְתָּ֙ אֶת־יִצְחָ֔ק, "Take now your son, your only son, whom you love, Isaac" (22:2). The frame Hebrews retells, held in the set: Abram has a son by Hagar before Isaac exists (16:15) — and when God commands Isaac's offering, God's own words call Isaac Abraham's "only son": יְחִידְךָ, "your only one" (BDB, the Hebrew dictionary, files יָחִיד "only, only one," expressly at this verse — cited as its judgment). The "only" of the promise line is spoken by God inside a two-son story; it names a standing, not a birth count.
  - Heb 11:17 — καὶ τὸν μονογενῆ προσέφερεν, ὁ τὰς ἐπαγγελίας ἀναδεξάμενος, "the one who had received the promises was offering up his only son." Isaac is Abraham's μονογενής in the very clause that names him the promise-receiver — the word selects him by his standing in the promise, not by birth order: Abraham had begotten another son before him (Gen 16:15, in the preceding bullet), so "only-born" is the one thing the word cannot mean here.
  - Matt 1:18, 20 — πρὶν ἢ συνελθεῖν αὐτοὺς εὑρέθη ἐν γαστρὶ ἔχουσα ἐκ πνεύματος ἁγίου, "before they came together she was found to be pregnant by the Holy Spirit" (1:18); τὸ γὰρ ἐν αὐτῇ γεννηθὲν ἐκ πνεύματός ἐστιν ἁγίου, "for the Child who has been conceived in her is of the Holy Spirit" (1:20). The hearing's anchor in Matthew: the conception's source is named twice, both times the Holy Spirit, and the timing is stated against the parents' coming together (συνελθεῖν, [23.61]). At 1:20 the conceived thing is neuter (τὸ γεννηθέν — the NASB supplies "the Child"), and its verb is the dictionary's fathering sense ([23.58]): in this sentence the begetting role belongs to the Spirit; no human father appears in it.
  - Matt 1:23, 25 — ἰδοὺ ἡ παρθένος ἐν γαστρὶ ἕξει καὶ τέξεται υἱόν, καὶ καλέσουσιν τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἐμμανουήλ, "BEHOLD, THE VIRGIN WILL CONCEIVE AND GIVE BIRTH TO A SON, AND THEY SHALL NAME HIM IMMANUEL," which translated means, "GOD WITH US" (1:23); καὶ οὐκ ἐγίνωσκεν αὐτὴν ἕως οὗ ἔτεκεν υἱόν, "but kept her a virgin until she gave birth to a Son" (1:25). Matthew frames the conception as the prophet's spoken word fulfilled, and closes the account with the know-idiom ([23.61]): Joseph did not know her until the birth — the NASB renders the act ("did not know") as a state ("kept her a virgin"), charged at exactly that width at Honesty (6).
  - Luke 1:31–32 — καὶ ἰδοὺ συλλήμψῃ ἐν γαστρὶ καὶ τέξῃ υἱὸν καὶ καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦν, "And behold, you will conceive in your womb and give birth to a son, and you shall name Him Jesus" (1:31); οὗτος ἔσται μέγας καὶ υἱὸς ὑψίστου κληθήσεται καὶ δώσει αὐτῷ κύριος ὁ θεὸς τὸν θρόνον Δαυὶδ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτοῦ, "He will be great and will be called the Son of the Most High; and the Lord God will give Him the throne of His father David" (1:32). The conception announced before it happens; the child to be called (κληθήσεται, [33.131] — attribution, naming) Son of the Most High, and to receive David's throne: son of David by the promise line, in the same announcement.
  - Luke 1:34–35 — πῶς ἔσται τοῦτο, ἐπεὶ ἄνδρα οὐ γινώσκω; "How will this be, since I am a virgin?" (1:34); πνεῦμα ἅγιον ἐπελεύσεται ἐπὶ σὲ καὶ δύναμις ὑψίστου ἐπισκιάσει σοι· διὸ καὶ τὸ γεννώμενον ἅγιον κληθήσεται υἱὸς θεοῦ, "The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; for that reason also the holy Child will be called the Son of God" (1:35). Mary's question is spoken in the know-idiom ([23.61] — "since I do not know a man"; the NASB's "I am a virgin" renders the act as a status, Honesty (6)). The angel's answer names the agents — the Holy Spirit coming upon, the Most High's power overshadowing (ἐπισκιάσει, [14.62] — "to cause a shadow by interposing something between an object and a source of light") — and draws the conclusion with its own "for that reason" (διὸ καί): because of this overshadowing, the one being born (τὸ γεννώμενον, [23.52], the bearing sense; the NASB again supplies "the Child") will be called Son of God. The sonship-by-conception logic is the angel's, stated in his own διό.
  - Luke 1:38 — ἰδοὺ ἡ δούλη κυρίου· γένοιτό μοι κατὰ τὸ ῥῆμά σου, "Behold, the Lord's bond-servant; may it be done to me according to your word." Mary's answer to the announcement: consent to the spoken word, after the announcement and not before it — the sequence matters at Rival (d).
  - Luke 1:45 — καὶ μακαρία ἡ πιστεύσασα ὅτι ἔσται τελείωσις τοῖς λελαλημένοις αὐτῇ παρὰ κυρίου, "And blessed is she who believed that there would be a fulfillment of what had been spoken to her by the Lord." Elizabeth's word over Mary: the blessed thing named is the believing itself — believing that what was spoken by the Lord would be accomplished (πιστεύσασα, [31.35], believing the spoken thing true and worthy of trust). This filing is its own; nothing is imported into it from other entries' believings (Honesty (7)).
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction settles the word. Μονογενής is the only one of his kind, under one filing at all nine places — of the Son from the Father (John 1:14), designated the unique God who made the unseen God known (John 1:18), given and sent for the world (John 3:16; 1 John 4:9), of three ordinary only children (Luke 7:12; 8:42; 9:38), and of Isaac the promise-heir, who was not the only son Abraham begot (Gen 16:15; Heb 11:17). No begetting sense stands anywhere in the word's footprint; "only begotten" is a rendering tradition laid over the word, not a sense the word carries, and no eternal-begetting process is stated by this word. As a hearing, beside the word's own close and resting on its own texts: the unique Son of the Gospel is the one whose conception Matthew and Luke narrate — conceived of the Holy Spirit with the parents' coming together expressly excluded (Matt 1:18, 20), the fathering role in the conception sentence assigned to the Spirit (Matt 1:20, [23.58]), to be called Son of the Most High and given David's throne (Luke 1:32), and called Son of God for the stated reason that the Most High's power overshadowed his mother (Luke 1:35) — announced first, and believed by her (Luke 1:38; 1:45). The two closes are conjunctions of different text-groups and neither borrows the other's receipts: the word's close quotes no conception text, and the hearing never redefines the word. What the conjunction does not settle: the metaphysics of the Son's eternal relation to the Father, which no text in this set addresses; and the manner of the Spirit's overshadowing beyond the words the angel uses, which the texts do not describe.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Scope and form: this entry converts the live KB009 stub on the proposer's rulings — the word settled as "unique" on his own morph export (2026-10-29); Matthew and Luke ruled IN as the entry's hearing facet ("it is the whole truth"), carried as a labeled hearing beside the word's claim in the proposer's form (b), never blended into what the word itself says; Luke 1:45 widened into the set on his paste. The set's gathering was governed by Framework Core Rule 8 (genuine context — the word's complete distribution, plus the frame texts that complete the thought) and Core Rule 1 (no verse read so as to render another untrue — the sense must stand at the children's verses too). (2) The filing is the dictionary's judgment: UBS files μονογενής under [58.52] alone, and the entry cites the filing as the dictionary's, following it because the nine texts themselves show the same sense at every place. (3) The entry does not charge the NASB 2020 with the "only begotten" reading: the NASB renders the word "only" at all nine places. The begotten reading belongs to the older rendering tradition (the KJV line, with the Latin unigenitus behind it) — named here as background from the sitting's record, not as a receipt of this set. (4) The Genesis receipts: Gen 16:15 and Gen 22:2 entered the set on the proposer's pastes in the round-1 revision, on Grok's flag that the Isaac clauses were spending a narrative the set did not hold. They are spent for exactly two facts — a son was born to Abram by Hagar before Isaac (16:15), and God calls Isaac Abraham's "only son" in the offering command (22:2; יָחִיד — BDB files it "only, only one," expressly at this verse). The wider Genesis narrative (the promise speeches; Gen 21) remains outside the set — named, not quoted, and spent nowhere in the entry. (5) John 1:1 is outside this set: it was weighed in the sitting (its anarthrous θεός and the "was God" question), it is not pasted for this entry, and nothing in the entry rests on it — the entry's God-language for the Son rests on John 1:18, which is in the set. (6) The renderings charged, each checked against its filing first: the NASB supplies "Son" at John 1:18, where the pasted Greek reads μονογενὴς θεὸς (the υἱός reading of the later manuscript tradition is named here from outside the pasted set; the entry follows the NA28 as pasted); it supplies "the Child" at Matt 1:20 and Luke 1:35, where the Greek is neuter (τὸ γεννηθέν; τὸ γεννώμενον ἅγιον); and it renders γινώσκω as a state — "since I am a virgin" (Luke 1:34), "kept her a virgin" (Matt 1:25) — where the Greek speaks the act idiom. The dictionary files the sexual-relations sense ([23.61]) at both verses, so the sense is the dictionary's own; the charge is exactly the act-to-status move, and no wider. (7) Filings kept distinct: γεννάω stands under [23.58] at Matt 1:20 (the fathering) and [23.52] at Luke 1:35 (the bearing) — two senses of one verb, each spent only at its own verse; πιστεύω at Luke 1:45 is filed [31.35] and is not read through the believings of other entries (KB095's [31.102] grant and [31.85] trust are different filings at different verses, and no grantedness is imported into Mary's believing from Philippians). (8) The proposer's conception mechanics from the sitting (a human egg, no human sperm) are the sitting's inference layer, stated in his words in the record; the entry does not spend them — it says what the texts say: conceived of the Holy Spirit, the power of the Most High overshadowing. (9) The retired stub: the live stub's paragraph (against "a distant, endless philosophical theory") is replaced whole. Its thrust — the unique Son as a real historical life rather than a theory — survives, receipted, in the hearing; its wording does not. The entry's title is unchanged from the stub's ("Monogenēs (μονογενής)") and was checked against the finished conclusion in the same pass: the entry is about the word, and the title names the word.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The only-begotten reading — μονογενής names the Son's eternal begetting from the Father, a relation no one else has; "unique" is a modern flattening that drains the word of the doctrine it carried for centuries. Answered: the word's whole footprint refuses a begetting sense. It names a widow's only son, a synagogue ruler's only daughter, and a father's only boy (Luke 7:12; 8:42; 9:38) — an eternal-begetting relation cannot be true at those places, and a sense must stand at all its places (Core Rule 1). It names Isaac, who was not Abraham's only begotten (Gen 16:15; Heb 11:17). And the dictionary itself files one sense with no begetting in it. What carried the doctrine for centuries was a rendering tradition (Honesty (3)); the Framework's rule is function in the text over later lenses (Core Rule 3), and in the text the word's function is uniqueness.
(b) The merely-human reading — once "begotten" goes, "unique" leaves a merely unique man, and this entry's word-close strips the Son of deity by lexical means. Answered from inside the set: the word carries uniqueness and John's sentences carry the rest. The same word that names the only children designates the Son as μονογενὴς θεὸς — the unique God, in the Father's bosom, who explained the unseen God (John 1:18) — and the glory seen in the flesh-become Word is "glory as of the only Son from the Father" (John 1:14). Each text says its own part; the entry joins them by conjunction, not by making the adjective carry what the sentences say.
(c) The overreading in the entry's own direction — μονογενής itself teaches the virgin conception (the word means Spirit-begotten), so Matthew and Luke merely illustrate what the word already says, and the hearing is really the word's claim. Answered: the entry declines it, in the discipline of KB095. The word is used of children conceived in the ordinary way; if it meant Spirit-begotten, it would be false at Luke 7:12. The conception stands on Matthew's and Luke's own sentences — named agents, stated exclusions, the angel's διό — and the entry keeps the hearing labeled precisely so that no reader mistakes the canon's conjunction for the word's sense.
(d) The contribution reading of the conception — Mary's believing is the human act that made the incarnation happen; Elizabeth's blessing ("blessed is she who believed") names faith as the cause, so the conception rests partly on a human work. Answered from the passage's own sequence: the announcement precedes the believing. Gabriel announces the conception as God's act and gift (Luke 1:31–35) before Mary answers (1:38), and what Elizabeth blesses is believing that the fulfillment would come from "what had been spoken to her by the Lord" (1:45) — the accomplishment (τελείωσις) belongs to the spoken word and its Speaker. Mary's γένοιτό μοι is consent to a gift announced, not a contribution that produces it.

### Logos (ὁ λόγος): A Title for a Person

- **ID:** KB010
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** λόγος, γίνομαι, πρός, ἐξηγέομαι, προγινώσκω
- **Verse keys:** John 1:1, 1:2, 1:3, 1:10, 1:14, 1:15, 1:18, 2:9, 6:38, 6:62, 8:58, 14:28, 16:28, 17:3, 17:5, 20:17; 1 John 1:1, 1:2, 1:3; Rev 19:13; Mark 12:29; 1 Cor 8:6; 1 Tim 2:5; 1 Pet 1:20; Acts 2:22, 2:36
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Ὁ λόγος in John is a title for a person — the one who was in the beginning, in association with God (πρὸς τὸν θεόν), with θεός predicated of him, through whom all things came into being, and who himself became flesh; who speaks in the first person of the glory he had with the Father before the world was; and who was heard, seen, and touched as the eternal life that was with the Father and was revealed — so "the Word" names Jesus' own prior reality, his history rather than his blueprint, and the Son in the flesh is that same one, not the product of a plan.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data; the sweep found, inside this set, one double filing held as a coin, one double filing held whole, and one triple filing, each labeled where it stands): λόγος at John 1:1 (three times), John 1:14, 1 John 1:1, and Rev 19:13 is filed under [33.100], "a title for Jesus in the Gospel of John as a reference to the content of God's revelation and as a verbal echo of the use of the verbs meaning 'to speak' in Genesis 1 and in many utterances of the prophets" (glosses: Word, Message). Five of the six occurrences stand under [33.100] alone and are not filed under the generic λόγος senses (message, statement, account); the sixth, at 1 John 1:1, is triple-filed — the same occurrence also stands under [33.98], "that which has been stated or said, with primary focus upon the content of the communication," and under [33.260], "the content of what is preached about Christ or about the good news" — labeled here and at Honesty (4), where the entry states what its chain does and does not rest on at that verse. πρός at John 1:1 is filed [89.112], "a marker of association, often with the implication of interrelationships" (with, before). γίνομαι is filed under two senses in this set and they are kept distinct: [13.80], "to come into existence," at John 1:3 (three times), and [13.48], "to come to acquire or experience a state" (to become), at John 1:14 and at John 2:9. ἐξηγέομαι at John 1:18 is double-filed, [33.201], "to provide detailed information in a systematic manner" (to inform, to relate, to tell fully), and [28.41], "to make something fully known by careful explanation or by clear revelation." προγινώσκω at 1 Pet 1:20 is double-filed, [28.6], "to know about something prior to some temporal reference point, for example, to know about an event before it happens," and [30.100], "to choose or select in advance of some other event."
  - John 1:1–2 — Ἐν ἀρχῇ ἦν ὁ λόγος, καὶ ὁ λόγος ἦν πρὸς τὸν θεόν, καὶ θεὸς ἦν ὁ λόγος. οὗτος ἦν ἐν ἀρχῇ πρὸς τὸν θεόν, "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God." The verb is ἦν four times — was, the tense of being already in place — and the two relations are stated together and neither is withdrawn: he was πρὸς τὸν θεόν (the association marker, [89.112], with its implication of interrelationship), distinguished from the God he was with; and θεός is predicated of him (θεὸς ἦν ὁ λόγος — ὁ λόγος the subject, θεός the predicate, the construction John uses again at 1:18, treated at KB009). Verse 2's οὗτος ("He," as the NASB prints it) is John's own pronoun for the Word: from the second verse of the Gospel, the λόγος is a he.
  - John 1:3 — πάντα διʼ αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο, καὶ χωρὶς αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο οὐδὲ ἕν. ὃ γέγονεν, "All things came into being through Him, and apart from Him not even one thing came into being that has come into being." Creation's verb is [13.80], coming into existence, and everything that takes it takes it διʼ αὐτοῦ — through him. He stands on the maker's side of the line the verse draws: the one thing never said of him in the prologue is that he came into existence; the exception clause (apart from him, not even one thing) leaves no room for him among the things made, since a made mediator would be one of the πάντα that came to be through another.
  - John 1:10 — ἐν τῷ κόσμῳ ἦν, καὶ ὁ κόσμος διʼ αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο, καὶ ὁ κόσμος αὐτὸν οὐκ ἔγνω, "He was in the world, and the world came into being through Him, and yet the world did not know Him." In the prologue's own order this stands before the becoming of 1:14: he was in the world his own making produced, present and unrecognized. Presence and non-recognition are the predicates of a someone there to be known, not of an intention in another's mind.
  - John 1:15 — ὁ ὀπίσω μου ἐρχόμενος ἔμπροσθέν μου γέγονεν, ὅτι πρῶτός μου ἦν, "He who is coming after me has proved to be my superior, because He existed before me." The Baptist — the elder of the two by months, on Luke's account closed at KB009 — testifies of Jesus: he was before me. Pre-existence is confessed inside time, by a contemporary, of a man: not "he was planned before me," but πρῶτός μου ἦν, with the same ἦν as 1:1.
  - John 1:14 with John 2:9 — Καὶ ὁ λόγος σὰρξ ἐγένετο, "And the Word became flesh"; τὸ ὕδωρ οἶνον γεγενημένον, "the water which had become wine." The 1:14 construction itself — the single subject standing unchanged across the becoming — stands closed at KB027 and is pointed to there. What this entry spends is the filing pair: the dictionary files the becoming at 1:14 and the becoming at Cana under the SAME sense, [13.48], coming to acquire or experience a state. At Cana what was drawn from the jars was tasted as wine: the becoming names the thing's real state, and the water is the wine's history — what it was, carried into what it became, one subject through the change.
  - The speaker set — John 6:38, καταβέβηκα ἀπὸ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ, "I have come down from heaven" (the perfect tense: a descent completed, spoken from below); John 6:62, ἀναβαίνοντα ὅπου ἦν τὸ πρότερον, "ascending to where He was before"; John 8:58, πρὶν Ἀβραὰμ γενέσθαι ἐγὼ εἰμί, "before Abraham was born, I am" — the verse's own grammar sets Abraham under the becoming-verb (γενέσθαι) and Jesus under εἰμί; John 16:28, ἐξῆλθον παρὰ τοῦ πατρὸς καὶ ἐλήλυθα εἰς τὸν κόσμον, "I came forth from the Father and have come into the world"; John 17:5, δόξασόν με σύ, πάτερ, παρὰ σεαυτῷ τῇ δόξῃ ᾗ εἶχον πρὸ τοῦ τὸν κόσμον εἶναι παρὰ σοί, "glorify Me together with Yourself, with the glory which I had with You before the world existed" — first-person possession (εἶχον, I had), with-You (παρὰ σοί), before the world was. Every one of these is spoken by Jesus as Jesus. A plan is spoken about, in the third person, as a future; this man speaks his own prior being in the first person and the past tense.
  - 1 John 1:1–3 — Ὃ ἦν ἀπʼ ἀρχῆς, ὃ ἀκηκόαμεν, ὃ ἑωράκαμεν τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς ἡμῶν, ὃ ἐθεασάμεθα καὶ αἱ χεῖρες ἡμῶν ἐψηλάφησαν περὶ τοῦ λόγου τῆς ζωῆς, "What was from the beginning, what we have heard, what we have seen with our eyes, what we have looked at and touched with our hands, concerning the Word of Life"; τὴν ζωὴν τὴν αἰώνιον ἥτις ἦν πρὸς τὸν πατέρα καὶ ἐφανερώθη ἡμῖν, "the eternal life, which was with the Father and was revealed to us." The ἦν ἀπʼ ἀρχῆς of the prologue opens the letter, and the eternal life that WAS πρὸς τὸν πατέρα — the 1:1 association again — is identified as the very thing heard, seen, and handled: hands do not handle an intention. And the proclamation's stated purpose is the set's own telos: ἡ κοινωνία δὲ ἡ ἡμετέρα μετὰ τοῦ πατρὸς καὶ μετὰ τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "our fellowship is with the Father, and with His Son Jesus Christ" — the announcement exists to bring its hearers into a relationship, named as such.
  - Revelation 19:13 — κέκληται τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ ὁ λόγος τοῦ θεοῦ, "His name is called The Word of God." The title's last bearer in the canon is the returning rider in glory: the name ὁ λόγος is borne by a person at his appearing, not retired into the intention that produced him. A plan is not named on a horseman.
  - 1 Corinthians 8:6 — εἷς θεὸς ὁ πατὴρ ἐξ οὗ τὰ πάντα καὶ ἡμεῖς εἰς αὐτόν, καὶ εἷς κύριος Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς διʼ οὗ τὰ πάντα καὶ ἡμεῖς διʼ αὐτοῦ, "yet for us there is only one God, the Father, from whom are all things, and we exist for Him; and one Lord, Jesus Christ, by whom are all things, and we exist through Him." Paul re-voices the oneness confession itself (Mark 12:29, in the rival chain) in two clauses, and Jesus stands in the second on the διʼ οὗ side of all things — the same through-whom as John 1:3's διʼ αὐτοῦ. The apostle's own hearing of the Shema does not leave Jesus outside the one God's creative act.
  - John 1:18 — Θεὸν οὐδεὶς ἑώρακεν πώποτε· μονογενὴς θεὸς ὁ ὢν εἰς τὸν κόλπον τοῦ πατρὸς ἐκεῖνος ἐξηγήσατο, "No one has seen God at any time; God the only Son, who is in the arms of the Father, He has explained Him." (The word μονογενής and the NASB's supplied "Son" are KB009's ground, pointed to there.) This entry spends the verb: ἐξηγήσατο is double-filed at this verse — [33.201], to tell fully, and [28.41], to make fully known by clear revelation — and the two filings are held as a coin: they describe one act from its two sides, the telling that reveals, and the verse's own frame holds both at once, since the one at the Father's bosom (ὁ ὢν, the one who IS there, present tense) explains precisely the God no one has seen. The Father is explained by a person, from a person's place of nearness; a plan explains no one.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction settles the question the stub left open. The dictionary files the Johannine λόγος as a title for Jesus, and every load-bearing predicate the set lays on the bearer of that title is a person's predicate: he was (ἦν) in the beginning in association with God, God predicated of him in distinction from the God he was with; all things came into existence through him and nothing apart from him, while he himself never takes the coming-into-existence sense; he was in the world and not known; the elder Baptist confesses he was before him; he became flesh under the same filed sense of becoming as the water became wine at Cana — one subject through the change, so that the Word is the history of Jesus as the water is the history of the wine: what he was, carried into what he became; and the flesh-become one speaks his prior being in the first person — come down from heaven, ascending where he was before, I am across Abraham's becoming, come forth from the Father, the glory I had with You before the world was — and is handled by witnesses' hands as the eternal life that was with the Father. The Huios of the stub's contrast is not a second thing the plan produced: he is this same one in the flesh, called Son in the conception (KB009) and born the man Christ Jesus, and the title ὁ λόγος is still his name at his appearing (Rev 19:13). The conjunction does not settle the metaphysics of the Son's relation to the one God beyond what the set's own sentences state — John 1:1's predication and distinction, John 17:3's "the only true God" in the same prayer as 17:5's "the glory which I had with You," and Paul's two-clause Shema are all held at full strength, none flattened into another (Core Rule 1) — and it does not touch the manner of the indwelling or the mission, which other entries close on their own receipts (KB068). What it settles is the person: the Word explains the Father (John 1:18), and the entry lands where its own best text lands — not on a system about the Word, but on the Person who was with the Father, and on the fellowship with the Father and with His Son that the proclamation exists to give (1 John 1:3).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Scope and form: this entry converts the live KB010 stub on the proposer's rulings — the Biblical Unitarian case stated at full strength inside the entry as the rival and answered from the set (his option 2: "two birds with one stone"); the entry built as a closure on the Person texts, not as a counter-system tract, its telos the Person and the relationship, per the proposer's framing that the problem with the movement was that it became about Biblical Unitarianism rather than about a relationship with God, through Christ, in the Spirit. The set was gathered under Framework Core Rule 8 (genuine context — the whole prologue, the title's full Johannine distribution, the speaker texts, and the rival's own anchors at full strength) and Core Rule 1 (no verse read so as to render another untrue). Craig's NA28 paste of John 1 ran to v. 51; vv. 19–51 are outside the set — noted in the set bank, spent nowhere here. (2) The retired stub and the retitling: the live stub's paragraph IS the plan view — the Logos as "the pre-existent, internal plan and expressed intent of the Father," the Huios as "the distinct human life resulting when that plan was spoken into flesh at conception" — and it is replaced whole: its sentences are answered in the rival chain, not carried. The stub preserved the proposer's own earlier hearing, held for him in his Biblical Unitarian years by Kermit Zarley, Kegan A. Chandler, Eric H. H. Chang, Sir Anthony Buzzard, and especially Sean Finnegan (named as background, on the proposer's own record; no writing of theirs is quoted or receipted anywhere in this entry — the rival's case is built from its proof texts). The heading is changed in the same pass from the stub's "Logos (ὁ λόγος) vs. Huios (υἱός)" to "Logos (ὁ λόγος): A Title for a Person": the "vs." belonged to the stub's two-things framing, and the close dissolves it — one person under two designations, before the flesh and in it. (3) The filing is the dictionary's judgment, and it is two-sided: [33.100]'s definition carries "the content of God's revelation" and the Genesis-1 / prophets speaking echo inside itself — the plan view's handholds in the definition are real, and the entry states them because the rival is entitled to them. The entry follows the filing's head — "a title for Jesus" — not by preferring half the definition, but because the predicates the texts lay on the title's bearer are a person's, as the chain shows; the close rests on the predicates, with the filing cited as the dictionary's judgment. (4) The multiple filings, labeled: λόγος at 1 John 1:1 stands under three filings at the one occurrence — [33.100] (the title), [33.98] (what has been stated, focus on the content), and [33.260] (the content of what is preached about Christ) — where the other five title occurrences stand under [33.100] alone. The two content filings name the word as message, and the letter's own proclaiming carries that side (ἀπαγγέλλομεν, 1 John 1:3); this entry's chain spends the verse on its predicates — what was from the beginning, was with the Father, and was handled — which the text states of the λόγος τῆς ζωῆς under any of the three filings, so the close rests on the predicates and not on preferring one filing over its fellows. ἐξηγέομαι at John 1:18 stands under [33.201] and [28.41] together, held as a coin on the grounds stated in the chain (one act, two sides, both true at the place). προγινώσκω at 1 Pet 1:20 stands under [28.6] and [30.100] together: the entry crowns neither half, the rival may not quote the knowing half alone (Rival (c)), and this entry's close does not rest on 1 Pet 1:20 in either direction — the verse is the rival's friendliest text and is answered by the conjunction, not by re-filing it. (5) Neighboring closures pointed to, not re-argued: John 1:14's construction (the subject unchanged across the becoming) is KB027's close, and this entry spends only the [13.48] filing pair of 1:14 with John 2:9; the conception-calling of Luke 1:35 (the διὸ καὶ naming the calling, not an origin) is KB009's close, and the rival's use of it is answered there; the NASB's supplied "Son" at John 1:14 and 1:18 is likewise KB009's ground — this entry rests nothing on the supplied word. The sending and indwelling complex (John 5:19; 12:49; 14:10) stands closed at KB068 and KB011 and is not re-closed here. (6) Unspent ground: the agency (shaliach) reading's absorption texts — John 10:30 and John 14:9 — are not in this set, and this entry answers the agency reading only as far as its own set reaches (Rival (a)); the classical backgrounds the sitting named (the Old Testament dabar, Philo's logos) are background only, opened nowhere in the entry's claims. (7) Framework check: the entry closes the person question — plan or person — and diagrams nothing beyond it. Its sentences are held alongside the Framework's Derived Truths as they stand (Strict Monotheism; Christology of Union) without revising either: the set's own oneness texts are quoted at full strength (Mark 12:29; John 17:3; 1 Cor 8:6), and the close expressly leaves the metaphysics of the Son's relation to the one God where the texts leave it. (8) The proposer's formulations in the close: "the Word is the history of Jesus as the water is the history of the wine" came to the proposer in this sitting (2026-10-29), new to him on his own statement ("that thought has never entered my mind until now"). His witness about it is recorded as he gave it: "I do not claim that sentence; I received it the same way you did. It came to me, and I know from whom it came. I will not take credit." The entry repeats the refusal here so that the record carries the sentence the way he carries it. Received, it still stands in the close on its receipts — the [13.48] filing pair (John 1:14; 2:9) and KB027's subject-continuity — and not as a quotation of any text. His companion sentence, "The Word explains Him," names John 1:18's verb in his own words.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The plan reading — ὁ λόγος is the Father's pre-existent internal plan and expressed intent: eternal as God's purpose is eternal, "with God" as a purpose is with the one who purposes it, "was God" as the plan is God's own, and "became flesh" as the plan was enacted when the man was conceived, sonship beginning at the conception. Stated at its strongest, this is the stub's own former text, held by the writers named at Honesty (2), and its favorite instruments are the definition's "content of God's revelation" (Honesty (3)) and the agency pattern, by which even John 10:30 is heard as the agent speaking for the sender. Answered from the set: the predicates will not reduce to an intention. Association with interrelationship (πρὸς τὸν θεόν, [89.112]) is what a person has with a person; creation's through-whom (διʼ αὐτοῦ, John 1:3) is an agent's place, and the exception clause leaves the mediator himself unmade; the Baptist's πρῶτός μου ἦν confesses a before-me of being, not of scheduling; the conception-calling the reading leans on is closed at KB009, where the angel's διό names the calling and not an origin; and above all the speaker set stands in the way: the reading must re-voice every first-person sentence Jesus speaks about his own prior being — "I have come down from heaven," "where He was before," "before Abraham was born, I am," "the glory which I had with You" — into "it was purposed for me," a re-voicing no text in the set performs and several forbid, since a purpose cannot have had glory with anyone, nor be handled by hands as the life that was with the Father (1 John 1:1–2).
(b) The oneness reading — the Father alone is God: "HEAR, ISRAEL! THE LORD IS OUR GOD, THE LORD IS ONE" (Mark 12:29 — the Shema on Jesus' own lips, the foremost commandment by his own answer); "that they may know You, the only true God (σὲ τὸν μόνον ἀληθινὸν θεόν), and Jesus Christ whom You have sent" (John 17:3); "for us there is only one God, the Father" (1 Cor 8:6). Answered: the entry affirms every one of these verses at its place — two of them stand in its own chain — and the question is only whether they un-say the prologue and the speaker set, which Core Rule 1 forbids. The same prayer that says "the only true God" says, two verses later, "the glory which I had with You before the world existed" (John 17:5): the rival must not let 17:3 spend 17:5, for both are Jesus' words in one breath. And Paul's re-voicing of the confession sets εἷς κύριος Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς διʼ οὗ τὰ πάντα inside the oneness, not outside it (1 Cor 8:6, in the chain): the apostle who wrote "one God, the Father" is the same apostle who wrote the second clause, and the entry takes both of his clauses together, as written.
(c) The notional pre-existence reading — Christ was before the foundation only as foreknown: "He was foreknown before the foundation of the world, but has appeared in these last times" (1 Pet 1:20), as Jeremiah was known before he was formed (Jer 1:5, the reading's background text, named and not quoted — outside this set). What is foreknown exists in the knower's knowledge, and "has appeared" marks the beginning of the man's real existence. Answered: the verse's filing is double, and the reading quotes half of it — προεγνωσμένου stands under [28.6], knowing about something prior to a temporal reference point, AND [30.100], choosing or selecting in advance of some other event, at the same occurrence (Honesty (4)); and even the whole filing predicates the foreknowing of HIM — the Lamb of 1:19, a person as the verse's object — with the manifestation (φανερωθέντος) as its counterpart, the same manifesting 1 John 1:2 predicates of the life that WAS with the Father: what is manifested is presented as having been, before its appearing, somewhere to be manifested from. The entry does not crown this verse for its own side either; it notes that foreknowledge-and-choosing language, at its widest, cannot carry the set's other predicates — ἦν πρὸς τὸν θεόν, εἶχον παρὰ σοί, πρὶν Ἀβραὰμ γενέσθαι ἐγὼ εἰμί — and the close rests on the conjunction, in which this verse stands true at its place.
(d) The subordination reading — the set's own remaining texts describe a made, attested, God-having man: "the Father is greater than I" (John 14:28, ὁ πατὴρ μείζων μού ἐστιν); "My God and your God" (John 20:17, θεόν μου καὶ θεὸν ὑμῶν); "the man Christ Jesus" set between God and mankind as mediator (1 Tim 2:5, ἄνθρωπος Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς); "a Man attested to you by God," the signs being what "God performed through Him" (Acts 2:22); "God has made Him both Lord and Christ—this Jesus whom you crucified" (Acts 2:36, κύριον αὐτὸν καὶ χριστὸν ἐποίησεν ὁ θεός). Answered: every one of these stands in this entry as true at its place, and none of them states the thing the reading needs — that he began. "Made Him both Lord and Christ" is spoken of τοῦτον τὸν Ἰησοῦν ὃν ὑμεῖς ἐσταυρώσατε, this Jesus whom you crucified: a conferral at the exaltation, on a man who is already there to receive it. The attestation of Acts 2:22 is of the ministry's signs, God's working through him in the midst of Israel; it does not reach back to John 1:3, where the same διʼ αὐτοῦ is creation's — the rival must not let the ministry's agency swallow the creation's. A greater Father confessed, a God named as his own, works done through him by the Father: these are exactly what a sent person looks like in these texts — distinction in the mission, which this entry affirms — and they are not evidence about whether the sent one was, before the sending. That question the speaker set answers, in his own voice.
### The Sent One (shalach / Apostolos)

- **ID:** KB011
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** κατανοήσατε τὸν ἀπόστολον καὶ ἀρχιερέα τῆς ὁμολογίας ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦν, ἄνδρα ἀποδεδειγμένον ἀπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ, οἷς ἐποίησεν διʼ αὐτοῦ ὁ θεὸς, ὁ δὲ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοὶ μένων ποιεῖ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ
- **Hebrew keys:** אֶת־מִי אֶשְׁלַח, הִנְנִי שְׁלָחֵנִי
- **Verse keys:** Heb 3:1; Acts 2:22; John 14:10; Isa 6:8
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Jesus is named "the Apostle and High Priest of our confession" (τὸν ἀπόστολον καὶ ἀρχιερέα τῆς ὁμολογίας ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦν, Heb 3:1) — the confession's Sent One. Peter presents this same Jesus as "a man attested by God" (ἄνδρα ἀποδεδειγμένον ἀπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ, Acts 2:22), the miracles and wonders and signs being what "God performed through Him" (οἷς ἐποίησεν διʼ αὐτοῦ ὁ θεὸς). And the Sent One's own account of the works is indwelling, not distance: "the Father, abiding in Me, does His works" (ὁ δὲ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοὶ μένων ποιεῖ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ, John 14:10 — receipts at KB068). Standing as Witness beside the title: the sending pattern of the prophets — "Whom shall I send?" (אֶת־מִי אֶשְׁלַח) and the answer, "Here am I, send me" (הִנְנִי שְׁלָחֵנִי, Isa 6:8).
- **The chain:**
  (1) The title is given, not inferred: the readers are told to consider (κατανοήσατε) "the Apostle and High Priest of our confession: Jesus" (Heb 3:1). *Apostolos* stands as His named title toward the confession, paired with High Priest.
  (2) The agency is stated at Pentecost: Jesus the Nazarene is "a man attested by God to you by miracles and wonders and signs, which God performed through Him in your midst" (Acts 2:22). The attestation runs from God; the doing is God's; the man is the one *through* whom (διʼ αὐτοῦ) God did them.
  (3) The mode is indwelling: "I am in the Father and the Father is in Me" (ἐγὼ ἐν τῷ πατρὶ καὶ ὁ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοί ἐστιν); "the words I speak to you I do not speak from Myself, but the Father, abiding in Me, does His works" (ὁ δὲ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοὶ μένων ποιεῖ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ, John 14:10; the verse's full receipts stand in KB068's set and are pointed to there).
  (4) *Witness:* the pattern is old. The LORD asks, "Whom shall I send (אֶשְׁלַח), and who will go for Us?" and Isaiah answers, "Here am I, send me (שְׁלָחֵנִי)" (Isa 6:8). BDB files this commission use of שלח under God as subject — a person sent with a charge (Exod 3:12; Isa 6:8; Jer 14:14–15), "especially of sending prophet" (Jer 7:25). The pattern stands beside the title as Witness; Isaiah 6:8 does not name Jesus, and no text in this set quotes it of Him.
  (5) The household's own sending on this pattern — "as the Father has sent Me, I also send you" — is closed at KB068 (The Commissioned Household) and is pointed to there, not re-argued here (Craig's scope ruling, 2026-10-24).
- **The close (settled):** The Sent One is not a distant messenger carrying a decree at arm's length. The texts name Him the confession's Apostle, present Him as the man through whom God Himself performed the signs, and give His own explanation: the Father abides in Him and does His works.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The former prose's "ultimate" is not carried: no verse in the set ranks this sending above another; Hebrews 3:1 names the title without a comparison (the Moses comparison of 3:2–3 is outside the set). (2) The set does not diagram the indwelling — how the Father in the Son does the works is stated as fact at John 14:10 and left there. (3) ἀποδεδειγμένον (Acts 2:22) is an attestation word, not a sending word: the verse receipts God's working through the man (οἷς ἐποίησεν διʼ αὐτοῦ ὁ θεὸς) and God's attestation of him (ἀποδεδειγμένον ἀπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ); the sending title itself rests on Hebrews 3:1. (4) The former prose's application to us ("we stop trying to act as remote representatives") is not carried in this entry: by Craig's ruling the household side lives at KB068, where its own receipts stand. (5) The lexical link between the prophets' שלח and the title ἀπόστολος is carried as Witness pattern only — two languages, two testaments; the entry claims the pattern, not a quotation or a word-history.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):**
  (a) The distant-messenger reading — an apostle is an envoy who carries a message from an absent sender and delivers it at arm's length. Answered by John 14:10 and Acts 2:22: the Father abides in the Sent One and does His works in him, and the signs are what "God performed through Him" — as these texts state it, the works are not absence bridged by a courier.
  (b) The independent-agent reading — once sent, the Sent One speaks and acts from Himself, on delegated authority exercised at His own discretion. Answered by John 14:10: "the words I speak to you I do not speak from Myself" (ἀπʼ ἐμαυτοῦ οὐ λαλῶ) — self-originated speech is denied in the same breath that names the abiding Father as the works' doer.
  (c) The incidental-title reading — "Apostle" occurs of Jesus once, so Hebrews 3:1 makes a passing label, not a title the confession holds. Answered by the verse itself: the readers are commanded to consider Him under this title — "the Apostle and High Priest *of our confession*" — a standing designation the household confesses, not a stray word; its weight does not rest on frequency.
  (d) The imported-pattern reading — the Isaiah link is the reader's typology laid over the texts: Isaiah 6:8 speaks of Isaiah's own sending, and joining it to the title of Jesus is the reader's construction, not the texts'. Answered by the entry's own register: Isaiah 6:8 is filed as Witness — the pattern stands beside the title and carries none of the close, which rests on Hebrews 3:1, Acts 2:22, and John 14:10 alone.
### Shaluach, Not Shaliach (שָׁלוּחַ / שָׁלִיחַ)

- **ID:** KB012
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Hebrew keys:** וְאָנֹכִי שָׁלוּחַ אֵלַיִךְ קָשֶׁה
- **Verse keys:** 1 Kings 14:6
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** From the root שלח, Biblical Hebrew names the sent person himself by the verb's
  passive participle, not by an agent noun: Ahijah names himself שָׁלוּחַ,
  "sent" (1 Kings 14:6) — the Qal passive participle of שלח; the noun שָׁלִיחַ
  — the word the stub warns has been carried back into these texts as a
  commissioned agent's title — has no entry in BDB's Biblical Hebrew; where the
  form stands in the biblical corpus it is Biblical Aramaic, and still a
  passive participle (שְׁלִיחַ, "sent," Ezra 7:14; Dan 5:24); and the nouns
  this root does form name the sending, never a title for the sent person:
  שִׁלֻּחִים, a dismissal or a parting gift; מִשְׁלַחַת, a discharge or a
  deputation; מִשְׁלוֹחַ, an outstretching or a sending of portions.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Kings 14:6: וְאָנֹכִי שָׁלוּחַ אֵלַיִךְ קָשֶׁה — Ahijah at the door,
    unmasking Jeroboam's disguised wife: "Come in, wife of Jeroboam... and I
    am sent to you [with] harsh [tidings]." The prophet names himself by
    the passive participle of שלח: he is one who has been sent.
  - BDB, שלח (H7971): the Qal participle line files the active שֹׁלֵחַ and
    the passive שָׁלוּחַ together, anchoring שָׁלוּחַ at this verse
    ("שָׁלוּחַ 1Kin 14:6"). The dictionary's own filing at the verse is the
    participle, under the verb's article — there is no noun article for an
    agent for it to be filed under. (The dictionary's judgment, cited as
    such.)
  - BDB has no Biblical Hebrew entry for שָׁלִיחַ at all. In BDB's Biblical
    Aramaic article שְׁלַח the form does stand — labeled Aramaic — as
    "Passive participle שְׁלִיחַ 7:14; Dan 5:24" — the participle itself
    standing in the biblical text, at both verses. An Aramaic participle
    meaning "sent" is the same verbal shape, not a Hebrew office noun.
  - HALOT, שִׁלֻּחִים (pp. 1505–6): one of the nouns Biblical Hebrew forms
    from this root names (1) dismissal (of a wife) — אַחַר שִׁלּוּחֶיהָ,
    "after her dismissal" (Ex 18:2); (2) a parting gift, dowry (1 Kings
    9:16); (3) farewell gifts (Mic 1:14). The same article's Mishnaic
    Hebrew noun שִׁלּוּחַ is likewise "dismissal; release." (HALOT's
    senses are its judgments, on verses cited in its article, not quoted
    here.)
  - BDB's other noun articles from this root run the same way.
    מִשְׁלַחַת (H4917): "discharge" (Eccl 8:8), and in construct at
    Ps 78:49, "a deputation (or sending) of angels of evil" — the noun
    names the sending as a collective body, and the sent ones in that
    very phrase are named by another root's noun (מַלְאֲכֵי).
    מִשְׁלַח / מִשְׁלוֹחַ (H4916): an "outstretching" (of the hand,
    Deut 12:7 and parallels) and a "sending" of portions (Est 9:19, 22).
    (Both articles are the dictionary's judgments, on verses cited
    there, not quoted here.)
  - The commission pattern the participle serves — God as subject, the
    accusative of the person, "especially of sending prophet" — is BDB's
    filing under the verb (H7971) and is receipted at KB011, which closes
    the Son's title on the Greek side (ἀπόστολος, Heb 3:1) with Isa 6:8
    standing as Witness. This entry fences the Hebrew word only; what the
    sending means for the Sent One and for the household is KB011's and
    KB068's ground, not re-closed here.
- **The close (settled):** From שלח, the Hebrew Bible's word for the
  sent person himself is the passive participle of the verb — one having
  been sent. No noun from this root names the sent one as an agent
  holding an office, and the later noun שָׁלִיחַ cannot be carried back
  into these texts without importing what their own vocabulary does not
  contain: this root's nouns cover the sending's act, its gift, and the
  deputation it sends, and the participle keeps the sent one named by
  the sending itself.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The fence rests on one quoted verse and on the
  dictionaries' coverage stated as coverage: BDB's having no entry is a
  fact about BDB's Biblical Hebrew, and the noun filings are the
  dictionaries' judgments on their cited verses (HALOT on שִׁלֻּחִים:
  Ex 18:2; 1 Kings 9:16; Mic 1:14; BDB on מִשְׁלַחַת and מִשְׁלוֹחַ:
  Eccl 8:8; Ps 78:49; Deut 12:7; Est 9:19, 22), not pastes. A
  dictionary's silence fences an import; it builds no doctrine by
  itself, and this entry builds none on it — the positive receipt is
  the participle standing in its verse. (2) The entry fences the root
  שלח only. Other roots name sent ones with nouns of their own —
  מַלְאָךְ, "messenger" (BDB H4397), is the standing example — and
  nothing here touches them; the fence is against carrying the later
  שָׁלִיחַ back into שלח texts, not against every Hebrew word for a
  messenger. (3) 1 Kings 14:6 names Ahijah's errand; the verse alone
  does not define every biblical sending — the verb's wider commission
  use is filed under the verb (BDB H7971) and receipted at KB011.
  (4) No receipt in this set covers what שָׁלִיחַ came to mean in its
  own later literature; the entry fences the word's import without
  defining it there, and the "office" characterization in rival (a) is
  the stub's warning, answered on the distribution. (5) The stub's
  payload is retired here: "relational agent," an authority "rooted
  entirely in indwelling union and unbroken dependency," and the
  warning against systematic theology's motives — motives are not
  receipts, and the union the stub pointed at is KB011's and KB068's
  ground. This entry keeps only the stub's lexical fence, which the
  receipts carry. (6) The stub's title, "Relational Agency," named that
  retired payload and is retired with it; the entry is titled by its
  two forms and its conclusion. (7) English renderings of 1 Kings 14:6
  supply a noun after "harsh" ("tidings," "message"); the Hebrew
  קָשֶׁה stands without one, and nothing here rests on the supplied
  word.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The harmless-shorthand reading —
  שָׁלִיחַ is simply the noun of the same root, and calling a biblical
  messenger a shaliach says nothing the texts do not. Answered: the
  texts had the root and formed their nouns from it for the act, the
  gift, and the deputation, never for the person; the person they name
  by the participle. And the later noun is not these texts' word in any
  case — carrying it back settles by vocabulary a question the texts
  answer by distribution. The fence is the texts' own distribution,
  not a preference. (b) The argument-from-silence objection — a
  dictionary's missing entry proves nothing about a language. Answered:
  agreed, and the entry needs no more from the silence than a fence:
  its positive side is the participle in its verse and the act, gift,
  and deputation senses of the nouns that do exist. What the silence
  bars is only the claim that the office noun is biblical. (c) The
  Aramaic objection — שְׁלִיחַ stands in Ezra and Daniel, so the form
  is biblical after all. Answered: it stands there labeled Biblical
  Aramaic, and it stands there as a passive participle, "sent," at
  both its verses (Ezra 7:14; Dan 5:24). An Aramaic participle
  confirms the pattern; it does not supply a Hebrew agent noun.

### The Most High in Daniel (Illay and Elyonin)

- **ID:** KB013
- **Facet of:** none
- **Hebrew keys:** עִלָּי, עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין, עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן
- **Verse keys:** Daniel 7:13, 7:14, 7:18, 7:22, 7:25, 7:27; Psalm 89:27
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** In Daniel the exalted title-forms both name God — עִלָּיָא, "the Most High" (Daniel 7:25), and עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין, the plural form BDB files as "plural of God," which stands only inside "saints of the Most High" (Daniel 7:18, 22, 25, 27) — while the one like a son of man who approaches the Ancient of Days is given dominion, honor, and a kingdom (Daniel 7:13–14) and is never given the title; the singular עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן is predicated of the Davidic king only with its modifier standing, "of the kings of the earth" (Psalm 89:27).
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionaries' own judgments, cited as such): BDB (H5943) files עִלָּי as an Aramaic adjective, "highest," always in its emphatic form, in two groups: "the Most High God" where it stands with אֱלָהָא, at Daniel 3:26, 32 and 5:18, 21; and standing alone, "the Most High," at Daniel 4:14, 21, 22, 29, 31 and 7:25 — the set’s verse in the alone group, as the NASB’s rendering there, "speak against the Most High," also shows. BDB (H5945, in the Aramaic note closing the entry) files עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין as the plural of the same word, expressly "plural of God" (a double plural), standing in קַדִּישֵׁ֖י עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין at Daniel 7:18, 22, 25, 27 and nowhere else. UBS Hebrew files the singular עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן as, among its senses, an epithet for the God of Israel, "Most High," and BDB (H5945, sense I.3) files it "of Davidic king exalted above monarchs," citing Psalm 89:28 in the Hebrew numbering (= 89:27 in the English).
  - Daniel 7:25 — both forms stand in one verse, and both name God: וּמִלִּ֗ין לְצַ֤ד עִלָּיָא יְמַלִּ֔ל וּלְקַדִּישֵׁ֥י עֶלְיוֹנִ֖ין יְבַלֵּ֑א, "he will speak against the Most High and wear down the saints of the Highest One." The horn's two objects are the Most High and the Most High's saints; no second title-bearer enters the verse.
  - The four places of עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין — the form never stands alone and never titles anyone: at 7:18 the saints of the Highest One receive the kingdom (קַדִּישֵׁ֖י עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין); at 7:22 judgment is passed in their favor (לְקַדִּישֵׁ֖י עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין); at 7:27 the kingdom is given to "the people of the saints of the Highest One" (קַדִּישֵׁ֖י עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין). Every occurrence is the possessive phrase: the word names whose the saints are.
  - Daniel 7:13–14 — what the approaching one actually receives: כְּבַ֥ר אֱנָ֖שׁ, "One like a son of man was coming, And He came up to the Ancient of Days And was presented before Him"; וְלֵ֨הּ יְהִ֤יב שָׁלְטָן֙ וִיקָ֣ר וּמַלְכ֔וּ, "And to Him was given dominion, Honor, and a kingdom." Dominion, honor, a kingdom, and the service of the peoples are given; across the chapter's complete distribution of both forms, no title-form is predicated of him.
  - Psalm 89:27 — the same word on the king, at its own place: אַף־אָ֭נִי בְּכ֣וֹר אֶתְּנֵ֑הוּ עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן לְמַלְכֵי־אָֽרֶץ, "I will also make him My firstborn, The highest of the kings of the earth." Here the singular עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן is predicated of the Davidic king by God's own appointment, with the modifier standing — highest *of the kings of the earth* — a true statement at its place, which neither transfers Daniel's plural form to the approaching one nor is erased by Daniel's use (Core Rules 1 and 8: each statement true at its own place).
- **The close (settled):** The distribution settles the title question in Daniel: עִלָּיָא names God, עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין is the plural-of-God form inside "saints of the Most High," and the approaching one's portion in the chapter is a given dominion, not a received title. A reading that hands עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין to the one like a son of man as his distinct title reads against both the dictionary's filing and every occurrence in the chapter.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Scope and form: this entry is a datum, not a closure of Daniel 7's theology; its set is the Daniel 7 excerpts and Psalm 89:27 as pasted, and its claim covers Daniel's use of the two forms plus the one Psalm place — the word's wider distribution (UBS Hebrew cites 57 references for עֶ֝לְי֗וֹן) is unexamined and unspent. (2) The stub, retired: the live stub (titled "The Exalted Title (Illay vs. Elyonin)") claimed the Sent One "attains the distinct title of the 'Highest One' (Elyonin)" on approaching the Ancient of Days; no verse in the set carries that claim, and the stub's remaining materials — perfection through the suffering of the flesh, and the family's allegiance to the Sent One as supreme Lord of the household — were imported from outside the chapter and are dropped, not carried. The retitling is proposed with this draft for Craig's ruling at promotion: the "vs." framed two rival title-bearers, and the datum finds one God under both forms. (3) "Plural of God" is BDB's filing (H5945), cited as the dictionary's judgment, not as this entry's coinage; BDB's own note calls the form a double plural. (4) No rendering is charged: the NASB's "the Most High" (7:25) and "the Highest One" (in the saints' phrase) are two English phrases for two forms of the one God's name, and the datum lives in the Aramaic forms, which the English plurals and singulars do not show — the boundary is stated so a reader of the English alone is not blamed for missing what his page does not print. (5) Numbering: Psalm 89:27 in the English is 89:28 in the LHB (the psalm's title counts as its first verse); the Hebrew receipt is banked under its LHB number so the set can be re-walked. (6) The Ancient of Days (עַתִּ֤יק יֽוֹמַיָּא֙, Daniel 7:13, 22) is a separate form, quoted only as the scene's grammar; this entry makes no claim on it.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The stub's own reading — the Father alone occupies the absolute position of "Most High" (Illay), while the Sent One, having approached the Ancient of Days, attains the distinct title of the "Highest One" (Elyonin), a title carrying no earthly modifier, unlike the Davidic king's in Psalm 89, which was limited to the kings of the earth. Stated at its strongest, the reading leans on the chapter's real drama: one figure does approach, and is exalted, and the saints' phrase does sound like a title in English. Answered on the receipts: עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין is filed by BDB as plural of God and occurs only inside the possessive phrase "saints of the Most High" — it names the saints' God at all four places, including 7:25, where it stands beside עִלָּיָא in the same verse; the approaching one is given dominion, honor, and a kingdom (7:14), and no verse predicates the form of him; and the Psalm's contrast inverts on inspection — Psalm 89:27 predicates the singular of the king precisely with the modifier the reading treats as the lesser thing, while Daniel's form carries no modifier because it is not the king's title at all but God's name in the plural form. (b) The English-driven reading — "the Most High" (7:25) and "the Highest One" (7:18, 22, 27) as two titles, and so possibly two bearers. Answered on Daniel 7:25 itself: both forms stand in the one verse, the horn speaking against the one and wearing down the other's saints, and the dictionary files both forms of the one God (H5943; H5945's Aramaic note); the two English phrases render an emphatic singular and a plural form, not two gods and not a title transferred.
### Ēgerthē (ἠγέρθη — Was Raised, and "I Will Raise It")

- **ID:** KB015
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἠγέρθη, ἐγερῶ, ἐξουσίαν, ἐντολὴν, ἐγείραντος
- **Verse keys:** Luke 24:6; John 2:19–22; John 10:17–18; Rom 8:11
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The resurrection is spoken of both ways, and neither voice cancels the other: the narration says Jesus was raised (ἠγέρθη, Luke 24:6; John 2:22), and Jesus Himself claims the act — "I will raise it" (ἐγερῶ αὐτόν, John 2:19), laying down His life of Himself, with authority to take it up again (John 10:17–18) — while naming that authority's source in the same breath: "this command I received from My Father" (ταύτην τὴν ἐντολὴν ἔλαβον παρὰ τοῦ πατρός μου). Paul states the raising actively: "the one who raised Jesus from the dead" (τοῦ ἐγείραντος τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐκ νεκρῶν, Rom 8:11).
- **The chain:**
  - Luke 24:6: οὐκ ἔστιν ὧδε, ἀλλʼ ἠγέρθη — "He is not here, but was raised." ἠγέρθη is aorist passive: it states the event and names no agent. The dictionary files the resurrection use of ἐγείρω at "to cause someone to live again after having once died" (UBS 23.94 — the dictionary's filing, cited as its judgment).
  - John 2:19: λύσατε τὸν ναὸν τοῦτον καὶ ἐν τρισὶν ἡμέραις ἐγερῶ αὐτόν — "Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it." ἐγερῶ is future active, first person, spoken by Jesus of the raising itself.
  - John 2:21–22: what the saying speaks of is John's own narration, not a reader's guess: ἐκεῖνος δὲ ἔλεγεν περὶ τοῦ ναοῦ τοῦ σώματος αὐτοῦ — "He was speaking of the temple of His body" — and the event, when it came, is narrated by the same evangelist in the passive: ὅτε οὖν ἠγέρθη ἐκ νεκρῶν — "when He was raised from the dead." Active saying and passive narration stand within four verses of one paragraph.
  - John 10:17–18: the laying-down and the taking-up are both His: τίθημι τὴν ψυχήν μου, ἵνα πάλιν λάβω αὐτήν — "I lay down My life, so that I may take it again"; οὐδεὶς αἴρει αὐτὴν ἀπʼ ἐμοῦ, ἀλλʼ ἐγὼ τίθημι αὐτὴν ἀπʼ ἐμαυτοῦ — "No one takes it from Me, but I lay it down of Myself"; ἐξουσίαν ἔχω θεῖναι αὐτήν, καὶ ἐξουσίαν ἔχω πάλιν λαβεῖν αὐτήν — "I have authority to lay it down, and I have authority to take it up again." Then the source of the authority, in the same sentence: ταύτην τὴν ἐντολὴν ἔλαβον παρὰ τοῦ πατρός μου — "this command I received from My Father."
  - Rom 8:11 (on file, KB024 set): τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἐγείραντος τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐκ νεκρῶν — "the Spirit of the one who raised Jesus from the dead" — the raising stated in the active voice ("the one who raised"), naming Jesus as its object; and the verse's second half spends it on the readers in its own words: ὁ ἐγείρας Χριστὸν ἐκ νεκρῶν ζῳοποιήσει καὶ τὰ θνητὰ σώματα ὑμῶν — "the one who raised Christ from the dead will also give life to your mortal bodies."
- **The close (settled):** Read together, the set closes the "independent initiative" question from both sides. Against pure passivity: Jesus' own words give Him real agency in His raising — an active verb for the act itself (John 2:19) and a doubled ἐξουσία over the laying-down and the taking-up (John 10:18). Against independence: that agency is not self-originated — in the sentence that claims it, He calls it a command received from His Father; beside it the narration's passive stands unretracted, and Paul speaks of 'the one who raised Jesus' (Rom 8:11) in the active voice. What the conjunction settles is origination: the Son's part in His resurrection is exercised as received authority, never as an act apart from the Father. It settles no mechanism beyond that.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) ἠγέρθη in Luke 24:6 and John 2:22 names no agent; the passive is a datum of voice, not a proof of who acted — the stub's "the passive confirms that Jesus did not raise Himself" is retired. Where this entry states active agency or names its source, a text does so: Rom 8:11 states the raising actively ("the one who raised Jesus from the dead"), and John 10:18 names the command's source ("from My Father"). (2) The set gives no mechanism for how the raising and the Son's taking-up fit together; the close covers origination and independence only. (3) Luke's verse as pasted ends at τῇ Γαλιλαίᾳ; the entry rests on its first clause only. (4) The stub's remaining glosses are retired: "the ultimate witness," "the Father vindicated His Sent One," "absolute confidence," "out of the dust," "will sustain every member of the Son's body" — Rom 8:11's own statement about our bodies is quoted in the chain and claimed no further. (5) The entry's former title, "The Divine Passive," is retired with the claim it named: the passive settles nothing about who acted (clause (1)), and a title may not teach what the entry retires. The heading now names the two voices the close holds together.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The self-resurrection reading — Jesus raised Himself, simply: His own verbs say so (ἐγερῶ, John 2:19; "authority to take it again," John 10:18), and the passive texts are narrative shorthand for the same self-act. Answered: those verses are this entry's own chain and stand at full strength; what the reading adds — origination in Himself, apart from the Father — is what its strongest proof text refuses, for John 10:18 ends by naming the authority a command received from the Father. The reading stands only by cutting its own verse in half. (b) The no-agency reading — ἠγέρθη shows the raising was done to Him entirely by another, and the John sayings speak figuratively, or only of His death being voluntary. Answered: John 2:19's ἐγερῶ is an active, first-person verb for the raising itself, and John assigns its object to His body (2:21); John 10:18 claims authority over the taking-up (πάλιν λαβεῖν), not only over the laying-down. Making both figurative explains the texts away rather than reading them. (c) The voice-settles-it reading — the passive grammar by itself proves the Father was the agent (the retired stub's own move). Answered: a passive names no agent; in this set the agent is settled where texts name one, not from voice — the discipline applied at KB025, where the passive was stated with no agent supplied and no metaphysics built on it. (d) The contradiction reading — passive narration and active sayings cannot both be true, so one register must be discounted. Answered: John prints both within four verses (2:19; 2:22); the evangelist who recorded the saying also chose the passive for the event. No verse is discounted to save the other; the conjunction is the reading.

### The Physical Witness (Heard, Seen, Handled)

- **ID:** KB016
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἀκηκόαμεν, ἑωράκαμεν, ἐθεασάμεθα, ἐψηλάφησαν, ἐφανερώθη, ἑωρακὼς, μεμαρτύρηκεν, ψηλαφήσατέ, θεωρεῖτε, σάρκα, ὀστέα
- **Verse keys:** 1 John 1:1–3, 5; John 19:35; Luke 24:39

- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The witness in these texts grounds itself in named perception: what we have heard and what we have seen stand in perfects wherever the proclamation is summed up (ἀκηκόαμεν, ἑωράκαμεν, 1 John 1:1–3, 5; ὁ ἑωρακὼς μεμαρτύρηκεν, John 19:35), while the beholding, the handling, and the manifesting stand in aorists (ἐθεασάμεθα, ἐψηλάφησαν, ἐφανερώθη, 1 John 1:1–2), and at Luke 24:39 the handling is invited in an aorist imperative (ψηλαφήσατέ), and the physical content is carried by the words themselves — eyes, hands, flesh and bones — not by any tense.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 John 1:1: the proclamation opens on perception, sorted by form: ὃ ἀκηκόαμεν — "what we have heard" (perfect); ὃ ἑωράκαμεν τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς ἡμῶν — "what we have seen with our eyes" (perfect, the organ named); ὃ ἐθεασάμεθα — "what we beheld" (aorist); καὶ αἱ χεῖρες ἡμῶν ἐψηλάφησαν — "and our hands handled" (aorist, the organ named). UBS files ψηλαφάω at this verse at 24.76, "to touch by feeling and handling" — the dictionary's judgment.
