  - 1 John 1:2–3: the event stands in aorists — καὶ ἡ ζωὴ ἐφανερώθη, "and the life was manifested," twice — and the perception is summed again in perfects for the proclaiming: ὃ ἑωράκαμεν καὶ ἀκηκόαμεν, ἀπαγγέλλομεν καὶ ὑμῖν — "what we have seen and heard we proclaim also to you" (perfects, then the present ἀπαγγέλλομεν). The stated end of the proclaiming is ἵνα καὶ ὑμεῖς κοινωνίαν ἔχητε μεθʼ ἡμῶν — "so that you also may have fellowship with us."
  - 1 John 1:5: the same perfect carries the message itself: ἡ ἀγγελία ἣν ἀκηκόαμεν ἀπʼ αὐτοῦ — "the message which we have heard from Him" — heard, and standing as what they announce (ἀναγγέλλομεν, present).
  - John 19:35: the testimony verse pairs the two perfects: καὶ ὁ ἑωρακὼς μεμαρτύρηκεν — "and the one who has seen has testified" — and sets beside them the verdict: καὶ ἀληθινὴ αὐτοῦ ἐστιν ἡ μαρτυρία, "and his testimony is true." UBS files μαρτυρέω at this verse at 33.262, "to provide information about a person or an event concerning which the speaker has direct knowledge" — the dictionary's judgment. The verse's stated aim: ἵνα καὶ ὑμεῖς πιστεύσητε — "so that you also may believe."
  - Luke 24:39: the risen Jesus Himself invites the handling, in aorist imperatives: ψηλαφήσατέ με καὶ ἴδετε — "touch Me and see" — and names what is to be perceived: ὅτι πνεῦμα σάρκα καὶ ὀστέα οὐκ ἔχει καθὼς ἐμὲ θεωρεῖτε ἔχοντα — "because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you observe Me having." The same verb as 1 John 1:1's handling, filed at the same sense (ψηλαφάω, 24.76); θεωρεῖτε is filed at 24.14, "to observe something with continuity and attention."
- **The close (settled):** Read together, the set closes the witness's ground and the tense question together. The ground is perception, named in words with its organs — heard, seen with our eyes, beheld, handled with our hands — and the testimony verse pairs its seeing and its testifying in perfects and calls the testimony true beside them. The settlement on tense is negative, and it is enough: the forms divide as the chain shows (perfects for the hearing, the seeing, and the testifying in the summaries; aorists for the beholding, the handling, and the manifesting; an imperative for the invitation), but the beholding itself stands in the aorist, so no tense-role rule survives — and no tense prints the stub's payload: the aorist does not certify its events "undeniable," and the perfect does not print an "unbroken action" or a "flow of life." What the testimony aims at, both testimony texts say in their own words: belief (John 19:35) and fellowship (1 John 1:3).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The claim is the distribution as the chain shows it in these texts; it is not a metaphysic of the Greek perfect, and another text's perfects are another text's question — the discipline of KB025, where a form was stated for what it is and nothing built on it beyond the verse. (2) The set closes what the witness texts claim and how they ground the claim; it does not itself prove the events happened. (3) Luke 24:39's ψηλαφήσατέ is an imperative inviting the handling, not a report that the disciples handled Him; the handling reported as done is 1 John 1:1's ἐψηλάφησαν, "our hands handled." (4) 1 John 1 as pasted runs to v. 10; the entry rests on vv. 1–3 and the ἀκηκόαμεν at v. 5; the rest is unspent and unquoted. (5) The stub's glosses are retired: "confirms the specific, undeniable, and physical events," "an unbroken action with an ongoing, living result," "a continuous, present-tense flow of life," "not in mystical shadows or philosophical ideas," "the touchable, observable reality of the Son… for us as branches in this current moment" — the branches image belongs to another text, not this set. (6) The UBS filings are cited as the dictionary's judgments (ψηλαφάω 24.76 at both 1 John 1:1 and Luke 24:39; μαρτυρέω 33.262 at John 19:35; θεωρέω 24.14 at Luke 24:39), not as the entry's own claims. (7) The entry's former subtitle, "(Perfect and Aorist Tenses)," is retired with the tense reading it pointed at: the entry concludes on the perception the texts name — heard, seen, handled — and a title may not point where the entry does not go.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The tense-theology reading (the retired stub's own) — the perfect tense itself teaches abiding results flowing from the events, and the aorist certifies them as punctiliar and undeniable. Answered: the set's own distribution refuses it — in a single verse (1 John 1:1) the seeing stands in the perfect and the beholding and handling in the aorist, beside the hearing in the perfect; the forms divide as the chain shows and by no rule that carries the theology, and the payload words ("unbroken," "flow of life," "undeniable") are filed under no form in the set. (b) The stylistic-accident reading — the perfects are mere variation and ground nothing. Answered: the entry claims only the placement the texts repeat — every summary of the proclaimed content puts the hearing and the seeing in perfects (1 John 1:3; 1:5), and John 19:35 pairs its seeing and its testifying in perfects and calls the testimony true beside them. That repeated placement is what the texts show; it is a description, not a rule — the same prologue that sums the perception in perfects sets the beholding in an aorist (1 John 1:1). (c) The message-not-body reading — the "word of life" witnessed is a teaching or an experience, not a handled body. Answered: the perception verbs take bodily organs and a bodily object — τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς ἡμῶν … αἱ χεῖρες ἡμῶν ἐψηλάφησαν — and Luke 24:39 names σάρκα καὶ ὀστέα as what a πνεῦμα does not have and what the witnesses are invited to touch on Him. Whatever else these texts claim, the witness presents its object as what eyes saw and hands handled (1 John 1:1), and, read with Luke 24:39, as flesh and bones invited to be touched — not as an idea. (d) The coercion reading — eyewitness testimony in these forms leaves denial no room ("undeniable"). Answered: both testimony texts name a response to be made, not a compulsion: ἵνα καὶ ὑμεῖς πιστεύσητε (John 19:35), ἵνα καὶ ὑμεῖς κοινωνίαν ἔχητε (1 John 1:3). The witness invites belief and fellowship; it does not claim to remove the hearer's choice.

### Eternal Life Is Knowing (John 17:3)

- **ID:** KB017
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** αὕτη δέ ἐστιν ἡ αἰώνιος ζωὴ, γινώσκωσιν, σὲ τὸν μόνον ἀληθινὸν θεὸν, ὃν ἀπέστειλας Ἰησοῦν Χριστόν
- **Verse keys:** John 17:3
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** John 17:3 defines eternal life in Jesus' own sentence — "And this is eternal life, that they may know You, the only true God, and Jesus Christ whom You have sent" — and the knowing it names is filed at this verse as learning to know a person through direct personal experience, implying a continuity of relationship, not as possessing information.
- **Chain of evidence:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, verified per occurrence against the local UBS data, `lexlook.py refs`): every key word of the verse carries exactly one filing at this occurrence, and the list is kept whole beside the chain — ζωή [23.88], gloss: life; αἰώνιος [67.96], "pertaining to an unlimited duration of time," gloss: eternal; γινώσκω [27.18], "to learn to know a person through direct personal experience, implying a continuity of relationship," gloss: to know, to become acquainted with, to be familiar with; μόνος [58.50], "the only entity in a class"; ἀληθινός [70.3], "pertaining to being real and not imaginary," gloss: true; ἀποστέλλω [15.66], "to cause someone to depart for a particular purpose," gloss: to send. No key word carries a second filing at this verse.
  - The definition frame: αὕτη δέ ἐστιν ἡ αἰώνιος ζωὴ — "and this is eternal life." The sentence is a definition in the present tense (ἐστιν, "is"), spoken by Jesus in prayer: what follows the "is" is what the life *is*, stated by the one who is praying, not a description of how the life is obtained or of where it is spent.
  - The content clause: ἵνα γινώσκωσιν — "that they may know." The ἵνα clause gives the definition its content, and its verb is γινώσκωσιν (present subjunctive of γινώσκω). At this verse the dictionary files that verb [27.18] — learning to know *a person*, through direct personal experience, with a continuity of relationship implied inside the filed definition itself (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such). The information sense of the same lemma, [28.1] "to possess information about," is not filed at this verse.
  - The first object: σὲ τὸν μόνον ἀληθινὸν θεὸν — "You, the only true God." The knowing's first object is a person addressed in the second person (σὲ) — the Father, whom Jesus is addressing throughout this prayer (the prayer's frame names Him at 17:5, σύ, πάτερ; the frame stands as context, the definition rests on 17:3 alone) — and He is named with the verse's own exclusives: μόνος [58.50], the only entity in His class, and ἀληθινός [70.3], real and not imaginary.
  - The second object: καὶ ὃν ἀπέστειλας Ἰησοῦν Χριστόν — "and Jesus Christ whom You have sent." One verb carries both objects: the same knowing that has the only true God for its object has Jesus Christ for its object too, named in the same clause by his sentness — ἀπέστειλας, from ἀποστέλλω [15.66], to send for a particular purpose. The definition does not know the Father instead of the sent one, or the sent one instead of the Father; it knows both, in one sentence, under one verb.
- **The close (settled):** The verse settles what eternal life *is*, in the only form a definition can settle it: by Jesus' own sentence, the life is a knowing — of the only true God, and of Jesus Christ whom He sent — and the knowing is the dictionary-filed kind: acquaintance with a person through direct personal experience, implying a continuity of relationship. Nothing in the sentence defines the life as information possessed about God, as a possession held apart from the knowing, or as a duration whose content is left unnamed: the duration word (αἰώνιος) modifies a life whose stated content is the knowing. What the definition is, this verse states whole; the entry claims the definition and nothing built on it.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its substance is sorted, not carried: the Son's authority dependent on the Father's abiding presence is closed at KB068 (John 5:19, 30; 12:49–50; 14:10) and KB011 (John 14:10; Acts 2:22), whose rival chain already answers the delegated-independence reading by name; life lived through another at both steps is KB068's chain on John 6:56–57; our abiding and the apart-from-Him nothing are KB068 on John 15:4–5; the Father and the Son dwelling with the believer is KB024 (John 14:23; Rom 8:9–11); Christ our life and the not-I are KB047 and KB046 (Gal 2:20 at both). The stub's "unbroken" and its "grafted directly into that same flow" are not carried in any form: "unbroken" is filed under no sense in this verse, and grafting is Romans 11's picture, already retired as an import at KB019's Honesty (1). The stub's title, "Abiding in the Father’s Life vs. Delegated Power," is retired with the framing it names; the new title names this entry's datum. (2) Continuity stands exactly where the filing puts it and no further: it is the *dictionary's definition* of the sense filed at this verse that implies a continuity of relationship, cited as the dictionary's judgment; the verse itself does not state that the knowing cannot be broken or lost, and no security or perseverance claim is made here — and no continuity claim in this entry rests on the verb's tense, the present subjunctive being reported as the clause's form, nothing more (the discipline of KB016). (3) The verse does not diagram the relation of the Father and the Son: it predicates "the only true God" of the One addressed and sentness of Jesus Christ, and holds both as objects of the one knowing; the person question those words raise is closed at KB010 on its own set and is pointed to there, not re-argued here, and the metaphysics of the relation stays where KB010 left it. (4) The neighbouring verses stand unspent: John 17:4 (the work accomplished) and 17:5 (the glory had with the Father before the world existed) were pasted with this verse for KB010 and stand on file as context; no claim in this entry rests on either — 17:5 is used once, as frame only, to name the prayer's addressee in the chain — and 17:5's spend is KB010's. (5) This entry closes this sentence's definition, not every statement Scripture makes about eternal life: the gift statement (Rom 6:23, eternal life as God's gracious gift in Christ Jesus), the hidden-and-manifested life (KB047, Col 3:3–4), and the life that was with the Father and was manifested (1 John 1:2, in KB010's and KB016's sets) each stand at their own places at full strength, unexamined here; no one of them is flattened into this definition, and this definition is not flattened into any of them.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The information reading — to "know" God and Christ is to hold true doctrine about them, and eternal life is constituted by correct belief held as knowledge. Answered by the dictionary's own assignment, cited as its judgment: at this verse γινώσκω is filed [27.18], learning to know a person through direct personal experience, and is not filed under [28.1], possessing information — the informational sense exists in the word's range and stands at other verses, and a sense filed at one verse is not the sense at another; the verse's objects are also persons addressed and named (σὲ … Ἰησοῦν Χριστόν), not propositions. (b) The future-prize reading — eternal life is a state or place entered after death, and this sentence names knowledge as merely the entry requirement for it. Answered by the sentence's own build: it is a definition in the present tense (αὕτη δέ ἐστιν), and the ἵνα clause gives the life's content, not a precondition stated beside it; the sentence names no place, no entry event, and no post-mortem state, and the future manifestation of the life that other texts state (KB047) is not denied here — it is simply not this sentence's statement, and a definition may not be re-read as an entry requirement on the strength of texts outside this entry's set. (c) The feeling reading — because the knowing is experiential, eternal life is a religious feeling or intensity of experience. Answered by the filed definition itself: the experience inside [27.18] is the means of acquaintance — "through direct personal experience," glossed "to become acquainted with, to be familiar with" — and its objects are the two named persons; no feeling, state, or intensity is named by the verse or by the filing, and an acquaintance is not a mood. (d) The Father-only reading — the knowing that is eternal life terminates on the only true God, Jesus Christ being named only as the one sent to announce it, not as himself an object of the knowing. Answered by the clause's grammar: one verb, γινώσκωσιν, governs both accusative objects joined by καί — σὲ τὸν μόνον ἀληθινὸν θεὸν and ὃν ἀπέστειλας Ἰησοῦν Χριστόν — so the sent one stands inside the definition as known, not outside it as its herald; a reading that removes him from the object must remove him from the sentence. (e) The unlosable reading — because the filed sense implies continuity, this verse teaches that the knowing, once begun, cannot be broken or lost. Answered by the placement of the continuity: it is an implication inside a dictionary definition, cited as the dictionary's judgment, and the verse states no unbreakability, names no guarantee, and addresses no question of loss; the entry carries the continuity exactly at the strength the filing gives it and crowns nothing beyond it (Honesty (2)).

### Epiousios (ἐπιούσιος): Daily, For Today (Matt 6:11; Luke 11:3)

- **ID:** KB018
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** τὸν ἄρτον ἡμῶν τὸν ἐπιούσιον, δὸς ἡμῖν σήμερον, δίδου ἡμῖν τὸ καθʼ ἡμέραν, παντὶ ῥήματι ἐκπορευομένῳ διὰ στόματος θεοῦ, ὁ ἄρτος τῆς ζωῆς, τὸν σταυρὸν αὐτοῦ καθʼ ἡμέραν
- **Hebrew keys:** הַמָּן, מָן הוּא, כָּל־מוֹצָא פִי־יְהוָה, הַלֶּחֶם
- **Verse keys:** Matt 6:11; Luke 11:3; and, in the hearing register only, Matt 6:9; Luke 11:2; Deut 8:3; Exod 16:15; Exod 16:19; Exod 16:20; Exod 16:23; Exod 16:24; Matt 4:3; Matt 4:4; Luke 9:23; Luke 9:24; John 6:31; John 6:32; John 6:33; John 6:34; John 6:35; John 6:48; John 6:49; John 6:50; John 6:51; John 6:58
- **Kind:** datum, with the proposer’s hearing carried labeled in its own register below the datum’s chain
- **Claim:** ἐπιούσιος — the word by which the bread is asked in the prayer Jesus taught — occurs in the New Testament only at Matthew 6:11 and Luke 11:3, and the dictionary files it under two senses at both places, [67.183] "pertaining to recurring on a daily basis" and [67.206] "pertaining to a day which is the same as the day of a discourse," so the petition’s bread is named by a time word the evidence leaves standing two ways at once: the bread that comes daily, and the bread for today.
- **Chain of evidence:**
  - The word’s whole set: ἐπιούσιος stands in the New Testament at exactly two verses, Matthew 6:11 (τὸν ἄρτον ἡμῶν τὸν ἐπιούσιον δὸς ἡμῖν σήμερον, "Give us this day our daily bread") and Luke 11:3 (τὸν ἄρτον ἡμῶν τὸν ἐπιούσιον δίδου ἡμῖν τὸ καθʼ ἡμέραν, "Give us each day our daily bread"). The dictionary’s data cites the lemma at four verse references — the two verses under each of its two senses. Both verses are petitions inside the prayer Jesus taught, addressed to the Father (Πάτερ ἡμῶν, Matt 6:9; Πάτερ, Luke 11:2 — the prayer’s frame, spent as frame only).
  - The filings (the dictionary’s own judgments, verified per occurrence against the local UBS data, `lexlook.py refs`): at Matthew 6:11 and at Luke 11:3 alike, ἐπιούσιος carries two assignments — [67.183], "pertaining to recurring on a daily basis," gloss: daily, on each day; and [67.206], "pertaining to a day which is the same as the day of a discourse," gloss: today, for today. Both filings are time filings: they say *when* the bread is for, not what it is. The companion words carry one filing each at their verses: ἄρτος [5.1], "any kind of food or nourishment," gloss: food, at both verses; σήμερον [67.205], "the same day as the day of a discourse," gloss: today, at Matthew 6:11; ἡμέρα [67.92], in the idiom τὸ καθʼ ἡμέραν, "day after day, every day," at Luke 11:3 (the [67.92] sense is defined "a relatively long period of time" and its gloss list spans the headword’s several idioms — "for a long time, for quite a while, day after day, every day"; the pair quoted here is the one that fits this idiom, and nothing in the entry rests on the rest of the list).
  - Matthew’s sentence: δὸς ἡμῖν σήμερον — "give us today." Matthew’s own adverb, σήμερον [67.205], stands on the *today* side of the fork, inside the same sentence where the adjective itself remains double-filed. The NASB renders ἐπιούσιον by the other side — "Give us **this day** our **daily** bread" — printing "daily" for the adjective while "this day" already carries σήμερον; the translation distributes the fork across the sentence, one side in each word.
  - Luke’s sentence: δίδου ἡμῖν τὸ καθʼ ἡμέραν — "give us, each day." Luke’s adverbial phrase, filed [67.92] as the day-after-day idiom, stands on the *daily* side of the fork, while his adjective likewise remains double-filed. The NASB doubles the daily in English — "Give us **each day** our **daily** bread" — "each day" for τὸ καθʼ ἡμέραν and "daily" again for ἐπιούσιον. The two evangelists’ sentences lean opposite ways in their adverbs; neither adverb reassigns the adjective’s filing, which the dictionary gives both ways at both verses. The giving verbs differ in form (δὸς, aorist imperative, Matthew; δίδου, present imperative, Luke) and are reported as forms only; the difference that carries weight stands in the adverbs, which are words with their own filings, not in tense alone.
  - The bread asked for: ἄρτος at both verses is filed [5.1], "any kind of food or nourishment" — the wide sense, not the loaf sense [5.8] under which the same lemma stands at Matthew 4:4. The petition asks the Father for the day’s nourishment, filed as food generally; the NASB’s "bread" renders it at both verses, and the entry keeps the filing and the rendering distinct, as it does at every verse in this set (at John 6 the same word stands under [5.1] at every occurrence in the hearing’s texts — 6:31, 32, 33, 34, 35, 48, 50, 51, 58 — verified per occurrence).
  - **The hearing (labeled; the proposer’s, resting on the texts quoted here and claiming nothing for ἐπιούσιος itself):** the prayer’s daily bread stands at the end of a line the Old Testament draws openly. God fed Israel in the wilderness with the manna — הַמָּן — which the sons of Israel named with their own question, מָן הוּא, "What is it? For they did not know what it was" (Exod 16:15; the dictionary of the Hebrew text, BDB H4478 II, judges the name most probably from that question, "What is it?"); Moses’ answer names its source and its kind: "It is the bread [הַלֶּחֶם] which the LORD has given you to eat." The manna’s keeping was by the word spoken over it, in both directions: left over against Moses’ command — "No one is to leave any of it until morning" (16:19) — "it bred worms and stank" (16:20); put aside by the Sabbath word — "Tomorrow is a Sabbath observance, a holy Sabbath to the LORD… all that is left over put aside to be kept until morning" (16:23) — "it did not stink nor was there a maggot in it" (16:24). And Deuteronomy states the lesson the feeding was *for*: "He humbled you and let you go hungry, and fed you with the manna which you did not know, nor did your fathers know, **in order to make you understand** that man shall not live on bread alone, but man shall live on everything that comes out of the mouth of the LORD" (Deut 8:3, כָּל־מוֹצָא פִי־יְהוָה — *everything* that comes out, wider than "word" alone, and left at its width here). Jesus takes that sentence as the rule of His own life: the tempter’s proposal is "If You are the Son of God, command that these stones become bread" (Matt 4:3), and He answers it, "It is written: ’MAN SHALL NOT LIVE ON BREAD ALONE, BUT ON EVERY WORD THAT COMES OUT OF THE MOUTH OF GOD’" (Matt 4:4) — οὐκ ἐπʼ ἄρτῳ μόνῳ ζήσεται ὁ ἄνθρωπος, ἀλλʼ ἐπὶ παντὶ ῥήματι ἐκπορευομένῳ διὰ στόματος θεοῦ, with ἄρτος filed [5.8], gloss: loaf of bread, at this verse, and ῥῆμα filed [33.98], "that which has been stated or said, with primary focus upon the content of the communication," gloss: word, saying, message, statement, question. The same Jesus then teaches the asking: in John 6 the crowd cites the manna — "Our fathers ate the manna in the wilderness; as it is written: ’HE GAVE THEM BREAD OUT OF HEAVEN TO EAT’" (6:31, μάννα filed [5.22], "the miraculous food given by God to the Israelites during their journey through the wilderness") — and Jesus corrects the giver and names the gift: "it is not Moses who has given you the bread out of heaven, but it is My Father who gives you the true bread out of heaven" (6:32, δίδωσιν; ἀληθινόν filed [70.3], "pertaining to being real and not imaginary," at this verse); "For the bread of God is that which comes down out of heaven and gives life to the world" (6:33). The crowd’s response is the always-ask in the text’s own words: "Lord, always give us this bread" (6:34, πάντοτε) — and the answer to the always-ask is not a supply but a Person: "I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will not be hungry, and the one who believes in Me will never be thirsty" (6:35). The contrast is then stated twice in the same shape — "I am the bread of life" (6:48): "Your fathers ate the manna in the wilderness, and they died. This is the bread that comes down out of heaven, so that anyone may eat from it and not die. I am the living bread that came down out of heaven; if anyone eats from this bread, he will live forever; and the bread which I will give for the life of the world also is My flesh" (6:49–51); "This is the bread that came down out of heaven, not as the fathers ate and died; the one who eats this bread will live forever" (6:58). Luke, who carries the prayer’s daily, carries the same daily in the disciple’s rule: "If anyone wants to come after Me, he must deny himself, take up his cross daily, and follow Me" (Luke 9:23) — τὸν σταυρὸν αὐτοῦ καθʼ ἡμέραν, with ἡμέρα filed [67.92] there, the same filing as Luke 11:3’s phrase, and σταυρός filed [6.27], "a pole stuck into the ground in an upright position with a crosspiece attached to its upper part; used as a means of execution," gloss: cross — an execution instrument, not a death a man administers to himself — "For whoever wants to save his life [τὴν ψυχὴν αὐτοῦ, ψυχή filed [23.88], gloss: life, at both its occurrences in the verse] will lose it, but whoever loses his life for My sake, this is the one who will save it" (9:24). In the proposer’s words: a word used only twice leaves us asking the same question Israel asked over the manna — "What is it?" — and the life the texts paint together is the normal Christian life: bread, Jesus, the word, and the Father, and some things to put off, all tied together — receiving daily from the Father’s hand and dying daily to the self that would be its own source; grace given and not used is grace that spoils, and grace kept by His word, on His day, does not.
- **The close (settled):** The datum first, at its own strength: ἐπιούσιος occurs in the New Testament only in the two tellings of the one prayer, and the dictionary files it two ways at both — daily-recurring, and for-the-day-of-the-discourse — and this entry crowns neither, because the evidence in the set crowns neither: the assignments are the dictionary’s own, made at both verses, and the evangelists’ own adverbs lean opposite ways (Matthew’s σήμερον toward today, Luke’s καθʼ ἡμέραν toward daily) without either one reassigning the word. The NASB’s settled "daily" is therefore one side of a genuine fork, printed at both verses; the fork itself is the datum, and it is closed as a fork — both filings standing, at both places, is the settled state of the evidence. What the petition asks for stands filed beside it: ἄρτος [5.1], nourishment, asked of the Father. And the hearing stands beside the datum, labeled, as the proposer’s hearing of the canon read whole: the bread whose keeping was by the word — spoiling when it was kept against the word, keeping sweet when it was kept by it — taught a nation to live by everything that comes out of God’s mouth; the Son who refused to make His own bread taught His people to ask the Father for the day’s; the crowd’s "always" was answered with a Person; and Luke binds the asking and the cross to the same daily. The two registers do not blend: the prayer verses say the word and its fork; the hearing hears where the bread came from and what life it pictures — and each is true at its own place.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its substance is sorted, not carried: "the unbroken, daily supply" is filed nowhere — the word at the stub’s center does not even settle *daily* against *for today*, let alone an unbrokenness — and its branch-and-root picture ("a branch cannot hoard sap but must remain constantly connected to the root") is John 15’s (15:4–5), whose abiding stands closed at KB068 and is pointed to there, not imported here; "the exact substance of the Son" is a sense of no word in this set — John 6’s "I am the bread of life" stands in the hearing as Jesus’ saying about Himself in His own discourse, not as a meaning of ἐπιούσιος; "the provision needed for the day that is coming" matches neither filing ([67.206] is the day *of the discourse*, not a coming day) and rests on an etymology derivation fenced at (3). The stub’s title, "Epiousios Artos (The Manna of the New Creation)," is retired with it: the manna enters this entry as hearing on its own receipts, and no text in the set receipts the title’s "New Creation"; the new title names the datum. (2) The fork is left standing deliberately, and the proposer’s disposition is recorded as his: both meanings at the same time, and the two verses are not required to use the same grammar for both to stand (Matthew’s δὸς with σήμερον, Luke’s δίδου with καθʼ ἡμέραν). No sentence in the datum’s chain or in the close adopts either filing against the other; where the NASB’s choice is reported, it is reported as the translation’s. The hearing’s "daily bread" is the prayer’s received English name — the NASB’s rendering, used as a name for the petition, not as a verdict on the filing. (3) The etymologies are fenced out: derivations of ἐπιούσιος from ἐπί with οὐσία ("bread for existence") or from ἡ ἐπιοῦσα, "the coming [day]," circulate in the commentary tradition; neither is a UBS sense at these verses, and no claim in this entry rests on a derivation. Likewise the word’s third ancient occurrence — the same petition in the Didache (8:2) — is the tradition’s copy of this prayer, background only under the Framework’s categories, named here so its absence from the set is a decision and not an oversight; nothing rests on it. (4) No claim in this entry rests on a verb’s tense: δὸς (Matthew) and δίδου (Luke) are reported as the clauses’ forms, and John 6:32’s δίδωσιν likewise; the daily pattern in the entry stands on the filed adverbs and the filings, which are words, not on imperative or indicative aspect alone (the discipline of KB016). (5) John 6 is spent only where it is quoted: vv. 31–35, 48–51, and 58 — and within those verses, narrowly: 6:51’s second half ("the bread which I will give for the life of the world also is My flesh") is quoted with its verse so the quotation is whole, and stands unspent; the hearing builds nothing on it. The discourse’s eating argument at 6:52–57 stands outside the set, unexamined — 6:56–57’s abiding and life-through-another stand closed at KB068 and are pointed to there — and the hearing builds nothing on the mechanics of the eating; likewise ἀληθινός at 6:32 is filed [70.3] *at that verse*, verified there, and no property is transferred between its filing here and its filing at John 17:3 (KB017) in either direction. (6) Deuteronomy 8:3 and Matthew 4:4 are kept unflattened against each other: Deuteronomy’s כָּל־מוֹצָא is *everything* that comes out of the LORD’s mouth; Matthew’s quotation says ἐπὶ παντὶ ῥήματι, *on every word* proceeding through God’s mouth. The entry narrows neither text into the other; the hearing’s line runs through both statements at their own widths. (7) The prayers stand pasted whole (Matt 6:9–13; Luke 11:2–4) and are spent narrowly: 6:11 and 11:3 carry the datum; the address (Πάτερ) is frame; the remaining petitions (the Name, the kingdom, debts and sins, temptation) are unexamined here and nothing in this entry rests on them. (8) Two sentences in the hearing are the proposer’s formulations, labeled where they stand: "grace given and not used is grace that spoils" — Exodus 16 states the spoiling (16:20) and the keeping (16:24) as facts about the manna; the grace sentence is his naming of them — and the "normal Christian life" picture, which no single text in the set states whole: it is what the conjunction of the set’s texts, each true at its own place, paints when they are allowed to stand together, and it is carried as hearing for exactly that reason.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The settled-daily reading: ἐπιούσιος simply means "daily," the second dictionary sense is a lexicon’s hedge over a rare word, and the NASB’s rendering closes the question. Answered at the datum’s level: the second sense is not a range note but an assignment — the dictionary’s data files [67.206] at Matthew 6:11 and at Luke 11:3, as it files [67.183] at both — and an assignment at a verse is the evidence this entry exists to record; the entry does not deny "daily" (it is one of the two filed senses, and Luke’s own adverb leans its way) but declines to crown it on evidence the set does not contain, and Matthew’s σήμερον standing in the same sentence keeps the today-side inside the verse itself, not imported to it. (b) The coming-day reading: the petition asks today for *tomorrow’s* bread — the bread of the coming day, whether as tomorrow’s provision or as the bread of the coming kingdom. Answered: no filing at either verse carries a coming day; [67.206], the nearest sense, is defined as the day *which is the same as the day of a discourse* — today — and Matthew’s σήμερον says the same in the sentence’s own words; the coming-day reading rests on the derivation from ἡ ἐπιοῦσα, which is an etymology, not a filing, and is fenced at Honesty (3); a meaning a word’s derivation suggests is not a meaning the dictionary files at the verse. (c) The stub’s supply reading, restated at full strength: the bread is a continuous spiritual supply — the Son’s own substance sustaining the believer in an unbroken flow, pictured by the branch in the root. Answered: no word in the set is filed with any such sense; the adjective is a time word under *both* its filings, the noun is filed as nourishment asked of the Father in a prayer, and the flow picture’s texts (John 15) belong to another entry’s set (KB068) and state abiding, not a provision-stream; John 6, which does join bread and the Son, joins them in Jesus’ first-person sayings about Himself — carried in this entry’s hearing as His sayings — not in a supply mechanism, and the crowd’s "always give us" (6:34) is answered there by a Person, not by a flow. (d) The sacramental reading: the petition’s real referent is the bread of the Lord’s Supper, daily bread being the church’s communion. Answered from the set: neither verse names a rite, a table, or a memorial; the petition stands inside a prayer Jesus taught for asking, addressed to the Father, and its noun is filed [5.1], nourishment — the day’s food asked of the Father by name. The eucharistic referent is not stated by either verse in its own sentence, and no text in this set carries it; the answer rests on the verses alone and on nothing outside them. (e) The bare-subsistence reading: the petition concerns physical food only, and the dependence-on-the-word lesson is an allegory laid over a grocery prayer. Answered: the lesson is not laid over the texts but stated inside them — Deuteronomy assigns the manna an explicit teaching purpose ("in order to make you understand," Deut 8:3), Jesus quotes the sentence as His own rule under temptation (Matt 4:4) and teaches the petition Himself, and John 6’s discourse is Jesus’ own expansion of the manna; the entry keeps the datum at full strength beside the hearing — the petition *is* for food, filed [5.1] — and declines to let either register eat the other.

### Koinōnia (κοινωνία)

- **ID:** KB019
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** κοινωνίαν ἔχητε μεθʼ ἡμῶν, ἡ κοινωνία ἡ ἡμετέρα, κοινωνίαν ἔχομεν μετʼ αὐτοῦ, κοινωνίαν ἔχομεν μετʼ ἀλλήλων, κοινωνίαν τινὰ ποιήσασθαι, τὴν κοινωνίαν τῆς διακονίας, ἁπλότητι τῆς κοινωνίας
- **Verse keys:** 1 John 1:3; 1 John 1:6; 1 John 1:7; Rom 15:26; 2 Cor 8:4; 2 Cor 9:13
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In this set κοινωνία is filed as close association at the 1 John verses — the witnesses' own association with the Father and with His Son, the readers' association with the witnesses, an association with Him that the walk in darkness falsifies, and association with one another in the light (1 John 1:3, 6, 7) — and as giving at the giving verses: a willing contribution for the poor among the saints (Rom 15:26), a sharing whose generosity moves its recipients to glorify God (2 Cor 9:13), and a share in the service to the saints begged for as a favor (2 Cor 8:4), where the giving sense the dictionary files is itself a sharing of possessions "with the implication of some kind of joint participation and mutual interest" ([57.98]); each sense stands at its own verses, no sense is imported into a verse that does not file it, and the overlap at 2 Cor 8:4 stands because the filing's own definition carries it.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 John 1:3: ὃ ἑωράκαμεν καὶ ἀκηκόαμεν, ἀπαγγέλλομεν καὶ ὑμῖν, ἵνα καὶ ὑμεῖς κοινωνίαν ἔχητε μεθʼ ἡμῶν. καὶ ἡ κοινωνία δὲ ἡ ἡμετέρα μετὰ τοῦ πατρὸς καὶ μετὰ τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ — "what we have seen and heard we proclaim to you also, so that you too may have fellowship with us; and indeed our fellowship is with the Father, and with His Son Jesus Christ." The verse carries the word twice: the readers are to have κοινωνία with the witnesses (μεθʼ ἡμῶν), and the witnesses' own κοινωνία is named by its partners — with the Father and with His Son. UBS files both occurrences [34.5], "an association involving close mutual relations and involvement" (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such).
  - 1 John 1:6: ἐὰν εἴπωμεν ὅτι κοινωνίαν ἔχομεν μετʼ αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐν τῷ σκότει περιπατῶμεν, ψευδόμεθα καὶ οὐ ποιοῦμεν τὴν ἀλήθειαν — "If we say that we have fellowship with Him and yet walk in the darkness, we lie and do not practice the truth;" Filed [34.5]. The verse makes the claim of association testable: association with Him and walking in darkness cannot both be true, and the claimed κοινωνία is the thing the walk falsifies.
  - 1 John 1:7: ἐὰν ἐν τῷ φωτὶ περιπατῶμεν, ὡς αὐτός ἐστιν ἐν τῷ φωτί, κοινωνίαν ἔχομεν μετʼ ἀλλήλων, καὶ τὸ αἷμα Ἰησοῦ τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ καθαρίζει ἡμᾶς ἀπὸ πάσης ἁμαρτίας — "but if we walk in the Light as He Himself is in the Light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus His Son cleanses us from all sin." Filed [34.5]. Here the association runs with one another (μετʼ ἀλλήλων), under the condition of walking in the light; the verse's blood clause stands in the quoted text as its own ground, unspent by this entry.
  - Rom 15:26: εὐδόκησαν γὰρ Μακεδονία καὶ Ἀχαΐα κοινωνίαν τινὰ ποιήσασθαι εἰς τοὺς πτωχοὺς τῶν ἁγίων τῶν ἐν Ἰερουσαλήμ — "For Macedonia and Achaia have been pleased to make a contribution for the poor among the saints in Jerusalem." Here the word is a thing made (κοινωνίαν τινὰ ποιήσασθαι), made willingly (εὐδόκησαν, "have been pleased"), and directed to the poor among the saints. UBS files this occurrence [57.101], "that which is readily shared" — gloss: willing gift, ready contribution (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such).
  - 2 Cor 8:4: μετὰ πολλῆς παρακλήσεως δεόμενοι ἡμῶν τὴν χάριν καὶ τὴν κοινωνίαν τῆς διακονίας τῆς εἰς τοὺς ἁγίους — "begging us with much urging for the favor of participation in the support of the saints," The sharing here is something begged for (δεόμενοι ἡμῶν) as a favor (τὴν χάριν) — a share in the service to the saints that the givers plead to be granted, not a duty imposed on them. UBS files this occurrence, alone among the set, [57.98]: "to share one's possessions, with the implication of some kind of joint participation and mutual interest" (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such).
  - 2 Cor 9:13: διὰ τῆς δοκιμῆς τῆς διακονίας ταύτης δοξάζοντες τὸν θεὸν ἐπὶ τῇ ὑποταγῇ τῆς ὁμολογίας ὑμῶν εἰς τὸ εὐαγγέλιον τοῦ Χριστοῦ καὶ ἁπλότητι τῆς κοινωνίας εἰς αὐτοὺς καὶ εἰς πάντας — "Because of the proof given by this ministry, they will glorify God for your obedience to your confession of the gospel of Christ and for the liberality of your contribution to them and to all," Filed [57.101]. The κοινωνία is measured by its ἁπλότης — its generosity — and its direction is named: εἰς αὐτοὺς καὶ εἰς πάντας, to them and to all; its effect in the recipients is that they glorify God.
- **The close (settled):** The range principle, stated plainly: a word's senses are not sealed compartments, and no sense may be imported into a verse that does not file it — but where the dictionary's definition of the sense filed at a verse itself includes another part of the range, the occurrence carries that too, and the filing is the receipt. Applied to this set: at the 1 John verses the filed sense is close association alone — with the Father, with His Son, with the witnesses, with one another — and no giving sense is filed at any of them, so "shared property" is not what the word says at 1 John; the property language belongs to the giving verses alone. At Rom 15:26 and 2 Cor 9:13 the filed sense is giving ([57.101]), a willing gift and ready contribution. And at 2 Cor 8:4 the filed giving sense's own definition ([57.98]) carries "joint participation and mutual interest," so there the giving is participation by the dictionary's filing, not by this entry's construction. No verse in the set is trimmed to fit another, and no link between the senses is claimed beyond the one the filing at 2 Cor 8:4 itself states.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub, and recorded here: "shared property" laid over the 1 John verses — the giving senses' territory ([57.98], [57.101]) read into verses where UBS files only [34.5]; "unbroken" — filed under no sense, and 1 John 1:6 itself contemplates a fellowship claim that is a lie, so unbrokenness is not the word's content; "grafted" — a picture from other texts, standing in no verse of this set; "flowing, relational wealth" — filed under no sense and stated by no verse; and the stub's frame of our being "grafted directly into the shared life of the Father and the Sent One" in "the Father's house," which imports other entries' subjects (the Sent One material is KB011's and KB068's ground; no verse in this set speaks of a house or of grafting). The stub's "joint participation" is retained only where the dictionary files it — inside [57.98]'s definition at 2 Cor 8:4 — and is not carried over the 1 John verses, where the filed content is close association. The title, "Koinōnia (κοινωνία)," stands unchanged: it names the word, not a claim, and no part of it is retired by this entry. (2) Set completeness: this entry closes seven occurrences in six verses, of the word's nineteen NT occurrences. The remaining occurrences — participation in the blood and body of Christ (1 Cor 10:16), the fellowship of His sufferings (Phil 3:10), the partnership of Gal 2:9, the fellowship of the Spirit (2 Cor 13:13; Phil 2:1), and the rest — are outside this instance, unspent and unclosed here; their texts stand on file as provenance (the full-range export), so that another sense of the same word can receive its own entry if a future exegesis needs one. (3) No verse in this set institutes common ownership of goods, names the Holy Spirit as the association's partner (the partners named are the Father, the Son, the witnesses, and one another), makes the giving compulsory — Rom 15:26's givers "have been pleased," and 2 Cor 8:4's plead to be allowed in — or states that the association, once claimed, cannot be falsified; 1 John 1:6 states the opposite test.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The social reading — κοινωνία is mere social interaction, polite chatting, religious sociability. Answered by the set: the sense UBS files at every 1 John occurrence is an association involving close mutual relations and involvement, whose named partners are the Father and His Son (1 John 1:3) and whose claim is falsified by a walk in darkness (1 John 1:6) — a test no sociability carries — and at the giving verses the same word moves goods to the poor (Rom 15:26), which chatting does not. (b) The property reading — at 1 John itself the word already means shared or communal property. Answered by the dictionary's own assignments, cited as its judgments: UBS files no giving sense at any 1 John verse; [57.98] stands at 2 Cor 8:4 alone and [57.101] at Rom 15:26 and 2 Cor 9:13, while every 1 John occurrence in the set is filed [34.5]. The 1 John verses' own words name partners (with Him, with one another), not goods; the property flavor is the giving verses' sense carried back into verses that do not file it — and a sense filed at one verse is not the sense at another. (c) The unrelated-senses reading — the giving verses use the word in a sense disconnected from the fellowship verses, so joining them in one entry is artificial. Answered first from the dictionary's own definition, as a receipt: UBS's [57.98] definition of the giving sense filed at 2 Cor 8:4 carries "joint participation and mutual interest" — the giving at that verse is filed as participation, not as a bare transfer, so at least one occurrence in the set joins giving and participation in a single filing. A further step is this entry's inference, labeled as such: the giving verses' objects are the saints — the poor *among* the saints (Rom 15:26), the service *to* the saints (2 Cor 8:4) — and since the 1 John verses name the association's partners as the Father, the Son, the witnesses, and one another, the sharing appears to run toward the association's own members; no verse in the set states that link, and the entry does not rest the conjunction on it. The entry claims only what the filings and texts present: the senses distinct at their own verses, and joined at 2 Cor 8:4 by the filing itself, each at full strength.

### The Son, the Father's Exact Imprint

- **ID:** KB093
- **Facet of:** KB020
- **Greek keys:** ἀπαύγασμα τῆς δόξης, χαρακτὴρ τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ
- **Verse keys:** Heb 1:3
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Son is the radiance of the Father's glory and the exact imprint of His underlying reality (Heb 1:3).
- **The chain:**
  - Heb 1:3: "ὃς ὢν ἀπαύγασμα τῆς δόξης καὶ χαρακτὴρ τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ" — "who, being the radiance of the glory and the exact imprint of His underlying reality." The subject is the Son (ὃς, the verse's opening relative, whose antecedent is the Son of Heb 1:2), and he carries two names in one breath: ἀπαύγασμα — the shining-out of the glory itself, not a picture of it — and χαρακτήρ — the die's exact reproduction of the ὑπόστασις, the underlying reality of Him (αὐτοῦ, the Father).
  - The verse as pasted continues — "φέρων τε τὰ πάντα τῷ ῥήματι τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ" ("bearing all things by the word of His power"), "καθαρισμὸν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ποιησάμενος" ("having made purification for sins"), "ἐκάθισεν ἐν δεξιᾷ τῆς μεγαλωσύνης ἐν ὑψηλοῖς" ("He sat down at the right hand of the Majesty on high") — and those clauses stand in the quoted text unspent by this facet: bearing, purifying, and sitting are other subjects' claims (the purification and the sitting belong to the Hebrews exaltation material), present here as the verse's own frame, not as links.
- **The close (settled):** Hebrews 1:3 names the Son as the Father's expression with two nouns that resist reduction in opposite directions: as ἀπαύγασμα he is the glory's own radiance (the expression is of the same shining), and as χαρακτήρ he is the exact reproduction of the Father's ὑπόστασις (the expression is exact, not approximate). What the bodily dwelling of the fullness adds is the companion facet's subject (KB094).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The identification of αὐτοῦ as the Father rests on the frame of Heb 1:1–2, pointed to here and not quoted (outside this entry's set); the facet's quoted text is v. 3. (2) The entry decides nothing about how imprint and reality relate metaphysically; the later creedal use of ὑπόστασις as a count-word for persons is a vocabulary this verse does not use — it speaks of one ὑπόστασις, His, and of the Son as its exact imprint.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The copy reading — an imprint is an external copy, lesser than and separate from the reality it reproduces. Answered by the verse's own pairing: the same subject is ἀπαύγασμα τῆς δόξης — the radiance *of* the glory, the shining itself and not a portrait of the shining — and the imprint is of the ὑπόστασις, the underlying reality, not of an appearance; a lesser external copy is neither the glory's radiance nor the reality's exact reproduction. (b) The person-count reading — ὑπόστασις here already carries the later sense of a distinct metaphysical person, making the verse a statement about persons in God. Answered by the verse's grammar: the word stands singular with a singular possessive (τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ), and the verse's claim runs from the reality to its imprint; the count-diagram is imported to the text, not read from it.


### All the Fullness Dwells in Him Bodily

- **ID:** KB094
- **Facet of:** KB020
- **Greek keys:** πᾶν τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς θεότητος, κατοικεῖ, σωματικῶς
- **Verse keys:** Col 2:9
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In Christ all the fullness of deity dwells bodily (Col 2:9).
- **The chain:**
  - Col 2:9: "ὅτι ἐν αὐτῷ κατοικεῖ πᾶν τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς θεότητος σωματικῶς" — "because in Him dwells all the fullness of deity bodily." The subject of the dwelling is πᾶν τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς θεότητος — *all* the fullness *of deity*; the verb κατοικεῖ is present tense, a settled residence, not a visit; the place is ἐν αὐτῷ, in Him; and the manner is σωματικῶς, bodily — the adverb the former prose turned into the noun "body" and made another verse's subject.
- **The close (settled):** Colossians 2:9 locates the whole of what God is in Christ, in a body: nothing of the fullness is held back (πᾶν), the fullness is deity's own (τῆς θεότητος), its residence in Him is present and settled (κατοικεῖ), and its mode is bodily (σωματικῶς). What the Son's imprint of the Father adds is the companion facet's subject (KB093).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The parallel fullness text, Col 1:19, stands outside this entry's set and is neither spent nor needed; this facet rests on 2:9's own words. (2) κατοικεῖ's present tense is noted as the text's own aspect; no doctrine of permanence is built beyond the residence the verb states.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The measured-indwelling reading — the fullness dwells in Christ as it dwells in believers, in part and by degree. Answered by the verse's first quantifier: πᾶν — *all* the fullness; a measured portion is not πᾶν, and the believer-indwelling texts state their own measures in their own places. (b) The qualities reading — θεότης names divine qualities or powers on display, not deity itself. Answered by the word on the page and its company: the text says the fullness *of deity* (τῆς θεότητος) *dwells* (κατοικεῖ) in Him — qualities are displayed; a fullness that dwells is a resident, and this resident is deity's own fullness, all of it, bodily.


### The Exact Representation (Heb 1:3, Col 2:9)

- **ID:** KB020
- **Facet of:** none
- **Facets:** KB093, KB094
- **Greek keys:** ἀπαύγασμα τῆς δόξης, χαρακτὴρ τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ, πᾶν τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς θεότητος, κατοικεῖ, σωματικῶς
- **Verse keys:** Heb 1:3; Col 2:9
- **Subject distribution:** expression (Heb 1:3): the Son — the Father's radiance and exact imprint, the direction running from the Father to His showing in the Son; presence (Col 2:9): all the fullness of deity — dwelling in Christ bodily, the direction running to the fullness's residence in the Son
- **Kind:** facet-set
- **Claim:** The Father stands fully expressed in the Son (Heb 1:3), and all the fullness of deity stands fully present in the Son (Col 2:9) — each text under its own subject, and neither alone the whole.
- **The chain:**
  - Facet KB093 (subject: THE SON) — "The Son is the radiance of the Father's glory and the exact imprint of His underlying reality (Heb 1:3)."
  - Facet KB094 (subject: THE FULLNESS) — "In Christ all the fullness of deity dwells bodily (Col 2:9)."
  - The distribution is the texts' own, and the two stand in reversed direction: Hebrews' genitive runs from the Father (τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ) to the Son who imprints it; Colossians' clause runs from the fullness (πᾶν τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς θεότητος) to the Christ in whom it dwells (ἐν αὐτῷ). The former prose entry spliced them — turning Colossians' adverb σωματικῶς into the noun "body" and making it Hebrews' subject ("the physical body of Jesus is the exact imprint") — and the facet form exists to keep that splice from happening: each verse predicates its own claim of its own subject, and the conjunction, not a blend, is the entry's whole.
- **The close (settled):** What only the conjunction settles: the question "how is the Father in Christ — as an expression of Him, or as a presence in Him?" is a subject-mismatch question. The texts answer both, each under its own subject: the Son is the Father's exact expression (KB093), and the fullness of deity is resident in the Son, bodily (KB094). Neither text alone says both, and neither statement trims the other, because each is true of the subject it names.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) No single facet below, taken alone, asserts the full claim: the imprint facet does not state the fullness's bodily dwelling, and the fullness facet does not state the Son's imprint of the Father. (2) The full claim is the conjunction of the facets and asserts nothing beyond what they jointly state. (3) The two texts do not diagram how expression and presence relate inside God; the metaphysical constitution question stays where the texts leave it — open — and this set neither asserts nor denies a diagram. (4) The former prose's "Because the Son does nothing on His own" is not carried: that truth stands closed on its own Johannine receipts in KB068 (The Commissioned Household — John 5:19, 30; 12:49; 14:10) and is pointed to there, not quoted here. (5) The former prose's "perfectly reflecting His character and intent" is a gloss on χαρακτήρ and is not carried as a claim; the facet states the imprint in the verse's own nouns.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The splice itself — one blended claim ("the body of Jesus is the exact imprint, fullness and all") treated as a single text's statement. Answered by the distribution: no single verse in the set says it; each half belongs to its own text and subject, and the conjunction is stated as a conjunction, in this entry, not smuggled into either facet. (b) Either facet stretched over the whole — Hebrews' imprint read as if it also located the fullness, or Colossians' dwelling read as if it also defined the expression. Answered the same way as (a): a facet's verses are read for exactly the subject they name, and the other half of the truth stands in the other facet at full strength.
### The Paired "Savior" Titles in the Pastorals

- **ID:** KB021
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** σωτήρ, τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ, διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν, ἐπιφάνεια, μεσίτης
- **Verse keys:** 1 Tim 1:1; 2:3–6; 4:10; 2 Tim 1:9–10; Titus 1:3–4; 2:10–14; 3:4–7
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Across the Pastorals, the title "Savior" is distributed by role without exception — God the Father bears it as the rescue's originator, who commands the apostleship, wills all to be saved, and saved us by his own purpose and mercy, and Jesus Christ bears it as the rescue's executor, the mediator who gave himself as ransom, whose appearing manifests the rescue, and through whom the Spirit is poured out.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Tim 1:1: κατ' ἐπιταγὴν θεοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τῆς ἐλπίδος ἡμῶν — Paul's commission runs "by command of God our Savior"; in the same breath Christ Jesus is named "our hope," not Savior. The greeting already distributes.
  - 1 Tim 2:3–4: ἐνώπιον τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ, ὃς πάντας ἀνθρώπους θέλει σωθῆναι — "before God our Savior, who wills all people to be saved": the title on God, bound to the originating will.
  - 1 Tim 2:5–6: εἷς καὶ μεσίτης θεοῦ καὶ ἀνθρώπων, ἄνθρωπος Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς, ὁ δοὺς ἑαυτὸν ἀντίλυτρον ὑπὲρ πάντων — beside the one God stands the one mediator, "the man Christ Jesus, who gave himself as ransom for all": the Son's saving act stated as mediation and self-giving. (The one-God/one-mediator distinction itself is KB023's closure; here the verse is distribution evidence.)
  - 1 Tim 4:10: θεῷ ζῶντι, ὅς ἐστιν σωτὴρ πάντων ἀνθρώπων μάλιστα πιστῶν — hope set "on the living God, who is Savior of all people, especially of believers": the title on God at its widest scope.
  - 2 Tim 1:9–10: τοῦ σώσαντος ἡμᾶς ... κατὰ ἰδίαν πρόθεσιν καὶ χάριν — God is the one "who saved us... according to his own purpose and grace"; and that grace was φανερωθεῖσαν δὲ νῦν διὰ τῆς ἐπιφανείας τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ, "manifested now through the appearing of our Savior Christ Jesus." One sentence: the origin in God's purpose, the manifestation through the Savior's appearing.
  - Titus 1:3: κατ' ἐπιταγὴν τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ — "by command of our Savior God": the article τοῦ stands on σωτῆρος ἡμῶν and θεοῦ follows in apposition, identifying who our Savior is — God.
  - Titus 1:4: χάρις καὶ εἰρήνη ἀπὸ θεοῦ πατρὸς καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν — the very next verse: grace "from God the Father and Christ Jesus our Savior." Within one greeting the title stands on God (1:3) and on Christ (1:4), the persons distinguished and the title distributed.
  - Titus 2:10: τὴν διδασκαλίαν τὴν τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ — "the teaching of God our Savior": the 1:3 construction again, the article on σωτῆρος, θεοῦ in apposition.
  - Titus 2:13–14: ἐπιφάνειαν τῆς δόξης τοῦ μεγάλου θεοῦ καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, ὃς ἔδωκεν ἑαυτὸν ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν — "our Savior Jesus Christ" named beside "the great God" (the referent question is KB022's closure, not re-argued here); the verse's own next clause assigns the Son the executor's act: "who gave himself for us."
  - Titus 3:4–6: ἡ χρηστότης καὶ ἡ φιλανθρωπία ἐπεφάνη τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ ... ἔσωσεν ἡμᾶς — "the kindness and love of God our Savior appeared... he saved us"; the same sentence continues to the Spirit, οὗ ἐξέχεεν ἐφ' ἡμᾶς πλουσίως διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν, "whom he poured out on us richly through Jesus Christ our Savior." Both poles in a single sentence, joined by διὰ with the genitive: God saved; the pouring runs through Christ.
- **The close (settled):** (1) The Pastorals' ten occurrences of the title (the noun σωτήρ) distribute without exception: on God — 1 Tim 1:1; 2:3; 4:10; Titus 1:3; 2:10; 3:4 — each bound to an originating act (commanding, willing, purposing, saving); on Christ — 2 Tim 1:10; Titus 1:4; 2:13; 3:6 — each bound to an executing act (appearing, mediating by self-giving, being the through-whom of the outpouring). (2) Three single sentences carry both poles with the persons distinct (2 Tim 1:9–10; Titus 1:3–4; 3:4–6), and no occurrence crosses the roles — the Son is never the commander or the willer of the rescue, the Father never its mediator or its channel. (3) The distribution is the letters' own, carried by their article and preposition choices (τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ; διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν), not imposed on them.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) "Originator" and "executor" are the proposer's labels for the distribution the chain states; no Greek word in the set says either. The chain closes the distribution of the title and of the acts bound to it — it does not close a ranking of the title's belonging, and the former entry's "belongs primarily... to the Father" is not carried. (2) Titus 2:13's referent question is KB022's closure; this entry spends the verse only as distribution evidence and re-argues nothing. (3) 1 Tim 4:10's extent question (Savior "of all people") is not decided here; the verse is quoted for the title's placement on God, and its μάλιστα πιστῶν distinction is left as it stands.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The stylistic-variation reading — "Savior" is predicated of both because both save, and loose language carries no roles. Answered: loose variation predicts crossings across ten occurrences in three letters — the Son once commanding or willing the rescue, the Father once its mediator or channel — and the set contains none; the tightest sentences (2 Tim 1:9–10; Titus 3:4–6) draw the distribution inside a single breath, with διὰ marking the through-side. (b) The Sharp one-person reading of Titus 2:13 — at the distribution's hardest verse the titles merge into one person. Answered: KB022's closure, from the author's own article usage across Titus, reads two persons; and the distribution does not rest on 2:13 — set the verse aside and nine occurrences, including Titus 1:3–4's back-to-back distribution inside one greeting, still close it. (c) The subordination reading — the distribution demotes the Son to a mere instrument, a channel with no saviorhood of his own. Answered: the texts give the Son the title itself, in the same construction as on God (τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν, Titus 1:4 beside 1:3), and his acts are personal — he gave *himself* (1 Tim 2:6; Titus 2:14); an instrument does not give itself. What the distribution distinguishes is role in the one rescue, not rank of being, which this chain does not take up.

### Granville Sharp Neutered at Titus 2:13

- **ID:** KB022
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Granville Sharp's rule does not determine the referent(s) of Titus 2:13's τοῦ μεγάλου θεοῦ καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ; the author's own article-usage across Titus is the nearer evidence. On that evidence the proposed reading is two persons — the great God, and our Savior Jesus Christ — with God-as-Savior (the Father) distinguished from Christ-as-Savior (the agent) as the letter itself distributes them.
- **The chain:**
  - Titus 1:4: ἀπὸ θεοῦ πατρὸς καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν — the letter's greeting distinguishes "God the Father" from "Christ Jesus our Savior" in one breath. The author applies σωτήρ to Christ *while distinguishing Him from God*. This single datum proves his σωτήρ-usage does not merge the persons.
  - Titus 1:3: κατʼ ἐπιταγὴν τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ — article on σωτῆρος, θεοῦ adjunct; the "our Savior God" collocation.
  - Titus 2:10: τὴν διδασκαλίαν τὴν τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ — same collocation.
  - Titus 3:4: τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ — same collocation, three of three identical.
  - Titus 3:6: διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν — Christ as Savior is the agent *through whom* (διά); executing, not originating.
  - Titus 2:13: τοῦ μεγάλου θεοῦ καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ — both of the letter's σωτήρ-collocations appear side by side, joined by καί; Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ in apposition to σωτῆρος alone matches 1:4's Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν exactly.
  - In-verse control: τὴν μακαρίαν ἐλπίδα καὶ ἐπιφάνειαν — the *same* single-article-two-noun construction in the *same verse*, genuinely ambiguous (two items or one unit). The author does not use the construction with Sharp's precision here, so it cannot be mechanically determinative for him here.
  - Pastorals-wide (supporting): 1 Tim 1:1 — θεοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ, "God our Savior" *and* "Christ Jesus," distinguished; 1 Tim 2:5 — one God, one mediator, the *man* Christ Jesus.
- **The close (settled):** Sharp's rule is neutered *as a determiner of this verse* — sent home as a trump card and as a proof-text engine. A general grammatical rule cannot override a demonstrated authorial distribution without begging the question; the rule would have to assume what it proves. For Paul to have meant one person at 2:13, he would have had to collapse, in a single phrase, a distinction he maintains everywhere else in the letter including its greeting. The burden is on the one-person reading, and its only support is the general rule — which the chain has neutralized for this passage.
- **The proposed close (marked):** on the idiolect evidence, two persons — τοῦ μεγάλου θεοῦ = the Father (per the 1:3/2:10/3:4 collocation); καὶ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ = "even/and our Savior Jesus Christ" (per 1:4/3:6). This is the proposed analysis, not a datum; the single verse's grammar alone does not decide it.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Prioritizing authorial idiolect over a cross-author grammatical generalization is the Framework's methodological commitment, labeled as such. (2) The identification of 1:3/2:10/3:4's τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ with the Father is itself the *proposed* reading, not a datum; it is supported by 1:4's explicit distinction and 1 Tim 1:1, and the chain is only as strong as that support. (3) "Originating" and "executing" are proposed functional labels for the distribution, not Greek terms. (4) This entry neuters Sharp *at Titus 2:13*; it does not refute the rule generally — that would require a larger corpus than Titus. (5) The entry does not claim the Pastorals never call Christ θεός on other grounds; it claims only what the chain shows about *this* construction in *this* letter. (6) NA28 prints 2:13's Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ between ⸂⸃ markers — a text-critical flag, observed but not adjudicated here; the entry's argument concerns the construction with the phrase present, and the idiolect distribution stands on the unmarked collocations.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** Sharp's rule — article–substantive–καί–substantive, both singular, personal, non-proper → same referent; 2:13 matches formally, so "our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ," one person. Best exhibit: 2 Pet 1:1, τοῦ θεοῦ ἡμῶν καὶ σωτῆρος Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ — the same construction, standardly one person. Answered: (a) different author; the Framework prioritizes the author's own usage (labeled commitment); (b) the in-verse ἐλπίδα καὶ ἐπιφάνειαν ambiguity shows this author does not wield the construction with rule-precision here; (c) a general tendency cannot compel a reading against specific contrary evidence from the same pen without circularity. (d) Within 2 Peter itself, the next verse (1:2, τοῦ θεοῦ καὶ Ἰησοῦ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν, "of God and of Jesus our Lord") distinguishes the persons — so the construction at 1:1 cannot be wielded as a mechanical universal even on its home ground; the author's own distinctions remain live.

### One God / One Mediator

- **ID:** KB023
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In 1 Timothy, the εἷς θεός ("one God") and the εἷς μεσίτης ("one
  mediator") of 2:5 are not the same referent: the verse itself names the mediator
  as ἄνθρωπος Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς, standing between God and men, and the letter's own
  distribution everywhere distinguishes God from Christ Jesus.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Tim 2:5 (the verse itself): Εἷς γὰρ θεός, εἷς καὶ μεσίτης θεοῦ καὶ
    ἀνθρώπων, ἄνθρωπος Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς — the two εἷς clauses stand in parallel,
    not in apposition: "one God; one also mediator *of God and of men* — the man
    Christ Jesus." The mediator's two parties are named in the genitives (θεοῦ
    καὶ ἀνθρώπων), and the appositional phrase ἄνθρωπος Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς
    immediately names which of those two categories the mediator himself belongs
    to: he is ἄνθρωπος — "a man, Christ Jesus" — sharing the kind of the human
    party, not the θεός party.
  - 1 Tim 2:3–4 (the ground, γάρ): the confession of 2:5 is given as the reason
    for prayer ἐνώπιον τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ, ὃς πάντας ἀνθρώπους θέλει σωθῆναι
    καὶ εἰς ἐπίγνωσιν ἀληθείας ἐλθεῖν — in τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν θεοῦ the article
    stands on σωτῆρος ἡμῶν and θεοῦ follows in apposition, the Pastorals' own
    pattern: "our Savior, God." Verse 3 names him, verse 4 says what he wills
    (πάντας ἀνθρώπους θέλει σωθῆναι), and verse 5's γάρ grounds that saving will
    in the oneness: Εἷς γὰρ θεός. The one God of 2:5 is the Savior God of 2:3,
    who *wills* all men to be saved; the mediator of 2:5 is the one who *acts* on
    that will: 2:6, ὁ δοὺς ἑαυτὸν ἀντίλυτρον ὑπὲρ πάντων, "who gave himself a
    ransom for all."
  - 1 Tim 1:1: κατʼ ἐπιταγὴν θεοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τῆς ἐλπίδος
    ἡμῶν — the letter opens with the same coordination it will confess at 2:5:
    "God our Savior" *and* "Christ Jesus our hope," two genitives joined by καί,
    distinguished in the letter's first sentence.
  - 1 Tim 1:2: ἀπὸ θεοῦ πατρὸς καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν — grace comes
    "from God the Father *and* from Christ Jesus our Lord." In this letter the
    word θεός, when a person is attached to it in these pairings, is the Father.
  - 1 Tim 1:15–17 (juxtaposition): Χριστὸς Ἰησοῦς ἦλθεν εἰς τὸν κόσμον
    ἁμαρτωλοὺς σῶσαι — Christ Jesus *came into the world*, a visible, datable
    career; the very next sentence doxologizes another: Τῷ δὲ βασιλεῖ τῶν αἰώνων,
    ἀφθάρτῳ ἀοράτῳ μόνῳ θεῷ — "to the King of the ages, incorruptible,
    *invisible*, the *only* God." The one who came and was seen is not the one
    confessed as ἀόρατος in the same breath.
  - 1 Tim 6:13: ἐνώπιον τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ ζῳογονοῦντος τὰ πάντα καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ
    τοῦ μαρτυρήσαντος ἐπὶ Ποντίου Πιλάτου τὴν καλὴν ὁμολογίαν — at the letter's
    end the pairing recurs: God, "who gives life to all things," *and* Christ
    Jesus, "who testified the good confession before Pontius Pilate" — the
    life-giving God and the man who stood in a Roman courtroom, coordinated and
    distinguished.
  - 1 Tim 6:14–16 (the attribute check): Timothy is to keep the commandment
    μέχρι τῆς ἐπιφανείας τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, ἣν καιροῖς ἰδίοις δείξει
    ὁ μακάριος καὶ μόνος δυνάστης … ὁ μόνος ἔχων ἀθανασίαν, φῶς οἰκῶν ἀπρόσιτον,
    ὃν εἶδεν οὐδεὶς ἀνθρώπων οὐδὲ ἰδεῖν δύναται — the appearing (ἐπιφάνεια) is
    Christ's (v. 14); the one who will *show* it (δείξει) at his own times is
    another, described with chapter 1's own cluster transferred word for word:
    μακάριος (1:11 τοῦ μακαρίου θεοῦ → 6:15 ὁ μακάριος), μόνος (1:17 μόνῳ θεῷ →
    6:15 μόνος δυνάστης, 6:16 ὁ μόνος ἔχων), ἀφθάρτῳ (1:17) → ἀθανασίαν (6:16),
    ἀοράτῳ (1:17) → ὃν εἶδεν οὐδεὶς ἀνθρώπων (6:16). The letter's vocabulary
    assigns the showing to the God of its doxology, not to the Christ who
    appears.
  - 1 Tim 3:16 (the control): NA28 prints ὃς ἐφανερώθη ἐν σαρκί — "who was
    manifested in flesh." The hymn's subject in the court-of-record text is the
    relative pronoun ὅς, not the noun θεός; the verse, as NA28 prints it, does
    not name God as the one manifested. Nor can ὅς be quietly attached to 3:15's
    θεοῦ ζῶντος: ὅς is masculine and does not agree with the neuter μυστήριον it
    follows either — it opens the six-line confession in its own right, whose
    subject is manifested in flesh and finally ἀνελήμφθη ἐν δόξῃ, "was taken up
    in glory."
- **The close (settled):** Within this letter, the referents are settled by the
  letter's own syntax and distribution. (1) At 2:5 the mediator is defined by the
  two parties he stands between — θεοῦ καὶ ἀνθρώπων — and identified as
  ἄνθρωπος; collapsing him into the God-party erases the verse's own structure.
  (2) Every coordinated pairing in the letter (1:1; 1:2; 6:13) distinguishes God
  from Christ Jesus, and the descriptive clusters (invisible, incorruptible,
  only, alone immortal, unseen by men) attach to God in the doxologies (1:17;
  6:15–16), never to the Christ who came into the world, testified before Pilate,
  and will appear. The one God and the one mediator are two, by the letter's own
  showing.
- **The proposed close (marked):** The εἷς θεός of 2:5 is God the Father. The
  letter nowhere says "the one God is the Father" in so many words at 2:5, but
  its pairings attach θεός to πατήρ (1:2 ἀπὸ θεοῦ πατρὸς καὶ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ) and
  its Savior-God language (1:1; 2:3) matches the Father-distribution already
  closed in the KB's Pastorals entries. This identification is the proposed
  reading of the distribution, kept distinct from the settled distinction above.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) "Go-between" as a gloss for μεσίτης is a lexical
  proposal; the entry's argument rests on the verse's own genitives (θεοῦ καὶ
  ἀνθρώπων) and apposition (ἄνθρωπος), not on the gloss. (2) This entry settles
  1 Timothy's referents only. It does not adjudicate how other books speak (other
  θεός-texts for Christ require their own chains, on their own pasted texts).
  (3) At 3:16 the entry rests on NA28's printed ὅς. It observes the printed text
  and makes no claim about any other reading's history or merits. (4) The 6:15–16
  identification of the "only Sovereign" with God rests on the syntax of δείξει
  (he *shows* Christ's appearing) and the 1:17 attribute cluster — an argument
  from the letter's distribution, stated as such, not a datum read off one word.
  (5) ἀντίλυτρον (2:6) is quoted as the mediator's act; this entry builds nothing
  on the ransom mechanics — that word needs its own chain.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The ontological reading: a mediator
  between God and men must himself be God, or the mediation fails — so εἷς θεός
  comprehends the mediator. Answered: that premise is supplied by the reader, not
  by any verse in the letter; the text identifies its mediator as ἄνθρωπος and
  his mediating act as self-giving (2:6), and never states the premise. A
  requirement the letter does not state cannot override the distinction the
  letter does state. (b) 1 Tim 3:16 read as "God was manifested in flesh."
  Answered: NA28 — the court of record — prints ὅς, not θεός; the rival reading
  rests on a text the supplied edition does not print. (c) 1 Tim 6:15's "King of
  kings and Lord of lords" read as Christ (the titles are his elsewhere).
  Answered within the letter: the relative ἥν takes Christ's ἐπιφάνεια (6:14) as
  its antecedent, and the subject of δείξει is the one who *displays* that
  appearing — described with 1:17's God-cluster (μόνος, immortality, unseen).
  Titles shared elsewhere do not transfer this sentence's referent against its
  own syntax and its author's own attribute distribution.

### The Spirit of the Father Dwells in the Temple

- **ID:** KB091
- **Facet of:** KB024
- **Greek keys:** πνεῦμα θεοῦ, τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἐγείραντος τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐκ νεκρῶν, οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν, ζῳοποιήσει, διὰ τοῦ ἐνοικοῦντος αὐτοῦ πνεύματος, ἐν ἑνὶ πνεύματι πρὸς τὸν πατέρα, ἀκρογωνιαίου αὐτοῦ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ, κατοικητήριον τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν πνεύματι, ναὸς τοῦ ἐν ὑμῖν ἁγίου πνεύματός
- **Verse keys:** Rom 8:9, 11; Eph 2:18–22; 1 Cor 6:19
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Spirit of God — the Spirit of the One who raised Jesus — dwells in the believer, is the channel through which the Raiser will give life to mortal bodies, and constitutes the built-together body as God's dwelling-place in the Spirit, the body itself His temple.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 8:9a: "εἴπερ πνεῦμα θεοῦ οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν" — "if indeed the Spirit of God dwells in you": the indwelling is named of God in the same verse that names it of Christ (KB092's text), under its own genitive.
  - Rom 8:11: "εἰ δὲ τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἐγείραντος τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐκ νεκρῶν οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν" — "if the Spirit of the One who raised Jesus from the dead dwells in you" — "ὁ ἐγείρας Χριστὸν ἐκ νεκρῶν ζῳοποιήσει καὶ τὰ θνητὰ σώματα ὑμῶν διὰ τοῦ ἐνοικοῦντος αὐτοῦ πνεύματος ἐν ὑμῖν" — "the One who raised Christ from the dead will give life also to your mortal bodies through His Spirit who dwells in you." The Raiser is the subject of the life-giving; the indwelling Spirit is its channel.
  - Eph 2:18, 20–22: "διʼ αὐτοῦ ἔχομεν τὴν προσαγωγὴν οἱ ἀμφότεροι ἐν ἑνὶ πνεύματι πρὸς τὸν πατέρα" — access to the Father in one Spirit; the building rests on Christ as its cornerstone: "ὄντος ἀκρογωνιαίου αὐτοῦ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ"; it grows "εἰς ναὸν ἅγιον ἐν κυρίῳ"; and the built-together are "συνοικοδομεῖσθε εἰς κατοικητήριον τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν πνεύματι" — "being built together into a dwelling-place of God in the Spirit." The One who inhabits the completed dwelling is God; the passage names Him toward the Father (v. 18).
  - 1 Cor 6:19: "τὸ σῶμα ὑμῶν ναὸς τοῦ ἐν ὑμῖν ἁγίου πνεύματός ἐστιν οὗ ἔχετε ἀπὸ θεοῦ" — "your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit in you, whom you have from God": the temple is the body, the indweller is the Holy Spirit, and the gift's source is God.
- **The close (settled):** Under this facet's subject, the texts name one indwelling of God in His people: personally (Rom 8:9 — the Spirit of God dwells in you; Rom 8:11 — the Spirit of the Raiser, through whom the mortal bodies' life-giving runs), corporately (Eph 2:22 — one κατοικητήριον of God in the Spirit, the building on Christ the cornerstone growing into a holy temple), and bodily (1 Cor 6:19 — the body itself the temple of the Holy Spirit who is in you, received from God). What the Son's naming adds is the companion facet's subject (KB092).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) "Father" for τοῦ ἐγείραντος (Rom 8:11) and τοῦ θεοῦ (Eph 2:22) rests on the set's own context — Eph 2:18 names τὸν πατέρα as the One toward whom access runs, and Rom 8:11's raiser is distinguished within the verse from the Jesus He raised — and on the Framework's settled reading (Core Rule 7; Derived Truths, "The Two Spirits in Union"), named here as its standing ground per the reviewers' guard, not re-decided by this facet. (2) At Rom 8:11 the phrase διὰ τοῦ ἐνοικοῦντος αὐτοῦ πνεύματος stands under an apparatus mark in NA28 (⸄...⸅); the entry quotes the printed text and rests nothing on the marked variant. (3) Eph 2:19 (συμπολῖται τῶν ἁγίων καὶ οἰκεῖοι τοῦ θεοῦ) also stands quoted in KB090's chain; it is quoted in this facet's set on Craig's own paste, and its household-of-God frame is background here, not a load-bearing link.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The indwelling of Rom 8 is Christ's only, "of God" a loose title. Answered within the verse: 8:11's indweller is the Spirit of the One who raised Jesus — the Raiser is not the raised — and the life-giving of the bodies is the Raiser's act through that indwelling. (b) The temple texts are architectural metaphor carrying no indwelling claim. Answered: the same Spirit who is named indwelling persons with the verb οἰκεῖ (Rom 8:9, 11) is the Spirit in whom the corporate dwelling stands (Eph 2:22) and who is in the body as His temple (1 Cor 6:19); the metaphors share one subject and one verb-family with the personal texts.


### The Life-Giving Spirit of Christ Marks Belonging

- **ID:** KB092
- **Facet of:** KB024
- **Greek keys:** πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ, Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν, τὸ δὲ πνεῦμα ζωὴ διὰ δικαιοσύνην, ὁ ἔσχατος Ἀδὰμ εἰς πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν, ὁ δὲ κολλώμενος τῷ κυρίῳ ἓν πνεῦμά ἐστιν
- **Verse keys:** Rom 8:9–10; 1 Cor 15:45; 1 Cor 6:17
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Spirit of Christ — the risen last Adam Himself become life-giving Spirit — indwells the believer, so that having Him is the belonging test and, joined to the Lord, the believer is one spirit with Him.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 8:9b: "εἰ δέ τις πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ οὐκ ἔχει, οὗτος οὐκ ἔστιν αὐτοῦ" — "if anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ, this one is not His": belonging to Christ is tested by having the Spirit of Christ — the naming carries the verdict.
  - Rom 8:10: "εἰ δὲ Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν, τὸ μὲν σῶμα νεκρὸν διὰ ἁμαρτίαν τὸ δὲ πνεῦμα ζωὴ διὰ δικαιοσύνην" — "if Christ is in you, the body is dead because of sin, but the Spirit is life because of righteousness": the indwelling under this facet is named as Christ Himself in the believer.
  - 1 Cor 15:45: "ἐγένετο ὁ πρῶτος ἄνθρωπος Ἀδὰμ εἰς ψυχὴν ζῶσαν, ὁ ἔσχατος Ἀδὰμ εἰς πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν" — "the first man Adam became a living soul; the last Adam, life-giving Spirit": the risen Christ is Himself the life-giving Spirit — the subject under whom Rom 8:9b's genitive stands.
  - 1 Cor 6:17: "ὁ δὲ κολλώμενος τῷ κυρίῳ ἓν πνεῦμά ἐστιν" — "the one joined to the Lord is one spirit [with Him]": the union's form under this facet — joined, and one spirit.
- **The close (settled):** Under this facet's subject, the indwelling is Christ's own: the naming πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ carries the belonging test (8:9b), the indwelling is named directly as Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν with its life-effect stated (8:10), the risen subject of the naming is supplied by 1 Cor 15:45 (the last Adam become πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν), and the union's form is 1 Cor 6:17's ἓν πνεῦμά with the Lord. What the Father's naming adds is the companion facet's subject (KB091).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Reading Rom 8:9b's πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ together with 1 Cor 15:45's πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν is this facet's distribution under one subject (Christ, whom both texts name in their own words); the Framework's settled reading (Core Rule 7; Derived Truths, "The Two Spirits in Union") states the identification outright and is named here as its standing ground, per the reviewers' guard, not re-argued by this facet.
