(2) The first-Adam half of 1 Cor 15:45 (εἰς ψυχὴν ζῶσαν — Genesis 2:7 as Paul cites it) is the contrast's premise, not this facet's subject. (3) First Corinthians 6:17 also stands quoted in KB064's chain; it is quoted in this facet's set on Craig's own paste (2026-10-23), and no claim here rests on KB064's use of it. (4) Galatians 2:20's "Christ lives in me" belongs to KB046's chain and is pointed to there, not quoted here.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ is a courtesy title for the Father's Spirit, with no Son-side reality of its own. Answered by this facet's texts: 8:10 names the indwelling Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν — Christ Himself in you, not a title about Him — and 15:45 gives the naming its risen subject; a courtesy title decides no belonging and is no one in particular, and this naming does and is. (b) 1 Cor 6:17's "one spirit" absorbs the believer into Christ without remainder. Answered by the facet's own texts: κολλώμενος is a joining of two, and 8:10 keeps the subjects distinct in the same breath (Christ in *you*; the body *yours*, dead because of sin); union here is stated as presence and belonging, not as dissolution.


### The Spirit of the Father and the Spirit of Christ in One Dwelling

- **ID:** KB024
- **Facet of:** none
- **Facets:** KB091, KB092
- **Greek keys:** πνεῦμα θεοῦ, τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἐγείραντος, οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν, ζῳοποιήσει, κατοικητήριον τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν πνεύματι, πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ, Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν, πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν, ἓν πνεῦμά, μονὴν παρʼ αὐτῷ ποιησόμεθα
- **Verse keys:** Rom 8:9–11; 1 Cor 15:45; John 14:23; Eph 2:18–22; 1 Cor 6:17, 19
- **Subject distribution:** the Father's indwelling (πνεῦμα θεοῦ / τοῦ ἐγείραντος): dwells in the believer, gives life to mortal bodies through the indwelling, constitutes the temple (Rom 8:9a, 11; Eph 2:22; 1 Cor 6:19); the Son's indwelling (πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ / πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν): marks belonging to Christ, is Christ in the believer, one spirit with the Lord (Rom 8:9b–10; 1 Cor 15:45; 1 Cor 6:17)
- **Kind:** facet-set
- **Claim:** The believer is indwelt under both namings at once — the Spirit of the Father who raised Jesus and the Spirit of Christ who became life-giving Spirit — in the one dwelling the Father and the Son promised to make (John 14:23).
- **The chain:**
  - Facet KB091 (subject: THE FATHER'S SPIRIT) — "The Spirit of God — the Spirit of the One who raised Jesus — dwells in the believer, is the channel through which the Raiser will give life to mortal bodies, and constitutes the built-together body as God's dwelling-place in the Spirit, the body itself His temple."
  - Facet KB092 (subject: THE SON'S SPIRIT) — "The Spirit of Christ — the risen last Adam Himself become life-giving Spirit — indwells the believer, so that having Him is the belonging test and, joined to the Lord, the believer is one spirit with Him."
  - The distribution is the texts' own, inside single passages. Romans 8:9–11 sets both namings side by side within three verses — πνεῦμα θεοῦ (v. 9), πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ (v. 9), τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἐγείραντος τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐκ νεκρῶν (v. 11) — with one belonging test and one indwelling through which the bodies are made alive. And John 14:23 gives the two subjects one dwelling in one sentence: "πρὸς αὐτὸν ἐλευσόμεθα καὶ μονὴν παρʼ αὐτῷ ποιησόμεθα" — *we* will come (Father and Son, plural verb) and make *our dwelling* (μονήν, singular) with the one who keeps Jesus' word: "ἐάν τις ἀγαπᾷ με τὸν λόγον μου τηρήσει."
- **The close (settled):** What only the conjunction settles: the question "whose Spirit indwells the believer — the Father's, or Christ's?" is a subject-mismatch question. The texts answer with both genitives at full strength, each under its own subject: the Father's Spirit dwells in the believer and runs the mortal bodies' life-giving through that indwelling (KB091); Christ's Spirit indwells the believer and carries the belonging test (KB092). Neither naming trims the other, because each is true of the subject it names; the dwelling is one (John 14:23's single μονή), and the indwelling is never divided between the facets in any text of the set.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) No single facet below, taken alone, asserts the full claim: the Father facet does not state the Son's belonging test, and the Christ facet does not state the Raiser's life-giving of mortal bodies. (2) The full claim is the conjunction of the facets and asserts nothing beyond what they jointly state. (3) The set closes the distribution of namings, subjects, and works; it does not count Spirits or diagram the Godhead — a metaphysical head-count is neither asserted nor denied by this chain. The Framework's settled reading (Core Rule 7; Derived Truths, "The Two Spirits in Union") is the standing ground these facets document, named here rather than re-decided. (4) John 14:23 names the Father and the Son and no πνεῦμα; it stands as the constellation's anchor for the two subjects' one dwelling, and no naming claim rests on it alone — the namings rest on Rom 8 and 1 Cor 15:45 under their facets. (5) The former prose entry's assertions are sorted, not carried whole: its double naming and its John 14:23 dwelling stand in the facets; its "Father's own personal breath (Ruach Elohim)" gloss, its True Vine image, and its Col 1:18 support had no receipts in this set and are not carried; its "not a third metaphysical person" is replaced by rival (c)'s answer and item (3) above — what the chain decides, and what it does not, stated separately.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The collapse — the namings are two titles for one undifferentiated indwelling, so the genitives carry no subject distinction. Answered by the distribution itself: Rom 8:11's indweller is the Spirit *of the One who raised Jesus* — a genitive that distinguishes the Raiser from the raised inside the verse — and John 14:23's comers are two subjects under a plural verb; a title-collapse must erase both. (b) Two competing indwellers — the Father's Spirit and Christ's Spirit as separate agents with separate works in the believer. Answered: the set never sets them against each other — one belonging test (8:9), one indwelling through which the bodies are made alive (8:11), one dwelling made (John 14:23); the facets differ by subject, not by rivalry. (c) A detached third agent — the indwelling Spirit as a third party alongside the Father and the Son, attached to neither. Answered as an argument from the attachments: every indwelling genitive in the set attaches to God (πνεῦμα θεοῦ; τοῦ ἐγείραντος; the Spirit "ἔχετε ἀπὸ θεοῦ," 1 Cor 6:19) or to Christ (πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ; πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν); no text in the set introduces an indweller under a third attachment. The argument is stated as what the attachments show; the metaphysical diagram it might feed is item (3)'s open ground, not this close.


### The Eternal Order (The Consummation)

- **ID:** KB025
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ὑποταγήσεται, τῷ ὑποτάξαντι αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα, ἵνα ᾖ ὁ θεὸς πάντα ἐν πᾶσιν
- **Verse keys:** 1 Cor 15:28
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** When all things have been subjected to him, the Son himself will be subjected to the One who subjected all things to him, so that God may be all in all (1 Cor 15:28).
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Cor 15:28 (the verse whole, a single sentence): "ὅταν δὲ ὑποταγῇ αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα, τότε [καὶ] αὐτὸς ὁ υἱὸς ὑποταγήσεται τῷ ὑποτάξαντι αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα, ἵνα ᾖ ὁ θεὸς [τὰ] πάντα ἐν πᾶσιν" — "when all things are subjected to him, then the Son himself also will be subjected to the One who subjected all things to him, so that God may be all in all." The sentence sets its own order with two subjection verbs: first the ὅταν-condition, ὑποταγῇ αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα — all things subjected *to him*; then, τότε, the Son's own subjection — ὑποταγήσεται τῷ ὑποτάξαντι αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα: the Son subjected to the One who subjected all things to him — the rendering follows the form; the same form bears the lexicon's "subject oneself" hearing ("the Son will subject himself"), and the entry decides neither (honesty (2)) — with the purpose stated (ἵνα ᾖ ὁ θεὸς [τὰ] πάντα ἐν πᾶσιν). The sentence's load-bearing parts are its order (ὅταν … τότε), its dative (the One to whom), and its purpose clause; the voice hearing of ὑποταγήσεται is carried openly in honesty (2), and no link in this chain rests on it.
- **The close (settled):** The verse closes its order in its own terms: the subjection of all things to the Son is not the sentence's last word. After it, the Son himself stands subjected to the One who subjected all things to him, and the stated end of the whole sequence is not a further arrangement but a fullness — God all in all, with no successor state named. The former prose's word "eternal" stands only as far as that purpose clause carries it: the ἵνα clause names the final state, and the verse names nothing after it.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) This entry's set is v. 28 alone: the sequence it concludes (vv. 24–27 — the kingdom delivered, every rule abolished, all things under his feet) is not quoted here and no claim rests on it; the chapter's consummation material at vv. 42–49 stands in KB064 and is pointed to there. (2) ὑποταγήσεται is passive in form, but the verse does not say by whom the Son will be subjected — and the form does not settle it: the lexicon (BDAG, ὑποτάσσω ①ⓑ, paste on file) files this verse's first subjection (28a, all things) under ①ⓑα, "become subject," but the Son's own subjection (28b) under ①ⓑβ, "subject oneself, be subjected or subordinated, obey" (to God); the verse's third use, the active participle for the One who subjected all things (28c), BDAG files under the active sense, as the chain renders it. The self-subjection hearing is therefore lexically live, the entry decides neither hearing, and it supplies no agent; the Claim's rendering follows the form, and the subjection, its dative, and its purpose stand on either hearing. (3) What the subjection means for the Son's being — the metaphysical constitution question — the verse does not address, and the entry decides nothing there. (4) NA28 prints two words of the verse in editorial brackets, [καὶ] and [τὰ], marking them textually uncertain; the Claim rests on neither — without καὶ the Son's subjection still follows the condition, and without τὰ the purpose clause still reads God all in all. (5) The former prose's "beautiful, enduring order of the New Creation" and "the eternal design of our shared home" are exposition glosses: v. 28 uses no new-creation or home language, and they are not carried as claims.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The subordination-of-nature reading — the Son's subjection proves a lesser being. Answered only as far as the verse answers: the subjection here is an event in an order (ὅταν … τότε), placed *after* all things are subjected to the Son — one sentence gives him everything and then his subjection; a verdict on his nature is not this verse's claim in either direction, and the entry builds none (honesty (3)). (b) The temporary-episode reading — the subjection is a passing arrangement, after which the order reverts. Answered by the purpose clause: the subjection is ordered toward the ἵνα clause, whose stated end is God all in all, and the verse names no state after that state; a reversion would have to be imported. (c) The humility-lesson reading — the verse, on whichever hearing of the voice, turned into an instruction in voluntary yielding. Answered by the sentence's own frame: the subjection is stated as the consummation's order (ὅταν … τότε) and ordered toward its purpose (God all in all); the verse commands nothing, addresses no imitator, and draws no lesson — the lesson is imported exposition, and it stands or falls as exposition, not as this verse's claim.

### Kata Panta (In All Things) and Genuine Temptation

- **ID:** KB026
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἀπείραστός ἐστιν κακῶν, πειράζει δὲ αὐτὸς οὐδένα, πεπειρασμένον κατὰ πάντα καθ' ὁμοιότητα, χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας
- **Verse keys:** James 1:13; Heb 4:15
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** God cannot be tempted by evil and tempts no one (James 1:13), and the Son was tempted in all things in likeness, without sin (Heb 4:15) — so the temptation the texts name for the Son is real temptation, of the same kind his people face.
- **The chain:**
  - James 1:13 (the verse whole): "μηδεὶς πειραζόμενος λεγέτω ὅτι ἀπὸ θεοῦ πειράζομαι· ὁ γὰρ θεὸς ἀπείραστός ἐστιν κακῶν, πειράζει δὲ αὐτὸς οὐδένα" — "let no one being tempted say, 'I am tempted from God'; for God is untemptable by evils, and he himself tempts no one." The verse first forbids a source-attribution — no one under temptation may name God as its source — then grounds the prohibition twice on its own subject, ὁ θεός: he is ἀπείραστος κακῶν, untemptable by evils, and the tempting of anyone is something he himself does not do.
  - Heb 4:15 (the verse whole): "οὐ γὰρ ἔχομεν ἀρχιερέα μὴ δυνάμενον συμπαθῆσαι ταῖς ἀσθενείαις ἡμῶν, πεπειρασμένον δὲ κατὰ πάντα καθ' ὁμοιότητα χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας" — "for we do not have a high priest unable to sympathize with our weaknesses, but one who has been tempted in all things in likeness, without sin." The frame names whose temptation this is: the high priest we have, defined first by his sympathy (συμπαθῆσαι ταῖς ἀσθενείαις ἡμῶν, to sympathize with our weaknesses); the predication is πεπειρασμένον — having been tempted, the perfect participle stating the testing as completed with its standing result in view — and its measure is κατὰ πάντα (in all things) καθ' ὁμοιότητα (according to likeness, the likeness being to the ἡμῶν of the frame), bounded by χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας (without sin).
- **The close (settled):** The two verses predicate on their two subjects, and the conjunction is the settlement: James denies temptation of God — as its source for anyone, and as an experience evils could work on him — and Hebrews affirms temptation of the Son at full measure, in all things and in likeness to his people, with the sin-boundary stated by the verse itself rather than by softening the temptation. The genuineness rests on Hebrews' own two phrases: κατὰ πάντα leaves no department of testing out, and καθ' ὁμοιότητα makes the testing like ours; neither phrase is qualified in the text.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The mechanics of the conjunction — how the Son of an untemptable God is genuinely tempted — neither verse states, and the entry decides nothing there; the former prose's "capable of failure" is one inference past the texts and is not carried, in either direction. (2) χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας stands over the whole temptation statement; the inward anatomy of the testing — what enticement is like from inside it — neither verse describes, and the entry does not anatomize it. (3) πειράζω ranges over testing and enticing in both verses alike; James's color is fixed by his own words (κακῶν, and the forbidden source-claim), Hebrews' by the likeness and the sin-boundary, and the entry does not split one verb into two words to make the conjunction easier. (4) The former prose's remaining material is not carried: "completely poured out" is Philippians 2:7's text, "made like his brothers" is Heb 2:17's, "no hidden, independent reservoir of divine power" is an inference, and "conquered entirely through absolute reliance on the Father's Spirit" is exposition about the manner of the victory that neither verse in this set states; each stands or falls in its own place.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The contradiction reading — the two verses cannot both be true: if God cannot be tempted, the Son's temptation was not real, or the Son is not God. Answered by the subjects and by James's own scope: James denies God as temptation's *source* and God's temptability *by evils*; he predicates nothing about the Son's testing, which Hebrews predicates of the high priest. The contradiction is assembled by moving each verse off its subject; on their subjects, both stand at full strength. (b) The apparent-temptation reading — the Son's temptation was real only in appearance, since a sinless one cannot be truly tempted. Answered by Hebrews' measure-words: κατὰ πάντα and καθ' ὁμοιότητα predicate the testing in all things and in likeness to us; an appearance is neither "in all things" nor "in likeness," and the verse's boundary is sin (χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας), not reality. (c) The sin-included reading — "in all things" includes sin's inward pull, and "without sin" covers only the outcome. Answered by the placement of the boundary: χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας stands over the whole participial statement, not over its last clause; what the pull of temptation is like from inside, the verse does not say, and the entry leaves that where honesty (2) leaves it — undecided, not smuggled into the claim.

### Egeneto (Transition of State) vs. Assumption of Flesh

- **ID:** KB027
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ὁ λόγος σὰρξ ἐγένετο, ἐσκήνωσεν ἐν ἡμῖν, ἐθεασάμεθα τὴν δόξαν αὐτοῦ
- **Verse keys:** John 1:14
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Word became flesh and dwelt among us (John 1:14).
- **The chain:**
  - John 1:14a: "Καὶ ὁ λόγος σὰρξ ἐγένετο" — "and the Word became flesh." The sentence has one subject, ὁ λόγος, standing unchanged across the verb; ἐγένετο (aorist of γίνομαι) predicates a becoming, and its result is named: σὰρξ, flesh. The verse does not introduce a second figure who takes the flesh up, nor a flesh that exists apart from the Word's becoming it.
  - John 1:14b: "καὶ ἐσκήνωσεν ἐν ἡμῖν" — "and he dwelt among us" (ἐσκήνωσεν, pitched tent, took up dwelling). The becoming issued in presence: the one who became flesh lived among the ἡμεῖς of the verse's next clause.
  - John 1:14c: "καὶ ἐθεασάμεθα τὴν δόξαν αὐτοῦ, δόξαν ὡς μονογενοῦς παρὰ πατρός" — "and we beheld his glory, glory as of an only-begotten from a father." The flesh-become one was seen — ἐθεασάμεθα, beheld, by the witnesses among whom he dwelt — and in the same sentence his glory is named with his relation intact: μονογενοῦς παρὰ πατρός, an only-begotten from beside a father. The becoming did not interrupt the relation the verse itself predicates of him.
- **The close (settled):** The heading's question is answered as far as the verse answers it, by the verse's grammar: the Word is the subject before the verb and after it, so the text knows no assumption *by* a subject other than the Word and no transition *away* from being the Word — what changed is predicated as his becoming flesh, and the flesh is what he became, not a garment a second party wore. The same sentence that predicates the flesh predicates the dwelling among witnesses and the beheld glory, with his relation παρὰ πατρός — from beside a father, in the verse's own indefinite words — still standing.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) What "became" means for the Word's being — the metaphysical mechanics of the union — the verse does not diagram, and the entry decides nothing there. (2) The former prose's "permanently" is not the verse's word: the verse names no undoing of the becoming, and the entry rests on what is stated, treating the silence as silence. (3) The former prose's definition of ὁ λόγος ("the Father's pre-existent thought and expressed command") is a gloss drawn from outside this verse — John 1:1's material is not in this entry's set — and is not carried; the entry takes ὁ λόγος as the verse's own subject-name, with the relation παρὰ πατρός that the verse itself states. (4) The verse's tail, πλήρης χάριτος καὶ ἀληθείας ("full of grace and truth"), stands in the pasted text unspent by this entry. (5) The same verb's other great predication — the last Adam became a life-giving spirit (1 Cor 15:45) — stands closed in KB064 and KB092 and is pointed to there, not quoted here.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The costume reading — a divine spirit put on a human shell, the flesh an instrument worn rather than the Word's own becoming. Answered by the single subject and the verb: the verse says the Word *became* flesh (σὰρξ ἐγένετο); it does not say he took flesh up, entered it, or wore it, and no wearer distinct from the Word appears in the sentence. (b) The exchange reading — the Word ceased to be what he was and was replaced by flesh. Answered by the same sentence's continuities: the one who became flesh is the one who dwelt among us and whose glory was beheld "as of an only-begotten from a father" — the relation παρὰ πατρός is predicated of the flesh-become one, in this verse, after the becoming. (c) The figure reading — "became flesh" as a manner of speaking for appearance, influence, or teaching. Answered by the verse's own concreteness: ἐσκήνωσεν ἐν ἡμῖν (he dwelt among us) and ἐθεασάμεθα (we beheld) are the statements of witnesses to a presence, not the vocabulary of a metaphor.

### Autos ho Kyrios (The Lord Himself)

- **ID:** KB030
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος, καταβήσεται ἀπʼ οὐρανοῦ, ἐν φωνῇ ἀρχαγγέλου, οἱ νεκροὶ ἐν Χριστῷ ἀναστήσονται πρῶτον, ἅμα σὺν αὐτοῖς ἁρπαγησόμεθα, εἰς ἀπάντησιν τοῦ κυρίου, πάντοτε σὺν κυρίῳ ἐσόμεθα, παρακαλεῖτε ἀλλήλους ἐν τοῖς λόγοις τούτοις
- **Verse keys:** 1 Thess 4:16; 1 Thess 4:17; 1 Thess 4:18
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Across 1 Thessalonians 4:16–18 the descent is the Lord's own — αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος, "the Lord Himself," is the subject of καταβήσεται, and everything else in the scene attends rather than acts: the archangel is present only as a voice among the accompaniments, the dead in Christ rise first at the descent, the living are caught up together with them to meet the Lord in the air, and the paragraph's stated end is πάντοτε σὺν κυρίῳ ἐσόμεθα, "always with the Lord" — the very words with which the readers are commanded to comfort one another.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Thess 4:16: ὅτι αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος ἐν κελεύσματι, ἐν φωνῇ ἀρχαγγέλου καὶ ἐν σάλπιγγι θεοῦ, καταβήσεται ἀπʼ οὐρανοῦ — "For the Lord Himself will descend from heaven with a shout, with the voice of the archangel and with the trumpet of God." The grammar is the datum: one subject (αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος), one verb of descent (καταβήσεται, "he will descend"), and three ἐν-phrases of accompaniment — a command-cry, an archangel's voice, God's trumpet. The NASB's "with … with … with" reproduces the ἐν construction exactly; the accompaniments attend the descent, none of them performs it.
  - 1 Thess 4:16: καὶ οἱ νεκροὶ ἐν Χριστῷ ἀναστήσονται πρῶτον — "and the dead in Christ will rise first." The first event named at the descent is not the living's removal but the dead's rising, and πρῶτον ("first") fixes the order inside the paragraph. (Who these dead are and how their sleep is named is the paragraph's first half, 4:13–15, closed at KB050, KB088, and KB089.)
  - 1 Thess 4:17: ἔπειτα ἡμεῖς οἱ ζῶντες οἱ περιλειπόμενοι ἅμα σὺν αὐτοῖς ἁρπαγησόμεθα ἐν νεφέλαις εἰς ἀπάντησιν τοῦ κυρίου εἰς ἀέρα — "Then we who are alive, who remain, will be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air." ἔπειτα ("then") answers 4:16's πρῶτον; ἅμα σὺν αὐτοῖς ("together with them") binds the living to the risen dead in one company; ἁρπαγησόμεθα ("we will be caught up") is passive — the living are taken, they do not ascend by their own motion; and the movement's stated goal is a meeting, εἰς ἀπάντησιν τοῦ κυρίου, "to meet the Lord."
  - 1 Thess 4:17: καὶ οὕτως πάντοτε σὺν κυρίῳ ἐσόμεθα — "and so we will always be with the Lord." The paragraph's landing is a state, not a location: οὕτως ("so, in this way"), πάντοτε ("always"), σὺν κυρίῳ ("with the Lord"). After the descent, the rising, and the catching up, what the text says remains is company — with him, without end.
  - 1 Thess 4:18: Ὥστε παρακαλεῖτε ἀλλήλους ἐν τοῖς λόγοις τούτοις — "Therefore, comfort one another with these words." The passage assigns its own use: παρακαλεῖτε is a present imperative, an ongoing mutual act, and its instrument is τοῖς λόγοις τούτοις — *these* words, the descent and the togetherness just stated, not a scheme built upon them.
- **The close (settled):** The Lord himself descends from heaven; no proxy performs any act in the passage — the only verbs of movement belong to the Lord (he descends), the dead in Christ (they rise, first), the living (they are caught up, together with the dead), and the whole company (they will be with the Lord). The archangel attends as a voice among the accompaniments. The paragraph's order is the dead first, then the living joined to them; its end is perpetual being-with-the-Lord; and its own stated application is that the readers comfort one another with these words.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub, and recorded here: "in unbroken union with the Father" — the passage names God's trumpet (σάλπιγγι θεοῦ) and states no Father-union (the conviction itself is Craig's and stands closed on its own Johannine receipts at KB068, The Commissioned Household — John 5:19, 30; 12:49; 14:10 — pointed to there, not carried here); the Good Shepherd who "personally comes for the sheep He knows by name" — John 10's picture, a different text with its own receipts, not this passage's words; and "the final harvest of the household" — the passage's gathering word is ἁρπαγησόμεθα, a catching up to a meeting, and no harvest image stands in it. What survives of the stub is its center, which is the verse's own: the descent is personal, αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος. (2) The accompaniments' agents are not assigned beyond the text: who utters the κέλευσμα and who sounds the trumpet the verse does not say, and whether the archangel's voice is the κέλευσμα is not stated; the entry records the archangel as a voice and assigns him no act. (3) The meeting's sequel is not stated: εἰς ἀπάντησιν τοῦ κυρίου names the meeting, and 4:17 does not say where the company goes from the air; the entry closes the meeting and the πάντοτε σὺν κυρίῳ, not a direction of travel after it. (4) Scope: this paragraph only. Its first half (4:13–15 — the sleepers, and God bringing them with Jesus, ἄξει σὺν αὐτῷ) is closed at KB050, KB088, and KB089 and is pointed to, not re-closed; the two halves are held as written, neither flattened into the other. Placements of this passage against other coming texts are other entries' work.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The stub's amplified reading: the personal descent carries a stated Father-union and the shepherd's name-knowing intimacy. Answered: neither stands in the passage — the only God-word in 4:16 is σάλπιγγι θεοῦ, God's trumpet among the accompaniments, and no sheep, shepherd, or names appear in 4:16–18; the intimacy the passage itself states is σὺν κυρίῳ, being with the Lord, which needs no import to be personal. (b) The proxy reading: the coming is executed by angelic agency — the archangel's voice and the trumpet are, in effect, the descent. Answered on the grammar: the three ἐν-phrases are accompaniments hanging on the Lord's own verb; αὐτὸς ὁ κύριος is the subject of the passage's only descent verb, and the archangel performs no verb at all in 4:16–18 — he is heard, and that is all the text gives him. (c) The living-first reading: the passage is the living generation's removal, the dead a preliminary detail. Answered: the order is written the other way — the dead rise πρῶτον, and the living are caught up ἅμα σὺν αὐτοῖς, together *with them*; the living do not go first, do not go alone, and do not go by their own motion (ἁρπαγησόμεθα, passive). (d) The timetable reading: the passage's value is its slot in a chronological system assembled from many texts. Answered at this passage's level: its internal sequence is two beats (πρῶτον, ἔπειτα) and a state (πάντοτε σὺν κυρίῳ ἐσόμεθα), and the use it assigns itself is comfort with these words (4:18); whatever larger assembly other texts may support, this paragraph's own close is the personal descent, the togetherness, and the comfort — and it is closed here at that strength.

### The Preemptive Hold (Luke 22:31-32)

- **ID:** KB031
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** ὁ σατανᾶς ἐξῃτήσατο ὑμᾶς τοῦ σινιάσαι ὡς τὸν σῖτον, ἐγὼ δὲ ἐδεήθην περὶ σοῦ ἵνα μὴ ἐκλίπῃ ἡ πίστις σου, καὶ σύ ποτε ἐπιστρέψας στήρισον τοὺς ἀδελφούς σου
- **Verse keys:** Luke 22:31–32
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Before Simon's stumble, Jesus tells him: Satan "asked for" the disciples — "to sift [you all] like wheat" (ὁ σατανᾶς ἐξῃτήσατο ὑμᾶς τοῦ σινιάσαι ὡς τὸν σῖτον) — "but I prayed for you" (ἐγὼ δὲ ἐδεήθην περὶ σοῦ), the prayer naming Simon in the singular out of the plural demand, "that your faith may not fail" (ἵνα μὴ ἐκλίπῃ ἡ πίστις σου). And the prayed-for man is given his own part in the same sentence: "and you, once you have turned back, strengthen your brothers" (καὶ σύ ποτε ἐπιστρέψας στήρισον τοὺς ἀδελφούς σου).
- **The chain:**
  (1) The demand is for the group: "Simon, Simon, behold, Satan asked for (ἐξῃτήσατο) you [plural, ὑμᾶς] to sift like wheat (τοῦ σινιάσαι ὡς τὸν σῖτον)." BDAG files this verse under "ask for, demand" — "to ask for with emphasis" (Field's note, cited there: "obtain by asking"). The verb is an asking, however emphatic, not a seizing; the sifting is Satan's stated aim, announced to Simon before it happens.
  (2) The prayer is for the one man, and it is already made: "but I (ἐγὼ δὲ) prayed for you (ἐδεήθην περὶ σοῦ)" — the δέ sets the prayer against the demand, the aorist states it as done, and the singular σοῦ narrows the prayer's named object from the demanded group to Simon. Jesus speaks the words before Simon's fall, which the charge in link (4) already presupposes.
  (3) The prayer's stated aim is not that Simon will not stumble but "that your faith may not fail" (ἵνα μὴ ἐκλίπῃ ἡ πίστις σου). ἐκλίπῃ is the failing of the faith, not the avoiding of the fall — and the next clause's "once you have turned back" confirms a departure stands between the prayer and the charge.
  (4) The prayed-for man has work: "and you, once you have turned back (καὶ σύ ποτε ἐπιστρέψας), strengthen your brothers (στήρισον τοὺς ἀδελφούς σου)" — an imperative. The sentence's last word for the sifted man is not his ruin but his brothers' strengthening, at his hand.
- **The close (settled):** Satan's demand and Jesus' prayer stand side by side in one breath, and the prayer was already made — before the sifting, before the fall. The prayer's stated aim is a faith that does not fail, and the same sentence charges the prayed-for man: turn back, and strengthen your brothers.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The former prose generalized this prayer into a doctrine of every believer's preservation ("our preservation relies entirely on the prevailing prayer of the Head," "not on the strength of our own grip"); the verses pray for Simon, and this entry closes his case only — the generalizations are dropped, not carried (Craig's ruling, 2026-10-24). (2) The verses do not record, in so many words, whether Satan's demand was granted or refused; they state the demand and the prayer, and the sifting's outcome is carried by the charge, not narrated here. (3) The denial and the restoration themselves stand outside the set (Luke 22:34, 54–62; John 21) and are pointed to, not quoted; within the set, the fall is present only as what "once you have turned back" presupposes.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):**
  (a) The self-recovery reading — Simon came back because his own resilience and repentance were stronger than his fall. Answered by the order of the verse: the prayer precedes the fall and states its aim beforehand ("that your faith may not fail"); the turning back stands inside a sentence whose first move toward Simon is Jesus' prayer already made, not Simon's grip reasserting itself.
  (b) The no-fall reading — because Jesus prayed, Simon's faith not failing means Simon never really fell. Answered by the verse's own ἐπιστρέψας: "once you have turned back" presupposes a departure to turn back from; the prayer's aim is the faith's not failing, not the stumble's not happening.
  (c) The general-guarantee reading — the verse promises every believer this same preservation, so the entry should close it for all. Answered by the prayer's grammar: its object is singular and named (περὶ σοῦ, to Simon, out of the plural ὑμᾶς Satan demanded). A wider claim needs its own texts and its own receipts; this entry declines to spend Simon's verse on it.
  (d) The unchecked-demand reading — Satan's demand shows him acting over the disciples on his own authority. Answered first by the verse's own verb: ἐξῃτήσατο is an asking — BDAG files Luke 22:31 under "ask for, demand," an asking made with emphasis and, BDAG adds, "with implication of having a right to do so" (Field: "obtain by asking") — but a right asked for is still asked for, and the verse records no grant; asking is addressed to someone who can grant or refuse; Satan stands in the sentence as a petitioner, not as a power seizing the disciples on his own motion. The demand also stands under the δέ of a prayer already prayed against it, and the sentence ends not with Satan's sifting but with Simon's charge to strengthen his brothers.
### Qeshet and Zakhar (The Bow in the Cloud, and God's Remembering)

- **ID:** KB032
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Hebrew keys:** קַשְׁתִּי, הַקֶּשֶׁת, וְזָכַרְתִּי, לִזְכֹּר
- **Verse keys:** Gen 9:10; Gen 9:11; Gen 9:12; Gen 9:13; Gen 9:14; Gen 9:15; Gen 9:16; Ezek 1:28; and, in the hearing register only, Gen 22:8; John 1:29; 2 Pet 3:5–7
- **Kind:** datum, with the proposer's hearing carried labeled in its own register below the datum's chain
- **Claim:** In Genesis 9, קֶשֶׁת stands in its rainbow sense — the sense the dictionaries file at Genesis 9:13, 9:14, and 9:16, with Ezekiel 1:28 its only other place — as the sign of God's covenant with Noah, his seed, and every living creature of all flesh, a sign God sets in the cloud and appoints for His own seeing and remembering: "I will look at it, to remember the everlasting covenant" (Gen 9:16) — so the promise's anchor, in the chapter's own words, is God's remembering, spoken by God of Himself.
- **Chain of evidence:**
  - The filings (the dictionaries' own judgments): קֶשֶׁת carries three senses — (1) the bow, "a strong but flexible piece of wood to which a string is attached for the firing of arrows," used for hunting or as a weapon of war (UBS; BDB H7198 sense 1 files the hunting and battle places there); (2) an arrow; (3) "= an arch of colors visible in the sky, shaped like a bow," glossed "rainbow" (UBS), which BDB H7198 sense 2 files at exactly four places: Gen 9:13–14, 16 and Ezek 1:28. The Genesis occurrences stand under sense 3, verified per occurrence in the UBS data; no Genesis 9 occurrence is filed under the weapon sense. זכר at Gen 9:15 and 9:16 stands under its first sense, the remembering by which humans or deities call to mind (UBS), and BDB H2142 II.3a files these two verses in its God-as-subject class "remember his own covenant," beside Exod 2:24 and 6:5.
  - The covenant the sign serves (Gen 9:11): "I establish My covenant with you; and all flesh shall never again be eliminated by the waters of a flood, nor shall there again be a flood to destroy the earth" — וַהֲקִמֹתִי אֶת־בְּרִיתִי אִתְּכֶם וְלֹא־יִכָּרֵת כָּל־בָּשָׂר עוֹד מִמֵּי הַמַּבּוּל. The covenant is God's establishing, its partners Noah and his descendants and "every living creature that is with you" (9:9–10), and its content the flood's non-return.
  - The sign given (Gen 9:12–13): "This is the sign of the covenant which I am making between Me and you and every living creature that is with you, for all future generations; I have set My rainbow in the cloud, and it shall serve as a sign of a covenant between Me and the earth" — אֶת־קַשְׁתִּי נָתַתִּי בֶּעָנָן וְהָיְתָה לְאוֹת בְּרִית בֵּינִי וּבֵין הָאָרֶץ. The Hebrew word is קַשְׁתִּי, "My bow," the weapon word with God's possessive on it; the NASB's "rainbow" renders the filed sense and does not carry the possessive word's identity (Honesty (5)).
  - The seeing and the remembering are God's (Gen 9:14–15): "when I make a cloud appear over the earth, that the rainbow will be seen in the cloud, and I will remember My covenant, which is between Me and you and every living creature of all flesh; and never again shall the water become a flood to destroy all flesh" — וְזָכַרְתִּי אֶת־בְּרִיתִי. Even the cloud's appearing is God's act (בְּעַנְנִי, "when I make a cloud appear"); no human act in the passage triggers the remembering.
  - The sign's stated errand (Gen 9:16–17): "When the rainbow is in the cloud, then I will look at it, to remember the everlasting covenant between God and every living creature of all flesh that is on the earth" — וְהָיְתָה הַקֶּשֶׁת בֶּעָנָן וּרְאִיתִיהָ לִזְכֹּר בְּרִית עוֹלָם. God states the sign's purpose as His own looking and remembering, and the speech closes by restating the sign to Noah (9:17).
  - The sense's set completed (Ezek 1:28): "Like the appearance of the rainbow in the clouds on a rainy day, so was the appearance of the surrounding radiance. Such was the appearance of the likeness of the glory of the LORD" — כְּמַרְאֵה הַקֶּשֶׁת אֲשֶׁר יִהְיֶה בֶעָנָן בְּיוֹם הַגֶּשֶׁם כֵּן מַרְאֵה הַנֹּגַהּ סָבִיב הוּא מַרְאֵה דְּמוּת כְּבוֹד־יְהוָה. The fourth and only other place of the rainbow sense: the same bow-appearance in the cloud attends the likeness of the glory, in Ezekiel's own hedged words (appearance of the likeness — Honesty (4)).
  - **The hearing (labeled; the proposer's, in his words' substance, resting on the texts quoted here and claiming nothing for Genesis 9's own saying):** the word God hangs in the cloud is His *bow* — the weapon word, filed at its own places for the hunting bow and the battle bow — and read in the New Testament's light the picture is heard whole: the flood was the judgment of God poured out on the world that then was — "the world at that time was destroyed by being flooded with water" (2 Pet 3:6, ὁ τότε κόσμος ὕδατι κατακλυσθεὶς ἀπώλετο) — the wrath of God against all unrighteousness, wrath as settled judgment and not as anger; the covenant's sign looks forward to the sacrifice God Himself would provide — Abraham's word before there was a lamb in sight, "God will provide for Himself the lamb for the burnt offering" (Gen 22:8, אֱלֹהִים יִרְאֶה־לּוֹ הַשֶּׂה לְעֹלָה), named at the Jordan as "the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world" (John 1:29, ἴδε ὁ ἀμνὸς τοῦ θεοῦ ὁ αἴρων τὴν ἁμαρτίαν τοῦ κόσμου); and the world the bow hangs over is not awaiting water again — "by His word the present heavens and earth are being reserved for fire, kept for the day of judgment and destruction of ungodly people" (2 Pet 3:7), reserved by the same word (τῷ αὐτῷ λόγῳ) that once flooded it. This is the Old Testament read in the light of the new — a hearing, standing labeled as one; the datum above neither needs it nor states it.
- **The close (settled):** The datum first, at its own strength: in Genesis 9 the bow-word stands in its rainbow sense, and God makes that bow in the cloud the sign of His covenant with every living creature — a sign whose stated errand is not man's remembering but God's: He will look at it, to remember. The chapter anchors its promise where the stub's center wanted it anchored — on God's own active remembering, God's act toward His own covenant — and it does so in God's own sentences, with no human trigger anywhere in them; even the cloud is of His making. Ezekiel completes the word's little set: the only other bow-in-the-cloud in Scripture shines around the likeness of the glory. And the hearing stands beside the datum, labeled, as the proposer's hearing of the whole canon: the weapon word hung in the cloud by the hand that once poured out the judgment, the promise looking past the flood to the Lamb God would provide for Himself, and this present world kept not for water but for fire. The two registers do not blend: Genesis 9 says the sign and the remembering; the hearing hears where the bow came from and where the story goes — and each is true at its own place.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub as statements of Genesis 9: "God laid aside His weapon of war" and the title's "unstrung" — no verse in the chapter says the bow was unstrung, laid aside, or that God disarmed; the Genesis occurrences file under the rainbow sense, not the weapon sense. The picture stands in this entry only in the hearing register, on the word's identity (קַשְׁתִּי, "My bow") and its NT receipts, labeled as a hearing and not as the chapter's claim. The stub's title, "Qeshet and Zakhar (The Unstrung Bow and Active Faithfulness)," is likewise retired; the entry's title names the bow in the cloud and God's remembering, which is what the entry concludes. (2) "He acts to save us independent of human behavior" is retired as a statement of this chapter: the same pericope that promises no second flood lays the blood-reckoning on man (Gen 9:4–6, closed at KB059), and this entry's claim is the narrower, stated one — the *remembering* has no human trigger in the text. (3) The stub's "the final judgment for human rebellion was absorbed by God Himself on the wood" is not carried: it has no Genesis receipt and is not what the hearing's texts say either; the hearing carries the forward look on its own receipts (Gen 22:8; John 1:29; 2 Pet 3:5–7) and no further. (4) Ezekiel 1:28 is quoted at its own strength: Ezekiel says the *appearance* of the bow and the *appearance of the likeness* of the glory; the entry does not upgrade his hedges into an identification of the bow with the glory itself. (5) A translation datum the entry itself uses: the NASB renders the filed sense, "rainbow," at Gen 9:13, 14, 16 and Ezek 1:28; the Hebrew prints the one word קֶשֶׁת, at 9:13 with God's possessive (קַשְׁתִּי, "My bow"). The datum rests on the filed sense; the hearing rests on the word's identity; neither is smuggled into the other. (6) Genesis 22:8 stands in the set as pasted — the verse alone, without v. 14's naming ("the LORD will provide"); nothing in the hearing rests on the unquoted verse. Genesis 9:4–7 is banked with this entry's passage but is spent here only as the boundary named in (2); its own closure is KB059.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The unstrung-bow reading as Genesis exegesis (the stub's own): God disarmed Himself at the flood, and the rainbow is His retired weapon, stated by the chapter. Answered: the chapter does not state it — its occurrences of the word file under the rainbow sense, its verb is "I have set" (נָתַתִּי), and its stated errand for the sign is God's remembering, not a disarmament; what survives of the picture survives as the labeled hearing, on the word's identity and the canon read whole, not as what Genesis 9 says. (b) The reminder-for-man reading: the sign is chiefly a mnemonic for Noah and his descendants, a comfort for human eyes after storms. Answered: the text assigns the seeing and the remembering to God — "I will look at it, to remember" (9:16); "I will remember My covenant" (9:15) — and names the covenant's parties as God, Noah's seed, and every living creature, with the sign placed "between Me and the earth" (9:13); human comfort may follow, but the passage's own grammar makes God the sign's viewer and rememberer. (c) The bare-phenomenon reading: "rainbow" names only weather, and the covenant speech is an ancient explanation of a natural sight. Answered at the datum's level: the dictionaries file the phenomenon sense, and the text itself names the phenomenon's Giver, placement, and errand — "I have set My bow in the cloud… it shall serve as a sign of a covenant" — a giving inside a covenant speech whose content (the flood's non-return, 9:11) is a promise, not a meteorology; the filing describes what the thing looks like, and the verses state whose it is and what it is for. (d) The eisegesis charge against the hearing: the forward look — provided Lamb, world reserved for fire — is read into Genesis from outside it. Answered in the hearing's own register: the entry claims none of it for Genesis 9's saying; the hearing is labeled, its texts are quoted and pasted (Gen 22:8; John 1:29; 2 Pet 3:5–7), and the Framework's category for it is the proposer's own — the Old Testament read in the light of the New, the New being the Old's exegesis; a reader who declines the hearing still holds the whole datum, which stands without it.

### Dia (διά): The Agency of Relationship

- **ID:** KB033
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** διά, τὸ ῥηθὲν ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου, πάντα διʼ αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο, διʼ οὗ τὰ πάντα, διʼ οὗ καὶ ἐποίησεν τοὺς αἰῶνας, ὁ διʼ ἀγγέλων λαληθεὶς λόγος, διαταγεὶς διʼ ἀγγέλων, εἰ μὴ διʼ ἐμοῦ, διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, διὰ πνεύματος ἁγίου, διὰ τοῦ πνεύματος, ἐξ αὐτοῦ καὶ διʼ αὐτοῦ καὶ εἰς αὐτὸν τὰ πάντα — contrast keys (not διά receipts): ὑπὸ κυρίου (the originator's preposition, Matt 1:22), ἐξ οὗ τὰ πάντα (the from-side, 1 Cor 8:6)
- **Verse keys:** Matt 1:22; Matt 18:7; Matt 26:24; John 1:3; John 1:17; John 3:17; John 14:6; Acts 1:2; Acts 2:43; Acts 10:36; Acts 15:12; Acts 28:25; Rom 1:2; Rom 1:8; Rom 2:16; Rom 5:1; Rom 5:5; Rom 5:9; Rom 11:36; Rom 15:18; Rom 16:27; 1 Cor 2:10; 1 Cor 3:5; 1 Cor 8:6; 1 Cor 12:8; 1 Cor 15:21; 2 Cor 5:18; 2 Cor 5:20; Gal 1:1; Gal 3:19; Eph 2:18; Col 1:16; Col 3:17; Heb 1:2; Heb 2:2; Heb 7:25; Heb 13:15; Titus 3:6; 1 Pet 2:5; 1 John 4:9
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** διά with the genitive — filed [90.4], "a marker of intermediate agent, with implicit or explicit causative agent" — carries the direction of God's action in His household: God speaks, promises, works, and saves, and His action runs through prophets, through angels, through apostles, and through the Spirit, and through His Son, through whom all things came into being and through whom alone men come to the Father; and the same filing carries the return traffic, as men approach God, thank Him, and offer praise and sacrifices through the Son.
- **The chain:**
  - The word and its filing (the dictionary's own judgments, verified per occurrence against the local UBS data, `lexlook.py refs`): διά is a preposition. With the genitive the dictionary files it under several senses; the sense this entry rests on is [90.4], Agent — "(with the genitive) a marker of intermediate agent, with implicit or explicit causative agent," gloss: through, by — assigned at 129 verse references in the dictionary's data. The definition itself is two-sided: an *intermediate* agent, and behind him a *causative* agent, implicit or explicit. The chain below is a declared selection of 39 of those 129 references (43 tokens), plus one declared member from a neighbouring filing (Eph 2:18, [89.76], labeled where it stands) — 40 verses in all, chosen by structure: the action carried is household-defining, and the causative agent is named in the verse or supplied by its immediate context, as the filing itself allows (implicit or explicit); never chosen by frequency. The whole distribution stands pasted in the proposer's lemma search (lemma.g:διά@P, NASB 2020 + NA28) as the evidence base, and the selection is declared at Honesty (2).
  - God speaks through prophets: Matt 1:22 — τοῦτο δὲ ὅλον γέγονεν ἵνα πληρωθῇ τὸ ῥηθὲν ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου λέγοντος, "what was spoken *by* the Lord [ὑπὸ κυρίου] *through* the prophet [διὰ τοῦ προφήτου]" — one clause carrying both sides of the filing: the Lord as the speaking originator (ὑπό), the prophet as the intermediate agent (διά). Rom 1:2 — ὃ προεπηγγείλατο διὰ τῶν προφητῶν αὐτοῦ, the gospel "which He promised beforehand through His prophets": God the promiser, the prophets the through-whom of the promise. Acts 28:25 — καλῶς τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον ἐλάλησεν διὰ Ἠσαΐου τοῦ προφήτου, "the Holy Spirit rightly spoke through Isaiah the prophet": the Spirit named as speaker, the prophet as His channel.
  - Through angels: Heb 2:2 — ὁ διʼ ἀγγέλων λαληθεὶς λόγος, "the word spoken through angels," which "proved unalterable": God speaking, angels the intermediate agents of the word. Gal 3:19 — διαταγεὶς διʼ ἀγγέλων, the Law "ordered through angels": the διά token carries the angels; the verse's tail, ἐν χειρὶ μεσίτου ("at the hand of a mediator"), is a different construction — ἐν with the dative, not a διά token, carrying no διά filing — and its mediator is not named in the verse; it is reported so its presence is a decision, and nothing in this entry rests on it.
  - Through Moses, and through Jesus Christ: John 1:17 — ὁ νόμος διὰ Μωϋσέως ἐδόθη, ἡ χάρις καὶ ἡ ἀλήθεια διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ἐγένετο, "the Law was given through Moses; grace and truth were realized through Jesus Christ" — διά files [90.4] at both tokens of the verse; the same construction carries the old gift and the new, and what differs is the agent and the gift, not the word.
  - Creation through the Son: John 1:3 — πάντα διʼ αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο, καὶ χωρὶς αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο οὐδὲ ἓν ὃ γέγονεν, "all things came into being through Him, and apart from Him not even one thing came into being that has come into being" — the verse states the through-side and then closes the exception: nothing made stands outside the διʼ αὐτοῦ. 1 Cor 8:6 — εἷς θεὸς ὁ πατὴρ ἐξ οὗ τὰ πάντα καὶ ἡμεῖς εἰς αὐτόν, καὶ εἷς κύριος Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς διʼ οὗ τὰ πάντα καὶ ἡμεῖς διʼ αὐτοῦ, "one God, the Father, *from whom* are all things… and one Lord, Jesus Christ, *through whom* are all things, and we through Him" — the origin side and the through side stated as a pair, over the same τὰ πάντα, both διά tokens filed [90.4]. Col 1:16 — τὰ πάντα διʼ αὐτοῦ καὶ εἰς αὐτὸν ἔκτισται, "all things have been created through Him and for Him." Heb 1:2 — διʼ οὗ καὶ ἐποίησεν τοὺς αἰῶνας, "through whom He also made the ages": God the maker, the Son the through-whom of the making.
  - Salvation through the Son: John 3:17 — οὐ γὰρ ἀπέστειλεν ὁ θεὸς τὸν υἱόν… ἀλλʼ ἵνα σωθῇ ὁ κόσμος διʼ αὐτοῦ, "God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him" — the sender and the through-whom in one sentence. Rom 5:1 — εἰρήνην ἔχομεν πρὸς τὸν θεὸν διὰ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ." Rom 5:9 — σωθησόμεθα διʼ αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τῆς ὀργῆς, "we shall be saved from wrath through Him." 2 Cor 5:18 — τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ καταλλάξαντος ἡμᾶς ἑαυτῷ διὰ Χριστοῦ, "God, who reconciled us to Himself through Christ" — God the reconciler, Christ the through-whom. Titus 3:6 — οὗ ἐξέχεεν ἐφʼ ἡμᾶς πλουσίως διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ σωτῆρος ἡμῶν, the Spirit "whom He richly poured out upon us through Jesus Christ our Savior" (the verse's through-side stands closed at KB021). Acts 10:36 — τὸν λόγον [ὃν] ἀπέστειλεν τοῖς υἱοῖς Ἰσραὴλ εὐαγγελιζόμενος εἰρήνην διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "the word which He sent to the sons of Israel, preaching peace through Jesus Christ" — the participle εὐαγγελιζόμενος is nominative, and a nominative participle hangs on the clause's nominative subject, the sender: God sent the word, Himself announcing the peace, and the peace runs through Jesus Christ — the originator stands as the subject of His own announcing, with the Son at the διά. 1 John 4:9 — ἀπέσταλκεν ὁ θεὸς… ἵνα ζήσωμεν διʼ αὐτοῦ, God "has sent His only Son into the world so that we may live through Him." Rom 2:16 — κρίνει ὁ θεὸς τὰ κρυπτὰ τῶν ἀνθρώπων… διὰ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ, "God will judge the secrets of mankind through Christ Jesus" — the agency reaches the judgment itself; the Son is the through-whom of God's judging as of His saving.
  - Through the Spirit: Rom 5:5 — ἡ ἀγάπη τοῦ θεοῦ ἐκκέχυται ἐν ταῖς καρδίαις ἡμῶν διὰ πνεύματος ἁγίου τοῦ δοθέντος ἡμῖν, "the love of God has been poured out within our hearts through the Holy Spirit who was given to us" — God pours, the Spirit is the through-whom of the pouring, and the destination is hearts, not a distance. 1 Cor 2:10 — ἡμῖν δὲ ἀπεκάλυψεν ὁ θεὸς διὰ τοῦ πνεύματος, "God revealed them through the Spirit": God remains the subject of the revealing. 1 Cor 12:8 — ᾧ μὲν γὰρ διὰ τοῦ πνεύματος δίδοται λόγος σοφίας, "to one is given the word of wisdom through the Spirit" — the giving runs through the Spirit. Acts 1:2 — ἐντειλάμενος τοῖς ἀποστόλοις διὰ πνεύματος ἁγίου, Jesus, "after He had given orders through the Holy Spirit to the apostles whom He had chosen": the Son instructs, the Spirit the through-whom, the apostles the recipients.
  - Through apostles and sent ones: Acts 2:43 — πολλά τε τέρατα καὶ σημεῖα διὰ τῶν ἀποστόλων ἐγίνετο, "many wonders and signs were taking place through the apostles." Acts 15:12 — ὅσα ἐποίησεν ὁ θεὸς σημεῖα καὶ τέρατα ἐν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν διʼ αὐτῶν, "all the signs and wonders that God had done through them among the Gentiles" — God the doer (ἐποίησεν ὁ θεός), Barnabas and Paul the through-whom. 2 Cor 5:20 — ὡς τοῦ θεοῦ παρακαλοῦντος διʼ ἡμῶν, "as though God were making His appeal through us": the apostles are the intermediate agents of God's own appeal. Rom 15:18 — ὧν οὐ κατειργάσατο Χριστὸς διʼ ἐμοῦ, "what Christ has accomplished through me" — Christ the worker, Paul the through-whom. Gal 1:1 — ἀπόστολος οὐκ ἀπʼ ἀνθρώπων οὐδὲ διʼ ἀνθρώπου ἀλλὰ διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ καὶ θεοῦ πατρός, "an apostle, not from men nor through a man, but through Jesus Christ and God the Father" — both διά tokens filed [90.4]; apostleship itself runs through the Son and the Father, and pointedly not through a human channel. The verse's second token is also this entry's second breaker, and is answered where the breakers stand: the Father Himself stands at the διά side here, with no causative agent behind Him named or implied — the filing marks the channel of an action (here, the constituting of an apostle), and it no more demotes the Father at Gal 1:1 than it demotes God at Rom 11:36. 1 Cor 3:5 — διάκονοι διʼ ὧν ἐπιστεύσατε, Paul and Apollos are "servants through whom you believed."
  - The return direction — men to God, through the Son: John 14:6 — οὐδεὶς ἔρχεται πρὸς τὸν πατέρα εἰ μὴ διʼ ἐμοῦ, "no one comes to the Father except through Me": the coming is ours, the through-whom is the Son, the destination is the Father. Col 3:17 — εὐχαριστοῦντες τῷ θεῷ πατρὶ διʼ αὐτοῦ, "giving thanks through Him to God the Father": thanksgiving travels the same word, upward. Rom 1:8 — εὐχαριστῶ τῷ θεῷ μου διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "I thank my God through Jesus Christ." Heb 13:15 — διʼ αὐτοῦ [οὖν] ἀναφέρωμεν θυσίαν αἰνέσεως… τῷ θεῷ, "through Him let us offer up a sacrifice of praise to God" (the [οὖν] stands in NA28's brackets — textually uncertain, and nothing here rests on it) (the verse carries a second διά token, διὰ παντὸς, the NASB's "continually," which files under the time senses and is not spent here — only the διʼ αὐτοῦ token is this entry's). 1 Pet 2:5 — ἀνενέγκαι πνευματικὰς θυσίας εὐπροσδέκτους θεῷ διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "to offer spiritual sacrifices acceptable to God through Jesus Christ." Heb 7:25 — τοὺς προσερχομένους διʼ αὐτοῦ τῷ θεῷ, He "is able to save forever those who come to God through Him." Rom 16:27 — μόνῳ σοφῷ θεῷ, διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, ᾧ ἡ δόξα, "to the only wise God, through Jesus Christ, be the glory forever" — the filing ([90.4] at this token, verified) reads the διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ as the channel of the routing, as the NASB's rendering has it: the glory is the only wise God's, carried through the Son. And the door stated whole: Eph 2:18 — διʼ αὐτοῦ ἔχομεν τὴν προσαγωγὴν οἱ ἀμφότεροι ἐν ἑνὶ πνεύματι πρὸς τὸν πατέρα, "through Him we both have our access in one Spirit to the Father" — carried at its own filing ([89.76], Means) at its own place, named here as the selection's one non-[90.4] member and not blended into the filing the claim rests on.
  - The breaker sweep, selected on purpose: Rom 11:36 — ἐξ αὐτοῦ καὶ διʼ αὐτοῦ καὶ εἰς αὐτὸν τὰ πάντα, "from Him and through Him and to Him are all things" — filed [90.4] with God Himself standing at the διʼ αὐτοῦ; and Gal 1:1's second token (quoted in its own section above) stands the Father at the διά side of Paul's apostleship, with no causative agent behind Him. Two God-at-the-through-side instances, not one: the filing marks the channel of an action, and the direction of any given instance is read from who originates, verse by verse; the word by itself demotes no one — not the Son, and not the Father. 1 Cor 15:21 — διʼ ἀνθρώπου θάνατος, καὶ διʼ ἀνθρώπου ἀνάστασις νεκρῶν, "through a man death came; through a man also the resurrection of the dead" — both tokens [90.4]: the construction is neutral, and the person in the channel decides what flows through it. Matt 18:7 — οὐαὶ τῷ ἀνθρώπῳ διʼ οὗ τὸ σκάνδαλον ἔρχεται, "woe to the person through whom the stumbling block comes"; Matt 26:24 — διʼ οὗ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου παραδίδοται, the man "through whom the Son of Man is betrayed" — the same word carries the dark traffic, and the entry's claim is about the word's job, not about the worth of everyone it is used of.
- **The close (settled):** The dictionary's filing settles the word's job: διά with the genitive, at the filing's verses in this set, marks an intermediate agent with an originating agent behind him, implicit or explicit. And the selected distribution settles the filing's occupants at these verses, in both directions. Downward: the speaking is spoken by the Lord through prophets and through angels; the promise is God's, through His prophets; creation, salvation, reconciliation, and the coming judgment run through the Son; the love is poured out within hearts through the Spirit (Rom 5:5), the Spirit Himself is poured out through the Son (Titus 3:6), and the revealing runs through the Spirit (1 Cor 2:10); the signs are God's doing, through apostles; the appeal is God's, through His sent ones. Where the construction names the originator, it names him as the actor — as the verb's subject (God *reconciled* us, 2 Cor 5:18; God *revealed* them, 1 Cor 2:10; God *had done* the signs, Acts 15:12) or by the agent phrase standing beside the construction (τὸ ῥηθὲν ὑπὸ κυρίου, "spoken *by* the Lord," Matt 1:22). Upward, under the same filing: men come to the Father through the Son alone (John 14:6), come to God through Him (Heb 7:25), and thank God, offer praise and spiritual sacrifices, and direct glory to Him through the Son (Rom 1:8; Col 3:17; Heb 13:15; 1 Pet 2:5; Rom 16:27) — instances, each at its own verse; the "alone" in this set is receipted for the coming (14:6) and is not transferred to the rest. The breakers stand inside the settled datum, bounding it: the same filing carries God Himself at the through-side (Rom 11:36; Gal 1:1), death and resurrection alike through a man (1 Cor 15:21), and the stumbling block and the betrayal through a man (Matt 18:7; 26:24) — the filing marks the channel of an action, whoever stands in it, and settles nothing about the worth or rank of the one it names.
- **The proposed close (marked):** The proposer's reading of the settled distribution, marked as his and claimed at no greater strength: the two directions are one household's traffic. The same small word that carries God's speaking, creating, saving, and appealing down through His agents carries men's coming, thanking, praising, and offering back up — and at both directions the Son is the hinge: downward, the through-whom of creation and salvation beside the Spirit's through-places and the prophets', angels', and apostles' own channels; upward, the through-whom of every return instance in this set (the door at Eph 2:18 counted at its own filing, [89.76]). "Household" is the proposer's name for the field these verses stand in (Honesty (6)); no verse in the set states the pattern whole, and the pattern is the conjunction of the instances, each true at its own place, and nothing beyond what they jointly state. The Son's yielded will inside this pattern — He does nothing from Himself, but seeks the will of the One who sent Him — stands at KB068 (John 5:30, with 5:19 and 12:49–50) and is pointed to there, not re-argued here.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its substance is sorted, not carried: its title, "The Household Order of Relationship," is retired with the framing it names, and the new title names the entry's datum and its field. The stub's first sentence — that the scriptural framework "relies exclusively on relational and authority-based nouns" — is not carried in any form: "exclusively" asserts a whole-canon census no set here receipts, and the softened "almost exclusively" asserts the same census with a hedge and is likewise not carried; the nouns themselves (Father, Son, Sent One, Head) stand in the entry only where a verse in the set names the person in the agency relation, and no claim about the canon's noun vocabulary is made. "Master" in the stub's list is unplaced: no entry in the Knowledge Base closes it as the stub used it, and nothing here rests on it. "Head" stands partly at KB054 (1 Cor 11:3, carried there in a labeled frame, cited not closed) and at KB064, whose chain carries Christ as Head of the new creation (Col 1:18); it is not closed here. (2) The selection is declared: 40 verses — 39 of the [90.4] filing's 129 dictionary references (43 tokens), plus Eph 2:18, the chain's one declared member from the neighbouring Means filing — chosen because the action carried is household-defining (speaking, creating, promising, saving, reconciling, judging, revealing, approaching, thanking) and the causative agent is named in the verse or supplied by its immediate context, as the filing's own definition allows (implicit or explicit), with the breaker sweep chosen adversarially; the remaining references stand in the pasted distribution (lemma.g:διά@P), unexamined one by one, and no claim in this entry rests on any of them individually — the claim rests on the filing's definition and the selected set, and the selection is a declared proving set, not a concordance and not a census. (3) The filings are kept separate, and no property is transferred between them: διά also files as Means [89.76], Instrument [90.8], and — with the accusative — as Reason [90.44] and Benefaction [90.38]; this entry spends the accusative uses nowhere (John 6:57's διὰ τὸν πατέρα is "because of the Father," filed [90.44], and stands at KB068 in that register), the spatial and time uses nowhere, and the means-channels at their own places only: the mouth, hand, and word constructions (Luke 1:70; Acts 1:16; 3:18; 5:12; John 17:20) file [89.76], [90.8], and [89.26] and stand outside this chain as witnesses in the same direction, claimed for nothing here; Rom 5:19's διά files [89.26] and is spent at KB051, and Rom 8:11's files [89.76] and is not spent here. Eph 2:18 is the chain's one declared exception, carried at [89.76] and labeled where it stands. (4) No filing is claimed where the dictionary's data assigns none: Jude 25, Rev 1:1, and 1 Pet 4:11 each carry a διά the NASB renders "through," and the dictionary data returns no sense assignment at those verses; they are quotable as text and are excluded from this set for exactly that reason, and no sentence here rests on them. (5) The stub's verb-table is sorted to the entries that closed its parts and is not re-argued here: the Son sent and the Father sending stand at KB011 and KB068; the mediator at KB023 (1 Tim 2:5); the Son's receiving and anointing in KB058's sequence; the Son revealing the Father at KB010 (John 1:18, ἐξηγήσατο); and the Son's yielded will — the oneness side of the stub's closing clause — stands at KB068 (John 5:30, with 5:19 and 12:49–50) — this entry's datum is the word that carries the direction those entries' verses also travel, and it adds the filing and the two-directional distribution to them, nothing more. (6) "Agency" and "agent" in this entry are the dictionary's own vocabulary — its [90.4] definition is written in those words ("intermediate agent," "causative agent") — cited as the dictionary's judgment, not imported from a system; "household" is the Framework's name for the field the verses stand in; "household" and "hinge" alike are the proposer's labels — for the entry's field, and for the Son's place in the pattern — not words any verse in the set files. No claim rests on the English word "through" itself: every instance spent is a διά token verified against the dictionary's per-sense assignments, and no instance is admitted or excluded by its English rendering — "by" and "through" alike stand or fall by the filing at the verse, inside the selection as declared at (2).
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The impersonal-means reading — διά is a colourless "by means of," marking instruments and methods, and no personal agency, let alone a household order, can be drawn from a preposition. Answered by the dictionary's own division of the word: it maintains an Agent filing, [90.4], distinct from its Means filing, [89.76], and assigns the personal channels of this set — prophets, angels, apostles, the Spirit, the Son — to the Agent filing, in a definition written for persons ("intermediate agent," "causative agent"); where the dictionary means means, it files means — at Eph 2:18, and inside Col 1:20 itself, whose three διά tokens split between the filings ([90.4] at the two διʼ αὐτοῦ tokens, [89.76] at διὰ τοῦ αἵματος, "through the blood") — so its agent assignments here are its judgment between its own senses, token by token, not a blur. And Matthew 1:22 sets the two agency prepositions side by side in one clause — ὑπὸ κυρίου for the originator, διὰ τοῦ προφήτου for the intermediate — a distinction the verse itself draws. (b) The demotion reading — whoever stands at the through-side of διά is thereby a lesser being, a creaturely instrument beneath the originator, so the Son's through-places fix His rank below God's. Answered from inside the set: the filing marks a role in an action, not a rank of being, and the same filing places God Himself at the through-side twice — at Rom 11:36 ("through Him are all things") and at Gal 1:1, where the Father stands at the διά of Paul's apostleship with no causative agent behind Him — while at 1 Cor 8:6 the same τὰ πάντα stand on both sides of the pairing, from the Father and through the Lord, and at John 1:3 the through-whom is the one apart from whom "not even one thing came into being"; a word that carries the Father's own through-ness cannot by itself demote the one it carries, and what the Son is stands closed at KB010 on its own set, not settled by a preposition here. (c) The distant-delegation reading — agency means God hands His work off to intermediaries and withdraws, a chain of command at arm's length. Answered by the set's own verses, not by the filing's definition (an implicit causative agent is, by itself, compatible with distance; the definition does not exclude the picture — the texts do): at the subject-verses the originator is the verb's own subject — "God had done" the signs that came through the apostles (Acts 15:12), "God revealed" through the Spirit (1 Cor 2:10), God "reconciled us to Himself" through Christ (2 Cor 5:18) — and the through-traffic terminates in indwelling, not distance: the love is poured *into our hearts* through the Spirit who was *given to us* (Rom 5:5), and the return direction is approach, not reporting up a chain (Heb 7:25; Eph 2:18 at its own filing). (d) The concordance reading — since "through" is the household's key word, every "through" in Scripture teaches this agency, and the entry should have taken them all. Answered by the word's own filings: διά with the accusative is reason, not agency (John 6:57); διά through a place is geography; διά through the night is time; and three strong "through Jesus Christ" verses (Jude 25; 1 Pet 4:11; with Rev 1:1's angel) carry no dictionary assignment at all and so cannot be spent as filings. The entry's discipline — the filing at the verse, the selection declared — is the answer: the agency datum is exactly as wide as the [90.4] assignments in the selected set, and no wider claim is made.

### Ellogeō (ἐλλογέω) & Dikaioō (δικαιόω)

- **ID:** KB034
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** οὐκ ἐλλογεῖται, τοῦτο ἐμοὶ ἐλλόγα, οὐ δικαιωθήσεται πᾶσα σάρξ, δικαιοῦντα τὸν ἀσεβῆ, δικαιωθέντες οὖν ἐκ πίστεως, θεὸς ὁ δικαιῶν
- **Verse keys:** Rom 2:13; Rom 3:20; Rom 3:24; Rom 3:26; Rom 3:28; Rom 3:30; Rom 4:2; Rom 4:5; Rom 5:1; Rom 5:9; Rom 5:13; Rom 8:30; Rom 8:33; Gal 2:16; Gal 2:17; Gal 3:8; Gal 3:11; Gal 3:24; Gal 5:4; Titus 3:7; Phlm 18
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In this set δικαιόω is filed at every occurrence under one sense — God's putting a person right with Himself ([34.46]) — never by works of the Law (Rom 3:20, 28; Gal 2:16) but by faith, as a gift by His grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus and by His blood (Rom 3:24; 5:9), with peace with God (Rom 5:1), salvation from wrath through Him (Rom 5:9), and heirship (Titus 3:7) as its named results, the same God who justified being the one who also glorified (Rom 8:30); and ἐλλογέω, the account word the stub pairs with it, stands only at Rom 5:13 — sin is not recorded where there is no law — and at Phlm 18 — Paul's "charge that to my account" — no verse in either word's distribution joins the two words, and no filing gives δικαιόω an account-clearing sense.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 2:13: οὐ γὰρ οἱ ἀκροαταὶ νόμου δίκαιοι παρὰ [τῷ] θεῷ, ἀλλʼ οἱ ποιηταὶ νόμου δικαιωθήσονται — "for it is not the hearers of the Law who are righteous before God, but the doers of the Law who will be justified." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιωθήσονται, future passive. The set's opening statement names *doers*, not hearers — ποιηταὶ νόμου, with no "by works of the Law" instrument phrase in the verse (that phrase, ἐξ ἔργων νόμου, is the one the denials below carry); the verse stands at full strength and is not trimmed by what follows.
  - Rom 3:20: διότι ἐξ ἔργων νόμου οὐ δικαιωθήσεται πᾶσα σὰρξ ἐνώπιον αὐτοῦ, διὰ γὰρ νόμου ἐπίγνωσις ἁμαρτίας — "because by the works of the Law none of mankind will be justified in His sight; for through the Law comes knowledge of sin." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιωθήσεται, future passive. The denial is universal (πᾶσα σάρξ, no flesh) and names its instrument: ἐξ ἔργων νόμου. The Law's work in the verse is knowledge of sin, not putting right.
  - Rom 3:24: δικαιούμενοι δωρεὰν τῇ αὐτοῦ χάριτι διὰ τῆς ἀπολυτρώσεως τῆς ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ — "being justified as a gift by His grace through the redemption which is in Christ Jesus," Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιούμενοι, present passive participle. The putting right here is δωρεάν — as a gift — by His grace, through the redemption in Christ Jesus; the verse names gift, grace, and redemption, and no account or scroll vocabulary.
  - Rom 3:26: ἐν τῇ ἀνοχῇ τοῦ θεοῦ, πρὸς τὴν ἔνδειξιν τῆς δικαιοσύνης αὐτοῦ ἐν τῷ νῦν καιρῷ, εἰς τὸ εἶναι αὐτὸν δίκαιον καὶ δικαιοῦντα τὸν ἐκ πίστεως Ἰησοῦ — "for the demonstration, that is, of His righteousness at the present time, so that He would be just and the justifier of the one who has faith in Jesus." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῦντα, present active participle, predicated of God. The justifier is God Himself (αὐτὸν δίκαιον καὶ δικαιοῦντα) — just, and the one who puts right the one of faith in Jesus (τὸν ἐκ πίστεως Ἰησοῦ).
  - Rom 3:28: λογιζόμεθα γὰρ δικαιοῦσθαι πίστει ἄνθρωπον χωρὶς ἔργων νόμου — "For we maintain that a person is justified by faith apart from works of the Law." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῦσθαι, present passive infinitive. The conclusion of the argument: a person is put right πίστει, by faith, χωρὶς ἔργων νόμου, apart from works of the Law. The reckoning word of the verse is λογιζόμεθα — "we maintain/reckon" — a third word, neither of this entry's two.
  - Rom 3:30: εἴπερ εἷς ὁ θεὸς ὃς δικαιώσει περιτομὴν ἐκ πίστεως καὶ ἀκροβυστίαν διὰ τῆς πίστεως — "since indeed God who will justify the circumcised by faith and the uncircumcised through faith is one." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιώσει, future active, God the subject. One God (εἷς ὁ θεός) will justify both groups, and the means is faith on both sides — ἐκ πίστεως for the circumcised, διὰ τῆς πίστεως for the uncircumcised.
  - Rom 4:2: εἰ γὰρ Ἀβραὰμ ἐξ ἔργων ἐδικαιώθη, ἔχει καύχημα, ἀλλʼ οὐ πρὸς θεόν — "For if Abraham was justified by works, he has something to boast about; but not before God." Filed [34.46]; the form is ἐδικαιώθη, aorist passive, inside the verse's εἰ condition. The verse is a stated condition (εἰ … ἐδικαιώθη, "if Abraham was justified by works"), and its own limit is in the verse: any boast from it is οὐ πρὸς θεόν, not before God.
  - Rom 4:5: τῷ δὲ μὴ ἐργαζομένῳ πιστεύοντι δὲ ἐπὶ τὸν δικαιοῦντα τὸν ἀσεβῆ λογίζεται ἡ πίστις αὐτοῦ εἰς δικαιοσύνην — "But to the one who does not work, but believes in Him who justifies the ungodly, his faith is credited as righteousness," Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῦντα, present active participle, predicated of God. The one put right here is the one who does *not* work but believes — and the justifier is named by his object: τὸν δικαιοῦντα τὸν ἀσεβῆ, the one who justifies *the ungodly*. The crediting word is again λογίζεται, not ἐλλογέω.
  - Rom 5:1: Δικαιωθέντες οὖν ἐκ πίστεως εἰρήνην ἔχομεν πρὸς τὸν θεὸν διὰ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ — "Therefore, having been justified by faith, we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ," Filed [34.46]; the form is Δικαιωθέντες, aorist passive participle. The named result of the putting right is a relation: εἰρήνην … πρὸς τὸν θεόν, peace with God — the [34.46] definition's "right relation" shown in the text's own word, through our Lord Jesus Christ.
  - Rom 5:9: πολλῷ οὖν μᾶλλον δικαιωθέντες νῦν ἐν τῷ αἵματι αὐτοῦ σωθησόμεθα διʼ αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τῆς ὀργῆς — "Much more then, having now been justified by His blood, we shall be saved from the wrath of God through Him." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιωθέντες, aorist passive participle. The putting right is ἐν τῷ αἵματι αὐτοῦ, by His blood, and its forward result is salvation from the wrath, διʼ αὐτοῦ, through Him.
  - Rom 5:13: ἄχρι γὰρ νόμου ἁμαρτία ἦν ἐν κόσμῳ, ἁμαρτία δὲ οὐκ ἐλλογεῖται μὴ ὄντος νόμου — "for until the Law sin was in the world, but sin is not counted against anyone when there is no law." This is ἐλλογέω's Romans occurrence, filed [33.45] "to keep a record of something; to record, to list" (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such); the form is ἐλλογεῖται, present passive. It stands inside the same letter, in the very chapter that carries two put-right verses (Rom 5:1, 9), and it is not a δικαιόω verse: what is *not recorded* is sin where there is no law — the record-word names a reckoning withheld, not an account cleared.
  - Rom 8:30: οὓς δὲ προώρισεν, τούτους καὶ ἐκάλεσεν· καὶ οὓς ἐκάλεσεν, τούτους καὶ ἐδικαίωσεν· οὓς δὲ ἐδικαίωσεν, τούτους καὶ ἐδόξασεν — "and these whom He predestined, He also called; and these whom He called, He also justified; and these whom He justified, He also glorified." Filed [34.46], twice; the form is ἐδικαίωσεν, aorist active, God the subject of the whole chain. The word stands in God's own chain of acts — predestined, called, justified, glorified — with God the subject throughout.
  - Rom 8:33: τίς ἐγκαλέσει κατὰ ἐκλεκτῶν θεοῦ; θεὸς ὁ δικαιῶν — "Who will bring charges against God's elect? God is the one who justifies;" Filed [34.46]; the form is ὁ δικαιῶν, present active participle, predicated of God. The courtroom charge-word of this verse is ἐγκαλέσει (will bring a charge) — again not ἐλλογέω — and the answer to it is a person: θεὸς ὁ δικαιῶν, God, the one who justifies.
  - Gal 2:16: εἰδότες [δὲ] ὅτι οὐ δικαιοῦται ἄνθρωπος ἐξ ἔργων νόμου ἐὰν μὴ διὰ πίστεως Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, καὶ ἡμεῖς εἰς Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν ἐπιστεύσαμεν, ἵνα δικαιωθῶμεν ἐκ πίστεως Χριστοῦ καὶ οὐκ ἐξ ἔργων νόμου, ὅτι ἐξ ἔργων νόμου οὐ δικαιωθήσεται πᾶσα σάρξ — "nevertheless, knowing that a person is not justified by works of the Law but through faith in Christ Jesus, even we have believed in Christ Jesus, so that we may be justified by faith in Christ and not by works of the Law; since by works of the Law no flesh will be justified." Filed [34.46], three times; the forms are δικαιοῦται (present passive), δικαιωθῶμεν (aorist passive subjunctive), and δικαιωθήσεται (future passive). The set's fullest statement: the means question is put and answered three ways in one verse — not by works of the Law, but through faith in Christ Jesus; justified by faith in Christ, not by works of the Law; by works of the Law no flesh will be justified.
  - Gal 2:17: εἰ δὲ ζητοῦντες δικαιωθῆναι ἐν Χριστῷ εὑρέθημεν καὶ αὐτοὶ ἁμαρτωλοί, ἆρα Χριστὸς ἁμαρτίας διάκονος; μὴ γένοιτο — "But if, while seeking to be justified in Christ, we ourselves have also been found sinners, is Christ then a servant of sin? Far from it!" Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιωθῆναι, aorist passive infinitive. The seeking is located ἐν Χριστῷ, in Christ — the put-right is sought in Him, not in an account procedure.
  - Gal 3:8: προϊδοῦσα δὲ ἡ γραφὴ ὅτι ἐκ πίστεως δικαιοῖ τὰ ἔθνη ὁ θεός, προευηγγελίσατο τῷ Ἀβραὰμ ὅτι ἐνευλογηθήσονται ἐν σοὶ πάντα τὰ ἔθνη — "The Scripture, foreseeing that God would justify the Gentiles by faith, preached the gospel beforehand to Abraham, saying, “ALL THE NATIONS WILL BE BLESSED IN YOU.”" Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῖ, present active, God the subject. God is the subject (δικαιοῖ … ὁ θεός), the nations the object, faith the means (ἐκ πίστεως) — and the verse dates the announcement back to Abraham.
  - Gal 3:11: ὅτι δὲ ἐν νόμῳ οὐδεὶς δικαιοῦται παρὰ τῷ θεῷ δῆλον, ὅτι ὁ δίκαιος ἐκ πίστεως ζήσεται — "Now, that no one is justified by the Law before God is evident; for, “THE RIGHTEOUS ONE WILL LIVE BY FAITH.”" Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῦται, present passive. No one is put right ἐν νόμῳ, by the Law, παρὰ τῷ θεῷ, before God; the verse's own ground is the quotation: the righteous one will live by faith.
  - Gal 3:24: ὥστε ὁ νόμος παιδαγωγὸς ἡμῶν γέγονεν εἰς Χριστόν, ἵνα ἐκ πίστεως δικαιωθῶμεν — "Therefore the Law has become our guardian to lead us to Christ, so that we may be justified by faith." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιωθῶμεν, aorist passive subjunctive. The Law's role is παιδαγωγός, guardian *toward* Christ (εἰς Χριστόν); the putting right is the purpose at the other end, ἐκ πίστεως, by faith.
  - Gal 5:4: κατηργήθητε ἀπὸ Χριστοῦ, οἵτινες ἐν νόμῳ δικαιοῦσθε, τῆς χάριτος ἐξεπέσατε — "You have been severed from Christ, you who are seeking to be justified by the Law; you have fallen from grace." Filed [34.46]; the form is δικαιοῦσθε, present passive. Seeking the put-right ἐν νόμῳ, in the Law, is not a second route to it: the verse names its results — severed from Christ, fallen from grace.
  - Titus 3:7: ἵνα δικαιωθέντες τῇ ἐκείνου χάριτι κληρονόμοι γενηθῶμεν κατʼ ἐλπίδα ζωῆς αἰωνίου — "so that being justified by His grace we would be made heirs according to the hope of eternal life." Filed [34.46] — the sense's one verse outside Romans and Galatians, standing inside the ruled filing; the form is δικαιωθέντες, aorist passive participle. The means is τῇ ἐκείνου χάριτι, by His grace, and the named result is heirship (κληρονόμοι) according to the hope of eternal life.
  - Phlm 18: εἰ δέ τι ἠδίκησέν σε ἢ ὀφείλει, τοῦτο ἐμοὶ ἐλλόγα — "But if he has wronged you in any way or owes you anything, charge that to my account;" This is ἐλλογέω's second and only other occurrence, filed [57.226] "to charge to an account; to charge" (the dictionary's judgment, cited as such); the form is ἐλλόγα, present active imperative — Paul directing, not describing. It is a real charge between real people: Paul directs Philemon to put Onesimus' wrong or debt (ἠδίκησέν … ἢ ὀφείλει) to Paul's own account (ἐμοὶ). The verse contains no δικαιόω, and the charge is not cleared, canceled, or zeroed in it — it is *accepted* by the one who says "charge it to me."
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction settles two things.
