  - 1 John 1:9: ἐὰν ὁμολογῶμεν τὰς ἁμαρτίας ἡμῶν, πιστός ἐστιν καὶ δίκαιος, ἵνα ἀφῇ ἡμῖν τὰς ἁμαρτίας — "If we confess our sins, He is faithful and righteous, so that He will forgive us our sins." Against 1:8's bare singular, the acts are plural and articular, twice in the verse: τὰς ἁμαρτίας ἡμῶν ("our sins") and then τὰς ἁμαρτίας with no possessive — literally "the sins" — which the NASB also prints as "our sins," flattening even the occurrence where the Greek itself says "the." John's differentiation of principle from acts runs on NUMBER — singular for sin as such, plural for the committed sins — where Paul's runs on the article.
  - 1 John 2:2: καὶ αὐτὸς ἱλασμός ἐστιν περὶ τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ἡμῶν, οὐ περὶ τῶν ἡμετέρων δὲ μόνον ἀλλὰ καὶ περὶ ὅλου τοῦ κόσμου — "and He Himself is the propitiation for our sins; and not for ours only, but also for the sins of the whole world." ἱλασμός anarthrous predicate (NASB supplies "the propitiation"); τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ἡμῶν articular plural again for the acts; and the NASB's second "the sins of" has no second Greek noun — the Greek says only "concerning the whole world," the ἁμαρτιῶν carrying over (a supplied repetition, named here so the English side of the comparison is stated exactly).
- **The close (settled):** Three things stand. First, the signal: in these chapters the article marks the referent as definite and identified in the discourse — the known reigning power (ἡ ἁμαρτία and her cases, 14 of 16 in Rom 6), the God the Word was with (τὸν θεόν), the life once introduced (ζωὴ → ἡ ζωή) — and its absence marks the positions that are not definite-reference: predicates stating what a subject is (θεός, John 1:1; φῶς, 1 John 1:5; σάρξ, John 1:14; ἱλασμός, 1 John 2:2), first introductions (ζωή; ἄνθρωπος), and category mentions (6:16's alternatives). The pattern's named exceptions stand inside the set itself: the two anarthrous ἁμαρτία occurrences in Rom 6 (6:14 the promise sentence; 6:16 the category pair) and John 1:4's τὸ φῶς, articular as a predicate at first mention; the signal is a discourse discipline, not a substitution code. Second, the authors differ in device: Paul differentiates the principle by the article (sin against *the* Sin, both in his one chapter), John by number (ἁμαρτίαν the singular against τὰς ἁμαρτίας the plural acts) — and a reading or rendering that attends to only one device will mishear the other author. Third, the consequence for English: the NASB flattens the signal in both directions — dropping the Greek's articles (all 16 of Romans 6's ἁμαρτία occurrences printed as bare "sin": the 14 articular ones flattened, the 2 anarthrous ones already bare; ἡ χάρις → "grace") and adding its own ("the Law" for νόμον; "the propitiation" for ἱλασμός) — so the distinctions are decisions a thought-for-thought rendering must make deliberately on the Greek, taking article and number both into account before saying what the text means in English; they cannot be inherited from the English page.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub, and recorded here: the title "The Anarthrous Essence vs. The Articular Action" and its scheme — anarthrous nouns define an essence, quality, or principle; articular nouns point to specific manifestations or actions (τὰς ἁμαρτίας as "the sins"). The scheme is broken by the set it cited: Romans' principle is articular (14 of 16), 1 John's specific acts are articular plural, and the scheme's headline anarthrous cases (θεός, φῶς) are predicates, a construction, not an essence signal. (2) Scope: three chapters, two authors. This entry closes the correlation for these chapters; it does not state a Koine-wide law of the article. Craig's Pastorals study (2026-10-12; διδασκαλία, 15 occurrences: 12 articular, every one the definite known teaching; 3 anarthrous, every one generic) is a prior one-word instance of the same definiteness pattern and stands as provenance, not re-closed here. (3) The pattern's exceptions are part of the close, not footnotes to it: Rom 6:14 and 6:16 show the same author leaving the principle anarthrous in a promise sentence and a category pair, so no rendering rule of the form "always print 'the Sin' for ἡ ἁμαρτία" is licensed by this entry; the datum governs deliberation, not substitution. (4) Bracketed and supplied matter: no load-bearing word in the chain rests on an NA28 bracket; on the English side, the NASB's additions named in the chain ("the Law," Rom 6:14; "the flesh," John 1:13; "the propitiation," 1 John 2:2; the repeated "the sins of," 1 John 2:2) are the translation's idiom decisions, cited as such, and the flattening finding is stated with them included, in both directions.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The essence/action reading (the stub's own): anarthrous nouns name essences and principles, articular nouns name manifestations and acts. Answered on the stub's own chapters: the personified principle of Romans 6 is articular in 14 of her 16 occurrences including her subject occurrence (6:12), the specific confessed acts of 1 John are articular plural (1:9; 2:2), and the anarthrous θεός and φῶς of John 1:1 and 1 John 1:5 are predicates — the absence there is construction, not essence. (b) The no-signal reading: the Greek article is idiom and emphasis at most; a translation loses nothing it needs by flattening it, and the English reader has the whole meaning. Answered: the distribution in the set is systematic, not decorative — first mention bare and back-reference articular within two clauses (John 1:4), the principle articular across a whole argument (Rom 6), a possessive-articular plural set against a bare singular within two verses (1 John 1:8–9) — and, standing as a pointer rather than a receipt of this entry, the same definiteness pattern in the Pastorals διδασκαλία set (Honesty (2)); a distinction both authors maintain this consistently is part of what their sentences say, and a page that cannot show it has lost something the writers spent. (c) The mechanical-mirror reading: a faithful rendering should print the article wherever Greek has it and "a" wherever it does not — "the Sin" for every ἡ ἁμαρτία, "a god" for the anarthrous θεός of John 1:1. Answered: the set breaks the machine in both directions — Rom 6:14 leaves the same reigning power anarthrous, and John 1:1's θεός is a preverbal predicate beside the articular subject ὁ λόγος, the construction this set uses for stating what the subject is (φῶς, 1 John 1:5; σάρξ, John 1:14; ἱλασμός, 1 John 2:2); in these chapters English "a" renders only newly introduced indefinite persons (ἄνθρωπος, John 1:6; ἀνδρός, 1:13), and the very sentence of John 1:1 names τὸν θεόν, the definite God, as the one the Word was with. The entry's datum informs the rendering decision; it does not automate it.
### The Reality of Sonship (Huiothesia)

- **ID:** KB045
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** υἱοθεσία, πνεῦμα υἱοθεσίας, ἡ υἱοθεσία, τὴν υἱοθεσίαν, εἰς υἱοθεσίαν, τὴν ἀπολύτρωσιν τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν
- **Verse keys:** Rom 8:15; Rom 8:23; Rom 9:4; Rom 9:5; Gal 4:5; Eph 1:5
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** υἱοθεσία is filed under one sense — [35.53], to formally and legally declare that someone who is not one's own child is henceforth to be treated and cared for as one's own child, including complete rights of inheritance — and the New Testament spends it exactly five times, singular at every place: received, where believers took not a spirit of slavery again into fear but a Spirit of adoption, in which we cry "Abba, the Father" (Rom 8:15); awaited, where those who hold the first fruits of the Spirit groan within themselves for the adoption Paul himself names as the redemption of our body (Rom 8:23); Israel's, heading the list of what belongs to the Israelites (Rom 9:4); the stated goal of the buying-out of those under law, that we might receive it (Gal 4:5); and foreordained, the destination God determined beforehand for us through Jesus Christ to Himself, according to the good pleasure of His will (Eph 1:5) — held in five relations across its five places: a present reception, a still-awaited redemption, Israel's own possession, redemption's purpose, and God's foreordained end, no one of them the whole of it; and the NASB renders it "adoption as sons and daughters" at all five places, an expansion the one Greek word does not carry.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 8:15: οὐ γὰρ ἐλάβετε πνεῦμα δουλείας πάλιν εἰς φόβον ἀλλʼ ἐλάβετε πνεῦμα υἱοθεσίας ἐν ᾧ κράζομεν· αββα ὁ πατήρ — "For you have not received a spirit of slavery leading to fear again, but you have received a spirit of adoption as sons and daughters by which we cry out, 'Abba! Father!'" The verb of reception is spent twice (ἐλάβετε ... ἐλάβετε): what was NOT received is a spirit of slavery again into fear; what WAS received is a Spirit of adoption (πνεῦμα υἱοθεσίας, genitive), and the cry is made in him — ἐν ᾧ κράζομεν, "in which we cry out." The adoption here is a stated, accomplished reception, not a hope.
  - Rom 8:23: οὐ μόνον δέ, ἀλλὰ καὶ αὐτοὶ τὴν ἀπαρχὴν τοῦ πνεύματος ἔχοντες, ἡμεῖς καὶ αὐτοὶ ἐν ἑαυτοῖς στενάζομεν υἱοθεσίαν ἀπεκδεχόμενοι, τὴν ἀπολύτρωσιν τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν — "And not only that, but also we ourselves, having the first fruits of the Spirit, even we ourselves groan within ourselves, waiting eagerly for our adoption as sons and daughters, the redemption of our body." The same word, eight verses later in the same chapter, is still awaited: ἀπεκδεχόμενοι (present — the waiting is ongoing), and the waiters are the ones who already hold the first fruits of the Spirit. Paul glosses the awaited adoption in his own apposition — it is τὴν ἀπολύτρωσιν τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν, the redemption of our body. The NASB supplies "our" before adoption; the Greek's possessive (ἡμῶν) stands on the body, not on the adoption.
  - Rom 9:4: οἵτινές εἰσιν Ἰσραηλῖται, ὧν ἡ υἱοθεσία καὶ ἡ δόξα καὶ αἱ διαθῆκαι καὶ ἡ νομοθεσία καὶ ἡ λατρεία καὶ αἱ ἐπαγγελίαι — "who are Israelites, to whom belongs the adoption as sons and daughters, the glory, the covenants, the giving of the Law, the temple service, and the promises." Ἡ υἱοθεσία — articular, nominative — heads a list of six belongings. The sentence's one verb is present — εἰσιν, "who ARE Israelites" — and the possession itself is stated with no verb at all: ὧν ἡ υἱοθεσία, "whose is the adoption," with no past marker anywhere in the sentence. The company it keeps in the list is her historic possessions — the glory, the covenants, the giving of the Law, the service, the promises — so the adoption is named as hers from of old, in a present frame, by the same apostle who has just stated the believers' reception (8:15); that juxtaposition is the very tension Romans 9–11 exists to answer.
  - Rom 9:5 (the sentence's completion, quoted in Greek as 9:4's frame under genuine context; no occurrence of the word stands in it, and no English rendering in this entry rests on it, its NASB text not being banked): ὧν οἱ πατέρες καὶ ἐξ ὧν ὁ Χριστὸς τὸ κατὰ σάρκα — "whose are the fathers, and from whom is the Christ according to the flesh" (rendered here literally; the verse is quoted only this far — its remaining words are not quoted and nothing rests on them: Honesty (3)). The list the adoption heads runs on to the fathers and to the Christ himself according to the flesh: the people whose the adoption is are the people from whom the Christ comes.
  - Gal 4:5: ἵνα τοὺς ὑπὸ νόμον ἐξαγοράσῃ, ἵνα τὴν υἱοθεσίαν ἀπολάβωμεν — "so that He might redeem those who were under the Law, that we might receive the adoption as sons and daughters." The verse is two purpose clauses: the buying-out of those under law, and its goal — ἵνα τὴν υἱοθεσίαν ἀπολάβωμεν, "that we might receive the adoption" (articular accusative; ἀπολάβωμεν, aorist subjunctive — the reception stated as the purpose whole). The adoption is not an incidental benefit of the redemption; it is the redemption's stated aim. The NASB renders ἐξαγοράσῃ "redeem"; the buying-out itself is closed at KB040, which renders it "buy out" — cited here as a pointer; this entry's receipt is the goal clause.
  - Eph 1:5: προορίσας ἡμᾶς εἰς υἱοθεσίαν διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ εἰς αὐτόν, κατὰ τὴν εὐδοκίαν τοῦ θελήματος αὐτοῦ — "He predestined us to adoption as sons and daughters through Jesus Christ to Himself, according to the good pleasure of His will," Προορίσας — having foreordained — takes ἡμᾶς εἰς υἱοθεσίαν: the adoption is a destination determined beforehand, reached διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (through Jesus Christ) and terminating εἰς αὐτόν (to Himself); its stated ground is not the adopted ones' quality but κατὰ τὴν εὐδοκίαν τοῦ θελήματος αὐτοῦ — according to the good pleasure of His will. The NASB's finite "He predestined" reads the participle with the sentence's governing verb (ἐξελέξατο, v.4, outside this set).
- **The close (settled):** The distribution itself, stated at its own strength. One word, one filing: adoption is a formal, legal placement — one not born a child declared and treated as one's own, with complete rights of inheritance — and none of its five places spends it as less: it is foreordained as a destination (Eph 1:5), set as the redemption's purpose (Gal 4:5), possessed (Rom 9:4), received (Rom 8:15), and awaited under Paul's own definition as the redemption of the body (Rom 8:23). The placement stands in two tenses, and the same author holds both in one chapter: already received — ἐλάβετε, a stated fact, with a Spirit of adoption in place of slavery and fear — and still awaited by the very people who hold the Spirit's first fruits, its completion named as the body's redemption; neither tense may be collapsed into the other, and a reading that keeps only one of Romans 8's two verses has not read the word's own chapter. The adoption is also Israel's: ὧν ἡ υἱοθεσία — "whose is the adoption," the clause itself verbless inside a present frame (εἰσιν) with no past marker — stands over the Israelites, heading the list of her historic belongings, and the believers' reception (Gal 4:5; Rom 8:15) and foreordination (Eph 1:5) are stated beside her possession, never as its revocation or transfer — no verse in the set moves the word from her to us; the two stand together, her possession and our reception, and the whole is the AND of them. And where the set names the placing's agent, the agent is God: foreordained by Him (Eph 1:5), and purposed in the buying-out, whose clauses hang on his sending of the Son (Gal 4:5, on v.4 — KB040's pointer); at the remaining places the verbs are reception, possession, and waiting — and at no place do the adopted adopt themselves. The English datum stands beside the Greek one: the NASB prints "adoption as sons and daughters" at all five places; the Greek prints the one word, singular every time, and this entry's claims rest on the Greek.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub, and recorded here: "the full inheritance and rights of the Firstborn" and "everyone in Christ … holds the status of a firstborn heir" — no firstborn word (πρωτότοκος) stands in the filing or in any of the five verses; the inheritance rights themselves STAND, because the filing states them ([35.53], "including complete rights of inheritance"), and they are carried at exactly that strength. Also retired: the stub's "decisive placement" as the finished whole (Rom 8:23 awaits the same adoption), its believers-only framing (Rom 9:4 names the adoption as the Israelites'), and its period dressing — "the exclusive right to inherit the family estate" — trimmed to the filing's own words. The title is kept: it names the word and its topic and asserts none of the retired claims (the title check is recorded in the findings packet). (2) "Male and female alike" (the stub) is not closed by this set, in either direction: its usual carrier, Gal 3:28, is outside the set. What the set holds is a translation fact (the NASB's "sons and daughters," five of five) and a Greek term that names no sex; the sentence belongs to Galatians 3's texts and is neither crowned nor denied here. (3) Frames, pointers, and sentence boundaries: Rom 9:5 is quoted in Greek only, as 9:4's sentence completion under genuine context (Framework Core Rule 8); its NASB text is not banked, no word-claim rests on it (it carries no occurrence), and the English rendering printed in its chain bullet is this entry's own literal rendering, labeled there. It is quoted only through "the Christ according to the flesh"; the verse's remaining words — a blessing whose punctuation the manuscripts do not supply, its reading (the Christ named as God over all, or a praise to God standing apart) a famous open question — are not quoted, and this entry takes no side in that question. Eph 1:5's governing verb (ἐξελέξατο) stands in v.4, outside the set; the participle προορίσας is read with its sentence, as the NASB's finite clause does. Eph 1:5 also ends mid-sentence (its completion, v.6's "to the praise of the glory of His grace," is not in the set, and nothing rests on it). Gal 4:5's two clauses hang on v.4's sending of the Son; the buying-out is closed at KB040, and Rom 11:29 — where the gifts and the calling are named ἀμεταμέλητα, irrevocable — is quoted at KB039; both are cited as pointers, not receipts of this entry. (4) The sitting's hearings (Craig, 2026-10-27, recorded in the groundwork as deliberation): grafting (Rom 11:17–24) and adoption as two ways of speaking to the one inheritance; abiding (John 15:2, 6) and its remaining-condition, verbally kin to Rom 11:22–23's ἐπιμένω; the fallen partaker who cannot be renewed (Heb 6:4–6). None was ruled into this entry; none stands in its set, chain, or close. (5) Apparatus: NA28's markers sit on καὶ αὐτοὶ and ἡμεῖς καὶ αὐτοὶ at Rom 8:23 (the "we ourselves" wording) and on αἱ διαθῆκαι and αἱ ἐπαγγελίαι at Rom 9:4; no claim in this entry rests on a marked word, and the adoption clauses themselves are unmarked. The asterisks in Craig's pastes are Logos export artifacts. (6) Scope of the close: this entry closes the word υἱοθεσία — its filing, its five places, and the relations those places state. It does not close sonship as a doctrine: υἱός and τέκνον are other words with their own distributions (Rom 8:16's τέκνα θεοῦ stands one verse from 8:15 and is not spent here), and what the heirs inherit (Rom 8:17–18) is not in the set.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The already-only reading (the stub's own "decisive placement" as the whole account): adoption is a completed transaction with nothing outstanding. Answered: Rom 8:23 — the adoption is still awaited, by name, by the people who already hold the first fruits of the Spirit, and Paul names the awaited thing as the redemption of the body; the finished-only reading must split 8:23's υἱοθεσία from 8:15's, and the filing gives the two one sense in one chapter. (b) The not-yet-only reading: adoption is wholly future, and present sonship language is anticipation. Answered: Rom 8:15's ἐλάβετε is aorist and spent twice — "you did NOT receive a spirit of slavery; you received a Spirit of adoption" — and Gal 4:5 states the reception as the redemption's purpose (ἵνα ... ἀπολάβωμεν); the future-only reading must erase the reception verses. (c) The replacement reading: the adoption was Israel's and has been transferred — hers in name only, the church's instead. Answered: Rom 9:4's frame is present — εἰσιν, "who ARE Israelites" — and its possession clause is verbless, "whose is the adoption," carrying no past marker, in the same letter, by the same apostle, as the reception of 8:15; no verse in the set revokes, transfers, or reassigns her possession, and the believers' reception is stated as reception, not confiscation; and at Rom 11:29 the gifts and the calling are named ἀμεταμέλητα — irrevocable — quoted at KB039 and cited here as a pointer. (d) The sentiment reading: adoption as a warm figure for God's affection or welcome, a relationship metaphor rather than a legal placement. Answered: the filing is a formal legal declaration carrying complete rights of inheritance; Eph 1:5 grounds the placement in foreordination and the good pleasure of God's will, and Gal 4:5 makes it the purpose clause of the redemption — affection is neither foreordained as a destination nor stated as a redemption's goal; the texts speak in the registers of law, purpose, and possession, and the entry follows them. (e) The firstborn reading (the stub's own): every believer holds the status and rights of a firstborn heir. Answered: no firstborn word stands in the filing or in any of the five verses — πρωτότοκος belongs to other texts (Rom 8:29 among them, outside this set); what the filing gives every adopted one is complete rights of inheritance as one's own child, and the entry carries the datum at that strength and no further (Honesty (1)). (f) The English-specification reading: the NASB's "sons and daughters" read as though the Greek term itself specified both sexes. Answered: at all five places the Greek is the one word υἱοθεσία; the expansion is the translation's uniform decision, recorded in the close as a translation datum. The entry rests no claim on the English addition — and, by the same discipline, draws no denial from the Greek's silence (Honesty (2)).

### The Execution of the "I" (Galatians 2:19-20)

- **ID:** KB046
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** συνεσταύρωμαι, συνεσταυρώθη, παλαιὸς ἄνθρωπος, ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς
- **Verse keys:** Gal 2:19–20; Rom 6:3, 5–6; Col 3:9–10; 2 Cor 5:14
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The believer's old, independent self was executed with Christ — a completed co-crucifixion whose standing result is that the life now lived is not the old "I" resumed or improved but Christ living in the believer.
- **The chain:**
  - Gal 2:19–20: Χριστῷ συνεσταύρωμαι — perfect passive, "I have been crucified with Christ," a completed act with a standing result, spoken in the first person; and its consequence in the same breath: ζῶ δὲ οὐκέτι ἐγώ, ζῇ δὲ ἐν ἐμοὶ Χριστός — "I no longer live, but Christ lives in me." The verse finishes the exchange in the flesh: ὃ δὲ νῦν ζῶ ἐν σαρκί, ἐν πίστει ζῶ τῇ τοῦ υἱοῦ τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ ἀγαπήσαντός με καὶ παραδόντος ἑαυτὸν ὑπὲρ ἐμοῦ — "the life I now live in the flesh I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave Himself for me" (Gal 2:20, pasted in full 2026-10-23).
  - Rom 6:6: ὁ παλαιὸς ἡμῶν ἄνθρωπος συνεσταυρώθη — the same verb, third person, names *what* was co-crucified: "our old man"; stated result: ἵνα καταργηθῇ τὸ σῶμα τῆς ἁμαρτίας, "that the body of sin might be rendered inoperative." The old man is the crucified thing; no verse carries him across, repairs him, or re-employs him.
  - Rom 6:3: ὅσοι ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν, εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ ἐβαπτίσθημεν — the entry into the co-crucifixion is immersion into Christ, self-glossed by the text as immersion into His death.
  - Rom 6:5: εἰ γὰρ σύμφυτοι γεγόναμεν τῷ ὁμοιώματι τοῦ θανάτου αὐτοῦ, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῆς ἀναστάσεως ἐσόμεθα — grown together with the likeness of His death (perfect γεγόναμεν); the resurrection side is future (ἐσόμεθα). The executed old life is not the thing raised.
  - Col 3:9–10: ἀπεκδυσάμενοι τὸν παλαιὸν ἄνθρωπον ... καὶ ἐνδυσάμενοι τὸν νέον — the old man stripped off, the new put on: a change of clothing, not an alteration of the same garment.
  - 2 Cor 5:14: εἷς ὑπὲρ πάντων ἀπέθανεν, ἄρα οἱ πάντες ἀπέθανον — Paul's own inference drawn as fact: one died for all, *therefore* all died. The participation is a death undergone, not a status reckoned from a distance.
- **The close (settled):** (1) Co-crucifixion with Christ is a completed act (perfect συνεσταύρωμαι) whose result stands — the independent "I" has been executed, not suspended. (2) What was executed is the old man — crucified (Rom 6:6) and stripped off (Col 3:9) — so the passage out of the old humanity runs by execution and new creation, and nothing in this chain relocates, repairs, or improves the old self. (3) The life that continues is not the old "I" resumed but Christ living in the believer (Gal 2:20), while bodily resurrection remains future (Rom 6:5). The transfer of the *person* from Adam into Christ is closed by the Transfer / Co-Location entry (pilot, 2026-10-18, pending its own promotion); this entry closes the road and the indwelling, and is consistent with it: the person is transferred; the old man is not.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) This chain quotes Gal 2:19–20's co-crucifixion and indwelling clauses; the verse's remaining clauses (death to law; the life in the flesh lived by faith in the Son who loved and gave Himself) stand in the Two Humanities closed set and are not re-quoted here — nothing in this close rests on them. (2) "Execution" in the title renders the crucifixion verbs' force for the old man; the texts' own word is *crucified* (συνεσταυρώθη). (3) The chain does not state how the Father animates the physical body (Rom 8:11 is not in this chain); the indwelling claim rests on ζῇ δὲ ἐν ἐμοὶ Χριστός alone.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The improvement reading — the old self reformed by discipline over time. Answered: the verbs are executions and strippings (συνεσταυρώθη, aorist; ἀπεκδυσάμενοι, aorist), and the new man is created and put on (Col 3:10), not the old one renovated. (b) The legal-fiction reading — "crucified with" as a forensic metaphor under which nothing really died. Answered: Paul argues from it as fact (2 Cor 5:14's ἄρα, "therefore all died") and states its result (the body of sin rendered inoperative, Rom 6:6) and its present content (Christ living in the believer, Gal 2:20); a fiction renders nothing inoperative. (c) The relocation reading — this entry's own former wording, in which the self is moved intact from the old humanity into the new. Answered: what crosses is the person, by the transfer closed elsewhere; the old man is crucified on the road — Rom 6:6 and Col 3:9 give him no arrival.

### Christos hē Zōē (Christ Our Life)

- **ID:** KB047
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν, κέκρυπται, φανερωθήσεσθε, ζῇ ἐν ἐμοί, Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν
- **Verse keys:** Col 3:3–4; Gal 2:20; Col 1:27; Eph 3:17
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The believer's life is Christ Himself — Paul names Him "your life" — hidden with Him in God now and living in the believer now, and to be manifested with Him in glory at His appearing.
- **The chain:**
  - Col 3:3–4: ἀπεθάνετε γὰρ καὶ ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν κέκρυπται σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ· ὅταν ὁ Χριστὸς φανερωθῇ, ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν, τότε καὶ ὑμεῖς σὺν αὐτῷ φανερωθήσεσθε ἐν δόξῃ — Paul does not say Christ gives, improves, or symbolizes the life; he names Him, in apposition, ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν, "your life." Its present state: hidden (κέκρυπται, perfect passive) with Christ in God. Its future: manifestation with Him (φανερωθήσεσθε, future passive) at His appearing (φανερωθῇ).
  - Gal 2:20: ζῶ δὲ οὐκέτι ἐγώ, ζῇ δὲ ἐν ἐμοὶ Χριστός — the same identification from inside the believer: the life presently lived is Christ living in the person.
  - Col 1:27: ὅ ἐστιν Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν, ἡ ἐλπὶς τῆς δόξης — the mystery's content is a location, Christ in you; and He is, in that location, "the hope of the glory" that Col 3:4 dates to His appearing.
  - Eph 3:17: κατοικῆσαι τὸν Χριστὸν διὰ τῆς πίστεως ἐν ταῖς καρδίαις ὑμῶν — Christ *dwelling* (κατοικῆσαι: settled residence, not visitation) in the hearts through faith, prayed for as the household's strengthening.
- **The close (settled):** (1) The identification is direct and personal: Christ *is* the believer's life (Col 3:4's apposition), not merely its source, example, or influence. (2) The life carries two tenses in one sentence — hidden with Christ in God now (perfect κέκρυπται), manifested with Him in glory then (future φανερωθήσεσθε) — and neither tense may be collapsed into the other. (3) The indwelling runs in both directions of the union: we with Him in God (Col 3:3) and He in us (Gal 2:20; Col 1:27; Eph 3:17) — co-location stated from both sides.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The former prose's wider claims — the Father as ultimate source of all life, the Son as the Vine (John 15), the body "organically animated" — are not in this chain and are not closed by it; they belong to other texts and other entries. (2) In Col 3:4, NA28 prints σὺν αὐτῷ within apparatus markers; the identification itself (ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν) is unmarked, and the manifestation's "with Him" is additionally carried by Col 3:3's unmarked σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ — the close notes the marker rather than hiding it. (3) The texts say *lives in*, *dwells in*, *is hidden* — residence and identity; no mechanics of "animation" is stated or claimed.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The metaphor reading — "your life" as a figure for Christ's influence or example. Answered: figures of influence are neither hidden in God nor manifested in glory alongside their subjects; the sentence pairs the identification with a hidden location (σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ) and a future joint manifestation, and the companion texts state residence (κατοικῆσαι, Eph 3:17; ζῇ ἐν ἐμοί, Gal 2:20), not influence. (b) The already-unveiled reading — because Christ lives in us now, the glory is fully present. Answered: Col 3:4 dates the manifestation to ὅταν ὁ Χριστὸς φανερωθῇ, "when Christ is manifested," and Col 1:27 names the present indwelling ἡ ἐλπὶς τῆς δόξης — the *hope* of the glory, not the glory itself.

### The Ekklesia

- **ID:** KB048
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** οἰκοδομήσω μου τὴν ἐκκλησίαν, πύλαι ᾅδου οὐ κατισχύσουσιν αὐτῆς, συμπολῖται τῶν ἁγίων καὶ οἰκεῖοι τοῦ θεοῦ, ἐποικοδομηθέντες ἐπὶ τῷ θεμελίῳ τῶν ἀποστόλων καὶ προφητῶν, ὄντος ἀκρογωνιαίου αὐτοῦ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ, πᾶσα οἰκοδομὴ συναρμολογουμένη αὔξει εἰς ναὸν ἅγιον ἐν κυρίῳ
- **Verse keys:** Matt 16:18; Eph 2:19–22
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Jesus names what He will build and whose it is: "I will build My assembly (οἰκοδομήσω μου τὴν ἐκκλησίαν), and the gates of Hades will not overpower it (καὶ πύλαι ᾅδου οὐ κατισχύσουσιν αὐτῆς)." Read together, Paul describes the building from inside: its members are "no longer strangers and resident aliens, but fellow citizens with the saints and members of God's household (οὐκέτι ἐστὲ ξένοι καὶ πάροικοι ἀλλ' ἐστὲ συμπολῖται τῶν ἁγίων καὶ οἰκεῖοι τοῦ θεοῦ), having been built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets (ἐποικοδομηθέντες ἐπὶ τῷ θεμελίῳ τῶν ἀποστόλων καὶ προφητῶν), Christ Jesus Himself being the cornerstone (ὄντος ἀκρογωνιαίου αὐτοῦ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ)" — and "the whole building, being joined together, grows into a holy temple in the Lord (πᾶσα οἰκοδομὴ συναρμολογουμένη αὔξει εἰς ναὸν ἅγιον ἐν κυρίῳ)."
- **The chain:**
  (1) The Builder and the word (Matt 16:18): "and I also say to you that you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build My assembly (ἐπὶ ταύτῃ τῇ πέτρᾳ οἰκοδομήσω μου τὴν ἐκκλησίαν)." The ekklesia stands here as Jesus' own project — future tense, first person: He builds, and it is *His* (μου). The verse's own wordplay stands in the text: Πέτρος addressed, πέτρᾳ built upon.
  (2) The promise attached to the building (Matt 16:18): "and the gates of Hades will not overpower it (πύλαι ᾅδου οὐ κατισχύσουσιν αὐτῆς)." The assembly's endurance is the Builder's spoken undertaking, attached to the building at its naming — not a quality the members supply.
  (3) The members' standing (Eph 2:19): "so then you are no longer strangers and resident aliens, but you are fellow citizens with the saints and members of God's household (Ἄρα οὖν οὐκέτι ἐστὲ ξένοι καὶ πάροικοι ἀλλ' ἐστὲ συμπολῖται τῶν ἁγίων καὶ οἰκεῖοι τοῦ θεοῦ)." What the building is made of, stated politically and domestically: citizens, household.
  (4) The foundation and the cornerstone (Eph 2:20): "having been built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets (ἐποικοδομηθέντες ἐπὶ τῷ θεμελίῳ τῶν ἀποστόλων καὶ προφητῶν), Christ Jesus Himself being the cornerstone (ὄντος ἀκρογωνιαίου αὐτοῦ Χριστοῦ Ἰησοῦ)." The foundation as Paul states it is plural — the apostles and prophets; the one singular position the verse names, the cornerstone, belongs to Christ Himself.
  (5) The growth (Eph 2:21): "in whom the whole building, being joined together, grows into a holy temple in the Lord (ἐν ᾧ πᾶσα οἰκοδομὴ συναρμολογουμένη αὔξει εἰς ναὸν ἅγιον ἐν κυρίῳ)." The building is alive in the text's own verb: it *grows*, present tense, and what it grows into is a temple. Verse 22's completing side — the same people built together into God's dwelling in the Spirit — is closed as the temple facet at KB091 and is pointed to there, not re-closed here.
- **The close (settled):** The ekklesia is Christ's own building: He names it, He builds it, and its endurance is His promise. From inside, it is citizens and household members on the apostles-and-prophets foundation, Christ Himself the cornerstone, the whole joined together and growing into a holy temple. In neither text is the building a structure of stone or an institution that owns the name — the building is the people, and the Builder is the Lord.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The former prose called the ekklesia a "called-out assembly summoned by the King" and a sanctuary of "living stones." Neither text states the "called-out" gloss (it is the word's etymology, not a statement of either verse), no summoning King appears in them, and "living stones" is 1 Peter 2:5's wording, outside this set — all three are dropped, not carried (Craig's two-text ruling, 2026-10-24). (2) This entry does not adjudicate the πέτρα's referent beyond what the two texts state together: Matthew's wordplay stands in his verse, and Paul names the foundation as the apostles and prophets with Christ the cornerstone; readings beyond that settlement are left where the texts leave them. (3) Matthew 16:19 (the keys of the kingdom) stands outside the set and is pointed to, not quoted.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):**
  (a) The building-and-institution reading — the ekklesia is a physical structure, or an institution that bears the name and can be located, joined, or left as premises are. Answered by both texts: the building in Matthew is what Jesus builds of people, and in Ephesians the "building" is citizens and household members growing into a temple; no text in the set makes the ekklesia a place or a corporation.
  (b) The etymology reading — *ekklesia* means "the called-out ones," defining the assembly by its separation from the world. Answered by the texts' silence and their speech: neither verse glosses the word; both define the thing by its Builder, its foundation, its membership, and its growth. A word's derivation is not a text's definition.
  (c) The one-man foundation reading — the rock is Peter alone, and the building therefore rests on one man's office and successors. Answered by the set read together, without settling Matthew's rock: Matthew's own sentence makes Jesus the Builder ("I will build *My* assembly"); Paul states the foundation as "of the apostles and prophets" — apostolic and plural, Peter among them as one of the apostles — with the one singular position, the cornerstone, held by "Christ Jesus Himself." A foundation of the apostles is not one man alone, and neither text in the set says anything about successors to anyone.
  (d) The members'-maintenance reading — the assembly endures as long as its members hold it together, and fails when they fail. Answered by the promise's placement: "the gates of Hades will not overpower it" is the Builder's undertaking spoken at the building's naming, and Ephesians' growth is the whole building's, "in the Lord" — endurance and growth are predicated of Christ's building, not of the members' grip.
### Real Parts, One Person (Spirit, Soul, and Body)

- **ID:** KB049
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** πνεῦμα, πνοή, ψυχή, ψυχικός, σῶμα, σάρξ, καρδία, διαλογισμός, διάνοια, νοῦς, ὁ ἔσω ἄνθρωπος
- **Hebrew keys:** רוּחַ, נֶפֶשׁ, לֵב, נְשָׁמָה, בָּשָׂר
- **Verse keys:** Gen 2:7; Eccl 12:7; 1 Sam 1:15; Job 34:14; Job 34:15; Ps 51:10; Ps 51:17; Ps 84:2; Isa 26:9; Isa 42:5; Ezek 36:26; Mark 2:6–8; Mark 12:30; Matt 10:28; Matt 22:37; Luke 2:35; Luke 9:24; Luke 10:27; John 12:25; Rom 5:5; Rom 7:22; Rom 8:13; Rom 8:16; Rom 8:23; Rom 12:2; 1 Cor 2:11; 1 Cor 2:12; 1 Cor 2:14; 1 Cor 5:3; 1 Cor 5:5; 1 Cor 6:19; 1 Cor 7:34; 2 Cor 4:16; Col 2:5; Gal 4:6; Gal 5:16; Eph 3:16; 1 Thess 5:23; Acts 17:25; Prov 20:27; Heb 4:12; Jas 2:26; Jas 3:15; Jude 19; 1 Cor 15:44; 1 Cor 15:46
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** Spirit, soul, and body are real parts of one person, not rooms in him — the God of peace is prayed to sanctify the man *entirely* and keep him *complete*, spirit and soul and body (1 Thess 5:23); the parts are real enough to part at death, when the body without the spirit is a corpse (Jas 2:26); and no part is given an exclusive job of its own, because the words cross constantly — the thinking the diagrams assign to the soul is given to the heart (Heb 4:12), heart and soul alike are totaled under God's "all" in the commandment (Matt 22:37), and heart and spirit meet without ever being made the same word — the scribes reasoning in their hearts and Jesus knowing in His spirit in one scene (Mark 2:6–8), a new heart given and a new spirit put within in one gift (Ezek 36:26).
- **The chain:**
  - The one verse that names all three is a prayer for wholeness, not a diagram: 1 Thess 5:23 — "Now may the God of peace Himself sanctify you entirely [ὁλοτελεῖς]; and may your spirit and soul and body be kept complete [ὁλόκληρον], without blame at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ" — and ὁλόκληρον is singular: one completeness word spread over the three nouns together, a prayer that the *whole* man be kept. The same whole-man grammar stands at Matt 10:28, where God can destroy "both soul and body" [καὶ ψυχὴν καὶ σῶμα] in Gehenna and the teaching is whom to fear, not how man is built.
  - The pairs speak of one self, not two tenants: 1 Sam 1:15 — Hannah is oppressed in spirit (קְשַׁת־רוּחַ, "hard of spirit") and has poured out her soul before the LORD, one woman and one distress under both words. Isa 26:9 — "At night my soul longs for You, Indeed, my spirit within me seeks You diligently" [נַפְשִׁי … רוּחִי]: longing and seeking, the same turning to God, split between soul and spirit in parallel lines. Ps 84:2 (84:3 in the Hebrew numbering of the pasted text) — "My soul longed and even yearned for the courtyards of the LORD; My heart and my flesh sing for joy to the living God" [לִבִּי וּבְשָׂרִי, "my heart and my flesh"]: three words, one man crying out.
  - The spirit is the man himself, present where his body is not: 1 Cor 5:3 — Paul is "absent in body but present in spirit" [ἀπὼν τῷ σώματι, παρὼν δὲ τῷ πνεύματι] with the church; Col 2:5 the same shape in a different absence-word — absent in the flesh [τῇ σαρκὶ ἄπειμι], present in the spirit [τῷ πνεύματι]: at Colossians it is the *flesh* Paul is absent in, and the pairing still holds. And the parts are told apart at a man's extremity, in the same letter: 1 Cor 5:5 — a man is handed over to Satan "for the destruction of his body, so that his spirit may be saved on the day of the Lord": so the NASB prints it; the word under its "body" is σάρξ [τῆς σαρκός], flesh — named here as the Greek's word, the translation's choice left as printed. Flesh destroyed and spirit saved in one sentence, the words neither merged nor confused. Jas 2:26 — "just as the body without the spirit is dead": when the spirit is gone what is left is not a man minus a part but a corpse, which is what makes the part real — and what makes the man more than any one part.
  - The heart does the work the diagrams assign to the soul: Heb 4:12 — the word is "even penetrating as far as the division of soul and spirit [μερισμοῦ ψυχῆς καὶ πνεύματος], of both joints and marrow," and it judges "the thoughts and intentions of the heart [ἐνθυμήσεων καὶ ἐννοιῶν καρδίας]": thinking and intending stand under *heart* in the very verse the diagrams build on. Luke 2:35 — a sword pierces Mary's soul, and what it exposes is "thoughts from many hearts" [ἐκ πολλῶν καρδιῶν διαλογισμοί]: the reasonings laid bare are hearts', in the verse where a soul is pierced. Mark 2:6–8 — the scribes reason in their hearts (v. 6), and Jesus, knowing in His spirit (v. 8) that they reason so, answers the reasoning: the heart reasons, the spirit knows, and neither word stays inside an assigned desk. Nor does the crossing need two men to work: in one man, his own spirit knows the things of the man (τὰ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, the NASB's "thoughts" at 1 Cor 2:11, in the chain below), while the thoughts and intentions themselves stand under the heart (Heb 4:12, this bullet) — the knowing the diagrams reserve for one part's special gift is every man's spirit doing its ordinary work, over thoughts the heart is said to hold. And a third word carries the same inside work: Rom 12:2 — "be transformed by the renewing of your mind" — μεταμορφοῦσθε, a passive imperative, the transforming done *to* the man, and its named means the renewing of the mind [τοῦ νοὸς]: the thinking the diagrams assign to the soul is given in turn to the heart, and to the mind, and the verse's grammar stops there: the transforming is done to the man, and the verse names the means and no agent.
  - Heart and spirit are not the same, and are never walled apart (Craig's noted observation, 2026-11-01): Ezek 36:26 — "Moreover, I will give you a new heart and put a new spirit within you" — one gift of a remade inside, both words kept, neither dropped. Ps 51:10 (51:12 in the Hebrew numbering of the pasted text) — "Create in me a clean heart, God, And renew a steadfast spirit within me"; Ps 51:17 (51:19 in the Hebrew numbering of the pasted text) — "The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit; A broken and a contrite heart, God, You will not despise" — the same broken man under both words in one verse. No verse in the set says the one is the other; no verse confines either to its own work.
  - Each word can carry the whole man under God's "all": Matt 22:37 — "You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind" [διανοίᾳ] — ἐν ὅλῃ, "with all," repeated at every term (Mark 12:30 and Luke 10:27 in the set, both adding strength, Luke adding the neighbor as well): the commandment does not divide the man into shares; it totals him under each word in turn.
  - The man's own spirit is his, and God's Spirit stands with it, never confused with it: 1 Cor 2:11 — "who among people knows the thoughts of a person except the spirit of the person that is in him [τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ ἀνθρώπου τὸ ἐν αὐτῷ]?" Rom 8:16 — "The Spirit Himself testifies with our spirit [συμμαρτυρεῖ τῷ πνεύματι ἡμῶν] that we are children of God": two witnesses, named separately, agreeing — not one spirit under two names. 1 Cor 2:12 — "we have not received the spirit of the world, but the Spirit who is from God": God's Spirit is *received*, which is why He can be told apart from ours. And the given Spirit is named in the heart and in the inner person: Gal 4:6 — God "has sent the Spirit of His Son into our hearts [εἰς τὰς καρδίας ἡμῶν]"; Rom 5:5 — His love "poured out within our hearts through the Holy Spirit who was given to us"; Eph 3:16 — strengthened "through His Spirit in the inner self" [εἰς τὸν ἔσω ἄνθρωπον]: the Greek says "the inner person," the wording this entry uses — the translation's "self" left as printed.
  - Paul also divides the man another way, which no three-part diagram survives: 2 Cor 4:16 — "though our outer person is decaying, yet our inner person is being renewed day by day"; Rom 7:22 — the man agrees with God's law "in the inner person [κατὰ τὸν ἔσω ἄνθρωπον]". Inner and outer is Paul's own other map, laid over the same man the three words name.
  - The soul-life can be lost and gained, and what rules it is the question: Luke 9:24 — "whoever wants to save his life [ψυχήν] will lose it, but whoever loses his life [ψυχήν] for My sake, this is the one who will save it" (the verse stands quoted in the keeper at KB018, ψυχή filed [23.88], "life," at both occurrences): the same word, the same man's life, in both halves of the saying. John 12:25 stands beside it in the same shape and carries it further: the one who hates his life [ψυχήν] in this world "will keep it [φυλάξει αὐτήν] to eternal life [εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον]" — the same ψυχή, kept, and the life it is kept to is named ζωή. Rom 8:13 — "if by the Spirit you are putting to death the deeds of the body, you will live": the putting to death is done *by the Spirit*, and the life on the other side of it is the man's own life under a new rule.
  - "Soulish" (ψυχικός) names kinds, never a part of one — the word's whole New Testament set: a whole person (1 Cor 2:14 — ψυχικὸς ἄνθρωπος, the "natural person" who "does not accept the things of the Spirit of God, for they are foolishness to him... because they are spiritually discerned"); a whole body (1 Cor 15:44 — σῶμα ψυχικόν sown, σῶμα πνευματικόν raised); an order (1 Cor 15:46 — "the spiritual is not first, but the natural [τὸ ψυχικόν]; then the spiritual"); a wisdom (Jas 3:15 — ἐπίγειος, ψυχική, δαιμονιώδης, "earthly, natural, demonic", in one descending series); a class of men (Jude 19 — ψυχικοί, the NASB's "worldly-minded," defined by πνεῦμα μὴ ἔχοντες, lit. "not having the Spirit" (the NASB prints "devoid of the Spirit")). The word's places divide by their own statements: a man defined by not accepting the Spirit's things (1 Cor 2:14); a body and an order defined by sequence — the natural first, the spiritual then (1 Cor 15:44, 46); a wisdom defined as earthly, natural, demonic, not from above (Jas 3:15); men defined by not having the Spirit (Jude 19) — and in four of the verses (five occurrences) the NASB renders the word "natural," at the fifth verse, Jude 19, "worldly-minded," so at no place does the soul-word the teaching builds on appear in the English it teaches from.
  - All flesh lives on lent breath: Gen 2:7 — God "breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and the man became a living person" — so the NASB prints it; the Hebrew says the man became a living *nephesh* (נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה), and the translation's "person" flattens the very word this entry tracks: the man did not receive a soul as an added compartment; in the Hebrew's saying, he *became* one. Isa 42:5 — God "gives breath [נְשָׁמָה] to the people on it And spirit [רוּחַ] to those who walk in it"; Acts 17:25 — He "gives to all people life and breath [πνοήν] and all things". Job 34:14 — "If He were to determine to do so, If He were to gather His spirit and His breath to Himself," and at v. 15 the NASB prints "Humanity would perish together" — the words under "Humanity" are כָּל־בָּשָׂר, all flesh, as the NASB's own footnote reads ("Lit All flesh"), and Craig reads the verse at the Hebrew's strength: if He gathered them, all flesh would perish together. The NASB's "determine to do so" renders the Hebrew's "if He sets His heart [לִבּוֹ] toward him" — the heart-word flattened into a resolve, and named here as the translation's choice; the spirit gathered is "His spirit" [רוּחוֹ] and the breath "His breath" [נִשְׁמָתוֹ], both His, both lent. Eccl 12:7 states the returning end of the lending: "the dust will return to the earth as it was, and the spirit will return to God who gave it" [וְהָרוּחַ תָּשׁוּב אֶל־הָאֱלֹהִים אֲשֶׁר נְתָנָהּ] — dust and spirit part at death to separate destinations, and the spirit's destination is the Giver. Prov 20:27 — "The spirit of a person is the lamp of the LORD," searching "all the innermost parts of his being": the word under "spirit" there is נִשְׁמַת, the breath-word — the translation itself crosses the breath into "spirit," while the Hebrew keeps its own word: God's lamp, man's breath, and neither owner is surrendered.
  - The body is the man himself in the world, and its future is redemption, not escape: 1 Cor 6:19 — the body is "a temple of the Holy Spirit within you" — a temple is where God dwells, not a house a tenant discards. 1 Cor 7:34 — the unmarried woman is anxious to be "holy both in body and spirit" [ἁγία καὶ τῷ σώματι καὶ τῷ πνεύματι]: body and spirit together under *holy*, no ranking. Rom 8:23 — we "groan within ourselves, waiting eagerly for our adoption as sons and daughters, the redemption of our body" [τὴν ἀπολύτρωσιν τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν]: the body is awaited, not left behind.
- **The close (settled):** The set settles a boundary, in both directions. Against every parts-diagram: no verse in the set assigns thinking, feeling, and choosing to the soul exclusively, or contact with God to the spirit exclusively, or living-in-the-world to the body as a tenant's house; the one verse that names all three is a prayer that the whole man be sanctified entirely and kept complete (1 Thess 5:23); the verse the diagrams build on gives the thoughts and intentions to the heart (Heb 4:12); and Paul himself maps the same man as inner and outer (2 Cor 4:16). The words keep their own emphases — breath given and gathered back is ruach's and neshamah's home ground in this set, the life that can be lost and saved is psyche's — and emphasis is all the set allows: no emphasis hardens into an exclusive desk without a verse saying so, and no verse in the set says so. Against every flattening: the parts are real — body and spirit part at death, and what remains is a corpse (Jas 2:26); the soul-life stands under lose-and-save in one saying (Luke 9:24); soul and spirit stand distinguishable before the word's dividing (Heb 4:12), and the entry claims no more than that from the verse; flesh [σαρκός] can be destroyed and the spirit saved in one sentence (1 Cor 5:5); and heart and spirit, named together, are both words kept and never equated (Ezek 36:26; Mark 2:6–8). And ψυχικός, across its whole set, names kinds — persons, a body, an order, a wisdom — defined by origin, sequence, or relation to the Holy Spirit, never a compartment a man could switch off. Real parts, one person, no compartments, no exclusive jobs.
- **The proposed close (marked):** Craig's readings, carried as his and claimed at no greater strength. The soul-life a man loses for Christ's sake is his life self-ruled — the desire of the flesh not carried out as he walks by the Spirit (Gal 5:16), and the deeds of the body put to death by the Spirit (Rom 8:13) — the man continuing through the losing, and he gains that same life back ruled by the Spirit rather than by the flesh, with ζωή, the life of God, gained beyond it (Luke 9:24, and John 12:25 beside it, where the kept ψυχή is kept εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον — the ζωή word standing in that verse's own text). And the lent breath makes men God's, not gods: if He gathered His breath back, all flesh would perish; the dependence is total, and it is dependence — we are Yahweh's ("He who causes all things to exist" — Craig's gloss on the Name, carried as his). What breaks self-dependence is God's own work, not a technique a man runs on his parts: the man who says "I need to crucify the flesh" — the sentence Craig named in this sitting as self-dependence's usual form — has appointed himself to do what God has done (Gal 2:20 stands outside this set, in the keeper at KB046, and is pointed to there, not spent here).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole: its title, "The Three-Fold Nature of the Human Being," named the design this entry's set refuses, and the new title names the entry's conclusion; its job assignments are dropped as named in the build record, and nothing of the stub is carried except its three words and its one verse. (2) No verse in the set defines man's parts, counts them, or describes how they are joined; 1 Thess 5:23 lists three words inside a prayer for completeness, and this entry claims the boundary the whole set draws, not an anatomy no verse states. (3) What Heb 4:12's "division of soul and spirit" divides, and how, the verse does not anatomize; the entry spends only what the verse carries — that soul and spirit stand distinguishable before the word, and that the thoughts and intentions the verse judges belong to the heart — and no claim about a technique of dividing them is made or allowed. (4) The ψυχικός set and the three single verses once declared as gaps (Eccl 12:7; John 12:25; Rom 12:2) were supplied by Craig on 2026-11-01 and are banked in the set (Searches 16–17); they are spent at their places in the chain, and the entry now carries no declared verse gaps. The ψυχικός bullet spends the word's whole New Testament distribution as pasted (five verses, six occurrences — 1 Cor 15:44 carries the word twice), not a selection. (5) Luke 9:24 is cited from the live keeper (KB018), where it stands quoted with its filing; it was not pasted in this sitting's searches, and the entry's use of it is at that strength — the one declared-check verse left in the keys. Job 34:15 stood declared the same way through rev. 17; Craig's paste of it (NASB 2020 + LHB, Search 20, 2026-11-01) matches the entry's quotation exactly and confirms כָּל־בָּשָׂר under the NASB's "Humanity," so from rev. 18 it stands on a paste, its footnote ("Lit All flesh") with it. Gal 5:16 entered the set on the same paste and is spent in the proposed close only. (6) The image and likeness of God are not closed here: where the image stands in the man is KB060's subject, and this entry points forward to KB060 for it — a forward pointer to an entry not yet converted, landing only at its conversion; 1 Thess 5:23's whole-man prayer may be called there as a witness against placing the image in one part, and is claimed for nothing more here. (7) "Parts" in this entry is Craig's word, held at his correction: a part is real and fundamental and can be lost without the man himself being lost — the entry's own texts carry that shape: flesh can be destroyed and the spirit saved (1 Cor 5:5), and a man can lose his soul-life and gain it back (Luke 9:24) — the word carries no compartments, no sealed rooms with their own work, and nothing in the entry should be read as restoring them. (8) Verse numbering follows the NASB; where the pasted Hebrew text numbers differently (Ps 84; Ps 51, where the Hebrew runs two verses ahead — NASB 51:10 stands at Hebrew 51:12, NASB 51:17 at Hebrew 51:19), the chain notes the Hebrew numbering at the verse. (9) The proposed close's "breaks self-dependence" is Craig's summary of Watchman Nee's *The Release of the Spirit*, given in this sitting's conversation and carried as his framing; its textual anchor inside the entry is the pointer to Gal 2:20 at KB046, and it is claimed at hearing strength only.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The three-part diagram with its function chart (Watchman Nee, *The Spiritual Man*, and the teaching built on it): spirit = conscience, intuition, fellowship; soul = mind, emotion, will; "the Bible never mixes the soul with the spirit," and the believer is taught to live from his spirit and not his soul. Answered from the set: the Bible mixes them constantly — Hannah's spirit and soul are one distress (1 Sam 1:15), Mary's soul is pierced and the thoughts exposed are hearts' (Luke 2:35), the thinking Nee assigns to the soul is given to the heart in the diagram's own proof verse (Heb 4:12), the knowing he assigns to the spirit's intuition is the heart's reasoning answered by the spirit's knowing in one scene (Mark 2:6–8), and no verse in the set commands a man to change which part he lives from — Rom 8:13 names the Spirit of God, not the man's spirit, as the instrument of the putting to death. And the teaching's own category word answers it: ψυχικός in Scripture names whole persons and things defined by not having the Spirit (Jude 19) and not accepting His things (1 Cor 2:14) — a state of the whole man toward God, not the activity of one part of a believer that a technique could redirect. (b) The interchangeability reading (the dichotomy systematics, and in modern form the aspects account — Wolff's anthropology, the Logos Study Assistant's summary): soul and spirit, heart too, are angles on the whole person, never parts, never fragmented. Answered from the set: angles cannot be buried — the body without the spirit is a corpse (Jas 2:26), which is a parting, not a viewpoint; nor is flesh destroyed while a viewpoint is saved (1 Cor 5:5); and words that were only angles would not both be kept, verse after verse, where either alone would do (Ezek 36:26; Ps 51:10, 17). (c) The heart-is-the-spirit reading (popular teaching): the heart simply is the spirit, so the words may be merged. Answered by the noted observation itself: Mark 2:6–8 and Ezek 36:26 keep both words in one breath and equate neither; a merger no verse performs is the reader's, not the text's.

### The Body Sleeps in the Dust

- **ID:** KB088
- **Facet of:** KB050
- **Greek keys:** κοιμάω, κεκοίμηται, ἐκοιμήθη, κοιμηθέντας, κοιμηθησόμεθα
- **Verse keys:** John 11:11–14; Acts 7:60; 1 Thess 4:13–15; 1 Cor 15:51–52; Dan 12:2
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** For those who die in the Lord, the texts' own name for their death is sleep — the body rests in the dust as one who will be woken, and in every use the sleep is bounded by a waking God performs: He will bring the sleepers with Jesus and raise the dead imperishable.
- **The chain:**
  - John 11:11–14: Λάζαρος ὁ φίλος ἡμῶν κεκοίμηται· ἀλλὰ πορεύομαι ἵνα ἐξυπνίσω αὐτόν — "our friend Lazarus has fallen asleep; but I go, to wake him." The disciples hear natural sleep (περὶ τῆς κοιμήσεως τοῦ ὕπνου); the narrator corrects the subject, not the word: εἰρήκει δὲ ὁ Ἰησοῦς περὶ τοῦ θανάτου αὐτοῦ — "Jesus had spoken of his death" — and Jesus then says it plainly: Λάζαρος ἀπέθανεν. In the Lord's own usage the sleep-word *is* a death-word, and its appointed pair is a waking He Himself performs (ἐξυπνίσω).
  - Acts 7:60: καὶ τοῦτο εἰπὼν ἐκοιμήθη — "and having said this, he fell asleep." Luke narrates a death by stoning with the sleep verb, at the moment of death: the word describes the death itself, not a state before it. (The same verse-pair carries the companion facet under the other subject: Stephen's prayer for his spirit's reception, v. 59, is KB089's text.)
  - 1 Thess 4:13–15: περὶ τῶν κοιμωμένων — the dead in Christ carry "those who sleep" as their standing designation (present participle, v. 13; aorist κοιμηθέντας, vv. 14–15). The comfort's ground: τοὺς κοιμηθέντας διὰ τοῦ Ἰησοῦ ἄξει σὺν αὐτῷ — "God will bring with Jesus those who fell asleep through Jesus"; and the living οὐ μὴ φθάσωμεν τοὺς κοιμηθέντας — will by no means precede the sleepers. The sleepers are persons God acts for at the coming, not persons who have ceased.
  - 1 Cor 15:51–52: πάντες οὐ κοιμηθησόμεθα, πάντες δὲ ἀλλαγησόμεθα ... οἱ νεκροὶ ἐγερθήσονται ἄφθαρτοι — in the resurrection chapter, to die before the trumpet is to sleep; the same dead (οἱ νεκροί) are raised imperishable, and those who did not sleep are changed. Sleep's terminus is resurrection, stated in the same sentence.
  - Dan 12:2 (LHB): וְרַבִּים מִיְּשֵׁנֵי אַדְמַת־עָפָר יָקִיצוּ — "and many of those who sleep in the dust of the earth will awake." The Hebrew Bible's own pairing locates the sleepers *in the dust of the earth* — the body's place — and pairs the sleep with a waking (יָקִיצוּ) to everlasting life.
- **The close (settled):** (1) Sleep is the texts' own name for the death of God's people — used by Jesus of a man already dead and glossed by Him as death (John 11), by Luke of a man dying violently (Acts 7:60), by Paul as the dead in Christ's standing designation (1 Thess 4; 1 Cor 15). (2) In every use the sleep is bounded by a waking God performs — ἐξυπνίσω (John 11:11), ἄξει σὺν αὐτῷ (1 Thess 4:14), ἐγερθήσονται ἄφθαρτοι (1 Cor 15:52), יָקִיצוּ (Dan 12:2); no text leaves the sleeper sleeping forever, and none treats sleep as extinction. (3) The sleep is predicated where the body is — Daniel puts the sleepers in the dust of the earth, and Paul's sleepers are the οἱ νεκροί who are raised. What the sleep-word says about the *person* is this facet's limit; the person's state is the companion facet's subject (KB089).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) John 11's Lazarus is woken to mortal life, not raised to the resurrection of 1 Cor 15; the passage is used for the Lord's vocabulary (sleep = death; the waking is His act), not as a specimen of the final resurrection. (2) Dan 12:2 quantifies with רַבִּים, "many"; the entry quotes it as written and builds nothing on the quantifier. (3) In 1 Thess 4:13–14 the apparatus markers ride on neighboring words; the sleep designations themselves (κοιμωμένων, κοιμηθέντας) are unmarked, and the close rests on them.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The softening reading — "sleep" as a polite euphemism conveying nothing. Answered: a euphemism still names the same reality, and this one is corrected by its own Speaker when misunderstood (John 11:13–14) and given a stated terminus — a waking, a raising — in every home text; it conveys death-with-a-waking, which is information, not decoration. (b) The extinction reading — the sleeper is no one until re-created. Answered within this facet: the sleepers are the subjects of God's future acts (brought *with* Jesus, raised imperishable) and are located (in the dust); the texts speak of someone sleeping and someone to be woken. The person's consciousness is not this facet's subject and is neither asserted nor denied here.

### The Person with Christ

- **ID:** KB089
- **Facet of:** KB050
- **Greek keys:** δέξαι τὸ πνεῦμά μου, ἀναλῦσαι, σὺν Χριστῷ εἶναι, ἐκδημῆσαι ἐκ τοῦ σώματος, ἐνδημῆσαι πρὸς τὸν κύριον, ψυχαί, σήμερον μετʼ ἐμοῦ
- **Verse keys:** Acts 7:59; Luke 23:42–43; Phil 1:21–23; 2 Cor 5:6–8; Rev 6:9–10; Heb 12:22–23; Luke 16:22–25 (see honesty)
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The one who dies in Christ is, in person, with Him from death — received by the Lord Jesus, departing to be with Christ, away from the body and at home with the Lord — conscious, speaking, and himself, while the body sleeps.
- **The chain:**
  - Acts 7:59: καὶ ἐλιθοβόλουν τὸν Στέφανον ἐπικαλούμενον καὶ λέγοντα· κύριε Ἰησοῦ, δέξαι τὸ πνεῦμά μου — *while* they were stoning him (present participles), Stephen prays: "Lord Jesus, receive my spirit." The prayer expects reception at death, addressed to the Lord Jesus Himself; Luke records no refusal, no delay, and no sleep of the spirit — the sleep-word of v. 60 is spoken only when the praying is done, under the other facet's subject.
  - Luke 23:43: ἀμήν σοι λέγω, σήμερον μετʼ ἐμοῦ ἔσῃ ἐν τῷ παραδείσῳ — "Truly I say to you, today you will be with Me in Paradise." The content is μετʼ ἐμοῦ (with Me) and the time-word is σήμερον (today), spoken to a man dying that day — before the resurrection, before the ascension.
  - Phil 1:21–23: Ἐμοὶ γὰρ τὸ ζῆν Χριστὸς καὶ τὸ ἀποθανεῖν κέρδος ... τὴν ἐπιθυμίαν ἔχων εἰς τὸ ἀναλῦσαι καὶ σὺν Χριστῷ εἶναι — dying is *gain* because departing (ἀναλῦσαι) and being with Christ (σὺν Χριστῷ εἶναι) are stated as one desire with one object; Paul inserts no interval, no sleep, and no waiting state between the departing and the being-with.
  - 2 Cor 5:6–8: ἐνδημοῦντες ἐν τῷ σώματι ἐκδημοῦμεν ἀπὸ τοῦ κυρίου ... εὐδοκοῦμεν μᾶλλον ἐκδημῆσαι ἐκ τοῦ σώματος καὶ ἐνδημῆσαι πρὸς τὸν κύριον — Paul's grammar admits two states and treats them as exhaustive alternatives: at home in the body is away from the Lord; to depart from the body is to be at home with the Lord. No third, in-between state is named anywhere in the passage.
  - Rev 6:9–10: εἶδον ὑποκάτω τοῦ θυσιαστηρίου τὰς ψυχὰς τῶν ἐσφαγμένων διὰ τὸν λόγον τοῦ θεοῦ ... καὶ ἔκραξαν φωνῇ μεγάλῃ λέγοντες· ἕως πότε, ὁ δεσπότης ὁ ἅγιος καὶ ἀληθινός, οὐ κρίνεις καὶ ἐκδικεῖς τὸ αἷμα ἡμῶν — the souls of the slain are present before God: conscious, speaking in the first person, remembering their own deaths, and asking about time (ἕως πότε, "how long"). Persons — aware and articulate — before the resurrection.
  - Heb 12:22–23: ἀλλὰ προσεληλύθατε ... καὶ πνεύμασιν δικαίων τετελειωμένων — "you *have come* (perfect: the arrival stands) ... to the spirits of the righteous made perfect." The assembly the living have already joined includes, as a present constituent, the perfected spirits of the righteous — persons in the heavenly Jerusalem now, not a future roster only.
- **The close (settled):** (1) At death the person in Christ is *with Christ* — stated as reception by the Lord Jesus at the moment of death (Acts 7:59), as same-day presence in Paradise (Luke 23:43), as departing's immediate content (Phil 1:23), and as the exhaustive alternative to life in the body (2 Cor 5:8). (2) The with-Christ dead are conscious and themselves: they pray (Acts 7:59), desire (Phil 1:23), speak, remember, and ask (Rev 6:10), and are named as present members of the assembly (Heb 12:23); no text in this facet predicates sleep, silence, or unawareness of the *person*. (3) This facet states nothing about the body's condition — that is KB088's subject; Acts 7:59–60 carries both facets in two verses precisely because the subjects differ.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Luke 23:43's punctuation is editorial (the Greek has no comma); σήμερον is read with the promise, on the formula's own evidence: ἀμήν σοι λέγω is a fixed opening across the Gospels and nowhere else carries the time adverb, which stands next to the ἔσῃ it times. (2) Phil 1:23's γάρ is bracketed in NA28 (πολλῷ [γὰρ] μᾶλλον); the close rests on ἀναλῦσαι καὶ σὺν Χριστῷ εἶναι, not on the bracketed word. (3) Rev 6 is apocalyptic vision; it is used for what the vision depicts — souls conscious and speaking under the altar — and no geography of the intermediate state is built on it. (4) Luke 16:22–25 was supplied as optional: the beggar carried by angels to Abraham's bosom, the rich man in Hades conscious, seeing, and speaking — consistent with this facet, and quoted nowhere in its chain, because the passage's genre is itself contested and no load-bearing claim rests on it.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The unconscious-interval reading — the whole person sleeps unaware until the resurrection. Answered: its proof-texts are the companion facet's sleep texts (which predicate sleep where the body is) and the Ecclesiastes texts (answered in the constellation, KB050); against them stand this facet's own words — reception at death, *today* with Me, depart-and-be-with as one desire, and souls who cry "how long" — with no interval marker in any of them. (b) The definitional reading — ψυχή means the whole living being, so a ψυχή cannot exist apart from the body. Answered by usage: Rev 6:9's ψυχαί are explicitly τῶν ἐσφαγμένων, *of the slain*, and they speak; the word's use in the text governs, not a definition imported to it.

### The Active Spirit and Bodily Sleep

- **ID:** KB050
- **Facet of:** none
- **Facets:** KB088, KB089
- **Greek keys:** κοιμάω, κεκοίμηται, ἐκοιμήθη, πνεῦμα, ψυχή, σὺν Χριστῷ
- **Verse keys:** John 11:11–14; Acts 7:59–60; 1 Thess 4:13–15; 1 Cor 15:51–52; Dan 12:2; Luke 23:42–43; Phil 1:21–23; 2 Cor 5:6–8; Rev 6:9–10; Heb 12:22–23; Eccl 9:5, 10; Ps 146:4
- **Subject distribution:** body: sleeps in the dust, is raised imperishable (Dan 12:2; 1 Cor 15:52); person: received by the Lord Jesus, with Christ, conscious and speaking (Acts 7:59; Phil 1:23; Rev 6:9–10)
- **Kind:** facet-set
- **Claim:** Death for the one in Christ is stated by the texts under two subjects at once — the body sleeps in the dust awaiting the resurrection waking, and the person is with Christ, conscious and received — and neither statement trims the other, because each is true of the subject it names.
- **The chain:**
  - Facet KB088 (subject: the BODY) — "For those who die in the Lord, the texts' own name for their death is sleep — the body rests in the dust as one who will be woken, and in every use the sleep is bounded by a waking God performs: He will bring the sleepers with Jesus and raise the dead imperishable."
  - Facet KB089 (subject: the PERSON) — "The one who dies in Christ is, in person, with Him from death — received by the Lord Jesus, departing to be with Christ, away from the body and at home with the Lord — conscious, speaking, and himself, while the body sleeps."
  - The distribution is the texts' own, inside single passages. Acts 7:59–60 gives one death both predicates in two verses: δέξαι τὸ πνεῦμά μου (the spirit, received by the Lord Jesus) and, the praying finished, ἐκοιμήθη (he fell asleep). And 1 Thess 4:14 lands both predicates on the same people in one clause: τοὺς κοιμηθέντας διὰ τοῦ Ἰησοῦ ἄξει σὺν αὐτῷ — *the sleepers* are the ones God brings *with* Jesus. Subject distribution is not a device imposed on these texts; it is read off them.
- **The close (settled):** What only the conjunction settles: the question "are the dead in Christ asleep, or awake with Him?" is a subject-mismatch question. The texts answer "asleep" of the body and "with Christ, conscious" of the person — in the same passages, twice in the same verse-pair — and each answer holds at full strength only while it keeps its own subject. The comfort of 1 Thess 4:13 (ἵνα μὴ λυπῆσθε, "so that you may not grieve") is the conjunction itself: the sleeper is with Jesus (4:14) *and* will be woken (1 Cor 15:52); grief is answered by neither half alone. The dead in Christ are, as whole persons, with Him now; their bodies sleep in the dust until He wakes them; and the resurrection — not the death — is where the two subjects are seen together again.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) No single facet below, taken alone, asserts the full claim: the sleep facet does not state the person's consciousness, and the with-Christ facet does not state the body's rest. (2) The full claim is the conjunction of the facets and asserts nothing beyond what they jointly state; "soul sleep" and "intermediate state" are the proposer's labels for the conjunction, not words of any verse in the set. (3) The former prose entry's assertions are carried whole by the facets — union unsevered (KB089), the flesh resting (KB088), boldness at death (2 Cor 5:6–8's θαρρέω stands in KB089's chain); its "gathered into the presence of the Highest One" phrasing is restated in the texts' own words (πρὸς τὸν κύριον).
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) Soul sleep proper — the whole person unconscious until the resurrection. Answered: it rests on Facet A's texts stretched over the person, answered by Facet B's reception, presence, and speech texts; and on the Hebrew texts following, read without their subjects. (b) The Hebrew rival texts. Answered on their own subjects. Eccl 9:5: הַמֵּתִים אֵינָם יוֹדְעִים מְאוּמָה — "the dead know nothing at all"; Eccl 9:10: אֵין מַעֲשֶׂה וְחֶשְׁבּוֹן וְדַעַת וְחָכְמָה בִּשְׁאוֹל — "there is no work, reckoning, knowledge, or wisdom in Sheol." Qohelet speaks of the dead in Sheol, under the sun — the grave's ledger, where no earthly activity continues; his statements are true of the subject he names, and he never predicates his Sheol-silence of a person received by God. Ps 146:4 distributes its own subjects in one breath: תֵּצֵא רוּחוֹ — "his breath *goes out*" (it departs; it is not extinguished) — יָשֻׁב לְאַדְמָתוֹ — "he returns to his ground" (the body's dust-return) — אָבְדוּ עֶשְׁתֹּנֹתָיו — "his plans perish that day": what perishes is the man's earthly designs, which is exactly Eccl 9:10's point. Read distributively, the rival texts agree with the facets; read with their subjects stretched, they are made to contradict the Lord's "today." (c) The opposite stretch — disembodied finality, the body disposable and the resurrection a formality. Answered by Facet A: the sleepers' waking *is* the resurrection, dated to the last trumpet (1 Cor 15:52), and 1 Thess 4 grounds its comfort in the sleepers' future and not only in their present.

### The Balanced Ledger of the Kinsman (Romans 5:15-19)

- **ID:** KB051
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** παράπτωμα, χάρισμα, δωρεά, κατεστάθησαν, κατασταθήσονται, δικαίωσις ζωῆς
- **Verse keys:** Rom 5:15, 18–19
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Paul's Adam–Christ reckoning runs on one man's act each way — by the one trespass the many died and were constituted sinners, by the one obedience the many are constituted righteous unto justification of life — and the gift's side is stated not as a bare match but as abounding beyond the trespass.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 5:15: Ἀλλʼ οὐχ ὡς τὸ παράπτωμα, οὕτως καὶ τὸ χάρισμα· εἰ γὰρ τῷ τοῦ ἑνὸς παραπτώματι οἱ πολλοὶ ἀπέθανον, πολλῷ μᾶλλον ἡ χάρις τοῦ θεοῦ καὶ ἡ δωρεὰ ἐν χάριτι τῇ τοῦ ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ εἰς τοὺς πολλοὺς ἐπερίσσευσεν — the parallel is drawn and immediately qualified: "not as the trespass, so also the gift." The many really died (ἀπέθανον, aorist) by the one trespass; the gift abounded (ἐπερίσσευσεν) to the many "much more" (πολλῷ μᾶλλον).
  - Rom 5:18: Ἄρα οὖν ὡς διʼ ἑνὸς παραπτώματος εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους εἰς κατάκριμα, οὕτως καὶ διʼ ἑνὸς δικαιώματος εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους εἰς δικαίωσιν ζωῆς — one trespass → condemnation for all; one righteous act → justification of life for all. The διʼ ... εἰς structure is identical on both sides; only the outcome nouns differ.
  - Rom 5:19: ὥσπερ γὰρ διὰ τῆς παρακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου ἁμαρτωλοὶ κατεστάθησαν οἱ πολλοί, οὕτως καὶ διὰ τῆς ὑπακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς δίκαιοι κατασταθήσονται οἱ πολλοί — the mechanism is named: constitution. The many were constituted (κατεστάθησαν, aorist passive) sinners by the one man's disobedience; the many will be constituted (κατασταθήσονται, future passive) righteous by the One's obedience. The standing is conferred by the head's act, not accumulated by the many.
- **The close (settled):** (1) The reckoning is genuinely parallel in structure — one act, the many, both directions — so neither side may be shrunk to save the other: the death is real (ἀπέθανον) and the constitution real (κατεστάθησαν), on the gift side as much as on the trespass side. (2) The parallel is explicitly *not* symmetrical in measure: οὐχ ὡς τὸ παράπτωμα at the clause's head and πολλῷ μᾶλλον with ἐπερίσσευσεν at its end state the gift exceeding the trespass; "matching" or "balancing" language — including this entry's former prose — understates the text's own asymmetry. "Ledger" and "balance" are the proposer's accounting images, retained in the title as labels, not as the text's claim. (3) The constitution verbs are passive: the many *are constituted* by the head's act — the standing is received, not achieved.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Rom 5:16–17 are not in this chain (not in the supplied set); nothing here rests on the κρίμα development or the reign-in-life clause. (2) Rom 5:19's future κατασταθήσονται is left open as the standing fork (sequence future from Adam's standpoint vs. eschatological future, per the Two Humanities entry's honesty clause); nothing in this close depends on resolving it. (3) The former prose's further assertions — that the wages of sin is strictly physical death and not an infinite metaphysical penalty, and the Kinsman/ransom framing of the title — are not closed by these verses and are not carried into this closure; they stand as open items in the build record. (4) The scope of 5:18's εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους on the gift side is left exactly as the verse states it; this entry closes the reckoning's structure, not a doctrine of its application.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The symmetry reading — grace exactly offsets sin, unit for unit, the "balanced ledger" taken literally. Answered: 5:15's own οὐχ ὡς and πολλῷ μᾶλλον refuse the equation before the parallel is even run. (b) The figurehead reading — Adam and Christ as illustrations of individual choice, the many constituting themselves by imitation or response. Answered: the passives (κατεστάθησαν / κατασταθήσονται) and the διὰ τῆς παρακοῆς / διὰ τῆς ὑπακοῆς construction place the constituting act in the one man's deed, not in the many's reaction.

### God Made Him Both Lord and Christ — and Us Fellow Heirs with Him (Epoiēsen and Echarisato)

- **ID:** KB052
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ποιέω, χαρίζομαι, ὑπερυψόω, ταπεινόω, κενόω, ὑπακοή, στεφανόω, πάθημα, πάσχω, κληρονομέω, κληρονόμος, κληρονομία, συγκληρονόμος, συμπάσχω, συνδοξάζω, ὄνομα, τίθημι, υἱοθεσία, ἀνταπόδοσις, ἀπολαμβάνω
- **Verse keys:** Acts 2:23–24, 2:30–36; Phil 2:6–11; Rom 5:19; Heb 5:8; Luke 24:26; Heb 1:2–4; Heb 2:7, 2:9–13, 2:17; Gal 3:18; Rom 8:14–18, 8:32; Col 3:24
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Son's lordship and name were God's grant, not His earnings — God made Him both Lord and Christ (Acts 2:36), and because He humbled Himself and became obedient to the point of death on a cross, God highly exalted Him and graciously gave Him the name which is above every name (Phil 2:8–9), and because of His suffering death He stands crowned with glory and honor (Heb 2:9); the inheritance runs the same way at the places named for Him and for Abraham — God appointed Him heir of all things (Heb 1:2), He has inherited a more excellent name (Heb 1:4), and the inheritance God granted to Abraham came by promise, expressly not out of law (Gal 3:18) — and we are included by the same Giver's same kind of act, not by any household law: God, who did not spare His own Son but delivered Him over for us all, will with Him freely give us all things (Rom 8:32), and if children, we are heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ, if indeed we suffer with Him so that we may also be glorified with Him (Rom 8:17).
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data): ποιέω at Acts 2:36 is filed [37.106], "to cause someone to assume a particular type of function" (Assign to a Role or Function) — the word carries an "earn, gain" sense in its range ([57.189]), and the dictionary does not file Acts 2:36 under it. χαρίζομαι is filed [57.102], "to give or grant graciously and generously, with the implication of good will on the part of the giver," at all three of this entry's places (Phil 2:9; Gal 3:18; Rom 8:32). κληρονομέω at Heb 1:4 is filed [57.131], "to receive something of considerable value which has not been earned" — not under [57.138], receiving from a deceased parent. κληρονόμος is filed [57.139], "the person who inherits possessions," at Heb 1:2 and at Rom 8:17 (both occurrences there); συγκληρονόμος at Rom 8:17 is filed [57.134], "one who receives a possession together with someone else." στεφανόω at Heb 2:7 and 2:9 is filed [87.13], to show particular honor to a person as the result of some type of victory — not under [57.122], to give a prize or reward. ὑπερυψόω at Phil 2:9 is filed [87.16], to give exceptional honor in view of high status — Phil 2:9 is the word's only New Testament verse. ταπεινόω at Phil 2:8 is filed [87.62], to cause someone to be in a low status. πάθημα at Heb 2:9 and 2:10 is filed [24.78], suffering. ὑπακοή at Rom 5:19 and Heb 5:8 is filed [36.15], obedience. ὄνομα is filed [33.126], "name," at Phil 2:9 (both occurrences) and at Heb 1:4 — the dictionary files the word at both places and identifies the name at neither. τίθημι at Heb 1:2 is filed [37.96], in the same Assign domain as Acts 2:36's ποιέω. υἱοθεσία at Rom 8:15 is filed [35.53], adoption, "including complete rights of inheritance."
