  - Acts 2:36, on the road Peter himself builds to it: God raised Him, ending death's hold (2:24, ὃν ὁ θεὸς ἀνέστησεν — "But God raised Him from the dead"); David the prophet knew that "GOD HAD SWORN TO HIM WITH AN OATH TO SEAT one OF HIS DESCENDANTS ON HIS THRONE" (2:30, ὅρκῳ ὤμοσεν αὐτῷ ὁ θεὸς … καθίσαι ἐπὶ τὸν θρόνον αὐτοῦ), and foresaw and spoke of the resurrection of the Christ (2:31, προϊδὼν ἐλάλησεν περὶ τῆς ἀναστάσεως τοῦ Χριστοῦ); "It is this Jesus whom God raised up, a fact to which we are all witnesses" (2:32, τοῦτον τὸν Ἰησοῦν ἀνέστησεν ὁ θεός, οὗ πάντες ἡμεῖς ἐσμεν μάρτυρες); exalted at God's right hand, He received the promise of the Holy Spirit from the Father and poured out what the crowd saw and heard (2:33, τῇ δεξιᾷ οὖν τοῦ θεοῦ ὑψωθείς, τήν τε ἐπαγγελίαν τοῦ πνεύματος τοῦ ἁγίου λαβὼν παρὰ τοῦ πατρός, ἐξέχεεν τοῦτο); and David, who did not ascend, said it: ‘THE LORD SAID TO MY LORD, “SIT AT MY RIGHT HAND, UNTIL I MAKE YOUR ENEMIES A FOOTSTOOL FOR YOUR FEET.” ’ (2:34–35, εἶπεν ὁ κύριος τῷ κυρίῳ μου· κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου, ἕως ἂν θῶ τοὺς ἐχθρούς σου ὑποπόδιον τῶν ποδῶν σου). Then the conclusion: καὶ κύριον αὐτὸν καὶ χριστὸν ἐποίησεν ὁ θεός, τοῦτον τὸν Ἰησοῦν ὃν ὑμεῖς ἐσταυρώσατε, "God has made Him both Lord and Christ—this Jesus whom you crucified." God is the subject; the objects are the paired titles; the man they land on is named as the crucified and raised Jesus; and the making is filed as assigning to a role or function ([37.106], the filings bullet above).
  - Phil 2:6–11 — the passage opens before the humbling: ὃς ἐν μορφῇ θεοῦ ὑπάρχων οὐχ ἁρπαγμὸν ἡγήσατο τὸ εἶναι ἴσα θεῷ, "who, as He already existed in the form of God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped" (2:6). The descent is His own act, in reflexive verbs: ἀλλʼ ἑαυτὸν ἐκένωσεν, "but emptied Himself" (2:7); ἐταπείνωσεν ἑαυτὸν γενόμενος ὑπήκοος μέχρι θανάτου, θανάτου δὲ σταυροῦ, "He humbled Himself by becoming obedient to the point of death: death on a cross" (2:8). Then the ascent, in God's verbs, with its connector showing: διὸ καὶ ὁ θεὸς αὐτὸν ὑπερύψωσεν καὶ ἐχαρίσατο αὐτῷ τὸ ὄνομα τὸ ὑπὲρ πᾶν ὄνομα, "For this reason also God highly exalted Him, and bestowed on Him the name which is above every name" (2:9) — διὸ καί, "for this reason also," reaches back to the obedience of 2:8; the giving verb is the gracious-giving word ([57.102]); and the gift aims at a confession: ἵνα ἐν τῷ ὀνόματι Ἰησοῦ πᾶν γόνυ κάμψῃ … καὶ πᾶσα γλῶσσα ἐξομολογήσηται ὅτι κύριος Ἰησοῦς Χριστός, "so that at the name of Jesus EVERY KNEE WILL BOW … and that every tongue will confess that Jesus Christ is Lord" (2:10–11).
  - His obedience and His suffering, in the words that name them His: Rom 5:19 — ὥσπερ γὰρ διὰ τῆς παρακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου ἁμαρτωλοὶ κατεστάθησαν οἱ πολλοί, οὕτως καὶ διὰ τῆς ὑπακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς δίκαιοι κατασταθήσονται οἱ πολλοί, "For as through the one man’s disobedience the many were made sinners, so also through the obedience of the One the many will be made righteous" — the obedience is the One's ([36.15]); Heb 5:8 — καίπερ ὢν υἱός, ἔμαθεν ἀφʼ ὧν ἔπαθεν τὴν ὑπακοήν, "Although He was a Son, He learned obedience from the things which He suffered" — obedience and suffering in one clause, the suffering the place the obedience was learned.
  - Heb 2:7, 9–10 — τὸν δὲ βραχύ τι παρʼ ἀγγέλους ἠλαττωμένον βλέπομεν Ἰησοῦν διὰ τὸ πάθημα τοῦ θανάτου δόξῃ καὶ τιμῇ ἐστεφανωμένον, "we do see Him who was made for a little while lower than the angels, namely, Jesus … crowned with glory and honor" — διὰ τὸ πάθημα τοῦ θανάτου — the supplied NASB renders the phrase "because of His suffering death" — is the crowning's stated ground, and the crowning is filed as honor ([87.13]), not as a prize ([57.122]). The Psalm sentence the chapter quotes names the crowner — God, the one addressed: δόξῃ καὶ τιμῇ ἐστεφάνωσας αὐτόν, "YOU HAVE CROWNED HIM WITH GLORY AND HONOR" (2:7). The next verse runs the same shape toward us: πολλοὺς υἱοὺς εἰς δόξαν ἀγαγόντα τὸν ἀρχηγὸν τῆς σωτηρίας αὐτῶν διὰ παθημάτων τελειῶσαι, "in bringing many sons to glory, to perfect the originator of their salvation through sufferings" (2:10, πάθημα [24.78]). Jesus states the shape Himself in Luke 24:26: οὐχὶ ταῦτα ἔδει παθεῖν τὸν χριστὸν καὶ εἰσελθεῖν εἰς τὴν δόξαν αὐτοῦ; "Was it not necessary for the Christ to suffer these things and to come into His glory?"
  - Heb 2:11–13, 17 — the brotherhood the stub reached for, in the text's own words: ὅ τε γὰρ ἁγιάζων καὶ οἱ ἁγιαζόμενοι ἐξ ἑνὸς πάντες· διʼ ἣν αἰτίαν οὐκ ἐπαισχύνεται ἀδελφοὺς αὐτοὺς καλεῖν, "For both He who sanctifies and those who are sanctified are all from one Father; for this reason He is not ashamed to call them brothers and sisters" (2:11 — the NASB supplies "Father"; the Greek says only "all from one"); the saying He then speaks is the Psalm's: ἀπαγγελῶ τὸ ὄνομά σου τοῖς ἀδελφοῖς μου, ἐν μέσῳ ἐκκλησίας ὑμνήσω σε, "I WILL PROCLAIM YOUR NAME TO MY BROTHERS, IN THE MIDST OF THE ASSEMBLY I WILL SING YOUR PRAISE." (2:12); ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ καὶ τὰ παιδία ἅ μοι ἔδωκεν ὁ θεός, "BEHOLD, I AND THE CHILDREN WHOM GOD HAS GIVEN ME." (2:13) — the children are God's gift to Him, the giving running God's way here too; ὅθεν ὤφειλεν κατὰ πάντα τοῖς ἀδελφοῖς ὁμοιωθῆναι, "Therefore, in all things He had to be made like His brothers" (2:17). Brothers by God's giving and by His likeness to us — no law is named as the mechanism anywhere in the passage.
  - Heb 1:2, 4 — ὃν ἔθηκεν κληρονόμον πάντων, διʼ οὗ καὶ ἐποίησεν τοὺς αἰῶνας, "whom He appointed heir of all things, through whom He also made the world" (1:2) — the heirship is God's appointment (τίθημι [37.96]), and the appointed heir is the one through whom the ages were made. Then the sitting down: καθαρισμὸν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ποιησάμενος ἐκάθισεν ἐν δεξιᾷ τῆς μεγαλωσύνης ἐν ὑψηλοῖς, "When He had made purification of sins, He sat down at the right hand of the Majesty on high" (1:3); and τοσούτῳ κρείττων γενόμενος τῶν ἀγγέλων ὅσῳ διαφορώτερον παρʼ αὐτοὺς κεκληρονόμηκεν ὄνομα, "having become so much better than the angels, to the extent that He has inherited a more excellent name than they" (1:4) — κεκληρονόμηκεν is perfect, "has inherited," and its filing is receiving what has not been earned ([57.131]).
  - Gal 3:18, spent at its own place: εἰ γὰρ ἐκ νόμου ἡ κληρονομία, οὐκέτι ἐξ ἐπαγγελίας· τῷ δὲ Ἀβραὰμ διʼ ἐπαγγελίας κεχάρισται ὁ θεός, "For if the inheritance is based on law, it is no longer based on a promise; but God has granted it to Abraham by means of a promise." The verse is about Abraham's inheritance, and it is spent for exactly what it says: where God grants an inheritance, the granting is by promise and not out of law, and its verb is the same gracious-giving word, under the same filing ([57.102]), as the name-giving at Phil 2:9.
  - Rom 8:14–18 — the Spirit-led are sons of God (8:14, ὅσοι γὰρ πνεύματι θεοῦ ἄγονται, οὗτοι υἱοὶ θεοῦ εἰσιν); what was received is a spirit of adoption (8:15, ἐλάβετε πνεῦμα υἱοθεσίας — adoption filed [35.53], with complete rights of inheritance in its definition); the Spirit's own witness stands in the verse: αὐτὸ τὸ πνεῦμα συμμαρτυρεῖ τῷ πνεύματι ἡμῶν ὅτι ἐσμὲν τέκνα θεοῦ, "The Spirit Himself testifies with our spirit that we are children of God" (8:16); then the step itself, whole: εἰ δὲ τέκνα, καὶ κληρονόμοι· κληρονόμοι μὲν θεοῦ, συγκληρονόμοι δὲ Χριστοῦ, εἴπερ συμπάσχομεν ἵνα καὶ συνδοξασθῶμεν, "and if children, heirs also, heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ, if indeed we suffer with Him so that we may also be glorified with Him" (8:17) — heirs of God (κληρονόμοι [57.139]), fellow heirs with Christ (συγκληρονόμοι [57.134], receiving together with another), and the condition standing in the verse, not trimmed off it: εἴπερ συμπάσχομεν, "if indeed we suffer with Him," aimed at συνδοξασθῶμεν, "we may also be glorified with Him." Paul's comparison follows in the same words: οὐκ ἄξια τὰ παθήματα τοῦ νῦν καιροῦ πρὸς τὴν μέλλουσαν δόξαν ἀποκαλυφθῆναι εἰς ἡμᾶς, "the sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory that is to be revealed to us" (8:18).
  - Rom 8:32 — ὅς γε τοῦ ἰδίου υἱοῦ οὐκ ἐφείσατο ἀλλʼ ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν πάντων παρέδωκεν αὐτόν, πῶς οὐχὶ καὶ σὺν αὐτῷ τὰ πάντα ἡμῖν χαρίσεται; "He who did not spare His own Son, but delivered Him over for us all, how will He not also with Him freely give us all things?" The mechanism of our inclusion in one verse: the Giver delivered His own Son for us all, and the giving to us is future, free, and *with Him* (σὺν αὐτῷ) — its verb χαρίσεται under the same filing as the name-giving ([57.102]).
  - Col 3:24 — the one place in the supplied sets where a reward word stands beside an inheritance: εἰδότες ὅτι ἀπὸ κυρίου ἀπολήμψεσθε τὴν ἀνταπόδοσιν τῆς κληρονομίας, "knowing that it is from the Lord that you will receive the reward of the inheritance." The receiving verb there (ἀπολήμψεσθε, ἀπολαμβάνω) is filed [57.128], Receive. Its subject is the servants addressed, and its inheritance is ours — no reward word in the sets is spoken of the Son or of His exaltation.
- **The close (settled):** (1) The making at Acts 2:36 is a conferral with real content, argued by Peter and not merely asserted: the crucified Jesus, raised by God, exalted at His right hand, seated under David's oath and Psalm 110, having received the Spirit's promise from the Father and poured it out — and the dictionary files the making verb there as assigning to a role or function. The division of labor is the texts' own at the places that state it: in Philippians the descent is Christ's, in reflexive verbs (He emptied Himself, He humbled Himself), and the ascent verbs of Philippians and Acts are God's (made, exalted, gave); God is the crowner in the Psalm sentence Hebrews quotes (Heb 2:7) and the appointer at Heb 1:2 — while Hebrews states the session with the Son as its subject (He sat down, Heb 1:3), and no cleaner division is claimed than the texts state. (2) The grant answers the obedience and the suffering — the texts state the because (διὸ καί, Phil 2:9; διὰ τὸ πάθημα τοῦ θανάτου, Heb 2:9) — and no text pays Him: no earning verb is filed at any of the verses this entry spends for the exaltation or the inheritance, the name is given with the gracious-giving word, and the name at Heb 1:4 is inherited under the filing "to receive something of considerable value which has not been earned." Granted by God in light of His obedience and His suffering is the whole statement the set supports: the ground stated, a wage denied, both from the texts. (3) Our inclusion is an act of the same kind and no other kind: adoption received, heirs of God, fellow heirs with Christ on the verse's own condition, all things freely given with Him — future, as Rom 8:32 states it — by the God who delivered Him for us all. No verse in the set assigns our share by a law, and none of it hands us His lordship or His name; what is given to us with Him is heirship and glory, in Romans' own words.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its old title, "The Kinsman's Reward (Epoiēsen and Echarisato)," is retired with it; the parts this build drops or reverses are named in the build record above the entry — "earned" (reversed), "household law dictates" (dropped; the set's law verse states the opposite for its inheritance), "that exact inheritance" (narrowed to Rom 8:17's own terms), and "Firstborn" (not carried; πρωτότοκος stands in the supplied set at eight places — Luke 2:7; Rom 8:29; Col 1:15; Col 1:18; Heb 1:6; Heb 11:28; Heb 12:23; Rev 1:5 — dual-filed at Rom 8:29 under [13.79] and [87.47], and no sentence in this entry rests on the word). (2) No verse in this set says "He was not Lord before" or "He already was Lord" in so many words; this entry closes the conferral as Peter argues it, and the question of who He was before the sending stands where KB010 closes it, on John's texts — this set neither adds to that close nor subtracts from it. (3) Which name Phil 2:9 gives is left exactly where the passage leaves it: 2:10 bows every knee "at the name of Jesus" and 2:11 confesses "Jesus Christ is Lord"; the dictionary files ὄνομα under [33.126] at both Phil 2:9 and Heb 1:4 and identifies the name at neither, and this entry does not identify Phil 2:9's name with Heb 1:4's — the two stand at their own places, both true, unblended. (4) Heb 1:2 places God's appointment of the Son as heir at the head of its sentence, before the purification and the sitting down are narrated (1:3); this entry builds no timeline for the appointment and states no moment for it beyond the text's own order. (5) The selection is declared (Framework Core Rule 8, Genuine Context): the chain rests on five passages supplied whole (Acts 2:22–36; Phil 2:5–11; Rom 8:14–18; Heb 1:1–4; Heb 2:5–18) and on the word sets as supplied — full lemma distributions for χαρίζομαι, ὑπερυψόω, στεφανόω, κληρονομέω, πάθημα, πάσχω, ὑπακοή, ταπεινόω, κληρονόμος, κληρονομία, πρωτότοκος, and συγκληρονόμος; exact-form sets for ποιέω (at Acts 2:36) and for ὄνομα (accusative singular, which receipts both of this entry's places, Phil 2:9 and Heb 1:4); and, for συμπάσχω, συνδοξάζω, τίθημι, and υἱοθεσία, the whole passages alone, with their filings stated in the filings bullet; verses from the sets are spent only where quoted here, and the large distributions (πάσχω, κληρονομία, κληρονόμος) stand supplied and unexamined verse by verse beyond what is quoted. (6) Gal 3:18 is spent at its own place — Abraham's inheritance; its joining to Phil 2:9 in this entry is the shared word under its shared filing, stated as such in its chain bullet, and the two grants are not fused into one event.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The earning reading — the stub's own: the Son earned the family inheritance by perfect obedience, and His exaltation is its reward. Answered: no verse in the set pays Him or calls the exaltation a wage; ποιέω has an "earn, gain" sense in its range and the dictionary does not file Acts 2:36 under it ([37.106] stands there instead); στεφανόω at Heb 2:9 files as honor, not prize ([87.13], not [57.122]); the inheritance verb at Heb 1:4 is filed as receiving what has not been earned ([57.131]); and the sets' one reward-of-the-inheritance word (Col 3:24) is spoken to servants about our inheritance, not about the Son. (b) The household-law reading — also the stub's: a family law dictates that the adopted share the Firstborn's inheritance. Answered: no law text in the set assigns any share; the law verse in the set (Gal 3:18) puts its inheritance on the promise side expressly against the law side; and Romans states our inclusion as adoption received, the Spirit's witness, and God's free giving with His Son (Rom 8:15–17, 32) — a Giver and a gift, never a rule that owes us. (c) The began-in-every-sense reading: "made Lord" means He began to be Lord in every sense at the exaltation. Answered: the making is filed as role-assignment, and Peter argues it as enthronement — oath, throne, right hand, Psalm 110 — of "this Jesus whom you crucified," a man already there to receive it; Phil 2:6 stands at the head of the giving passage (the one who humbled Himself already existed in the form of God), and KB010 closes the before-question on John's texts. What began at the exaltation, on this set, is the installed and confessed lordship Acts and Philippians describe. (d) The nothing-changed reading: the conferral is only a public recognition of what was already exercised in the same way. Answered: Acts 2's own content is events, not a relabeling — crucified ("you nailed to a cross … and put Him to death," 2:23), then raised (2:32), exalted and seated (2:33–35), the Spirit received from the Father and poured out (2:33); Philippians adds a name given and a confession still to be made by every tongue (2:9–11). Something happened at the exaltation, and the texts say what.

### Now Faith Is (The Underlying Reality of Things Hoped For)

- **ID:** KB053
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** πληρόω, δωρέομαι, κοινωνός, ὑπόστασις, ἔλεγχος, πίστις, πιστεύω, ἐλπίς, ἐλπίζω, βλέπω, ὑπομονή, εἶδος, κομίζω, τέλος, σωτηρία, καύχησις, βέβαιος, μέτοχος
- **Verse keys:** Col 2:10; 2 Pet 1:3; 2 Pet 1:4; Heb 11:1; Rom 8:24; Rom 8:25; 2 Cor 5:7; 1 Pet 1:8; 1 Pet 1:9; 2 Cor 9:4; 2 Cor 11:17; Heb 3:14
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Believers are made complete in Christ and have been granted everything for life and godliness, and faith itself is the underlying reality of the things hoped for and the proof of things not seen — so what is hoped for is waited for with perseverance, the walk goes by faith and not by sight, and the outcome of faith, the salvation of the soul, is being received even now, unseen.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data): πληρόω at Col 2:10 is filed [59.33], "to make something total or complete." δωρέομαι is filed [57.83] (glosses: to give, to bestow, to grant), at both of its places in this set — 2 Pet 1:3 and 2 Pet 1:4. κοινωνός at 2 Pet 1:4 is filed [34.6], "one who participates with another in some enterprise or matter of joint concern." ὑπόστασις at Heb 11:1 is double-filed — [58.1], "the essential or basic nature of an entity" (glosses: substance, nature, essence, real being), and [31.84], "that which provides the basis for trust and reliance" (glosses: trust, confidence, assurance); its whole New Testament set is five places — Heb 1:3 and Heb 11:1 under [58.1], and 2 Cor 9:4, 2 Cor 11:17, and Heb 3:14 under [31.84] alone, with Heb 11:1 under both. ἔλεγχος at Heb 11:1 is filed [72.8], "the evidence … as to the truth or reality of something" (glosses: proof, verification, evidence for). ἐλπίζω at Rom 8:24 and Rom 8:25 is filed [25.59], "to look forward with confidence to that which is good and beneficial"; ἐλπίς at Rom 8:24 stands under the same [25.59] and also under [25.61], "that which is hoped for," twice at that verse. πίστις at 2 Cor 5:7 is filed [31.85], "to believe to the extent of complete trust and reliance," and πιστεύω at 1 Pet 1:8 is filed under the same [31.85]. εἶδος at 2 Cor 5:7 is filed [24.1] (glosses: sight, seeing). κομίζω at 1 Pet 1:9 is filed [57.126] (glosses: to receive, to obtain). τέλος at 1 Pet 1:9 is double-filed — [89.40], "the result of an event or process …" (glosses: outcome, result, end), and [89.55] (glosses: purpose, intent, goal); the NASB's "outcome" takes the [89.40] side, and the entry spends that side only.
  - Made complete, stated in the present (Col 2:10): καὶ ἐστὲ ἐν αὐτῷ πεπληρωμένοι, ὅς ἐστιν ἡ κεφαλὴ πάσης ἀρχῆς καὶ ἐξουσίας. — "and in Him you have been made complete, and He is the head over every ruler and authority;" The verb is ἐστὲ, "you are," in the present: the state is stated as the readers' present standing in Him, and the word for it is the [59.33] word, made complete. The sentence's first half — all the fullness of deity dwelling in Him bodily (2:9) — is another entry's subject, closed at KB094, and is pointed to there, not spent here; this facet spends only the second half, what is said of the readers.
  - Granted, everything for life and godliness (2 Pet 1:3–4): Ὡς πάντα ἡμῖν τῆς θείας δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ τὰ πρὸς ζωὴν καὶ εὐσέβειαν δεδωρημένης διὰ τῆς ἐπιγνώσεως τοῦ καλέσαντος ἡμᾶς ἰδίᾳ δόξῃ καὶ ἀρετῇ διʼ ὧν τὰ τίμια καὶ μέγιστα ἡμῖν ἐπαγγέλματα δεδώρηται, ἵνα διὰ τούτων γένησθε θείας κοινωνοὶ φύσεως ἀποφυγόντες τῆς ἐν τῷ κόσμῳ ἐν ἐπιθυμίᾳ φθορᾶς. — "for His divine power has granted to us everything pertaining to life and godliness, through the true knowledge of Him who called us by His own glory and excellence. Through these He has granted to us His precious and magnificent promises, so that by them you may become partakers of the divine nature, having escaped the corruption that is in the world on account of lust." The granting word stands twice, at 1:3 and at 1:4, under the one filing [57.83] both times: everything for life and godliness granted, and the promises granted. The verse also states the grant's purpose with ἵνα: that by the promises the readers become partakers (κοινωνοί [34.6], those who share in it) of the divine nature, having escaped the corruption that is in the world. The facet spends the granting and its stated purpose, and nothing beyond them (Honesty (5)).
  - Faith is (Heb 11:1): Ἔστιν δὲ πίστις ἐλπιζομένων ὑπόστασις, πραγμάτων ἔλεγχος οὐ βλεπομένων. — "Now faith is the certainty of things hoped for, a proof of things not seen." The verse opens with Ἔστιν, "is": faith *is*, in the present, two things — the ὑπόστασις of things hoped for, and the ἔλεγχος, the proof [72.8], of things not seen. The NASB's "certainty" spends only the dictionary's second filing of ὑπόστασις at this verse, [31.84]; the same dictionary also files the verse under [58.1], the essential nature of a thing — the same filing it gives the word at Heb 1:3, where the Son is the exact imprint of the Father's ὑπόστασις (closed at KB093, pointed to, not re-argued here). The letter's third use cuts the other way: at Heb 3:14 the same dictionary files the word under [31.84] alone, so Hebrews' own three uses do not settle this verse's filing by themselves, and the entry does not pretend they do. The word's own ancient life runs the same way (Abbott-Smith: sense 1, "a support, base or foundation"; sense 2, "substance"; sense 3, "steadiness, firmness, hence assurance, confidence" — and Abbott-Smith's own verse assignments cut both ways here: it files Heb 1:3 under sense 2, and this verse under sense 3, adding at this verse "here perhaps title-deed, as that which gives reality or guarantee"; in sense 3 the assurance arrives with a "hence," derived from the firmness): in the ancient writers the word names what stands under a thing — what settles under a liquid, a support bearing weight, the plan standing under a work. Read under its [58.1] filing, the verse says faith is the underlying reality of the things hoped for — the reality standing under them now, while they are not seen. On that reading, assurance is what a man has because something stands under him; it is not what the word names.
  - The same word at its other three places (2 Cor 9:4; 2 Cor 11:17; Heb 3:14): at 2 Cor 9:4 Paul writes ἐν τῇ ὑποστάσει ταύτῃ — the NASB, "by this confidence"; at 11:17, ἐν ταύτῃ τῇ ὑποστάσει τῆς καυχήσεως — the NASB, "in this confidence of boasting"; at Heb 3:14, μέτοχοι γὰρ τοῦ Χριστοῦ γεγόναμεν, ἐάνπερ τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆς ὑποστάσεως μέχρι τέλους βεβαίαν κατάσχωμεν — "if we keep the beginning of our commitment firm until the end," where the NASB's word is "commitment" and the Greek says the beginning of the ὑπόστασις is to be held firm (βεβαίαν) to the end. At these three places the dictionary files the word under [31.84] alone, and the NASB's three English words — confidence, confidence, commitment — all spend that side. What the places themselves state is a confidence a man could be put to shame in, a confidence of boasting, and a beginning to be held firm to the end. The entry's settled close does not rest on these three places; what they carry here is stated at the proposed close, marked.
  - Hope that is seen is not hope (Rom 8:24–25): τῇ γὰρ ἐλπίδι ἐσώθημεν· ἐλπὶς δὲ βλεπομένη οὐκ ἔστιν ἐλπίς· ὃ γὰρ βλέπει τίς ἐλπίζει; εἰ δὲ ὃ οὐ βλέπομεν ἐλπίζομεν, διʼ ὑπομονῆς ἀπεκδεχόμεθα. — "For in hope we have been saved, but hope that is seen is not hope; for who hopes for what he already sees? But if we hope for what we do not see, through perseverance we wait eagerly for it." Hope, at these verses, is of what is *not seen* — a thing seen is no longer hoped for, by the word's own statement — and the present mode of holding it is stated too: διʼ ὑπομονῆς, through perseverance, we wait eagerly. The facet states both the not-seeing and the waiting; it does not state that the thing waited for is unreal, and Heb 11:1 has already said what stands under it.
  - The walk (2 Cor 5:7): διὰ πίστεως γὰρ περιπατοῦμεν, οὐ διὰ εἴδους· — "for we walk by faith, not by sight—" The walk's two measures are named side by side: διὰ πίστεως, by faith [31.85], and not διὰ εἴδους, not by sight [24.1]. The verse assigns the present life a way of going that is not sight, and names faith as that way.
  - Not seeing, yet believing — and receiving (1 Pet 1:8–9): ὃν οὐκ ἰδόντες ἀγαπᾶτε, εἰς ὃν ἄρτι μὴ ὁρῶντες, πιστεύοντες δὲ ἀγαλλιᾶσθε χαρᾷ ἀνεκλαλήτῳ καὶ δεδοξασμένῃ κομιζόμενοι τὸ τέλος τῆς πίστεως ὑμῶν σωτηρίαν ψυχῶν. — "and though you have not seen Him, you love Him, and though you do not see Him now, but believe in Him, you greatly rejoice with joy inexpressible and full of glory, obtaining as the outcome of your faith, the salvation of your souls." The readers have not seen Him and do not see Him now (ἄρτι μὴ ὁρῶντες); they believe (πιστεύοντες, [31.85], the same filing as 2 Cor 5:7's faith); and in the present they are receiving — κομιζόμενοι [57.126] — the outcome [89.40] of their faith, the salvation of their souls. The facet holds all three together: not seeing, believing, and receiving, at the same time, of the same people.
- **The close (settled):** (1) The "already" side of this set is stated as present fact, in the texts' own words: believers *are* in Him made complete (Col 2:10), everything for life and godliness *has been granted* and the promises *granted* (2 Pet 1:3–4), and faith *is* — present tense, Ἔστιν — the underlying reality of things hoped for and the proof of things not seen (Heb 11:1). None of these is stated as a future hope or a present feeling; each is stated as what stands. (2) The "not yet" side is stated with the same plainness, and it is a statement about seeing, not about reality: the things are *not seen* (Heb 11:1), hope is of *what we do not see* (Rom 8:24–25), the readers *do not see Him now* (1 Pet 1:8). Read together — no verse crowned over another — the set's shape is: real now, not seen yet. What rules the realized-only reading out is the waiting these same writers state; what rules the not-real-until-seen reading out is Heb 11:1 itself, which names faith the underlying reality and the proof of the very things that are not seen. (3) Between the two, the set assigns the believer a present mode, and it is activity, not cessation: wait eagerly through perseverance (Rom 8:25), walk by faith and not by sight (2 Cor 5:7), love and believe and rejoice without seeing, receiving the outcome of faith even now (1 Pet 1:8–9). The conjunction asserts nothing beyond these facets jointly stated; no single facet, taken alone, asserts the Claim.
- **The proposed close (marked):** At ὑπόστασις's other three places the same underlying-reality reading stands open and reads straight — the ground Paul stands on at 2 Cor 9:4, in which he says we would be put to shame, the ground of his boasting named as such at 2 Cor 11:17, and the beginning that is to be held firm to the end at Heb 3:14 — the confidence at those places being what a man has because a ground stands under him. This is the proposer's reading, offered on the word's ancient usage and on Abbott-Smith's sense order (foundation and substance first, assurance derived with a "hence"), against the modern dictionary's single [31.84] filing at those three verses; it is marked as proposed, and the settled close above does not rest on it.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its old title, "The Completed Inheritance (Dedōrēmenēs / Peplērōmenoi)," is retired with it. The parts dropped are named in the build record above this entry — the perfect-tense payload and the "continuous, present results" built on it, the "cease all self-dependent labor" sentence, the crowned "absolute confidence of our finished standing," and the word "inheritance," which no verse in this set names. This entry is a new assertion built on the pasted texts, not a restatement of the stub. (2) Colossians 2:9 is not spent: the fullness of deity dwelling in Christ bodily is KB094's subject (with KB093 and the KB020 constellation), pointed to and not quoted here; this entry spends only 2:10, what the same sentence says of the readers. Hebrews 1:3 is likewise pointed to at KB093 and not quoted: the Father's ὑπόστασις and the Son as its exact imprint stand closed there, and this entry borrows the verse only as the dictionary's fellow [58.1] filing — a filing datum, not a receipt spent twice. (3) The filings are a modern dictionary's judgments (UBS, 2023, from Louw & Nida, 1988–89), named as such wherever the chain uses them; a filing is a witness about a word, never the authority over a verse, and where the entry reads ὑπόστασις at Heb 11:1 under [58.1] rather than under the NASB's "certainty," the ground is the verse read with the word's ancient usage — the [31.84] filing at that verse is named in the chain, not hidden. The choice between the dictionary's two filings at this verse is the entry's central judgment: Craig ruled it settled under [58.1] on 2026-11-02, with the counter-evidence — the [31.84] filing and its definition, Abbott-Smith's sense-3 assignment at this verse, and Hebrews 3:14's [31.84]-only filing — carried in the chain and answered at rival (c). The broader ancient-usage material (Aristotle, Hippocrates, Polybius, and the papyrus-era uses Abbott-Smith and Lampe collect) is background evidence about the word's life, not a New Testament receipt; no settled sentence above rests on it alone. Lampe's own reading of the New Testament places is a third one — 'plan, project' at all five, faith at Hebrews 11:1 as the counting on of the things hoped for — and the entry does not follow it; his survey is spent here as background on the word's ancient life only. (4) τέλος at 1 Pet 1:9 is double-filed, [89.40] outcome and [89.55] purpose; the entry spends the outcome side, which the NASB also takes, and decides nothing about the purpose side. ἐλπίς at Rom 8:24 stands under two filings, [25.59] and [25.61]; the facet rests on the verse's own statement — hope seen is not hope — and on neither filing against the other. (5) Second Peter 1:4's purpose clause is spent only as it is stated: by the granted promises, partakers of the divine nature, having escaped the corruption in the world. What partaking of the divine nature is, beyond the verse's own words, this entry does not decide, and no sentence in the Claim rests on a meaning of that phrase the verse does not state. (6) This set does not say when seeing replaces faith, or what the arrival looks like; it says hope seen is not hope (Rom 8:24) and faith is of things not seen (Heb 11:1), and it leaves the exchange itself unstated. It also does not name the hoped-for things beyond the verses' own words — the outcome of faith (1 Pet 1:9) and what is waited for (Rom 8:25) are spent as the verses leave them, unnamed here. The marks in Craig's pastes (asterisks and apparatus signs) are Logos export artifacts and stand in no quotation here; the NASB's section heading at Hebrews 11 ("The Triumphs of Faith") is editorial and is not quoted.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The realized-only reading: the standing is finished, the grants are complete, and the waiting language is a failure to believe what is already so — hope and perseverance belong to a lesser stage. Answered from the same writers: Paul states, in the present, "if we hope for what we do not see, through perseverance we wait eagerly for it" (Rom 8:25), and names the walk itself "by faith, not by sight" (2 Cor 5:7); Peter's readers are believing and rejoicing precisely while they "do not see Him now" (1 Pet 1:8). Crowning Colossians and 2 Peter over Romans, Corinthians, and 1 Peter renders those statements untrue as present Christian life; the set ANDs them instead. (b) The not-real-until-seen reading: what is not seen is not yet real — the present statements are promises about a future state, and faith is the holding pattern until sight makes them facts. Answered by the verses' own tenses and terms: "in Him you have been made complete" (Col 2:10), the grants stated as granted (2 Pet 1:3–4), "faith *is*" (Heb 11:1), and readers presently "obtaining as the outcome of your faith" (1 Pet 1:9). And Heb 11:1 names faith the underlying reality of the hoped-for things themselves, not a note about a reality that begins later; the not-seeing in this set is about sight, not about being. (c) The assurance reading, at its strongest: Hebrews 11:1 defines faith by the dictionary's other filing at this very verse — ὑπόστασις as [31.84], "that which provides the basis for trust and reliance," which the NASB's "certainty" spends — and Abbott-Smith files this verse under its assurance sense as well, while the writer's other use in this letter, Hebrews 3:14, stands under [31.84] alone; so the verse contributes a settled assurance about the hoped-for things, and the rest is future. Answered: the dictionary that files [31.84] at this verse also files [58.1] at it — at the word's other three places it files [31.84] alone, and at this verse it did not — and Abbott-Smith, filing the verse under assurance, hedges at this verse alone with "perhaps title-deed, as that which gives reality or guarantee." Greek has its own word for confidence, πεποίθησις, filed by the same dictionary under [31.82] (glosses: confidence, trust), and the writer of Hebrews did not use it; he used the word whose ancient life is what stands under a thing. The verse's second half runs the same direction: ἔλεγχος is *proof*, evidence as to the reality of something [72.8] — a thing that can be pointed to, not a state of mind. Assurance follows where there is an underlying reality; the verse names the reality. (d) The self-completion reading: the gap between the granted standing and the unseen reality is the believer's to close by his own labor, discipline, and resolve — the gift begins the work and the self finishes it. Answered by the set's own assignments: the granting is God's ("His divine power has granted to us everything pertaining to life and godliness," 2 Pet 1:3), the completeness is *in Him* (Col 2:10), and the believer's named activities in these verses are hoping, waiting with perseverance, walking by faith, believing, and rejoicing — receiving (1 Pet 1:9), never completing the grant. A labor that aimed to finish what these texts state as granted would be working at what the texts say is already given, and mistaking the walk for the gift.

### Adelphoi (Brethren / The Shared Family Name)

- **ID:** KB054
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἀδελφός, ἀδελφή
- **Verse keys:** Acts 1:14; 1:15; 1:16; 1:21–22; 1:25–26; Rom 8:29; Rom 16:1–2; Rom 16:7; Rom 16:14; Rom 16:15; 1 Cor 7:12–15; 1 Cor 9:5; 1 Cor 12:1; Phlm 1:1–2; Phlm 1:16; Jas 2:15–16; frame: Gal 3:28–29; 1 Cor 11:3; 1 Cor 11:11–12; Eph 2:14–16; Rev 5:9; 1 Cor 6:17
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Two words carry the household's family name in their believer senses — ἀδελφός, filed by the dictionary as the fellow believer [11.23], and ἀδελφή, as the fellow-believing sister [11.24] — and across the places assembled here the one masculine plural names the whole company with the women demonstrably inside it (Acts 1:14–15; Rom 16:14), named women bear the feminine form at full standing (Rom 16:1; Phlm 1:2), at 1 Cor 7:15 and Jas 2:15 the writers themselves write the pair ἀδελφὸς ἢ ἀδελφὴ for a brother or a sister individually, the name re-names a slave as a beloved brother in the flesh and in the Lord (Phlm 1:16), and the whole foreknown company stands under the Firstborn as his ἀδελφοί (Rom 8:29) — while the same passages distinguish callings inside the one name without dividing it, the Twelve's witness-place running on the men who accompanied Jesus (Acts 1:21–22, 25–26); and the NASB 2020 renders the plural "brothers and sisters" at Acts 1:15, Rom 8:29, Rom 16:14, and 1 Cor 12:1, while dissolving the family word itself at 1 Cor 7:14 and 9:5 and rendering ἄνδρες ἀδελφοί simply "Brothers" at Acts 1:16.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments): ἀδελφός carries five senses across 347 verse references — [10.49] a male having the same father (and usually mother), "brother," 84 references; [11.23] a close associate of a group with well-defined membership, in the NT specifically fellow believers in Christ, "fellow believer, (Christian) brother," 217 references; [11.25] a fellow countryman or associate, 24; [11.57] a fellow countryman, 6; [11.89] a neighbor, 16. ἀδελφή carries two senses across 26 references — [10.50] a female having the same father and mother, "sister," 21 references; [11.24] a close female associate of a group with well-defined membership, in the NT fellow believers in Christ, "fellow believer, sister in Christ," exactly 5 references: Rom 16:1; 1 Cor 7:15; 1 Cor 9:5; Phlm 1:2; Jas 2:15. This entry is drawn from the two believer senses under the selection rule stated in Honesty (1); the kinship senses enter only where a quoted verse itself carries them, distinguished there.
  - Acts 1:14 — the gathering is defined before it is named: οὗτοι πάντες ἦσαν προσκαρτεροῦντες ὁμοθυμαδὸν τῇ προσευχῇ σὺν γυναιξὶν καὶ Μαριὰμ τῇ μητρὶ τοῦ Ἰησοῦ καὶ τοῖς ἀδελφοῖς αὐτοῦ, "all these were continuing together in prayer, with the women and Mary the mother of Jesus and His brothers." The women stand in the company's own list; τοῖς ἀδελφοῖς αὐτοῦ is the Lord's literal brothers [10.49], inside the same list.
  - Acts 1:15 — two sentences on, the company is named: ἀναστὰς Πέτρος ἐν μέσῳ τῶν ἀδελφῶν εἶπεν· ἦν τε ὄχλος ὀνομάτων ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτὸ ὡσεὶ ἑκατὸν εἴκοσι, "Peter, standing in the midst of the brothers, said — and there was a crowd of names together of about a hundred and twenty." The ~120 [11.23] is the gathering 1:14 has just defined, women in it; the one plural covers it whole. The NASB renders "Peter stood up among the brothers and sisters."
  - Acts 1:16 — Peter's address to that company opens ἄνδρες ἀδελφοί, "Men, brothers." The NASB prints "Brothers," the ἄνδρες unrendered; the Greek address carries both words and the entry records both facts. The speech the address opens proceeds to the business of the men (1:21); the address's own scope is taken up at rival (a).
  - Acts 1:21–22, 25–26 — inside the same speech to the ἀδελφοί, the vacant witness-place is specified by criteria, and the criteria run on men: δεῖ οὖν τῶν συνελθόντων ἡμῖν ἀνδρῶν ἐν παντὶ χρόνῳ ᾧ εἰσῆλθεν καὶ ἐξῆλθεν ἐφʼ ἡμᾶς ὁ κύριος Ἰησοῦς, ἀρξάμενος ἀπὸ τοῦ βαπτίσματος Ἰωάννου ἕως τῆς ἡμέρας ἧς ἀνελήμφθη ἀφʼ ἡμῶν, μάρτυρα τῆς ἀναστάσεως αὐτοῦ σὺν ἡμῖν γενέσθαι ἕνα τούτων — "of the men who accompanied us all the time the Lord Jesus went in and out among us, beginning from the baptism of John until the day he was taken up from us, one of these must become a witness of his resurrection with us." The place is named τὸν τόπον τῆς διακονίας ταύτης καὶ ἀποστολῆς, "the place of this ministry and apostleship" (1:25), and Matthias συγκατεψηφίσθη μετὰ τῶν ἕνδεκα ἀποστόλων, "was numbered with the eleven apostles" (1:26). The differentiation is the passage's own: the whole company bears the one name while the Twelve's place is fenced by a stated, historical qualification — accompaniment and resurrection witness — not by a rank of worth.
  - Rom 8:29 — ὅτι οὓς προέγνω, καὶ προώρισεν συμμόρφους τῆς εἰκόνος τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ, εἰς τὸ εἶναι αὐτὸν πρωτότοκον ἐν πολλοῖς ἀδελφοῖς, "because those he foreknew he also foreordained conformed to the image of his Son, so that he is the firstborn among many brothers." The company named ἀδελφοῖς [11.23] is the whole foreknown company, conformed to the Son's image with no division stated in it, standing beside the Firstborn under the one family name. The NASB renders "the firstborn among many brothers and sisters."
  - Rom 16:1–2 — Συνίστημι δὲ ὑμῖν Φοίβην τὴν ἀδελφὴν ἡμῶν, οὖσαν διάκονον τῆς ἐκκλησίας τῆς ἐν Κεγχρεαῖς, "I commend to you Phoebe our sister [11.24], being a servant of the church at Cenchrea" — the ἡμῶν is the text's own: she is *our* sister, the company's. Her standing is stated in functions: προστάτις πολλῶν ἐγενήθη καὶ ἐμοῦ αὐτοῦ, "she has been a patron of many, and of myself as well." The NASB renders "our sister Phoebe, who is a servant of the church."
  - Rom 16:14–15 — τοὺς σὺν αὐτοῖς ἀδελφούς, "the brothers [11.23] with them," the company greeted with Asyncritus's group (NASB: "the brothers and sisters with them"); the next verse greets Φιλόλογον καὶ Ἰουλίαν, Νηρέα καὶ τὴν ἀδελφὴν αὐτοῦ, "Philologus and Julia, Nereus and his sister" — Julia named inside the greeted company, and Nereus's ἀδελφή filed by the dictionary under the kinship sense [10.50]. Romans 16 uses ἀδελφή in both its senses within two verses, and the filings keep them apart: the believer sense of 16:1 is no spillover of kinship.
  - Rom 16:7 — ἀσπάσασθε Ἀνδρόνικον καὶ Ἰουνίαν τοὺς συγγενεῖς μου καὶ συναιχμαλώτους μου, οἵτινές εἰσιν ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις, οἳ καὶ πρὸ ἐμοῦ γέγοναν ἐν Χριστῷ, "Greet Andronicus and Junia, my kinsfolk and my fellow prisoners, who are ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις, who were also in Christ before me." The phrase ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις runs "notable among the apostles" as the words stand; the NASB renders "outstanding in the view of the apostles." The dictionary files the ἐν of ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις under [83.9], "among, with" — its judgment, taken up at the proposed close. Which company the two are placed in by the phrase is a genuine fork — carried at the proposed close (marked) and rival (e), and nothing in the settled close rests on this verse.
  - 1 Cor 7:12–15 — the believing husband is named by the family word: εἴ τις ἀδελφὸς γυναῖκα ἔχει ἄπιστον, "if any brother [11.23] has an unbelieving wife"; his believing counterpart at 7:13 is γυνή, "a woman who has an unbelieving husband" — the text's own asymmetry of naming, recorded as it stands. At 7:14 ἡγίασται ἡ γυνὴ ἡ ἄπιστος ἐν τῷ ἀδελφῷ, "the unbelieving wife has been sanctified in the brother" — the family word, not a marriage word, carries the relation; the NASB dissolves it: "sanctified through her believing husband," supplying "believing" and replacing ἀδελφῷ with "husband." At 7:15 the case is stated for either sex in the pair: οὐ δεδούλωται ὁ ἀδελφὸς ἢ ἡ ἀδελφὴ ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις, "the brother or the sister is not bound in such cases." At this place, where the ruling must hold for a brother or a sister individually, Paul writes both forms.
  - 1 Cor 9:5 — μὴ οὐκ ἔχομεν ἐξουσίαν ἀδελφὴν γυναῖκα περιάγειν, "do we not have the right to take along a sister, a wife [11.24]" — the believing wife named by the family word; the NASB renders "a believing wife," the ἀδελφήν dissolved and "believing" supplied in its place. The same sentence names οἱ λοιποὶ ἀπόστολοι, "the rest of the apostles," καὶ οἱ ἀδελφοὶ τοῦ κυρίου, "and the brothers of the Lord" [10.49], καὶ Κηφᾶς — callings and both senses of the family word side by side in one verse, unconfused.
  - 1 Cor 12:1 — Περὶ δὲ τῶν πνευματικῶν, ἀδελφοί, οὐ θέλω ὑμᾶς ἀγνοεῖν, "Now concerning the spiritual things, brothers, I do not want you to be unaware." The vocative plural [11.23] stands at the head of the letter's section on the spiritual things (chs. 12–14) (NASB: "brothers and sisters"). A placement datum: the one name addresses the whole gathering where the gathering's differentiated life is about to be taught; the chapter's contents beyond this verse are frame, not receipts (Honesty (4)).
  - Phlm 1:1–2 — Τιμόθεος ὁ ἀδελφὸς [11.23], "Timothy the brother," stands in the sender line (the NASB supplies "our": "Timothy our brother"); Philemon is addressed τῷ ἀγαπητῷ καὶ συνεργῷ ἡμῶν, "the beloved and our fellow worker" — the NASB supplies the family word: "our beloved brother and fellow worker." Ἀπφίᾳ τῇ ἀδελφῇ [11.24], "to Apphia the sister," stands in the letter's own address line beside Archippus and the church in the house; the NASB renders "Apphia our sister," the "our" supplied — where Rom 16:1 carries ἡμῶν in the Greek, this verse does not.
  - Phlm 1:16 — Onesimus is to be received οὐκέτι ὡς δοῦλον ἀλλʼ ὑπὲρ δοῦλον, ἀδελφὸν ἀγαπητόν, μάλιστα ἐμοί, πόσῳ δὲ μᾶλλον σοὶ καὶ ἐν σαρκὶ καὶ ἐν κυρίῳ, "no longer as a slave but more than a slave, a beloved brother [11.23], most of all to me, and how much more to you, both in the flesh and in the Lord." The name is laid over the slave status and holds in the flesh as in the Lord.
  - Jas 2:15–16 — ἐὰν ἀδελφὸς ἢ ἀδελφὴ γυμνοὶ ὑπάρχωσιν καὶ λειπόμενοι ὦσιν τῆς ἐφημέρου τροφῆς, "if a brother or a sister [11.23 + 11.24] is naked and lacking the day's food" — the pair again, a brother or a sister named individually; and the test James sets is τὰ ἐπιτήδεια τοῦ σώματος, "the necessities of the body," given or withheld.
  - The frame (labeled, under Core Rule 8; the word-claims above rest on the core alone): Gal 3:28–29 — οὐκ ἔνι ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ, "there is no male and female," the third pair in creation's own words (Gen 1:27 — cited, not quoted in this set), and κατʼ ἐπαγγελίαν κληρονόμοι, "heirs according to promise"; 1 Cor 11:3 — κεφαλὴ δὲ γυναικὸς ὁ ἀνήρ, κεφαλὴ δὲ τοῦ Χριστοῦ ὁ θεός, with 11:11–12 — οὔτε γυνὴ χωρὶς ἀνδρὸς οὔτε ἀνὴρ χωρὶς γυναικὸς ἐν κυρίῳ, "neither woman apart from man nor man apart from woman in the Lord"; Eph 2:14–16 — τὸ μεσότοιχον τοῦ φραγμοῦ λύσας, "having broken down the middle wall of the partition," ἵνα τοὺς δύο κτίσῃ ἐν αὐτῷ εἰς ἕνα καινὸν ἄνθρωπον, "so that he might create the two in himself into one new man"; Rev 5:9 — ἠγόρασας τῷ θεῷ ἐν τῷ αἵματί σου ἐκ πάσης φυλῆς καὶ γλώσσης καὶ λαοῦ καὶ ἔθνους, "you bought for God by your blood out of every tribe and tongue and people and nation"; 1 Cor 6:17 — ὁ δὲ κολλώμενος τῷ κυρίῳ ἓν πνεῦμά ἐστιν, "the one joined to the Lord is one spirit" (carried also at Framework Core Rule 7). The frame states the sitting's conjunction — distinctions standing at their own texts, walls and tiers fallen — and the close draws on it only as labeled.
  - The NASB 2020 pattern across the set, stated as data: at the plural it renders "brothers and sisters," four of four in this set (Acts 1:15; Rom 8:29; Rom 16:14; 1 Cor 12:1) — of which Acts 1:15 and Rom 16:14 stand in contexts that name women inside the company (Acts 1:14; the greeting run of Rom 16:1–15), Rom 8:29's referent is the whole foreknown company, and 1 Cor 12:1 is a vocative to the whole assembly; it preserves the feminine forms as "sister" (Rom 16:1; 16:15; Phlm 1:2; the pairs at 1 Cor 7:15 and Jas 2:15); it dissolves the family word at 1 Cor 7:14 ("her believing husband") and 1 Cor 9:5 ("a believing wife"); it drops the Greek's ἄνδρες at Acts 1:16; it supplies the family word for Philemon ("our beloved brother," Phlm 1:1) and "our" for Timothy and Apphia (Phlm 1:1–2); and at Rom 16:7 it renders ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις "in the view of the apostles," the fork datum of the proposed close.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction of the set: the household's family name is one, and it covers the whole company at undiminished standing — women inside it on the texts' own showing, the company Luke defines with the women in it then named by the one word (Acts 1:14–15), named women bearing the feminine form as the company's own (Phoebe ἡ ἀδελφὴ ἡμῶν, servant of the church and patron of many; Apphia in the address line), the pair written where a brother or a sister is named individually (1 Cor 7:15; Jas 2:15), the slave re-named brother in the flesh and in the Lord, and the whole foreknown company standing as ἀδελφοί beside the Firstborn whose image they are conformed to. And the same receipts keep a distinction the name never erases: the Twelve's witness-place fenced by its stated historical criteria and refilled to its number (Acts 1:21–26), the Lord's literal brothers distinguished from the company inside a single paragraph (Acts 1:14–15) — callings are divided in these texts; the name and the standing are not, and the whole of it is the AND of both. On the translation question the set settles the narrower point: at Acts 1:15 and Rom 16:14, "brothers and sisters" spells out a company whose context names women inside it (Acts 1:14; the greeting run of Rom 16:1–15); at Rom 8:29 it renders the whole foreknown company the verse names, and at 1 Cor 12:1 the whole assembly the vocative addresses — at none of the four does the addition sort the household by flesh, and the charge that it does is not sustained; the diminishment of the family word that the receipts do show is its removal — dissolved at 1 Cor 7:14 and 9:5.
- **The proposed close (marked):** On Rom 16:7 the entry proposes, and marks as proposed: ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις places Andronicus and Junia among the apostles — "notable among the apostles" — on the phrase's ordinary run with a plural of persons, on the verse's own pattern, in which every other company the verse names is one the two are inside (my kinsfolk, my fellow prisoners, in Christ before me), and on the dictionary's filing at this phrase, which places the ἐν of ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις under [83.9], "among, with" — the dictionary's judgment, cited as such; no filing of that ἐν carries the NASB's "in the view of." A dictionary filing supports a proposed close without settling it, and the fork stands at rival (e). The distinction the verse would then carry is the one the settled close carries on Acts 1: the name ἀδελφοί is borne by all, the callings by some. The exclusive reading — "outstanding in the view of the apostles," the NASB's rendering — is stated at rival (e); the ἀπόστολος distribution that could close the fork is unbuilt (Honesty (4)), and the settled close above rests nothing on this verse.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) This set is a probative selection, not the distribution: from [11.23]'s 217 assignments and [11.24]'s 5, the core was drawn by a stated rule — every place in the two believer senses where the verse's own context demonstrably includes women in the name's referent set; where the masculine and feminine forms stand paired for a single referent (1 Cor 7:15; Jas 2:15); where a named woman bears the feminine believer sense; and where ἀδελφός stands in one clause with πρωτότοκος over the whole foreknown company (Rom 8:29) or in explicit contrast with δοῦλος (Phlm 1:16) — with the frame ruled in as labeled context. The unquoted remainder of [11.23] is unexamined here, and the claim is stated at the set's strength: at every place in this set, the usage is as the chain shows. (2) The stub's "exact same status of full, equal heirs," and its title: sameness of name and of family standing is what the set sustains; sameness of office and calling is not claimed — the set differentiates callings in the same breath (Acts 1:21–26). "Heirs" (κληρονόμοι) stands at Gal 3:29 in the labeled frame and at Rom 8:17, which is outside this set and unquoted; Rom 8:29 itself carries conformity and the Firstborn, not the heir word. The entry's settled content is the shared family name, and the title is narrowed to match: the stub's "Adelphoi (Brethren / Equal Heirs)" becomes "Adelphoi (Brethren / The Shared Family Name)," the old title recorded here under the title rule. (3) The stub's translation verdict — that adding "and sisters" reinstates the fleshly distinctions the Spirit has permanently erased and diminishes the shared inheritance — is not carried in that form: on this set the addition, at the places itemized in the close, names the referent each passage itself carries, and the distinctions are not erased at their own texts (the frame's 1 Cor 11:3 stands); what the frame's texts erase is the distinction as wall and as inheritance tier (Eph 2:14–16; Gal 3:28–29, labeled frame). The diminishment charge is sustained only as re-aimed in the close, at the two renderings that remove the family word. (4) The sitting's deliberation — the body of many distinct members (1 Cor 12 beyond its first verse), the wider sent ones beyond this set's two passages (Paul, Barnabas, Epaphroditus, the churches' messengers), and Babel's walls read against Acts 2 (Acts 2:1–26 was pasted with this batch, Joel's sons and daughters at 2:17–18 included) — is recorded in the conversion groundwork as the sitting's hearing; it is not receipts of this entry, and the ἀπόστολος distribution (two senses, 80 references) remains unbuilt. (5) Acts 1:15's ἀδελφῶν carries a marker in the NA28 apparatus; the entry quotes the printed text. Luke 22:32's ἀδελφούς, whose referent set is the Eleven, is outside this set by the selection rule and is already quoted in a converted entry. (6) At Rom 16:7 the dictionary files the name as the masculine Ἰουνιᾶς [93.178], glossed "Junias," "a Jewish Christian greeted" at this verse. The printed text quoted in the chain reads Ἰουνίαν, the feminine name Junia, and the entry follows the printed text throughout, recording the dictionary's contrary filing here as its judgment. Nothing in the settled close rests on the verse (see the proposed close, marked), but the disagreement is the verse's own datum and is not passed over in silence.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The males-only reading — ἀδελφοί names the men of the company, and women are covered, if at all, by courtesy of the masculine form. Answered: the courtesy theory cannot carry Acts 1, where Luke defines the company with the women in it (1:14) and then names that company ἀδελφῶν (1:15); nor the writers' own pair-forms, writing ἀδελφὸς ἢ ἀδελφὴ for a brother or a sister individually (1 Cor 7:15; Jas 2:15) — the plural's coverage is the word's ordinary use, and named women bear the feminine believer sense in their own right (Rom 16:1; Phlm 1:2). The dictionary's own comment at this filing states the coverage as its judgment: "The masculine form ἀδελφός may include both men and women" (at [11.23]). The rival's strongest single text is Acts 1:16 itself, where Peter opens ἄνδρες ἀδελφοί, "Men, brothers," to the same room, in the speech whose business then runs on the men (1:21). The entry answers it only so far: the address form does not rename the company 1:15 has just named — the referent is fixed by Luke's own narrative sequence, not by the vocative — and an address to the men for the act at hand no more restricts 1:15's ἀδελφῶν than 1:21's criteria do; but the vocative's own scope (the whole company under a formal address, or the men of the company for this business) is not decided by anything in this set, and the entry claims nothing from 1:16 beyond the rendering datum in the chain.
(b) The erasure reading — in Christ the created distinctions themselves are abolished, so differentiation of any kind (headship, office, the masculine form of the name) is fleshly residue to be outgrown. Answered: the texts in the set and its frame tear down walls, not distinctions — Acts 1 specifies ἀνδρῶν for the witness-place in the speech that addresses all as ἀδελφοί; the frame's headship (1 Cor 11:3) stands beside its mutuality (11:11–12); and the redeemed are bought *out of* every tribe and tongue and people and nation, their origins named in the song itself (Rev 5:9, frame).
(c) The stub's own charge — "brothers and sisters" in translation reinstates the fleshly distinctions and diminishes the radical, shared inheritance. Answered in the close's narrower settlement: at the places itemized there, the addition names the referent each passage itself carries (Acts 1:14 stands immediately before 1:15's "brothers and sisters"), so the sorting charge fails at those places; the diminishment the set does prove is located at the two renderings that remove the word — 1 Cor 7:14 and 1 Cor 9:5 — and the rival stands answered only there.
(d) The two-tier reading — the name is common to all, but the household is stratified into classes of belonging, the Twelve's circle above the rest, men above women as classes. Answered: the set's differentiations are of calling and of historical qualification — Acts 1:21–22's criteria are accompaniment and resurrection witness, facts of history, not degrees of worth — while the standing the name carries is undivided: the slave is brother ἐν σαρκί as ἐν κυρίῳ (Phlm 1:16), and the whole foreknown company shares the one conformity to the Son's image (Rom 8:29).
(e) The Junia-outside reading — ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις commends Andronicus and Junia to the apostles' high regard without placing them among the apostles. The NASB renders it "outstanding in the view of the apostles." Stated, and left standing as a fork: the phrase with ἐν and a plural of persons ordinarily states notability within the group named, and the verse's own company-phrases are all inclusive — the grounds of the proposed close (marked) — but the exclusive reading is genuinely held on the same phrase, the ἀπόστολος distribution is unbuilt, and this entry does not settle the fork. What the fork does not touch: the differentiation of calling from name, which the settled close carries on Acts 1 alone.
(f) The kinship-spillover reading — because the word also names literal siblings, its community uses are kinship extended by courtesy and carry kinship's boundaries with them. Answered: the dictionary files the senses separately, and the texts keep them separate inside single passages — His brothers [10.49] and the company [11.23] in one Acts paragraph; Phoebe [11.24] and Nereus's sister [10.50] in one greeting run; a sister-wife [11.24] and the Lord's brothers [10.49] in one verse — the believer sense has its own referents and its own force, not a borrowed one.

### Ekalesen (The Calling)

- **ID:** KB055
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** καλέω, κλῆσις, κλητός
- **Verse keys:** Matt 4:21; Mark 1:20; Rom 1:1; 1:6; 1:7; 4:17; 8:28; 8:30; 9:12; 9:24; 11:29; 1 Cor 1:1; 1:2; 1:9; 1:24; 1:26; 7:15; 7:17–24; Gal 1:6; 1:15; 5:8; 5:13; Eph 1:18; 4:1; 4:4; Phil 3:14; Col 3:15; 1 Thess 2:12; 4:7; 5:24; 2 Thess 1:11; 2:14; 1 Tim 6:12; 2 Tim 1:9; Heb 3:1; 5:4; 11:8; 1 Pet 1:15; 2:21; 3:9; 2 Pet 1:3; 1:10; Jude 1:1; Rev 17:14
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** One word family carries the New Testament's calling — καλέω in the dictionary's sense of an urgent invitation to accept responsibilities for a task, implying a new relationship to the one who calls [33.312], its noun κλῆσις under the same number and as the state of having been called [33.313], and its adjective κλητός, "called" [33.314] — and across all fifty-six places the caller is God, or Christ in person, and the call names both work and destinations: fishermen leaving their nets to follow (Matt 4:21; Mark 1:20), Abraham obeying by going out when called (Heb 11:8), the foreordained called and justified in one unbroken chain (Rom 8:30), believers called into fellowship with his Son (1 Cor 1:9), into his own kingdom and glory (1 Thess 2:12), to freedom (Gal 5:13), in peace (1 Cor 7:15), not for impurity but in sanctification (1 Thess 4:7), to follow a suffering Christ (1 Pet 2:21), to inherit a blessing (1 Pet 3:9), not according to works but according to his own purpose and grace (2 Tim 1:9) — while the called are named κλητοί, saints and an apostle and those who stand with the Lamb (Rom 1:7; 1 Cor 1:1; Rev 17:14), the Caller is named faithful, who also will do it (1 Thess 5:24), and the same set commands the called to remain in the station of their calling (1 Cor 7:17–24), records the called deserting the one who called them (Gal 1:6), and charges them to walk worthy of the calling and to make their calling and election sure (Eph 4:1; 2 Pet 1:10).
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments): καλέω carries five senses across 150 references; the calling family is one numbered sense, [33.312] "to urgently invite someone to accept responsibilities for a particular task, implying a new relationship to the one who does the calling," 36 references on 33 verses. κλῆσις is filed under the same number [33.312] at 6 places and as "the state of having been called to a particular task and/or relation" [33.313] at 5; κλητός is "pertaining to having been called" [33.314] at 9. This entry is drawn from those four filings complete — 56 places on 49 verses — under the scope ruling recorded in Honesty (1); 1 Cor 7:20's κλῆσις, filed apart as "station in life" [87.2], rides inside its passage as a labeled boundary datum and is no part of the 56. One quoted verse carries a second filing besides: 1 Cor 1:9's ἐκλήθητε is double-filed by the dictionary, under [33.315], its hospitality-invitation sense, as well as the family's [33.312] — labeled at its bullet and in Honesty (1).
  - Matt 4:21; Mark 1:20 — the call in person: καὶ ἐκάλεσεν αὐτούς, "and He called them" — James and John in the boat with their father, mending nets; Mark adds καὶ εὐθὺς ἐκάλεσεν αὐτούς, "and immediately He called them," and the response: they left Zebedee in the boat and ἀπῆλθον ὀπίσω αὐτοῦ, went away after him. The family's first call is to follow a person, and it is answered by leaving a livelihood, not by a change of legal address.
  - Heb 11:8; 5:4 — καλούμενος Ἀβραὰμ ὑπήκουσεν ἐξελθεῖν, "Abraham, when he was called, obeyed by going out" to the place he was to receive as inheritance, not knowing where he was going — the set's one literal going-out, and it is Abraham's; and the priestly honor: οὐχ ἑαυτῷ τις λαμβάνει τὴν τιμήν ἀλλὰ καλούμενος ὑπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ καθώσπερ καὶ Ἀαρών, "no one takes the honor for himself, but when called by God, just as Aaron also was" — the call as the only door to the office.
  - Rom 8:28, 30 — τοῖς κατὰ πρόθεσιν κλητοῖς οὖσιν, "to those who are the called according to purpose," and the chain the stub's own word stands in: οὓς δὲ προώρισεν, τούτους καὶ ἐκάλεσεν· καὶ οὓς ἐκάλεσεν, τούτους καὶ ἐδικαίωσεν, "those he foreordained, these he also called; and those he called, these he also justified" — the calling here is one link in God's own sequence, with no gap stated between its links.
  - Rom 9:12, 24 — οὐκ ἐξ ἔργων ἀλλʼ ἐκ τοῦ καλοῦντος, "not from works but from the one who calls," said to Rebekah before the twins had done good or bad; and οὓς καὶ ἐκάλεσεν ἡμᾶς οὐ μόνον ἐξ Ἰουδαίων ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐξ ἐθνῶν, "us, whom he also called, not only from among Jews but also from among Gentiles" — the "from" of the call is from-among both peoples in parallel, not out of one corrupted domain.
  - Rom 4:17 — θεοῦ τοῦ ζῳοποιοῦντος τοὺς νεκροὺς καὶ καλοῦντος τὰ μὴ ὄντα ὡς ὄντα, "God, who makes the dead live and calls the things that are not as being" — the Caller named at his most creative, in the verse of Abraham made father of many nations.
  - Rom 11:29 — ἀμεταμέλητα γὰρ τὰ χαρίσματα καὶ ἡ κλῆσις τοῦ θεοῦ, "for the gifts and the calling of God are irrevocable" — the family's irrevocability sentence, standing in Romans 11's argument over Israel, on the ground of 11:28's "beloved for the fathers' sake"; its placement is part of its meaning and is handled at Honesty (3).
  - Rom 1:1, 6, 7; 1 Cor 1:1, 2 — κλητός as a name borne: κλητὸς ἀπόστολος, "called apostle" (Paul, διὰ θελήματος θεοῦ, by God's will); the Romans ἐν οἷς ἐστε καὶ ὑμεῖς κλητοὶ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "among whom you also are the called of Jesus Christ," addressed as κλητοῖς ἁγίοις, "called saints"; Corinth's church ἡγιασμένοις ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ, κλητοῖς ἁγίοις — sanctified in Christ Jesus, called saints. The adjective names persons and companies, not an event only.
  - 1 Cor 1:9 — πιστὸς ὁ θεός, διʼ οὗ ἐκλήθητε εἰς κοινωνίαν τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "God is faithful, through whom you were called into fellowship with his Son, Jesus Christ" — the call's named destination is fellowship with a person, and its ground is stated first: the Caller is faithful. The dictionary double-files this ἐκλήθητε: the same occurrence stands under [33.315], "to invite" to hospitality, as well as the family's [33.312]; the dual filing is recorded here and in Honesty (1), and nothing in the entry turns on choosing between the two — the verse's destination (εἰς κοινωνίαν) is stated either way.
  - 1 Cor 1:24, 26 — αὐτοῖς δὲ τοῖς κλητοῖς, Ἰουδαίοις τε καὶ Ἕλλησιν, Χριστὸν θεοῦ δύναμιν καὶ θεοῦ σοφίαν, "to those who are the called, both Jews and Greeks, Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God" — one name over both peoples; and βλέπετε γὰρ τὴν κλῆσιν ὑμῶν, ἀδελφοί, "consider your calling, brothers" — οὐ πολλοὶ σοφοὶ κατὰ σάρκα, "not many wise by fleshly measure": the calling's human material is stated without flattery.
  - 1 Cor 7:15 — ἐν δὲ εἰρήνῃ κέκληκεν ὑμᾶς ὁ θεός, "but God has called you in peace" (perfect: has called, with the state standing) — stated as the rule inside a mixed marriage's hard case: the calling's atmosphere is peace, and it does not dissolve the marriage.
  - 1 Cor 7:17–24 — the family's densest passage, quoted whole because the boundary runs through it: ἕκαστον ὡς κέκληκεν ὁ θεός, οὕτως περιπατείτω, "as God has called each, so let him walk" (7:17); περιτετμημένος τις ἐκλήθη, μὴ ἐπισπάσθω· ἐν ἀκροβυστίᾳ κέκληταί τις, μὴ περιτεμνέσθω, "was anyone called circumcised? Let him not undo it; has anyone been called in uncircumcision? Let him not be circumcised" (7:18 — the verse's two callings, both counted in the 36); ἕκαστος ἐν τῇ κλήσει ᾗ ἐκλήθη, ἐν ταύτῃ μενέτω, "each one, in the calling in which he was called, in this let him remain" (7:20) — where the noun κλήσει in this sentence is the one the dictionary files apart as "station in life" [87.2] while the verb ἐκλήθη in the same clause is the family's [33.312]: the boundary datum, labeled as ruled; δοῦλος ἐκλήθης, μή σοι μελέτω, "were you called a slave? Let it not concern you" (7:21); ὁ ἐν κυρίῳ κληθεὶς δοῦλος ἀπελεύθερος κυρίου ἐστίν, ὁμοίως ὁ ἐλεύθερος κληθεὶς δοῦλός ἐστιν Χριστοῦ, "the slave called in the Lord is the Lord's freedperson; likewise the free man called is Christ's slave" (7:22); ἕκαστος ἐν ᾧ ἐκλήθη, ἀδελφοί, ἐν τούτῳ μενέτω παρὰ θεῷ, "each one, in the state in which he was called, brothers, in this let him remain with God" (7:24). Three times the passage commands remaining (μενέτω); the call re-names the slave and the free in place; it does not extract either from his station.
  - Gal 1:6, 15; 5:8 — μετατίθεσθε ἀπὸ τοῦ καλέσαντος ὑμᾶς ἐν χάριτι [Χριστοῦ], "you are deserting the one who called you in the grace of [Christ]" (the Χριστοῦ stands bracketed in the printed text) — the called in the act of leaving the Caller, stated as present fact; and ἡ πεισμονὴ οὐκ ἐκ τοῦ καλοῦντος ὑμᾶς, "this persuasion is not from the one who calls you"; Paul's own: ὁ ἀφορίσας με ἐκ κοιλίας μητρός μου καὶ καλέσας διὰ τῆς χάριτος αὐτοῦ, "the one who set me apart from my mother's womb and called me through his grace."
  - Gal 5:13 — ὑμεῖς γὰρ ἐπʼ ἐλευθερίᾳ ἐκλήθητε, ἀδελφοί, "you were called to freedom, brothers" — with its fence in the same breath: μόνον μὴ τὴν ἐλευθερίαν εἰς ἀφορμὴν τῇ σαρκί, "only not the freedom as an opportunity for the flesh," but service through love.
  - Eph 1:18; 4:1, 4 — τίς ἐστιν ἡ ἐλπὶς τῆς κλήσεως αὐτοῦ, "what is the hope of his calling" — the calling has a hope to be known; ἀξίως περιπατῆσαι τῆς κλήσεως ἧς ἐκλήθητε, "to walk worthily of the calling with which you were called" — the calling measures the walk, and the walk is exhorted, not assumed; καθὼς καὶ ἐκλήθητε ἐν μιᾷ ἐλπίδι τῆς κλήσεως ὑμῶν, "just as you were also called in one hope of your calling" — one hope, as one body and one Spirit (4:4's own frame).
  - Phil 3:14 — κατὰ σκοπὸν διώκω εἰς τὸ βραβεῖον τῆς ἄνω κλήσεως τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ, "I press on toward the goal for the prize of the upward calling of God in Christ Jesus" — the calling of God has a prize still ahead of the pressing man who names it.
  - Col 3:15 — ἡ εἰρήνη τοῦ Χριστοῦ βραβευέτω ἐν ταῖς καρδίαις ὑμῶν, εἰς ἣν καὶ ἐκλήθητε ἐν ἑνὶ σώματι, "let the peace of Christ rule in your hearts, to which you were indeed called in one body" — the destination locates the called together, in one body.
  - 1 Thess 2:12; 4:7; 5:24 — τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ καλοῦντος ὑμᾶς εἰς τὴν ἑαυτοῦ βασιλείαν καὶ δόξαν, "the God who calls you into his own kingdom and glory" — the set's clearest "into," and its frame is an exhortation to walk worthily of that God; οὐ γὰρ ἐκάλεσεν ἡμᾶς ὁ θεὸς ἐπὶ ἀκαθαρσίᾳ ἀλλʼ ἐν ἁγιασμῷ, "God has not called us for impurity but in sanctification" — the call has a moral direction, stated as a "not this, but this"; πιστὸς ὁ καλῶν ὑμᾶς, ὃς καὶ ποιήσει, "faithful is the one who calls you, who also will do it" — the family's security sentence, and its subject is the Caller's faithfulness, not the called's grip.
  - 2 Thess 1:11; 2:14 — ἵνα ὑμᾶς ἀξιώσῃ τῆς κλήσεως ὁ θεὸς ἡμῶν, "that our God consider you worthy of the calling" — prayed for, continually, by an apostle for the called; εἰς ὃ ἐκάλεσεν ὑμᾶς διὰ τοῦ εὐαγγελίου ἡμῶν εἰς περιποίησιν δόξης τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, "for this he called you through our gospel, to obtain the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ" — the call arrives mediated, through a preached gospel, and its destination is the Lord's glory obtained.
  - 1 Tim 6:12; 2 Tim 1:9 — ἐπιλαβοῦ τῆς αἰωνίου ζωῆς, εἰς ἣν ἐκλήθης, "take hold of the eternal life, to which you were called" — the destination is eternal life, and the called man is told to take hold of it; τοῦ σώσαντος ἡμᾶς καὶ καλέσαντος κλήσει ἁγίᾳ, οὐ κατὰ τὰ ἔργα ἡμῶν ἀλλὰ κατὰ ἰδίαν πρόθεσιν καὶ χάριν, "who saved us and called us with a holy calling, not according to our works but according to his own purpose and grace" — verb and noun together, and the call's basis expressly not works.
  - Heb 3:1 — ἀδελφοὶ ἅγιοι, κλήσεως ἐπουρανίου μέτοχοι, "holy brothers, partakers of a heavenly calling" — the calling is a thing shared (μέτοχοι), and the dictionary files this verse's noun under both [33.312] and [33.313].
  - 1 Pet 1:15; 2:21; 3:9 — κατὰ τὸν καλέσαντα ὑμᾶς ἅγιον καὶ αὐτοὶ ἅγιοι ἐν πάσῃ ἀναστροφῇ γενήθητε, "like the Holy One who called you, be holy yourselves in all conduct" — the Caller's character is the called's pattern; εἰς τοῦτο γὰρ ἐκλήθητε, ὅτι καὶ Χριστὸς ἔπαθεν ὑπὲρ ὑμῶν, "for to this you were called, because Christ also suffered for you" — called to a suffering followed in his steps; εἰς τοῦτο ἐκλήθητε, ἵνα εὐλογίαν κληρονομήσητε, "to this you were called, that you might inherit a blessing" — called to bless insulters and so inherit.
  - 2 Pet 1:3, 10 — τοῦ καλέσαντος ἡμᾶς ἰδίᾳ δόξῃ καὶ ἀρετῇ, "the one who called us by his own glory and excellence," through whose knowledge all things for life and godliness are granted; and σπουδάσατε βεβαίαν ὑμῶν τὴν κλῆσιν καὶ ἐκλογὴν ποιεῖσθαι, "be diligent to make your calling and election sure" — an imperative spoken to the called about their own calling, with its promise: ταῦτα γὰρ ποιοῦντες οὐ μὴ πταίσητέ ποτε, "doing these things you will never stumble." The dictionary files this verse's βεβαίαν under [71.15], "certain, sure — on the basis of being well established," so the NASB's rendering, "to make certain about His calling and choice of you," follows the dictionary for the adjective; what the rendering does move is the possessive — ὑμῶν τὴν κλῆσιν καὶ ἐκλογήν, "your calling and election," becomes "His calling and choice of you." The entry's datum is the imperative itself: the called are told, with diligence, to make their own calling and election certain — an activity commanded of them, not a state pronounced over them.
  - Jude 1:1; Rev 17:14 — τοῖς ἐν θεῷ πατρὶ ἠγαπημένοις καὶ Ἰησοῦ Χριστῷ τετηρημένοις κλητοῖς, "to the called, beloved in God the Father and kept for Jesus Christ" — called, beloved, and kept stand as three names of the same people; οἱ μετʼ αὐτοῦ κλητοὶ καὶ ἐκλεκτοὶ καὶ πιστοί, "those with him are called and chosen and faithful" — the called stand with the Lamb who is κύριος κυρίων καὶ βασιλεὺς βασιλέων, Lord of lords and King of kings, and their third name is faithful.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction of the set: the calling is God's — and in person, Christ's — urgent invitation into a new relationship with its work, and it is neither small nor vague: it runs in God's unbroken chain (Rom 8:30), rests on no works (Rom 9:12; 2 Tim 1:9), and names real destinations — fellowship with his Son, his own kingdom and glory, freedom, peace, sanctification, eternal life, a blessing inherited, glory obtained, one body, one hope. And the same set fences every inflation of it: the call does not relocate the called out of their earthly stations — its densest passage commands slave and free, circumcised and uncircumcised, to remain and be re-named in place (1 Cor 7:17–24); it does not operate as a decree that cannot be left — the called are found deserting the Caller (Gal 1:6) and are charged to walk worthy of it, to make it sure, to press toward its prize, and are prayed worthy of it (Eph 4:1; 2 Pet 1:10; Phil 3:14; 2 Thess 1:11). The security the set actually states stands where the texts put it: on the Caller — God is faithful, through whom you were called (1 Cor 1:9); faithful is the one who calls you, who also will do it (1 Thess 5:24); the called are beloved and kept (Jude 1:1); and his gifts and his calling are irrevocable in the argument where that sentence lives (Rom 11:29, Honesty (3)). Feelings are a topic no text in the family raises.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) This set is the four filings complete — καλέω [33.312] (36 references), κλῆσις [33.312] (6), κλῆσις [33.313] (5), κλητός [33.314] (9) — 56 places on 49 verses, all quoted. Outside it, by the sitting's scope ruling: καλέω's remaining senses (naming by proper name [33.129], calling by attribution [33.131], summoning to come [33.307], inviting to hospitality [33.315]) and κλητός [33.318] "invited" (1 place), unexamined here — with one labeled exception inside the set: 1 Cor 1:9's ἐκλήθητε, double-filed by the dictionary under [33.315] as well as the family's [33.312], recorded at that verse in the chain; the claim is stated at the family's strength. (2) The stub's title, "Ekalesen (The Royal Summons)," is narrowed to "Ekalesen (The Calling)," the old title recorded here under the title rule: the filing's sense is an urgent invitation, not a summons, and "royal" rests on the call's destinations and company — his own kingdom (1 Thess 2:12), the King of kings beside the called (Rev 17:14) — not on the call's mode, which the set shows can be deserted (Gal 1:6). (3) Rom 11:29's ἀμεταμέλητα — "the gifts and the calling of God are irrevocable" — stands in Romans 11's argument over Israel, immediately after "beloved for the fathers' sake" (11:28); the entry states the sentence where the text states it and does not transfer it, by silence, into a general doctrine of an individual believer's security. What it settles is that God does not repent of his callings. (4) 1 Cor 7:20's κλῆσις [87.2] is a labeled boundary datum under the scope ruling — outside the 56 and outside the Claim; the NASB at 7:20 renders ἐν τῇ κλήσει as "in that state," following the station filing — recorded as a datum in the chain. (5) The stub's "providing absolute security independent of our fluctuating internal feelings" is retired as stated, for the reasons at rival (c): the family's assurance rests on the Caller's faithfulness and keeping, is everywhere paired with exhortation to the called, and feelings are nowhere its topic.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The royal-decree reading — the calling is a king's authoritative summons, effectual by decree, which the summoned cannot finally refuse or lose. Answered: the dictionary's own filing for the family is an urgent invitation to a task, implying a new relationship, not a decree; the set's grammar is response — the fishermen follow (Mark 1:20), Abraham obeys (Heb 11:8), and the Galatians are in the act of deserting the one who called them (Gal 1:6), a fact the decree reading cannot state; and the called are commanded to make their calling sure (2 Pet 1:10), an imperative with no object under the decree. The call's effectuality in Rom 8:30's chain is God's sequence of his own acts, which the entry keeps whole; it does not convert the family's invitation into a mechanism that overrides the family's own desertion text.
(b) The relocation reading — the calling moves the believer out of a corrupted domain and its stations into a separated standing, so that earthly station is left behind. Answered: the family's densest passage commands the opposite movement — remain, three times, in the station of one's calling (1 Cor 7:17–24), for the slave and the circumcised expressly, while the standing is re-named in place (the Lord's freedperson; Christ's slave); and the call's "from" is from-among Jews and from-among Gentiles alike (Rom 9:24), not extraction from one domain. The destinations are real and are kept in the close — fellowship, kingdom, glory, life — but no text in the set makes the call a change of earthly address; Abraham's going-out stands alone in the set — one man's literal departure, which does not relocate the whole company (Heb 11:8).
(c) The stub's own security reading — the calling provides absolute security independent of the believer's fluctuating internal feelings. Answered in the close's narrower settlement: the assurance the texts state is grounded in the Caller — his faithfulness (1 Cor 1:9; 1 Thess 5:24) and his keeping (Jude 1:1) — and is paired in the same texts with the called's own diligence (Eph 4:1; 2 Pet 1:10; 2 Thess 1:11); "independent of feelings" answers a question no text in the family asks; and the family's one certainty text is an imperative — the called are to make their calling and election certain, with diligence (2 Pet 1:10; βεβαίαν filed "certain, on the basis of being well established" [71.15]) — certainty there is something the called do toward their calling, not a feeling the calling guarantees. The rival stands answered only there.
(d) The station-only reading — κλῆσις and the calling are, at bottom, one's station in life, as 1 Cor 7:20's filing [87.2] suggests, so the calling family is a doctrine of contentment in one's lot. Answered: the dictionary itself files 7:20's noun apart from the family, and files the same clause's verb inside it — the boundary is the dictionary's own; and the family's other 55 places carry what no station reading can: a Caller who calls into fellowship with his Son and his own kingdom (1 Cor 1:9; 1 Thess 2:12), a chain linking the call to justification (Rom 8:30), and a prize of an upward calling (Phil 3:14). Contentment in one's station is 1 Cor 7's own teaching at its own place; generalized over the family it erases the set.
(e) The ministry-only reading — calling language belongs to offices and tasks: apostles, priests, preachers. Answered: the tasks are real in the set — the fishermen's following (Matt 4:21), Aaron's priesthood (Heb 5:4), Paul's apostleship (Rom 1:1; Gal 1:15) — but the family's center is the whole company's calling: the Romans and Corinthians are κλητοί as saints (Rom 1:7; 1 Cor 1:2), the called are Jews and Greeks together (1 Cor 1:24), the exhortations run to whole churches (Eph 4:1; 2 Pet 1:10), and the called stand with the Lamb as a company (Rev 17:14). The office places are the family's edge, not its whole.

### Theodidaktoi (God-Taught)

- **ID:** KB056
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** θεοδίδακτος, διδακτός, παιδεύω
- **Verse keys:** Matt 16:15–17; John 3:3–8; 6:44; 6:45; 6:65; 16:13–15; Rom 1:18–21; 10:17; 1 Cor 2:13; 1 Thess 4:9; Heb 12:6
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** The Father teaches His own — "taught by God" stands once in the New Testament, said of a people who need no letter about loving one another because the teaching's content and its fruit are one act, taught about loving one another and taught so that they do love one another (1 Thess 4:9) — as the prophets' promise had it, "they shall all be taught of God," which Jesus' own next sentence glosses: everyone who has heard and learned from the Father comes to the Son (John 6:45) — and the taught words are known by their teacher, the Spirit's set against human wisdom's (1 Cor 2:13).
- **The chain:**
