  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments): θεοδίδακτος carries ONE sense, [33.228] "pertaining to being taught by God," with exactly ONE reference, 1 Thess 4:9 — the word stands once in the New Testament. διδακτός, shelved immediately beside it at [33.227] "pertaining to that which is taught," carries three references: John 6:45, and 1 Cor 2:13 twice. The anchors below are those two filings complete — three verses. One of the anchor verse's own small words is filed two ways at the same occurrence: the εἰς of 1 Thess 4:9 (εἰς τὸ ἀγαπᾶν ἀλλήλους) stands under [90.23], a marker of content ("concerning, about"), AND under [89.48], a marker of result ("with the result that, so that") — both filings recorded at that verse's bullet, and ruled on in the close.
  - 1 Thess 4:9 — Περὶ δὲ τῆς φιλαδελφίας οὐ χρείαν ἔχετε γράφειν ὑμῖν, αὐτοὶ γὰρ ὑμεῖς θεοδίδακτοί ἐστε εἰς τὸ ἀγαπᾶν ἀλλήλους, "Now as to the love of the brothers and sisters, you have no need for anyone to write to you, for you yourselves are taught by God to love one another" — the word's one standing place. The "no need" is narrow and stated: no need for anyone to WRITE to them, on this one topic, and the reason given is not a principle against instruction but a fact about them — they are taught by God. The εἰς-phrase that follows is the dictionary's own double filing ([90.23] content; [89.48] result): taught by God ABOUT loving one another, and taught by God SO THAT they love one another. The NASB's plain infinitive, "taught by God to love one another," decides neither filing in English.
  - John 6:45 — ἔστιν γεγραμμένον ἐν τοῖς προφήταις· καὶ ἔσονται πάντες διδακτοὶ θεοῦ· πᾶς ὁ ἀκούσας παρὰ τοῦ πατρὸς καὶ μαθὼν ἔρχεται πρὸς ἐμέ, "It is written in the Prophets: 'AND THEY SHALL ALL BE TAUGHT OF GOD.' Everyone who has heard and learned from the Father, comes to Me." The verse carries the prophets' promise (Isa 54:13 as quoted) and then glosses itself: the teaching is hearing and learning παρὰ τοῦ πατρός, FROM the Father, and its named outcome is not a location or a mechanism but a direction — the taught one COMES TO the Son. The teacher is named (the Father), the destination is named (the Son); where the teaching happens is not named.
  - 1 Cor 2:13 — ἃ καὶ λαλοῦμεν οὐκ ἐν διδακτοῖς ἀνθρωπίνης σοφίας λόγοις ἀλλʼ ἐν διδακτοῖς πνεύματος, πνευματικοῖς πνευματικὰ συγκρίνοντες, "We also speak these things, not in words taught by human wisdom, but in those taught by the Spirit, combining spiritual thoughts with spiritual words." The family's second word stands twice in one verse, once for each teacher: διδακτοῖς of human wisdom, διδακτοῖς of the Spirit. Taught words are told apart by WHO taught them — and the verse's subject is speaking (λαλοῦμεν): the teaching shows up as words spoken.
  - The hearing register — the proposer's gathered texts, banked as hearing (Scripture speaking about Scripture) under the scope and form rulings recorded in Honesty (1). Each is labeled with only what it says at its own place; they are printed in no sequence and numbered as no steps; the close does not rest on them, and no one of them is spent as proof of the Claim. Read across its texts, the register shows one shape at each text's own place — the Father revealing, drawing, granting; the Spirit the birth's source and the guide who discloses; faith arriving by hearing — and at its edge, the same God made Himself known where the knowing was refused, and the outcome was darkness (Rom 1:18–21).
  - Matt 16:15–17 — ὑμεῖς δὲ τίνα με λέγετε εἶναι; … σὺ εἶ ὁ χριστὸς ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος … μακάριος εἶ, Σίμων Βαριωνᾶ, ὅτι σὰρξ καὶ αἷμα οὐκ ἀπεκάλυψέν σοι ἀλλʼ ὁ πατήρ μου ὁ ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς, "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God" … "Blessed are you, Simon Barjona, because flesh and blood did not reveal this to you, but My Father who is in heaven." The confession's source is named by exclusion and by gift: not flesh and blood; the Father revealed it.
  - John 3:3–8 — ἐὰν μή τις γεννηθῇ ἄνωθεν, οὐ δύναται ἰδεῖν τὴν βασιλείαν τοῦ θεοῦ … πῶς δύναται ἄνθρωπος γεννηθῆναι γέρων ὤν; … ἐὰν μή τις γεννηθῇ ἐξ ὕδατος καὶ πνεύματος, οὐ δύναται εἰσελθεῖν εἰς τὴν βασιλείαν τοῦ θεοῦ. τὸ γεγεννημένον ἐκ τῆς σαρκὸς σάρξ ἐστιν, καὶ τὸ γεγεννημένον ἐκ τοῦ πνεύματος πνεῦμά ἐστιν. μὴ θαυμάσῃς ὅτι εἶπόν σοι· δεῖ ὑμᾶς γεννηθῆναι ἄνωθεν. τὸ πνεῦμα ὅπου θέλει πνεῖ … οὕτως ἐστὶν πᾶς ὁ γεγεννημένος ἐκ τοῦ πνεύματος, "unless someone is born again [ἄνωθεν: from above], he cannot see the kingdom of God" … "How can a person be born when he is old?" … "unless someone is born of water and the Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God. That which has been born of the flesh is flesh, and that which has been born of the Spirit is spirit. Do not be amazed that I said to you, 'You must be born again.' The wind blows where it wishes … so is everyone who has been born of the Spirit." A birth flesh cannot perform: each birth produces its own kind, and this one is from above, of the Spirit, unmastered like the wind. The dictionary files ἄνωθεν at this verse (and at 3:7) under BOTH [84.13] "from above" and [67.55] "again" (the phrase also [41.53], "new birth"), and the passage's own drama runs on the two hearings: Nicodemus answers with the womb (v.4), Jesus answers with water and the Spirit (v.5) — the two filings do not stand together here as a coin; ruled, with grounds, in Honesty (6).
  - John 6:44 — οὐδεὶς δύναται ἐλθεῖν πρός με ἐὰν μὴ ὁ πατὴρ ὁ πέμψας με ἑλκύσῃ αὐτόν, κἀγὼ ἀναστήσω αὐτὸν ἐν τῇ ἐσχάτῃ ἡμέρᾳ, "No one can come to Me unless the Father who sent Me draws him; and I will raise him up on the last day." The Father who SENT the Son draws to the Son; the drawn one's stated end is resurrection, spoken by the Son. The verse stands one verse before the promise of 6:45 in John's own order.
  - John 6:65 — οὐδεὶς δύναται ἐλθεῖν πρός με ἐὰν μὴ ᾖ δεδομένον αὐτῷ ἐκ τοῦ πατρός, "no one can come to Me unless it has been granted him from the Father." What is granted is the COMING, and its source is the Father. The verse does not name faith as the granted thing.
  - John 16:13–15 — ὅταν δὲ ἔλθῃ ἐκεῖνος, τὸ πνεῦμα τῆς ἀληθείας, ὁδηγήσει ὑμᾶς ἐν τῇ ἀληθείᾳ πάσῃ· οὐ γὰρ λαλήσει ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ, ἀλλʼ ὅσα ἀκούσει λαλήσει καὶ τὰ ἐρχόμενα ἀναγγελεῖ ὑμῖν. ἐκεῖνος ἐμὲ δοξάσει, ὅτι ἐκ τοῦ ἐμοῦ λήμψεται καὶ ἀναγγελεῖ ὑμῖν. πάντα ὅσα ἔχει ὁ πατὴρ ἐμά ἐστιν, "when He, the Spirit of truth, comes, He will guide you into all the truth; for He will not speak on His own, but whatever He hears He will speak; and He will disclose to you what is to come. He will glorify Me, for He will take from Mine and will disclose it to you. All things that the Father has are Mine." The Spirit's teaching is itself a hearing-and-disclosing: He speaks what He hears, takes from what is the Son's — which is the Father's — and the printed Greek reads ὁδηγήσει ὑμᾶς ἐν τῇ ἀληθείᾳ πάσῃ, "guide you IN all the truth," where the NASB prints "into."
  - Rom 10:17 — ἄρα ἡ πίστις ἐξ ἀκοῆς, ἡ δὲ ἀκοὴ διὰ ῥήματος Χριστοῦ, "So faith comes from hearing, and hearing by the word of Christ." Faith's ORIGIN is a hearing, and the hearing arrives through a spoken word. The verse does not call faith a gift; it names faith's road.
  - Rom 1:18–21 (the marked edge) — διότι τὸ γνωστὸν τοῦ θεοῦ φανερόν ἐστιν ἐν αὐτοῖς· ὁ θεὸς γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐφανέρωσεν. τὰ γὰρ ἀόρατα αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ κτίσεως κόσμου τοῖς ποιήμασιν νοούμενα καθορᾶται, ἥ τε ἀΐδιος αὐτοῦ δύναμις καὶ θειότης … διότι γνόντες τὸν θεὸν οὐχ ὡς θεὸν ἐδόξασαν ἢ ηὐχαρίστησαν, ἀλλʼ ἐματαιώθησαν ἐν τοῖς διαλογισμοῖς αὐτῶν καὶ ἐσκοτίσθη ἡ ἀσύνετος αὐτῶν καρδία, "because that which is known about God is evident within them; for God made it evident to them. For since the creation of the world His invisible attributes, that is, His eternal power and divine nature, have been clearly perceived, being understood by what has been made" … "For even though they knew God, they did not honor Him as God or give thanks, but they became futile in their reasonings, and their senseless hearts were darkened." God's making-known is real at the edge too — the knowing is stated as fact (γνόντες τὸν θεὸν) — and its outcome there is suppression (τὴν ἀλήθειαν ἐν ἀδικίᾳ κατεχόντων, 1:18) and darkness, not love and not coming to the Son. The contrast is left standing, deliberately: it is the boundary that keeps the circle honest.
  - The witness line (Heb 12:6) — ὃν γὰρ ἀγαπᾷ κύριος παιδεύει, μαστιγοῖ δὲ πάντα υἱὸν ὃν παραδέχεται, "FOR WHOM THE LORD LOVES HE DISCIPLINES, AND HE PUNISHES EVERY SON WHOM HE ACCEPTS." One line only, by the reshape ruling (Honesty (5)): the coin seen a second time. παιδεύω is double-filed on the one word at this verse — [36.10] "to train" and [38.4] "to punish" at the same occurrence (also at 12:7 and 12:10; παιδεία likewise at every occurrence in the passage) — training and punishment in a single word, in the Proverbs sentence the passage quotes, where the Lord's discipline and the son He accepts stand in one breath. μαστιγοῖ is a different word (μαστιγόω, [19.9] "to whip"; [38.11] "to punish severely") and carries neither of παιδεύω's filings.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction of the anchors: God teaches, and the texts name the lesson and its landing together. At the word's one standing place, a people are taught by God ABOUT loving one another and taught SO THAT they do love one another — the dictionary files the εἰς both ways at the same occurrence, and at this place the two filings fit without strain, both at once, in one act: this entry states both together, a coin ruled at this place (Honesty (2)), not a fork to be chosen and not a rule for other places. The promise behind the word glosses the teaching's direction: heard and learned FROM the Father, the taught come TO the Son (John 6:45) — no text in the set names an inner location for the teaching, and none sets the taught word against the written or spoken word: the apostle within this very set speaks words taught by the Spirit (1 Cor 2:13), and the letter that says "no need to write" is itself a letter, teaching. What the stub claimed beyond this — one shared indwelling Spirit as the mechanism, a shared mind of Christ, guidance from within replacing reliance on external instruction — no anchor verse states, and it is retired in Honesty (3). The subject of the entry is the Father who teaches; the coin is what the entry found about His teaching.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Scope and form, as ruled by the proposer across the 2026-10-28 sittings: the entry is one unit about the larger issue — the Father teaching His people — carrying its hearing inside it in the labeled register above (form (b)); the anchors are the two filings complete (θεοδίδακτος [33.228], one reference; διδακτός [33.227], three references on two verses) and the close rests on the anchors; the hearing register is the proposer's gathered set as reshaped — fourteen texts gathered, ten carried — each labeled with its own say, in no sequence, carrying no part of the close. The set's gathering was governed by Framework Core Rule 8 (genuine context) and Core Rule 1 (no verse read so as to render another untrue). (2) The coin is the proposer's judgment, ruled at these places with its grounds — at 1 Thess 4:9 the two filings of εἰς fit one act without strain; in Heb 12's word the passage itself joins training and punishment — and it is NOT a rule: a double filing elsewhere may be the dictionary undecided between two readings of which the text means one, as at Rom 16:7 (KB054), which this Knowledge Base leaves standing as a fork. No later reader is to spend the coin as a blanket reading of double filings. (3) The stub's claims are retired as follows: "we share the exact same indwelling Spirit" — no anchor states a mechanism of indwelling, and the phrase is not carried; "the shared mind of Christ" — 1 Cor 2:16 is not among this entry's texts and not a receipt here; "we do not rely on external rules" — falls on the anchor's own terms (the "no need" is for a letter on one topic, inside a letter) and against Rom 10:17's teaching-by-hearing; "guided from the inside out" / "from within" — John 6:45 names the teaching's source and direction and no location. The stub's title, "Theodidaktoi (God-Taught)," stands unchanged: it names what the finished entry concludes. (4) The faith cluster is NOT in this entry — a boundary, not a denial: Mark 9:23 and Matt 17:20 were moved out by the reshape ruling as a thought of their own, and this entry's texts say less than the proposer's conviction ("even faith is given") — Rom 10:17 names faith's origin and John 6:65 grants the coming — so it is not spent here as a receipt. The point stands closed on its own texts: Phil 1:29 (believing granted) and Eph 2:8–9 (the whole fact named God's gift), carried in KB095 (Even Faith Is Given). (5) Hebrews 12:4–11 is receipted whole in the sitting's record and quoted here at one line only, by the reshape ruling: the passage's own weight — the Father disciplining the sons He accepts, toward sharing His holiness (12:10) — belongs to a discipline thought of its own and is not closed here; only the double filing of its teaching word is witnessed. (6) Two rendering data, recorded and not charged: the NASB renders φιλαδελφία at 1 Thess 4:9 "the love of the brothers and sisters" (cf. KB054); and at John 3 it renders γεννηθῇ ἄνωθεν "born again," where the dictionary files the word at this verse (3:3, and again 3:7) under [84.13] "from above," [67.55] "again," and the phrase under [41.53] "new birth" — the NASB's choice is among the verse's own filings. The two word-filings do NOT stand together at this place as a coin — ruled by the proposer (2026-10-28), the per-place judgment of Honesty (2) applied here the other way: the passage stages the two hearings in correction, not conjunction — the repetition word (δεύτερον, "a second time") stands in Nicodemus' mouth alone (3:4) and never in Jesus', whose answer runs on source throughout (of water and the Spirit, 3:5; what is born of the Spirit is spirit, 3:6; the wind — οὐκ οἶδας πόθεν ἔρχεται καὶ ποῦ ὑπάγει, “you do not know where it is coming from and where it is going” — 3:8). The "again" hearing is the text's own foil; the entry's labels use "from above" as the saying's sense at this place — "from above," that is, of the Spirit, as 3:5–6 name the birth's source. (7) John 16:13's printed ἐν ("guide you in all the truth") against the NASB's "into" is recorded at that bullet; nothing in the entry turns on it.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The lexical-fence reading — θεοδίδακτος occurs once, so the entry is one verse and a dictionary line, and everything past 1 Thess 4:9 is devotion dressed as data. Answered: the anchors are not one verse but the two dictionary filings complete — the word's own neighbor διδακτός, whose John 6:45 occurrence is the prophets' promise the Thessalonian word answers, and whose 1 Cor 2:13 occurrences set taught-words by their teacher; the close rests on those three verses alone. The hearing beyond them is fenced as hearing in Honesty (1) — labeled, sequential in nothing, load-bearing in nothing — and the entry's subject was ruled by the proposer to be the thought, the Father teaching His people, with the rare word as its door; a fence around the word would record the door and lose the thought the dictionary itself points through it.
(b) The stub's own internalized reading — because the household is taught by God, it is guided from within and does not rely on external rules or instruction. Answered at the anchor: 1 Thess 4:9's "no need" is expressly a need for anyone to WRITE to them about one topic, spoken inside a written letter that teaches throughout; its ground is not a principle against instruction but the fact that they are already taught. And the set's own hearing runs the other way: faith comes by HEARING a word (Rom 10:17), the Spirit of truth speaks what He HEARS and discloses it (John 16:13–14), and the taught words at 1 Cor 2:13 are SPOKEN words (λαλοῦμεν). No text in the set sets God's teaching against the heard, written, or spoken word; the opposition is the stub's import, retired in Honesty (3).
(c) The system reading — the drawing and granting of John 6:44, 65 state a mechanism of salvation: an effectual, irresistible act by which the Father secures the coming of particular men. Answered: the verses state the Father's acts and their outcomes and no mechanism — the draw's stated end is the Son raising the drawn one at the last day (6:44), and what is granted is the coming itself (6:65); neither verse speaks of resistance overcome or of any who are drawn and do not come, and the anchor's taught people are a congregation already loving one another, exhorted in a letter (1 Thess 4:9). The entry records the acts at their places; the mechanism is the system's addition, and the conjunction of the set neither states it nor needs it.
(d) The universal-knowledge reading — Rom 1 shows God making Himself known to all men, so "taught by God" names a universal endowment and the household's taught-ness dissolves into it. Answered by the entry's own marked edge: Rom 1:18–21 is carried precisely as the boundary where God's making-known (ὁ θεὸς γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐφανέρωσεν) met suppression and issued in futility and darkened hearts — knowing without honoring. The anchors name the other outcome: a people taught into loving one another (1 Thess 4:9) and coming to the Son (John 6:45). The difference between edge and circle is not the entry's preference; it is the texts' own stated outcomes, and the entry keeps both standing.

### Even Faith Is Given

- **ID:** KB095
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** χαρίζομαι, δῶρον, πίστις, πιστεύω, ὀλιγοπιστία
- **Verse keys:** Mark 9:23; Matt 17:20; Phil 1:29; Eph 2:8–9
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Even faith is given: the believing itself is one of the things granted — "to you it has been granted, for Christ's sake, not only to believe in Him, but also to suffer on His behalf" (Phil 1:29) — and the whole fact "by grace you have been saved through faith" is named God's gift, "not of yourselves… not of works, so that no one may boast" (Eph 2:8–9); so the believing is not the one part of salvation that man supplies for himself, but is itself received.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data; the sweep found a single assignment at every set verse — no dual filings inside this set): χαρίζομαι at Phil 1:29 is filed [57.102], "to give or grant graciously and generously, with the implication of good will on the part of the giver" — domain-57 gift territory, beside KB039's family. δῶρον at Eph 2:8 is filed [57.84], the same filing KB039 closed for δωρεά and δώρημα. The believing and the faith carry two filings between them, kept distinct in Honesty (7): πιστεύω at Phil 1:29 and πίστις at Eph 2:8 are both filed [31.102], "to believe in the good news about Jesus Christ and to become a follower"; πιστεύω at Mark 9:23 and πίστις at Matt 17:20 are both filed [31.85], "to believe to the extent of complete trust and reliance"; and Matt 17:20's ὀλιγοπιστία is filed [31.95], "the state of having little or inadequate faith."
  - Phil 1:29 — ὅτι ὑμῖν ἐχαρίσθη τὸ ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ, οὐ μόνον τὸ εἰς αὐτὸν πιστεύειν ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ πάσχειν, "For to you it has been granted for Christ's sake, not only to believe in Him, but also to suffer on His behalf." The anchor. The granting verb is aorist passive with the recipients named (ὑμῖν): the granting is done TO them. Its content is two articular infinitives in parallel under one "for Christ's sake" — τὸ εἰς αὐτὸν πιστεύειν, the believing in Him, and τὸ ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ πάσχειν, the suffering on His behalf. Believing itself is a granted item inside the verse's own construction; nothing is imported to say so, and the believing granted is the dictionary's Christian believing ([31.102], the filing above).
  - Eph 2:8–9 — Τῇ γὰρ χάριτί ἐστε σεσῳσμένοι διὰ πίστεως· καὶ τοῦτο οὐκ ἐξ ὑμῶν, θεοῦ τὸ δῶρον· οὐκ ἐξ ἔργων, ἵνα μή τις καυχήσηται, "For by grace you have been saved through faith; and this is not of yourselves, it is the gift of God; not a result of works, so that no one may boast." The frame. The saved state is perfect passive (ἐστε σεσῳσμένοι) — settled, and received, not achieved. "And this" (τοῦτο) takes up the fact just stated — saved by grace through faith — and the sentence fences it whole: not of yourselves; God's, the gift (δῶρον, [57.84]); not of works; with boasting as the stated thing excluded. A part of that fact standing as self-supplied — the faith, say — would breach the fence at exactly that part and reopen the boasting the sentence exists to close. The grammar of τοῦτο, and the proposer's ruling that both poles of the fact — the grace and the faith, its channel (διὰ πίστεως) — stand inside the gift, stand in Honesty (2).
  - Mark 9:23 — ὁ δὲ Ἰησοῦς εἶπεν αὐτῷ· τὸ εἰ δύνῃ, πάντα δυνατὰ τῷ πιστεύοντι, "'If You can?' All things are possible for the one who believes." The faith at its human place: believing as a man exercises it — τῷ πιστεύοντι, "the one who believes" (πιστεύω filed [31.85], complete trust and reliance). The verse says what believing does and nothing of where believing comes from. It stands here as a witness, not a receipt: the faith men exercise, in its own filing — and nothing in it contradicts the grant texts, which speak of the believing in theirs. This entry carries no predicate across the two filings: the verse is not made to say its faith is received, and the grant texts are not read through it.
  - Matt 17:20 — ὁ δὲ λέγει αὐτοῖς· διὰ τὴν ὀλιγοπιστίαν ὑμῶν· ἀμὴν γὰρ λέγω ὑμῖν, ἐὰν ἔχητε πίστιν ὡς κόκκον σινάπεως, ἐρεῖτε τῷ ὄρει τούτῳ· μετάβα ἔνθεν ἐκεῖ, καὶ μεταβήσεται· καὶ οὐδὲν ἀδυνατήσει ὑμῖν, "And He said to them, 'Because of your meager faith; for truly I say to you, if you have faith the size of a mustard seed, you will say to this mountain, "Move from here to there," and it will move; and nothing will be impossible for you.'" The same human place, from the measure side: faith as the disciples have it (ἔχητε πίστιν, πίστις filed [31.85]) and its shortfall named (ὀλιγοπιστία, [31.95]; NASB renders it "meager faith"). The verse measures faith's exercise; of its origin it says nothing — Mark 9:23's standing exactly: a witness at the human place, contradicting nothing in the grant texts and receipting nothing about source.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction settles faith's source. The believing is granted outright (Phil 1:29), and the fact in which that believing stands — saved, by grace, through faith — is named, whole, God's gift and not of yourselves, its works-source barred and its boasting closed (Eph 2:8–9): the fact is fenced whole, and no part of it is assigned to yourselves. That both poles of that fact — the grace and the faith — stand inside the gift is the proposer's ruling, carried here and in Honesty (2) under his name, by his standard: a reading must be true and allowed by the grammar; it need not be untouchable. The Gospels pair stand as witnesses beside the close, not inside it: the faith men exercise, present in measure and sometimes little (Mark 9:23; Matt 17:20), is stated in its own filing ([31.85]) and contradicts nothing the grant texts say of the believing in theirs ([31.102]) — this entry claims no more from them than that. What the conjunction does not settle: the distribution of faith — how it is measured out to each — which stands outside this entry (Honesty (4)); and faith's necessity, which this entry does not take up (Honesty (6)).
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Scope and form: this entry is the thought moved out of KB056 by the proposer's reshape ruling (KB056, Honesty (4)) — the proposer's conviction, "even faith is given," receipted here on its own pasted texts. Four candidate texts were pasted and examined; the proposer's rulings disposed of all four — Phil 1:29 and Eph 2:8–9 in, Rom 12:3 out (Honesty (4)), 2 Pet 1:1 held in reserve, unspent and unquoted here. Mark 9:23 and Matt 17:20, pasted in KB056's gathering, stand in this entry as witnesses only — quoted, and receipting nothing (Honesty (7)). The set's gathering was governed by Framework Core Rule 8 (genuine context — selection by the thought's own texts, never by frequency) and Core Rule 1 (no verse read so as to render another untrue). (2) The τοῦτο of Eph 2:8: its antecedent is the immediately preceding clause — "by grace you have been saved through faith" (2:8a, echoing 2:5) — not the paragraph before it and not chapter 1. The pronoun is neuter against two feminine nouns (χάρις, πίστις); the whole-fact reach is the grammatical majority hearing (same-letter parallels stand in the sitting's record as deliberation evidence — unpasted, unspent). That BOTH poles of that fact — the grace and the faith — stand inside the gift is the proposer's ruling, carried in the close under his name, by his standard: a reading must be true and allowed by the grammar; it need not be untouchable. The Claim states only what Ephesians' sentence states — the whole fact named God's gift. The entry nowhere prints "the pronoun points at πίστις" as a grammatical claim; the direct-reference hearing stands here as the labeled minority. (3) Precision of scope: no text in this set names the NOUN πίστις the direct referent of the gift-word δῶρον. Ephesians names the whole saved-through-faith fact the gift; Philippians grants the believing — the VERB πιστεύειν, inside the granted pair. The entry's statement is exactly as wide as those two constructions, and no wider. (4) Rom 12:3 was pasted, examined, and ruled out by the proposer: its allotting (ἐμέρισεν [57.89]) is the distribution language of its own passage, running into the differing gifts of 12:6, and its μέτρον [81.1] stands between two hearings — faith in amounts; faith as a measuring standard — that this entry does not need and does not decide. (5) The neighbors are not receipts: Rom 10:17 names faith's ORIGIN (hearing through the word of Christ) and John 6:65 grants the COMING, not the faith — both stand as KB056's own texts, pointed to there and not quoted here. Acts 16:14 (the Lord opening Lydia's heart to respond) is unpasted, outside this set, and enters no part of the close. (6) The proposer's faith/love asymmetry frame — the impossibility sentence names faith alone (Heb 11:6); love's sentence is about knowing God (1 John 4:7–8); genuine faith is proven by selfless love (Gal 5:6) — stands in this entry's sitting record as his teaching frame; its texts are unpasted here, it is not spent in the close, and this entry, which closes faith's SOURCE, does not take up faith's necessity. (7) The two πιστ- filings are distinct dictionary senses and are not collapsed here: [31.85] (trust and reliance, the Gospels pair) and [31.102] (the Christian believing, the grant pair). No conclusion of this entry joins them: the close rests on the grant pair alone, and the Gospels pair stand as witnesses whose whole work here is that they contradict nothing in it. No predicate is transferred across the filings — the exercised faith of [31.85] is nowhere in this entry called received, and the granted believing of [31.102] is nowhere read through the Gospels' measure language — and the Gospels pair's silence about source is a source claim in neither direction.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The contribution reading — God gives the grace; man supplies the faith: faith is the one human act inside salvation, its human surface showing in the Gospels ("if you have faith"; διὰ τὴν ὀλιγοπιστίαν ὑμῶν), and Ephesians' "this" naming the salvation as the gift, not the faith. Answered: Philippians grants the believing itself, in a single construction with the suffering: one granting verb (ἐχαρίσθη) governs both articular infinitives as parallel items, at one strength — the construction that grants the suffering grants the believing. Ephesians then fences the whole fact: a faith "of yourselves" inside a whole declared "not of yourselves… so that no one may boast" reopens, at the faith point, the very boasting the sentence exists to close. And the Gospels' surface is possession-and-exercise language — faith in human hands, measured and sometimes little — which is true at its place and silent on source; silence is not a source claim.
(b) The opportunity reading of Phil 1:29 — what was granted is the privilege, the occasion, of believing (and of suffering) under persecution, not the believing itself. Answered: the granted content in the verse's own grammar is the articular infinitives — τὸ… πιστεύειν, τὸ… πάσχειν — the acts themselves, not occasions for them; the verb is the gracious-granting verb ([57.102]) in the passive, with the Philippians as its recipients. The parallel decides the strength: the gift's content on the suffering side is the suffering, borne — not an opportunity to suffer — and the construction gives the believing side the same content at the same strength.
(c) The overreading in the entry's own direction — τοῦτο points at πίστις alone, so Ephesians by itself proves faith the gift and Philippians is decoration. Answered: the entry declines it. The pronoun is neuter against two feminine nouns and takes up the fact just stated (Honesty (2)); a close rested on the minority grammar would be built on the grammar war instead of on the receipts. The close stands where the texts are outright — the believing granted (Phil 1:29), the whole fact named gift (Eph 2:8–9), the poles' standing inside it ruled under the proposer's name at Honesty (2) — and needs no verse to say more than it says.
(d) The love displacement — where selfless love is present, faith is dispensable ("all you need is love"), so its givenness is moot. Answered within this entry's scope: the displacement argues faith's NECESSITY, which this entry does not take up (Honesty (6)); it does not touch faith's SOURCE, which is all this entry closes. And it cannot borrow this entry's frame text: inside Ephesians' gift sentence itself, the faith stands within the fact named gift — "by grace you have been saved through faith; and this is not of yourselves" — so the verse the displacement would need leaves the faith exactly where the entry puts it.

### The Pattern of the Firstborn (Indwelling Life vs. Pentecostal Power)

- **ID:** KB058
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** πνεῦμα ἅγιον ἐπελεύσεται ἐπὶ σέ; τὸ γεννώμενον ἅγιον; καταβῆναι τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον ἐπ᾽ αὐτόν; ἀρχόμενος ὡσεὶ ἐτῶν τριάκοντα; πνεῦμα κυρίου ἐπ᾽ ἐμὲ, ἔχρισέν με; ἀμνοῦ ἀμώμου καὶ ἀσπίλου; χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας; ἐνεφύσησεν, λάβετε πνεῦμα ἅγιον; γεννηθῇ ἄνωθεν; τὸ πνεῦμα ὅπου θέλει πνεῖ; λήμψεσθε δύναμιν ἐπελθόντος τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος ἐφ᾽ ὑμᾶς; ἐπλήσθησαν πάντες πνεύματος ἁγίου; τὴν ἐπαγγελίαν τοῦ πνεύματος τοῦ ἁγίου λαβὼν παρὰ τοῦ πατρός, ἐξέχεεν; ναὸς τοῦ ἐν ὑμῖν ἁγίου πνεύματός
- **Hebrew keys:** וַיִּפַּח בְּאַפָּיו נִשְׁמַת חַיִּים; וּפְחִי בַּהֲרוּגִים הָאֵלֶּה וְיִחְיוּ; וַתָּבוֹא בָהֶם הָרוּחַ וַיִּחְיוּ; וְנָתַתִּי רוּחִי בָכֶם וִחְיִיתֶם
- **Verse keys:** Luke 1:34–35; 3:21–23; 4:18–19; 1 Pet 1:19; Heb 4:15; John 20:22; Gen 2:7; Ezek 37:9–10, 14; John 3:3–8; 1 Cor 6:19; Acts 1:8; 2:1–4, 32–33
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Read together, the texts give one sequence for the Firstborn: conceived by the Holy Spirit's coming upon Mary, the one fathered being holy and called Son of God (Luke 1:35); tempted in all things in likeness, without sin (Heb 4:15), His blood the blood of a lamb unblemished and spotless (1 Pet 1:19); at about thirty, at His beginning, the Holy Spirit descended upon Him and He proclaimed the anointing (Luke 3:22–23; 4:18); risen, He breathed on His disciples and said, "Receive the Holy Spirit" (John 20:22); exalted at God's right hand, He received the promise of the Holy Spirit from the Father and poured out this which you both see and hear — power, when the Holy Spirit has come upon them, for witness (Acts 2:33; 1:8; 2:4). Standing as Witnesses alongside the upper room: God breathing the breath of life into the man (Gen 2:7) and the breath entering the slain, who lived (Ezek 37:9–10), with the promise "I will put My Spirit within you and you will live" (Ezek 37:14); and birth from above by the one πνεῦμα that blows where it wishes (John 3:3–8).
- **The chain:** (1) The conception: Mary asks, πῶς ἔσται τοῦτο, ἐπεὶ ἄνδρα οὐ γινώσκω; ("How will this be, since I do not know a man?" Luke 1:34), and the answer names the agent and the result in one sentence: πνεῦμα ἅγιον ἐπελεύσεται ἐπὶ σὲ καὶ δύναμις ὑψίστου ἐπισκιάσει σοι· διὸ καὶ τὸ γεννώμενον ἅγιον κληθήσεται υἱὸς θεοῦ ("Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; for that reason also the holy one being fathered will be called Son of God," 1:35) — the holiness belongs to the fathering itself, before any ministry. (2) The life and the offering: the high priest is πεπειρασμένον δὲ κατὰ πάντα καθ' ὁμοιότητα χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας ("tempted in all things in likeness, without sin," Heb 4:15; KB026's set), and the redemption price is τιμίῳ αἵματι ὡς ἀμνοῦ ἀμώμου καὶ ἀσπίλου Χριστοῦ ("precious blood, as of a lamb unblemished and spotless, Christ's," 1 Pet 1:19). (3) The beginning, at about thirty: at the baptism the heaven opened καὶ καταβῆναι τὸ πνεῦμα τὸ ἅγιον σωματικῷ εἴδει ὡς περιστερὰν ἐπ᾽ αὐτόν ("and the Holy Spirit descended in bodily form like a dove upon Him," Luke 3:22), with the voice σὺ εἶ ὁ υἱός μου ὁ ἀγαπητός, ἐν σοὶ εὐδόκησα ("You are My beloved Son; in You I am well pleased"); the next sentence dates it — Καὶ αὐτὸς ἦν Ἰησοῦς ἀρχόμενος ὡσεὶ ἐτῶν τριάκοντα ("and Jesus Himself was about thirty years old, at His beginning," 3:23) — and His own first proclamation in Luke reads the descent's errand: πνεῦμα κυρίου ἐπ᾽ ἐμὲ οὗ εἵνεκεν ἔχρισέν με εὐαγγελίσασθαι πτωχοῖς ("The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He anointed Me to bring good news to the poor," 4:18). The descent and the anointing belong to the beginning of the ministry, some thirty years after the holy conception, and their stated business is proclamation. (4) The upper room: καὶ τοῦτο εἰπὼν ἐνεφύσησεν καὶ λέγει αὐτοῖς· λάβετε πνεῦμα ἅγιον ("and saying this, He breathed on them and says to them: Receive the Holy Spirit," John 20:22; KB068's receipts) — spoken by the risen one on resurrection evening, an imperative, the Holy Spirit named as what is received. (5) Witness — the breathing that gives life: וַיִּפַּח בְּאַפָּיו נִשְׁמַת חַיִּים וַיְהִי הָאָדָם לְנֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה ("and He breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and the man became a living being," Gen 2:7); Ezekiel is commanded with the same root, נפח: וּפְחִי בַּהֲרוּגִים הָאֵלֶּה וְיִחְיוּ ("breathe on these slain, and they will live," Ezek 37:9), and it happens — וַתָּבוֹא בָהֶם הָרוּחַ וַיִּחְיוּ ("and the breath/Spirit entered them, and they lived," 37:10) — under the explanation וְנָתַתִּי רוּחִי בָכֶם וִחְיִיתֶם ("and I will put My Spirit within you, and you will live," 37:14). John names neither text; they stand as Witness, each saying its own thing: God's breathing is how life enters what was not living. (6) Witness — birth from above: ἐὰν μή τις γεννηθῇ ἄνωθεν, οὐ δύναται ἰδεῖν τὴν βασιλείαν τοῦ θεοῦ ("unless one is born from above, he cannot see the kingdom of God," John 3:3, repeated 3:7); the birth is ἐξ ὕδατος καὶ πνεύματος (3:5), and τὸ γεγεννημένον ἐκ τοῦ πνεύματος πνεῦμά ἐστιν ("what has been born of the Spirit is spirit," 3:6); and the illustration runs on the same word — τὸ πνεῦμα ὅπου θέλει πνεῖ ("the wind/Spirit blows where it wishes," 3:8), οὕτως ἐστὶν πᾶς ὁ γεγεννημένος ἐκ τοῦ πνεύματος ("so is everyone born of the Spirit") — one πνεῦμα across the whole saying: breath, wind, Spirit. (7) Pentecost: the promise is λήμψεσθε δύναμιν ἐπελθόντος τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος ἐφ᾽ ὑμᾶς ("you will receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you," Acts 1:8); the day opens with ἦχος ὥσπερ φερομένης πνοῆς βιαίας ("a noise like a violent rushing breath/wind," 2:2), tongues as of fire resting ἐφ᾽ ἕνα ἕκαστον αὐτῶν ("on each one of them," 2:3), καὶ ἐπλήσθησαν πάντες πνεύματος ἁγίου ("and they were all filled with the Holy Spirit," 2:4); and Peter states the ground, not a theory: τῇ δεξιᾷ οὖν τοῦ θεοῦ ὑψωθείς, τήν τε ἐπαγγελίαν τοῦ πνεύματος τοῦ ἁγίου λαβὼν παρὰ τοῦ πατρός, ἐξέχεεν ("exalted at the right hand of God, and having received the promise of the Holy Spirit from the Father, He poured out," 2:33) — the pourer's ground is His exaltation, and the gift's source is the Father's promise. (8) The temple, stated as fact and pointed, not dated: τὸ σῶμα ὑμῶν ναὸς τοῦ ἐν ὑμῖν ἁγίου πνεύματός ἐστιν οὗ ἔχετε ἀπὸ θεοῦ ("your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit in you, whom you have from God," 1 Cor 6:19; KB091's set) — the indwelling that makes the temple is this same Holy Spirit, from God.
- **The close (settled):** The sequence stands as the texts give it, read together, each step on its own words: holy conception by the Spirit's coming upon (Luke 1:35), a life without sin and a spotless lamb's blood (Heb 4:15; 1 Pet 1:19), the Spirit's descent upon Him at about thirty for the anointed proclamation (Luke 3:22–23; 4:18), the risen one's breathing and "Receive the Holy Spirit" (John 20:22), and the exalted one's pouring out of the Father's promised Spirit, for power and witness (Acts 1:8; 2:4, 33). The Witnesses stand in their own register beside the upper room — the breathing that made the man live and the slain live (Gen 2:7; Ezek 37:9–10, 14), and birth from above by the one πνεῦμα (John 3:3–8) — heard alongside John 20:22, never quoted by it. One Spirit is named at every step; what differs is the verb — come upon, descend upon, breathed, received, poured out, filled — and the errand each verb carries.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) How the upper room's reception and Pentecost's filling relate — two givings, or one gift in two moments — no text in this set says; the entry records both events at full strength in their own words and ranks neither. (2) The temple: 1 Cor 6:19 states the fact without attaching its constitution to a moment; whether the household was constituted temple in the upper room, at Pentecost, or otherwise is not decided here, and the stub's "constitute them as God's temple" at John 20:22 is not carried as that verse's claim. (3) The Witness register governs links (5) and (6): Genesis, Ezekiel, and John 3 are not quoted by John 20, and the hearing that joins them is labeled, not smuggled into John 20's exegesis; the 2026-10-17 audit's flag on this stub was exactly this point, and it is met by the register, not by deleting the hearing. (4) "Firstborn" (πρωτότοκος) is the stub's title word; no verse in this set names Him firstborn, and the pattern is what closes — the naming stands in its own texts (Col 1:15, 18; Rom 8:29), pointed to, not quoted. (5) The stub's "no preaching or sign until the Jordan" is carried only as far as Luke's own printing within this set carries it — the descent (3:22) and the beginning (3:23) precede the first proclamation quoted (4:18) — not as an audit of every Gospel's ordering. (6) Luke 4:18 is quoted from the NA28 text as pasted, which does not carry the "heal the brokenhearted" clause; nothing in the close rests on that clause in either form. (7) That the household's giving follows the Firstborn's sequence is this entry's thesis, drawn from the sequence the texts state for Him and the givings they state for the household; no single verse states the pattern as a rule, and the Claim asserts the sequence, not the thesis.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The foretaste reading — John 20:22 was a symbol or pledge only; nothing was received until Pentecost. Answered: λάβετε is an imperative spoken in the act (ἐνεφύσησεν … λάβετε πνεῦμα ἅγιον, John 20:22), and Acts grounds Pentecost in the pourer's exaltation and His reception of the promise from the Father (2:33), a ground John 20 does not state; the set gives each event its own stated ground and demotes neither. (b) The completed-at-the-upper-room reading — Pentecost added power only, no giving of the Spirit. Answered: Acts states its own event in its own words — ἐπλήσθησαν πάντες πνεύματος ἁγίου (2:4), ἐξέχεεν (2:33) — a filling and a pouring, not merely an empowerment of an already-indwelt assembly; the entry keeps both givings as written (honesty (1)). (c) The two-Spirits reading — different stages mean different spirits or different gifts in kind. Answered: one name runs through every step — πνεῦμα ἅγιον (Luke 1:35; 3:22; John 20:22; Acts 1:8; 2:4, 33), πνεῦμα κυρίου for the anointing (Luke 4:18) — and the variation is in the verbs of distribution (come upon, descend upon, breathed, poured out, filled), not in the Spirit named. (d) The redundancy reading — if He was conceived by the Spirit, a descent at thirty is superfluous, so the pattern is incoherent. Answered: each text states its own object: the conception's result is a holy birth (τὸ γεννώμενον ἅγιον, Luke 1:35); the descent's errand is anointing for proclamation (ἔχρισέν με εὐαγγελίσασθαι πτωχοῖς, Luke 4:18); and Luke prints them thirty years apart in his own order (1:35; 3:22–23, ἀρχόμενος) — the sequence the rival calls incoherent is the evangelist's own arrangement. (e) The exegesis-smuggling reading — "born from above in the upper room" as a claim about what John 20:22 itself says. Answered by the register: John 20's own words are breathing and receiving; the birth-from-above wording is John 3's, standing as Witness (link (6)), labeled a hearing — the entry makes no claim that John 20 names birth, and the Witness is not dressed as a quotation.

### Nephesh and Dam (Life and Blood)

- **ID:** KB059
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Hebrew keys:** בְּנַפְשׁוֹ דָמוֹ, נֶפֶשׁ הַבָּשָׂר בַּדָּם, הַדָּם הוּא הַנָּפֶשׁ, דִּמְכֶם לְנַפְשֹׁתֵיכֶם
- **Verse keys:** Gen 9:4; Gen 9:5; Gen 9:6; Lev 17:10; Lev 17:11; Lev 17:12; Lev 17:13; Lev 17:14; Deut 12:23; Deut 12:24
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** At its three stated places the Torah states one equation in three forms — flesh may not be eaten "with its life, that is, its blood" (Gen 9:4), "the life of the flesh is in the blood" (Lev 17:11), and "the blood is the life" (Deut 12:23) — and the equation carries the blood's whole handling in both directions: toward man it is prohibition and reckoning, for God requires the lifeblood back from beast and from brother (Gen 9:5) and sets His face against the person who eats blood (Lev 17:10); and its one appointed use is God's own giving — "I have given it to you on the altar to make atonement for your souls; for it is the blood by reason of the life that makes atonement" (Lev 17:11).
- **Chain of evidence:**
  - The filings (the dictionaries' own judgments, verified per occurrence in the UBS Hebrew data): נֶפֶשׁ at Gen 9:4, 9:5, Lev 17:11, 17:14, and Deut 12:23 stands under sense 3 (606 references): "the life principle that animates both humans and animals, turns them into breathing living beings… it is believed to reside in the blood." At Lev 17:10 and 17:12 the same word stands under sense 5 (104 references), "= a human being, as defined by the life principle that animates him/her" — the person addressed by the prohibition. דָּם at Gen 9:4, at every Leviticus 17:10–14 occurrence, and at Deut 12:23 stands under sense 1 (202 references): the blood itself, "used in rituals such as sacrifices, purification and covenant ceremonies, and never to be eaten; regarded as the seat of life (נֶפֶשׁ)." At Gen 9:5 and 9:6 alone in this set it stands under sense 4 (154 references), "blood > bloodshed," the killing that brings guilt. BDB H5315 §3a assembles this entry's set itself: the sense "whose life resides in the blood," first stated at Deut 12:23–24, "enlarged in H, Lev 17:10–11, 12, 14," and standing also at Gen 9:4–5.
  - The first statement (Gen 9:4): "But you shall not eat flesh with its life, that is, its blood" — אַךְ־בָּשָׂר בְּנַפְשׁוֹ דָמוֹ לֹא תֹאכֵלוּ. The verse itself joins the two words in one apposition: the flesh's nephesh, its dam — and the prohibition is as old as the permission to eat flesh that it bounds.
  - The requiring (Gen 9:5–6): "I certainly will require your lifeblood; from every animal I will require it. And from every person, from every man as his brother I will require the life of a person" — אֶת־דִּמְכֶם לְנַפְשֹׁתֵיכֶם אֶדְרֹשׁ… אֶדְרֹשׁ אֶת־נֶפֶשׁ הָאָדָם. Three requirings, God the subject of each; and the reckoning's stated ground is given in the next breath: "Whoever sheds human blood, By man his blood shall be shed, For in the image of God He made mankind" (9:6) — the image clause is this verse's own reason, quoted here as its ground; the image itself is KB060's subject and is not closed here. Here dam turns to its bloodshed filing: what is required back is life for shed blood.
  - The prohibition's addressee (Lev 17:10, 12): "anyone… who eats any blood, I will set My face against that person who eats the blood, and will cut him off from among his people" — וְנָתַתִּי פָנַי בַּנֶּפֶשׁ הָאֹכֶלֶת אֶת־הַדָּם; "No person among you may eat blood" — כָּל־נֶפֶשׁ מִכֶּם לֹא־תֹאכַל דָּם. The eater is himself a nephesh, under the word's person filing: in one pericope, the nephesh (person) may not eat the blood because the nephesh (life) is in it — the two filings stand a verse apart and are not blended (Honesty (4)).
  - The equation and the gift (Lev 17:11): "For the life of the flesh is in the blood, and I have given it to you on the altar to make atonement for your souls; for it is the blood by reason of the life that makes atonement" — כִּי נֶפֶשׁ הַבָּשָׂר בַּדָּם הִוא וַאֲנִי נְתַתִּיו לָכֶם עַל־הַמִּזְבֵּחַ לְכַפֵּר עַל־נַפְשֹׁתֵיכֶם כִּי־הַדָּם הוּא בַּנֶּפֶשׁ יְכַפֵּר. The verse holds both halves in one sentence: the prohibition's ground (the life is in the blood) and the blood's one permitted destination, which is not man's taking but God's giving — נְתַתִּיו, "I have given it" — and the atonement's stated reason is the life (בַּנֶּפֶשׁ, "by reason of the life"). The atonement verb is כִּפֵּר; no filing is claimed for it here (Honesty (7)).
  - The handling and the restatement (Lev 17:13–14): the hunted animal's blood is poured out and covered with dirt — וְשָׁפַךְ אֶת־דָּמוֹ וְכִסָּהוּ בֶּעָפָר — returned to the ground, not taken to the table; and the equation is stated a second time over all flesh: "for the life of all flesh is its blood; whoever eats it shall be cut off" — כִּי נֶפֶשׁ כָּל־בָּשָׂר דָּמוֹ הִוא כָּל־אֹכְלָיו יִכָּרֵת.
  - The shortest form (Deut 12:23–24): "Only be sure not to eat the blood, for the blood is the life, and you shall not eat the life with the flesh. You shall not eat it; you shall pour it out on the ground like water" — כִּי הַדָּם הוּא הַנָּפֶשׁ וְלֹא־תֹאכַל הַנֶּפֶשׁ עִם־הַבָּשָׂר… עַל־הָאָרֶץ תִּשְׁפְּכֶנּוּ כַּמָּיִם. Deuteronomy's statement of the equation is the blunt one — the blood *is* the nephesh — and its handling matches Leviticus: poured out on the ground.
- **The close (settled):** The set's three places say one thing in one voice: the life is in the blood — stated as apposition at the flood's new beginning (Gen 9:4), as the altar's charter (Lev 17:11), and as a flat identity (Deut 12:23) — and everything the texts do with blood follows from that one equation. Because the life in it is not man's, man may not eat it: God requires the lifeblood back, from the animal and from the brother (Gen 9:5), sets His face against the eater and cuts him off (Lev 17:10), and appoints the spilled blood of the hunt for the ground, poured out and covered (Lev 17:13; Deut 12:24). And because the life in it is God's to give, the blood has exactly one altar in these texts, and it stands there by gift, not by seizure: "I have given it to you on the altar," and the atonement it makes is made "by reason of the life." The stub reached for this datum with an ownership word — "belongs exclusively to the Creator" — and the texts say it better in their own verbs: the life is *required* back by God and the blood is *given* by God. What man does with blood, in every verse of this set, is only this: not eat it, and pour it out — except where God Himself has placed it, on the altar, for atonement for men's lives.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub's "belongs exclusively to the Creator" is not carried as the entry's phrasing: no text or filing in the set states an ownership abstraction; the texts state God's requiring (Gen 9:5) and God's giving (Lev 17:11), and the entry carries the datum in those verbs. (2) The stub's "we cease trying to live on our own corrupted, independent strength" is retired — a devotional import with no text in the set. (3) The stub's "partake entirely of the indestructible blood and life offered by the Son" is retired from this entry: in this set blood is forbidden as food and given on the altar, and no verse predicates indestructibility of the nephesh — the life here can be required back, poured out, and eaten-with at peril of cutting off. The partaking texts (John 6 and its company) are another set, unreceipted here; this entry neither spends them nor denies them. (4) The nephesh fork is kept, not blended: sense 3 (the life principle residing in the blood) at Gen 9:4–5, Lev 17:11, 14, Deut 12:23; sense 5 (a person) at Lev 17:10, 12. A third filing of the word — sense 4, "a living creature," at Gen 9:10, 12, 15, 16 (נֶפֶשׁ חַיָּה) — stands in the covenant block, which KB032 treats for the covenant and the sign without taking up this word; it is not spent here either. (5) The dam fork is likewise kept: the blood itself (sense 1) everywhere in the set except Gen 9:5–6, where the filing is bloodshed — the pericope's turn from prohibition to reckoning is the dictionary's own split, and the entry asserts it at exactly those verses. (6) Genesis 9:6's image clause is quoted as the reckoning's stated ground; the image of God is KB060's subject and is not closed here. Genesis 9:7 (the blessing renewed) stands in the pasted passage as frame and is not quoted or spent. (7) The atonement verb כִּפֵּר is quoted inside Lev 17:11's own sentence and carries no filing claim in this entry; the entry rests on the verse's statement, including its stated reason (בַּנֶּפֶשׁ), not on a lexical build for the verb. The stub's title is kept: it names the two words and their topic and asserts none of the retired claims.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The taboo reading: the blood prohibition is a primitive food taboo or a hygiene rule, its rationale lost or merely ritual. Answered: the texts state their own rationale, three times, in one sentence-form — the life is in the blood — and build law on it in both directions (a capital reckoning, Gen 9:5–6; cutting off, Lev 17:10; the altar's atonement, Lev 17:11); a taboo does not state its ground as a fact about life, nor make atonement by reason of it. The entry follows the texts' stated reason over the guessed one. (b) The ownership-system reading (the stub's own): blood is the Creator's exclusive property, and the believer's part is to partake of the Son's offered blood instead. Answered in its two halves: the ownership abstraction is stated by no verse in the set (the texts say *required* and *given* — Honesty (1)), and the partaking half has no receipt here at all — in this set the blood is never eaten by God's appointment, only poured out or placed on the altar by His gift; the Son's blood and its partaking stand or fall on their own texts, which this entry does not spend (Honesty (3)). (c) The figure reading: "the life is in the blood" is a vivid metaphor for vitality, not a statement the law actually runs on. Answered: the equation does legal work no figure is asked to do — a man's blood is required back from his brother's hand on its ground (Gen 9:5–6, where the dictionary files the word as bloodshed), the eater is cut off on its ground (Lev 17:10, 14), and atonement is made "by reason of the life" on its ground (Lev 17:11); the texts hang consequences on the equation as on a fact, and Deuteronomy states it as a flat identity (הַדָּם הוּא הַנָּפֶשׁ). (d) The soul-substance reading: nephesh here is man's immortal, indestructible soul, and the blood texts are about its fate. Answered: the filing at these verses is the life principle of humans *and animals* — Genesis 9:4's flesh is animal flesh, Leviticus 17:14 says "the life of *all* flesh" — and the set's nephesh is eaten-with, required back, and poured out with the blood; nothing in these verses predicates immortality or indestructibility of it, and what nephesh means across its whole distribution is a larger question this entry does not close.

### B'tselem Elohim (The Image of God)

- **ID:** KB060
- **Facet of:** none
- **Hebrew keys:** צֶלֶם, דְּמוּת
- **Greek keys:** εἰκών, ὁμοίωσις, μεταμορφόω, πρόσωπον, δόξα, ἀόρατος
- **Verse keys:** Gen 1:26–27; Gen 5:1–3; Gen 9:6; Col 1:15–20; 2 Cor 4:3–6; 2 Cor 3:18; 1 Cor 11:7; Jas 3:9
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** The words Scripture uses for God's making of man are likeness words. God made man in His image (צֶלֶם) and according to His likeness (דְּמוּת) — the pair together at the making (Gen 1:26), each word also standing alone (Gen 1:27; 5:1), the pair together again, in the other order, on the son a father begets (Gen 5:3), and the image still standing after the flood, given as the reason for the blood law (Gen 9:6). The New Testament's word (εἰκών) is filed, at every place in this entry where it names a bearer, as "that which has the same form as something else" — of Christ, the image of the invisible God (Col 1:15; 2 Cor 4:4); of the man, the image and glory of God (1 Cor 11:7); and of believers, who are being transformed into the same image (2 Cor 3:18) — and James grounds how we speak about people in their having been made according to God's likeness (Jas 3:9), that word's only New Testament occurrence.
- **The chain:**
  - The making (Gen 1:26–27): "Then God said, “Let Us make mankind in Our image, according to Our likeness; and let them rule over the fish of the sea and over the birds of the sky and over the livestock and over all the earth, and over every crawling thing that crawls on the earth.”" Both words stand in the one sentence, with God's own suffixes on them — בְּצַלְמֵנוּ, "in Our image," כִּדְמוּתֵנוּ, "according to Our likeness" — and the ruling over the creatures is spoken next in the same sentence. The making itself is then told with צֶלֶם alone, twice: "So God created man in His own image, in the image of God He created him; male and female He created them." The bearer named is the man — mankind, male and female.
  - The pair across Genesis (Gen 5:1–3; 9:6): the recap of the making uses דְּמוּת alone — "On the day when God created man, He made him in the likeness of God" (5:1) — and one generation on, both words stand again, in the other order and with a man's suffixes: "he fathered a son in his own likeness, according to his image, and named him Seth" (5:3; בִּדְמוּתוֹ כְּצַלְמוֹ) — the words spoken of Adam's making are spoken again of Seth's fathering, with a man's suffixes where God's stood. After the flood, צֶלֶם alone carries the weight of the blood law: "Whoever sheds human blood, By man his blood shall be shed, For in the image of God He made mankind" (9:6) — the image is given as the because of the law, spoken to the world after the flood.
  - The dictionaries divide the words the same way the texts do (the filings, each checked at its verse at this station): BDB (H6754) files צֶלֶם's God-making places under its sense 2, "image, likeness, of resemblance" — Gen 1:26, 1:27 (twice), 9:6, and 5:3, and no other places — apart from its sense 1 (carved, molten, and painted images) and its sense 3 (a mere semblance, a shadow). For דְּמוּת, BDB (H1823) notes the word "of son in likeness of father" at Gen 5:3 and "so also of man in likeness of God" at 1:26 and 5:1; the UBS Hebrew dictionary defines the word as the state in which things "are, or are perceived as having something in common with other objects" — likeness, resemblance.
  - The word of Christ (Col 1:15; 2 Cor 4:4): "He is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of all creation:" — εἰκών is filed [58.35], "that which has the same form as something else" (the domain of Same or Equivalent Kind or Class), and ἀόρατος, "invisible," is filed [24.4], "pertaining to that which cannot be seen": the God who cannot be seen has an image, and the image is a "He," not a thing. Paul says it again of the gospel he preaches: "the light of the gospel of the glory of Christ, who is the image of God" (2 Cor 4:4) — εἰκών filed [58.35] here too, and δόξα, "glory," filed [14.49], "the state of brightness or shining," in the verse about light.
  - The word of man, and of believers (1 Cor 11:7; 2 Cor 3:18): "he is the image and glory of God" — Paul's present-tense statement about the man (ἀνήρ, the verse's own subject, in the sentence that sets the woman as the glory of man beside him; εἰκὼν καὶ δόξα θεοῦ ὑπάρχων), εἰκών filed [58.35] again and δόξα filed [79.18], "the quality of splendid, remarkable appearance," at its first occurrence in the verse (its second stands in the glory-of-man clause — see Honesty (5)). And of believers: "But we all, with unveiled faces, looking as in a mirror at the glory of the Lord, are being transformed into the same image from glory to glory, just as from the Lord, the Spirit." (2 Cor 3:18) — τὴν αὐτὴν εἰκόνα, "the same image," εἰκών filed [58.35] a fourth time; μεταμορφόω, "are being transformed," filed [13.53], "to change the essential form or nature of something" — not under its other sense, a change of outward appearance; δόξα filed [79.18] at all three occurrences in the verse.
  - The likeness word in the New Testament (Jas 3:9): "With it we bless our Lord and Father, and with it we curse people, who have been made in the likeness of God;" — James's word is not εἰκών but ὁμοίωσις (καθ' ὁμοίωσιν θεοῦ), and it occurs at this verse only in the New Testament, filed [64.3], "the state of being similar to something." The people cursed are people generally, and what they have been made according to is God's likeness; the verse hangs the blessing and the cursing on the making, as Genesis 9:6 hangs the blood law on the image.
  - The face (2 Cor 4:6): "For God, who said, “Light shall shine out of darkness,” is the One who has shone in our hearts to give the Light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Christ." πρόσωπον, "face," is filed [8.18], its plain sense, "the front part of the human head"; δόξα is filed [79.18] here as at 3:18. The God who cannot be seen (Col 1:15) makes His glory known in a face — the face of the One who is His image (4:4).
- **The close (settled):** (1) In this set the image is never placed in one part of the man. Its bearer is the man as made — mankind, male and female (Gen 1:27); the people we curse (Jas 3:9); "we all" who are being transformed (2 Cor 3:18). (Pointer, at witness strength: KB049 concludes the same from its own ground — the image's bearer is the whole man, body included — and offers 1 Thess 5:23 as a witness against placing the image in one part; the witness is claimed as a pointer, not as a text of this set.) (2) The set speaks of the image as standing after the flood and in the present: it grounds the blood law spoken to Noah's world (Gen 9:6), Paul states it of man in the present tense (ὑπάρχων, 1 Cor 11:7), and James speaks of people as those who "have been made" in God's likeness (γεγονότας, Jas 3:9). Whatever the fall changed, these texts do not speak of the image as gone.
- **The close (proposed):** Craig's hearing, in his words, labeled and not crowned as the verses' saying (2 Cor 4:6 with 3:18): "Man is created in the image and the likeness of God; when you see a man in whom Christ is living, you are seeing God in the face of Christ, but the man is not God."
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired: its construction — mankind as a physical body designed to be indwelt and animated, a receptive home for the Father's breath — is stated by no text in this set. The breath ground it leans on is Genesis 2:7, which is KB049's closed ground, not this entry's; the construction is neither carried nor denied here, and stands or falls at its own texts. (2) The rest of each word's places are not in this entry, and here is why: those places use the words for things men make or see — carved and molten images and painted pictures (צֶלֶם's first sense in BDB), a man as a mere shadow (its third sense), Ezekiel's "the likeness of…" vision descriptions (the greater part of דְּמוּת's places, on BDB's own list), Caesar's picture on a coin and the beast's statue in Revelation (εἰκών's other places) — and the dictionaries file those places under different senses from the places where God makes man and where Christ and man are named. An entry closes one use of a word on the texts that use it that way; it is not a claim about every place the word appears, and these texts are not needed for the places named here to say what they say. Romans 8:29, Colossians 3:10, and 1 Corinthians 15:49, where believers are conformed to or bear the image of the Son, are spent in their own entries and stand reachable by pointer; they are not re-spent here. (3) No text in this set says what the resemblance consists in — no faculty is named, no list is given. The entry closes the words' filings and what the texts themselves attach to the making — the ruling spoken with it in one sentence (Gen 1:26), the blood law grounded in it (Gen 9:6), the blessing and the cursing grounded in it (Jas 3:9), glory (1 Cor 11:7), transformation (2 Cor 3:18) — not a definition of the image's content, which the texts leave unstated. (4) No account of the fall stands in this set; close (2) says only what the post-flood and New Testament texts themselves say, in their own tenses, and nothing about what the fall did or did not do to the image beyond that. (5) 1 Corinthians 11:7 is spent for its image statement alone, and the statement's subject is the verse's own: the man (ἀνήρ), set in the same sentence over against the woman as the glory of man; the entry does not widen the verse's subject to mankind at large, and the head-covering argument the statement serves, and the glory-of-man clause beyond the filing noted in the chain, are not closed here. (6) No Septuagint text stands in this set: the New Testament side rests on εἰκών's own filings at its verses, and no claim is made here about how the Greek word carries the Hebrew pair. (7) John 14:9 is not in this set, and nothing in this entry rests on it; the proposed close's face-of-Christ sentence rests on 2 Cor 4:6, with 3:18, both in the set.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The statue reading: the image of God is a carved or visible portrait — man as God's statue or physical copy. Answered: the carved-object places are real, and the dictionaries file them apart — צֶלֶם's statues and pictures under its first sense, εἰκών's formed-object sense ([6.96]) at its own places. At the places where God makes man, BDB files צֶלֶם "of resemblance," and at all four places where εἰκών names a bearer in this set it is filed [58.35], "that which has the same form as something else." The word can name a statue — elsewhere, and filed so; at these verses it does not. (b) The lost-image reading: whatever the image was, it was lost at the fall, and men no longer bear it. Answered: the texts that speak after the fall do not speak that way — Genesis 9:6 grounds the blood law in the image for Noah's world, Paul says man *is* the image and glory of God, and James's cursed people are those who have been made in God's likeness. What the fall did beyond this set's speech is left where Honesty (4) leaves it. (c) The one-part reading: the image is a single part or faculty of the man — his spirit, or his reason, or his moral sense. Answered: no text in the set locates the image in a part, and the bearers named are whole people — mankind male and female, the cursed, "we all" — and Honesty (3) records that the set never names a content the faculty could be. KB049's witness stands beside this: the bearer is the whole man, body included. (d) The function-only reading: "image" names only what man does — his rule over creation — or only his relationships, not anything about what he is. Answered: Genesis 1:26 speaks the making first and the ruling as what follows it ("in Our image, according to Our likeness; *and* let them rule"), and the later texts hang the image on the making, not on the ruling — the blood law because "in the image of God He *made* mankind" (9:6), the tongue because people "have been *made*" in God's likeness (Jas 3:9). The rule rides on the making in these texts; it is not offered as the making's definition. (e) The Christ-only reading: Christ is the image; men are called image only in Him and derivatively, never in themselves. Answered: Genesis speaks the image of mankind as made, before any question of renewal; Paul states it of the man (1 Cor 11:7), and James of people generally, including the ones being cursed (Jas 3:9). The renewal texts that do speak of the image in Christ (Rom 8:29; Col 3:10; 1 Cor 15:49) are spent in their own entries; nothing in them is needed to answer this reading, and nothing here denies them.

### The Two Mankinds (In Adam and In Christ)

- **ID:** KB061
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** Ἀδάμ, ἄνθρωπος, τύπος, δικαίωμα, δικαίωσις, παρακοή, ὑπακοή, χοϊκός, ἐπουράνιος, καινός
- **Hebrew keys:** אָדָם, אֲדָמָה, יֵצֶר, אִישׁ הָאֲדָמָה
- **Verse keys:** Gen 1:26–27; Gen 2:7; Gen 3:17; Gen 3:19; Gen 5:1–3; Gen 5:29; Gen 6:5; Gen 8:21; Gen 9:20; Rom 5:12–21; 1 Cor 15:22–23; 1 Cor 15:45; 1 Cor 15:47–49; 2 Cor 5:17; Eph 2:15
- **Kind:** datum
- **Claim:** The earthy ones (οἱ χοϊκοί) stand under the first man, Adam — formed from the ground (הָאֲדָמָה), the intent of man's heart standing under God's verdict of evil before the flood and again after it (Gen 6:5; 8:21), death reigning through him (Rom 5:14) — and the heavenly ones (οἱ ἐπουράνιοι) under the second man, the last Adam, who in Himself made one new man (καινὸν ἄνθρωπον) (Eph 2:15), and through whose one act of righteousness “the result was justification of life to all mankind” (Rom 5:18).
- **The chain:**
  - The name is the kind's name (Gen 1:26–27; 5:1–3): God makes אָדָם — “Let Us make mankind in Our image” — and the recap says it outright: “He created them male and female, and He blessed them and named them ‘mankind’ on the day when they were created” (5:2) — the word rendered “mankind” is אָדָם. The dictionaries keep the double use in one lemma: BDB (H120) glosses the headword itself “man, mankind.” The one word is the first man's name and his kind's name together, and Genesis spends it both ways.
  - Made from the ground (Gen 2:7): “Then the LORD God formed the man of dust from the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and the man became a living person” (אֶת־הָֽאָדָם… מִן־הָאֲדָמָה). BDB (H127) glosses אֲדָמָה “ground, land”; the UBS Hebrew definition carries the arc in its own sentence: the surface “containing the substance that God used to create animals and humans, and to which they return when they die.”
  - The ground cursed, the return appointed (Gen 3:17, 19): “Cursed is the ground because of you; With hard labor you shall eat from it All the days of your life… Until you return to the ground, Because from it you were taken; For you are dust, And to dust you shall return” (אֲרוּרָה הָאֲדָמָה… אֶל־הָאֲדָמָה). The first mankind's whole span stands in two verses: taken from the ground, laboring on it under its curse, returning to it.
  - Comfort hoped for from the cursed ground (Gen 5:29): at Noah's naming his father speaks the hope the verdict will answer: “This one will give us comfort from our work and from the hard labor of our hands caused by the ground which the LORD has cursed” (מִן־הָאֲדָמָה אֲשֶׁר אֵרְרָהּ יְהוָה). The hope is real and named; what the flood changes is God's dealing, not the man's heart — as the verdict itself, stated on either side of the water, shows.
  - The verdict, on both sides of the flood (Gen 6:5; 8:21): before the flood, “every intent of the thoughts of their hearts was only evil continually” (וְכָל־יֵצֶר מַחְשְׁבֹת לִבּוֹ רַק רַע כָּל־הַיּוֹם); after it, God states the verdict as His reason for mercy: “for the intent of man's heart is evil from his youth” (כִּי יֵצֶר לֵב הָאָדָם רַע מִנְּעֻרָיו). יֵצֶר (BDB H3336, “form, framing, purpose”) names the heart's formed bent in both places. The two statements share their core — יֵצֶר, the heart, evil (רַע) — and differ at their edges: before the flood, “only evil continually” (רַק רַע כָּל־הַיּוֹם); after it, “evil from his youth” (רַע מִנְּעֻרָיו). In both, the heart diagnosed is the one heart of man — לִבּוֹ, “his heart,” at 6:5; לֵב הָאָדָם, “the heart of man,” at 8:21; the NASB's plural “their hearts” at 6:5 is the translation's smoothing, not the Hebrew. The flood passes; the core stands. What God changes after the flood is His own dealing (“I will never again curse the ground on account of man”), and the verse itself gives the verdict as His reason.
  - The man through whom mankind restarts is designated by the soil (Gen 9:20): “Then Noah began farming and planted a vineyard” — Noah is the one man in Scripture designated אִישׁ הָאֲדָמָה, the man of the ground: the description Adam's making gave (formed of dust from the ground, Gen 2:7) now standing as the restart man's designation. The pattern restarts with him where it started: a man of the ground, his hands in a planting. The new beginning is headed by the old kind of man.
  - The New Testament confirms the judgment, and sets the other end in the same passage (Rom 5:12–21): “through one man sin entered into the world, and death through sin, and so death spread to all mankind, because all sinned” (5:12); “death reigned from Adam until Moses… who is a type of Him who was to come” (5:14 — τύπος filed [58.63], Ἀδάμ [93.11]). Against that reign the passage sets its own other end: “through one act of righteousness the result was justification of life to all mankind” (5:18). The dictionary itself draws the passage's line: δικαίωμα at 5:16 is filed [56.34], the verdict the gift resulted in (and δικαίωσις at 5:18 is [56.34] too), while δικαίωμα at 5:18 is filed [88.14], the righteous act itself — and the NASB tracks the split (“resulting in justification” / “one act of righteousness”). The pair of the passage is obedience and disobedience, filed as a pair (παρακοή [36.27], ὑπακοή [36.15]): “through the one man's disobedience the many were made sinners, so also through the obedience of the One the many will be made righteous” (5:19). Each mankind is constituted by its head's act — were made, will be made — and the passage ends with the two reigns: “as sin reigned in death, so also grace would reign through righteousness to eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord” (5:21). (Pointer: the sons-and-heirs side of this reign, Rom 8:14–18, is KB052's ground, and Rom 8:17's fellow-heir horizon is quoted at KB064.)
  - The two “all”s, bounded by the passage itself (Rom 5:17–18): both ends of 5:18 run “to all mankind,” but the passage has already named the membership of those in Christ: “those who receive the abundance of grace and of the gift of righteousness,” of whom the verse says they “reign in life through the One, Jesus Christ” (5:17). The first “all” stands under the passage's own statement — “death spread to all mankind, because all sinned” (5:12); the second is bounded by receiving. The entry carries both bounds as the passage states them and no further (Honesty (2)).
  - The kinds named (1 Cor 15:22–23, 45, 47–49): “For as in Adam all die, so also in Christ all will be made alive” (ἐν τῷ Ἀδάμ… ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ, 15:22). Both “all”s stand inside the verse's own locatives — the first bounded by “in Adam,” the second by “in Christ” — the bound the verse itself carries (Honesty (2)). The next verse names the second company's members in Paul's own words: “But each in his own order: Christ the first fruits, after that those who are Christ's at His coming” (15:23). Paul names the heads both ways: “‘The first MAN, Adam, BECAME A LIVING PERSON.’ The last Adam was a life-giving spirit” (15:45), and “The first man is from the earth, earthy; the second man is from heaven” (15:47) — χοϊκός. Then the kinds, in so many words: “As is the earthy one, so also are those who are earthy; and as is the heavenly one, so also are those who are heavenly” (15:48) — οἱ χοϊκοί, οἱ ἐπουράνιοι — and the image carried across: “Just as we have borne the image of the earthy, we will also bear the image of the heavenly” (15:49; the image itself is KB060's subject). χοϊκός is double-filed at 15:47 — [2.16], “pertaining to being made or consisting of dust or soil,” and [1.42], “having the nature of earthly existence in contrast with that which is heavenly” — both filings stand; the entry crowns neither (Honesty (4)).
  - The new man (2 Cor 5:17; Eph 2:15): “if anyone is in Christ, this person is a new creation; the old things passed away; behold, new things have come” (καινὴ κτίσις) (2 Cor 5:17 — the identity clause is verbless in the Greek, and the NASB's “this person is” is supplied; Honesty (6)). And those in Him named as one man: in Himself He made “the two one new person” — εἰς ἕνα καινὸν ἄνθρωπον, “into one new man” (Eph 2:15). καινός is filed [58.71] at both verses (at 5:17, twice) — the new creation of the person and the new man of the peoples carry one filing. The newness is the filing's own kind of new — its domain is “New, Old [primarily non-temporal],” and its definition sets the new over the old as superior — not the first company merely refreshed; and what is made is one man, not two — the pair Paul treats in Ephesians is Jew and Gentile, and the word he spends is the corporate singular ἄνθρωπος, made in Christ Himself (ἐν αὐτῷ).
- **The close (settled):** (1) Each company takes its head's kind: earthy under Adam, whose heart's intent stands under God's verdict of evil on both sides of the flood; heavenly under Christ, the last Adam and second man, through whose one act of righteousness the result was justification of life to all mankind. (2) The first mankind is not reformed anywhere in this set: the flood changes God's dealing with the ground, and God Himself states the unchanged verdict as His reason; the way out of the first mankind is not its improvement but the second head and the receiving of His gift (Rom 5:17). (3) The second company is one new man — καινός, the new set over the old — and its door is “anyone in Christ” (2 Cor 5:17): one ἄνθρωπος made in Himself (Eph 2:15), the pair of its own passage, Jew and Gentile, made one in Him.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired: its title (“Ish Ha'adamah (The Man of the Soil)”) names only the first mankind, and its vine-and-grafting remedy (John 15; Rom 11) is not spent here — both passages stand outside this entry's set, their reasons recorded in the closed set file; the entry's remedy side is carried by Rom 5 and the καινός verses Craig ruled in. The stub's “human heroes are merely repetitions of Adam” is carried at the strength the texts give — the verdict pair and Noah's designation — and “heroes” as a class is not spent. (2) The two “all”s are carried at the passage's own bounds (Rom 5:12, 17) and no further: 1 Cor 15:22's own bound is its locative: its second “all” stands ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ, as its first stands ἐν τῷ Ἀδάμ. 1 Cor 15:23, pasted by Craig and spent in the chain, names the same bound in Paul's words: “those who are Christ's at His coming.” The extent of the second “all” beyond the texts' bounds is not decided by this entry. (3) Gal 3:28's wording is not spent: Craig's inclusivity summary (“no Jew, Greek, slave, free, male, female”) is his, and stands recorded as his ruling on Eph 2:15's scope; the close's third point rests on the in-set texts (the corporate singular ἄνθρωπος, made in Him; “anyone in Christ”), and the verse itself — whose word is εἷς, “one,” not καινός — is not quoted or banked in this set. (4) χοϊκός's double filing ([2.16] and [1.42]) is kept as a fork, not blended: the entry does not rest the kind-reading on either filing alone but on 15:48's own sentence, which sorts the peoples by their heads. (5) Gen 9:21–27 (Noah's drunkenness, nakedness, and the curse on Canaan) is not spent: the entry spends the designation verse, 9:20 and the verdicts, and the narrative stands in its own passage, neither used nor denied. (6) Translation notes, carried not hidden: at 2 Cor 5:17 the NASB supplies “this person is” where the Greek identity clause (εἴ τις ἐν Χριστῷ, καινὴ κτίσις) has no verb; at Rom 5:18 the Greek likewise has no verb — the NASB's “the result was” is supplied — and the Claim carries that verse in its quoted words; at Gen 5:2 the NASB renders אָדָם “mankind” where the Hebrew is the same word as the man's name; at Gen 6:5 the NASB's plural “their hearts” smooths the Hebrew's singular לִבּוֹ, “his heart”; at Rom 5:16/18 the NASB's differing renderings of δικαίωμα track the dictionary's split filing. No claim in this entry rests on a supplied or expanded word. (7) יֵצֶר's other places (Deut 31:21; 1 Chr 28:9; 29:18; Ps 103:14, “our form”; Isa 26:3, “steadfast of mind”; the formed-thing places Isa 29:16; Hab 2:18) stand listed in the closed set file: the entry closes the verdict pair, and the verdict is God's stated assessment of man's heart, not a definition of the word. Deut 31:21 — the verdict extended to Israel before the land — is named in the closed set as the pair's own continuation, by pointer. (8) No verse in this set uses the phrase “two mankinds”: the title is Craig's, and the chain carries the thought in the texts' own words — the heads (first man, last Adam, second man) and the kinds (οἱ χοϊκοί, οἱ ἐπουράνιοι; in Adam, in Christ). (9) אִישׁ with a noun is a common construct for a man characterized by a thing (a man of war, a man of the field); what is singular at Gen 9:20 is its object — the ground itself — and that the designation occurs once in Scripture, of the man through whom mankind restarts. Cain's phrase is the verb form, a worker of the ground (עֹבֵד אֲדָמָה), named here as a wording contrast only; his panel is unspent.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The universalist reading: both “all”s of Rom 5:18 name the same totality, so all mankind stands in Christ as all stands in Adam. Answered: the passage bounds its own second “all” — the second mankind is “those who receive the abundance of grace” (5:17), and its verb is future (“will be made righteous,” 5:19), where the first company's constitution is stated as accomplished fact by the passage's own verb (“the many were made sinners,” 5:19; “death spread to all… because all sinned,” 5:12). The entry carries the passage's bounds and decides nothing beyond them (Honesty (2)). (b) The reset reading: the flood washed the world and Noah's righteousness restarted mankind clean; the old nature was judged away. Answered: God's post-flood statement gives the pre-flood verdict again in its core words — “the intent of man's heart is evil from his youth” — and gives it as the reason for His mercy, not as a thing the water cured; and the man through whom mankind restarts is designated man of the soil (Gen 9:20), the first man's own description, not a new kind. (c) The composition reading: χοϊκός is about body material only — dust versus spirit-stuff — and sorts no one morally or by kind.
