Answered: the word's own sentence sorts peoples, not materials — “as is the earthy one, so also are those who are earthy” (15:48) — and 15:49 carries it as an image borne by persons; the dictionary files it in the earthly-existence sense ([1.42]) alongside the dust sense ([2.16]), and the entry crowns neither filing because the verse does the sorting itself. (d) The example reading: Adam in Rom 5 is an individual illustration, and headship is a system built on the passage rather than read from it. Answered: the passage's grammar is corporate on its face — Adam is “a type of Him who was to come” (τύπος [58.63], 5:14), death “reigned” through him (5:17), and the many “were made sinners” through his disobedience (5:19); the rival must dissolve the reign and constitution verbs, not merely the type word. (e) The two-peoples-only reading of Eph 2:15: the “one new man” is a Jew–Gentile polity arrangement and names nothing about mankind in Christ as such. Answered: the pair Paul treats there is indeed Jew and Gentile — the entry says so in its own chain — but the thing made is εἰς ἕνα καινὸν ἄνθρωπον, one new *man*, the same καινός [58.71] as the new creation that meets “anyone in Christ” (2 Cor 5:17). The inclusive scope is Craig's ruling on this verse, recorded here as a ruling and not as a receipt — the answer above stands on the verse's own words.

### Pneumasin (Spirits) vs. Psychais (Souls)

- **ID:** KB062
- **Facet of:** *none*
- **Greek keys:** τοῖς ἐν φυλακῇ πνεύμασιν, πνεύμασιν δικαίων τετελειωμένων, πνεύμασιν πλάνοις, τῷ πατρὶ τῶν πνευμάτων, πνεύματα προφητῶν, ὀκτὼ ψυχαί, τὰς ψυχὰς τῶν ἐσφαγμένων
- **Verse keys:** 1 Pet 3:19; 1 Pet 3:20; Heb 12:23; Heb 12:9; 1 Tim 4:1; 1 Cor 14:32; 1 Cor 14:12; 1 John 4:1; Heb 1:14; Acts 2:41; Acts 27:37; Rev 6:9; Rev 20:4
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Across the New Testament's whole plural usage — πνεῦμα 34 occurrences, ψυχή 26, both sets fixed by morphology rather than picked — plural πνεῦμα names spirit-beings (evil, of God, angelic, or standing to be tested) or human spirits apart from the body, and never once counts or addresses living persons, while plural ψυχή names persons themselves — counted whole (Acts 2:41; Acts 27:37; 1 Peter 3:20), their lives staked or laid down, their souls strengthened and shepherded, and still ψυχαί when slain (Revelation 6:9; 20:4) — so that at 1 Peter 3:19–20 the proclamation's audience are πνεύμασιν, spirits apart from bodies as at Hebrews 12:23, and the saved are ὀκτὼ ψυχαί, eight persons, each word used as the complete sets use them.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Pet 3:19–20: ἐν ᾧ καὶ τοῖς ἐν φυλακῇ πνεύμασιν πορευθεὶς ἐκήρυξεν ἀπειθήσασίν ποτε, ὅτε ἀπεξεδέχετο ἡ τοῦ θεοῦ μακροθυμία ἐν ἡμέραις Νῶε κατασκευαζομένης κιβωτοῦ εἰς ἣν ὀλίγοι, τοῦτʼ ἔστιν ὀκτὼ ψυχαί, διεσώθησαν διʼ ὕδατος — "in which He also went and made proclamation to the spirits in prison, who once were disobedient when the patience of God kept waiting in the days of Noah, during the construction of the ark, in which a few, that is, eight persons, were brought safely through the water." The instance itself: the audience of the proclamation are πνεύμασιν (dative plural) and the saved of the same sentence are ψυχαί (nominative plural) — the two words of this entry, spent by one writer in one breath, each on its own side of the distribution the rest of the chain displays.
  - Heb 12:23: καὶ πνεύμασιν δικαίων τετελειωμένων — "and to the spirits of the righteous made perfect." The only other place the plural names human spirits apart from the body: they stand in the assembly the writer says his readers *have come to* — myriads of angels, the church of the firstborn enrolled in heaven, God the Judge of all, and these πνεύμασιν. Human, righteous, perfected — and named with the being-word, not the person-word.
  - 1 Tim 4:1: προσέχοντες πνεύμασιν πλάνοις καὶ διδασκαλίαις δαιμονίων — "paying attention to deceitful spirits and teachings of demons." The dative plural's other pole: beings who deceive. With Mark 1:27 and Luke 4:36 (unclean spirits, dative), the dative class is five: three evil-being, two human-apart-from-body — and no living person in it.
  - Heb 1:14: πνεύματα (nominative) — "Are they not all ministering spirits, sent out to provide service for the sake of those who will inherit salvation?" The angels named with the plural as a class of beings; at 1:7 the same word stands accusative of the same angels ("He makes His angels πνεύματα," NASB "winds"). Angelic beings are inside the plural's range; living human persons are not.
  - 1 John 4:1: τὰ πνεύματα (accusative) — "do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits to see whether they are from God." The tested class: plural πνεύματα as sources standing for discernment, never as a congregation of living people addressed or counted.
  - Heb 12:9: οὐ πολὺ μᾶλλον ὑποταγησόμεθα τῷ πατρὶ τῶν πνευμάτων καὶ ζήσομεν — "shall we not much more be subject to the Father of spirits, and live?" τῶν πνευμάτων stands absolute — no dependent genitive names whose spirits — and the verse's own contrast carries the human reading: fathers *of our flesh* (τῆς σαρκὸς ἡμῶν πατέρας) on one side, the Father of *the spirits* on the other. The entry records the construction as it stands: a spirits-of-persons use, its genitive unattached.
  - 1 Cor 14:32: πνεύματα προφητῶν — "and the spirits of prophets are subject to prophets." The faculty use: living prophets' own spirits, named as what is subject to them — the plural here names something *of* persons, never the persons themselves; the persons in the verse are προφῆται, not πνεύματα. Revelation 22:6 ("the God of the spirits of the prophets," τῶν πνευμάτων τῶν προφητῶν) carries the same phrase; whether its prophets are living or departed the word does not decide, and the entry does not decide it either.
  - 1 Cor 14:12: πνευμάτων (genitive) — "since you are eager to possess spiritual gifts" (NASB). The set's one non-referent plural: manifestations coveted, not beings named. It stands inside the 34 and touches neither side of the correlation — it names no persons, living or dead.
  - Acts 2:41: ψυχαί (nominative) — "there were added about three thousand souls." The counting use in its plainest form: whole living persons, numbered as they are added. The word that counts people is ψυχή; in 34 plurals πνεῦμα never does this once.
  - Acts 27:37: ψυχαί (nominative) — "We were 276 people on the ship in all." The exact twin of the instance's second half: persons on a vessel, in peril of water, counted as ψυχαί — as the eight in the ark are counted at 1 Peter 3:20. (Acts 7:14 counts the same way, seventy-five persons, dative; Revelation 18:13 lists ψυχὰς ἀνθρώπων, human lives, as cargo.)
  - Rev 6:9–10: εἶδον ὑποκάτω τοῦ θυσιαστηρίου τὰς ψυχὰς τῶν ἐσφαγμένων διὰ τὸν λόγον τοῦ θεοῦ καὶ διὰ τὴν μαρτυρίαν ἣν εἶχον· καὶ ἔκραξαν φωνῇ μεγάλῃ — "I saw underneath the altar the souls of those who had been killed because of the word of God, and because of the testimony which they had maintained; and they cried out with a loud voice." The breaker of the stub's wording and the making of the corrected one: the slain are ψυχαί — the person-word, spent on the dead, who are still themselves and still speaking.
  - Rev 20:4: ψυχάς (accusative) — "And I saw the souls of those who had been beheaded because of their testimony of Jesus and because of the word of God … and they came to life and reigned with Christ for a thousand years." The second martyr text: ψυχαί of the executed, seen as persons before they come to life — the word names who they are, not whether they are breathing.
- **The close (settled):** Three things stand on the complete sets. First, the πνεῦμα side: of 34 plurals, 20 name evil or unclean spirit-beings (Matt 8:16; 10:1; 12:45; Mark 1:27; 3:11; 5:13; 6:7; Luke 4:36; 6:18; 7:21; 8:2; 10:20; 11:26; Acts 5:16; 8:7; 19:12; 19:13; 1 Tim 4:1; Rev 16:13; 16:14), 4 the seven Spirits of God (Rev 1:4; 3:1; 4:5; 5:6), 2 angels (Heb 1:7; 1:14), 2 spirits standing to be tested or discerned (1 Cor 12:10; 1 John 4:1), 2 human spirits apart from the body (Heb 12:23; 1 Pet 3:19), 3 the spirits *of* persons (1 Cor 14:32; Heb 12:9; Rev 22:6), and 1 names no referent at all (1 Cor 14:12) — and across all 34 the plural never counts, addresses, or names a group of living human persons. Second, the ψυχή side: of 26 plurals, 5 count whole living persons or trade them as people (Acts 2:41; 7:14; 27:37; 1 Pet 3:20; Rev 18:13), 4 stake or lay down lives (Acts 15:26; 27:10; 1 Thess 2:8; 1 John 3:16), 14 name the souls or selves of living persons — rested, strengthened, upset, saved, purified, watched over, guarded (Matt 11:29; Luke 21:19; Acts 14:2; 14:22; 15:24; 2 Cor 12:15; Heb 12:3; 13:17; James 1:21; 1 Pet 1:9; 1:22; 2:25; 4:19; 2 Pet 2:14), 1 names living creatures (Rev 8:9), and 2 name the martyred dead as persons (Rev 6:9; 20:4) — the word tracks the person in every state, never the breath. Third, the instance: 1 Peter 3:19–20 spends both words exactly as the sets spend them — πνεύμασιν for the audience apart from bodies, ὀκτὼ ψυχαί for the persons in the ark — and the counting parallel is exact: when the New Testament counts living people it always counts ψυχαί (Acts 2:41; 7:14; 27:37), never πνεύματα.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Retired from the stub, and recorded here: its ψυχή gloss — "*psychais* refers to living, breathing humans" — broken by the stub's own word at Revelation 6:9 and 20:4, where ψυχαί are the slain. The stub's πneῦma gloss stands only as refined by the set: it had no room for the spirits-*of*-persons uses (1 Cor 14:32; Heb 12:9; Rev 22:6) or the gift-use (1 Cor 14:12), which stand in the close as qualifications. The title stands unchanged because it names the two forms at the instance, not the stub's glosses. (2) What the distribution decides and what it does not: the sets narrow 1 Peter 3:19's πνεύμασιν to spirits apart from bodies — the form never names living humans on earth (0 of 34) — but the distribution alone does not choose between the human spirits of Noah's generation and non-human spirit-beings — nor does it need to: the verse identifies its audience itself, as the ones who disobeyed when God's patience waited in the days of Noah, a dated act of the flood generation, the same moment whose eight survivors the next clause names as ψυχαί; Hebrews 12:23 stands as the plural's other human use. (3) Hebrews 12:9's τῶν πνευμάτων carries no dependent genitive; its human reading rests on the verse's flesh/spirits contrast and is recorded as carried by that contrast. Revelation 22:6's prophets — living or departed — are not decided by the phrase and are not decided here. (4) Scope: the plurals only. The singulars stand outside this close — the singular πneῦμα is the Holy Spirit's own name across the same books, and the singular ψυχή has its own range; nothing here is a claim about either. (5) The stub's closing sentence — the proclamation "witnessing to a life that remains active and gathered to the Lord even while the flesh rests in the dust" — is the closed conclusion of KB050, KB088, and KB089 and stands as a pointer from this entry, not as a claim re-closed by it.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The stub's own reading: plural ψυχή names living, breathing humans, so the dead are never ψυχαί. Answered: the word names the slain under the altar, who cry out (Rev 6:9–10), and the beheaded, who come to life and reign (Rev 20:4) — the plural names persons in every state the texts present, and its living uses divide into counting, staking, and shepherding, none of which is a breath test. (b) The fallen-angel reading of 1 Peter 3:19: the πνεύμασιν are the imprisoned beings of the Genesis 6 tradition, not human dead. Answered at the level the evidence reaches: the distribution gives this reading its room — 26 of the 34 plurals are non-human — and takes away its compulsion, since the plural also names human spirits apart from the body (Heb 12:23); the verse's own words then decide what the form leaves open: the spirits' disobedience is dated to the days of Noah, *when the patience of God kept waiting* — the waiting that the same sentence ties to the ark and its eight ψυχαί — and Peter names no other company in the passage; the identification is the Noahic generation's spirits, identified by the verse's own dated description (Honesty (2)) — and the angel reading, to stand, must add what the text does not state: Genesis 6's sons of God read as angels and a prison tradition Peter never mentions. (c) The through-Noah reading (Augustine's): Christ preached through Noah to people then living, who are spirits in prison only now. Answered: the audience stands in the dative as πνεύμασιν at the proclamation itself — ἐν ᾧ … τοῖς ἐν φυλακῇ πνεύμασιν πορευθεὶς ἐκήρυξεν, "in which, having gone to the spirits in prison, He proclaimed" — the going and the proclaiming are the Anointed One's acts in the state 3:18 has just named (put to death in flesh, made alive in spirit), and the plural he spends on the audience is a form that names living persons zero times in 34; the people alive in Noah's day enter his sentence under the other word, as the eight ψυχαί who were being saved through the water. (d) The soul-sleep flattening: ψυχή is the living being and nothing else, so where breath has stopped no one is named or present anywhere. Answered within this set: the texts name the dead with the person-word itself — τὰς ψυχὰς τῶν ἐσφαγμένων, crying out for judgment (Rev 6:9–10) — and the full close of that constellation stands at KB050, KB088, and KB089, pointed to here, not re-argued.

### The Mind Set on the Flesh (Romans 8:5–8)

- **ID:** KB063
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** φρόνημα, φρονέω, κατά, σάρξ, πνεῦμα, θάνατος, ζωή, εἰρήνη, ἔχθρα, νόμος, ὑποτάσσω, δύναμαι, ἀρέσκω
- **Verse keys:** Rom 8:5; Rom 8:6; Rom 8:7; Rom 8:8
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Romans 8:5–8 sets two minds against each other — those who are in accord with the flesh set their minds on the things of the flesh, and those who are in accord with the Spirit, the things of the Spirit; the mind set on the flesh is death, hostile toward God, for it does not subject itself to the law of God, for it is not even able to do so, and those who are in the flesh cannot please God — while the mind set on the Spirit is life and peace.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments, each verified at its verse in the local UBS data): φρόνημα is filed [26.15], "the psychological faculty of thoughtful planning, often with the implication of being wise and provident" (glosses: thoughtful planning, way of thinking, outlook), at every occurrence in this set — twice at Rom 8:6 and once at Rom 8:7. φρονέω at Rom 8:5 is double-filed — [30.20], "to keep on giving serious consideration to something" (glosses: to ponder, to let one's mind dwell on, to fix one's attention on), and [87.12], the honor sense, "to acknowledge the high status of a person or event" — and neither filing is taken here as the sense (Honesty (3)). σάρξ is filed [26.7], "the psychological aspect of human nature which contrasts with the spiritual nature…," at every occurrence in this set — twice at 8:5, and once each at 8:6, 8:7, and 8:8 — not under its body senses at these verses. πνεῦμα is filed [12.18], the Spirit as a title (glosses: Spirit, Spirit of God, Holy Spirit), at 8:5 (twice) and at 8:6 — not under [26.9], the man's own inner being, at these verses. ἔχθρα at 8:7 is filed [39.10], "a state of enmity with someone" (gloss: enmity). ὑποτάσσω at 8:7 is filed [36.18], "to submit to the orders or directives of someone" (glosses: to obey, to submit to). ἀρέσκω at 8:8 is filed [25.90], "to cause someone to be pleased with someone or something" (gloss: to please).
  - The sorting, by what each side has its mind on (Rom 8:5): οἱ γὰρ κατὰ σάρκα ὄντες τὰ τῆς σαρκὸς φρονοῦσιν, οἱ δὲ κατὰ πνεῦμα τὰ τοῦ πνεύματος. — "For those who are in accord with the flesh set their minds on the things of the flesh, but those who are in accord with the Spirit, the things of the Spirit." The verse sorts people by two κατὰ phrases — according to the flesh, according to the Spirit — and the verb φρονοῦσιν stands once, in the first clause, and is carried over to the second: on each side its object is the things of that side — τὰ τῆς σαρκὸς on the one side, τὰ τοῦ πνεύματος on the other. No ἁμαρτία word stands in the verse, and no second self is named in it; the subjects are οἱ ὄντες, the ones who are — ὄντες, like the verb, stands in the first clause and is carried over to the second.
  - What each way of thinking is (Rom 8:6): τὸ γὰρ φρόνημα τῆς σαρκὸς θάνατος, τὸ δὲ φρόνημα τοῦ πνεύματος ζωὴ καὶ εἰρήνη· — "For the mind set on the flesh is death, but the mind set on the Spirit is life and peace," The noun is φρόνημα, a way of thinking, one word on both sides, and the verse says what each side's way of thinking is, in one word or two: the flesh's way of thinking *is* death; the Spirit's *is* life and peace. Both clauses are verbless in the Greek — the NASB's "is" is supplied, on both sides alike — so the entry rests nothing on a supplied word that one side has and the other lacks.
  - Why the flesh's mind is death (Rom 8:7): διότι τὸ φρόνημα τῆς σαρκὸς ἔχθρα εἰς θεόν, τῷ γὰρ νόμῳ τοῦ θεοῦ οὐχ ὑποτάσσεται, οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται· — "because the mind set on the flesh is hostile toward God; for it does not subject itself to the law of God, for it is not even able to do so," The reason is given with διότι, "because": the flesh's way of thinking is ἔχθρα, hostility, directed εἰς θεόν, toward God. Then two statements about it and God's law: it does not submit itself (οὐχ ὑποτάσσεται, present tense), and οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται — it is not even able. The verse states both, in this order: it does not submit, and it is not able.
  - The same inability, stated of the people (Rom 8:8): οἱ δὲ ἐν σαρκὶ ὄντες θεῷ ἀρέσαι οὐ δύνανται. — "and those who are in the flesh cannot please God." What 8:7 says of the mind, 8:8 says of the ones who are ἐν σαρκὶ, in the flesh: οὐ δύνανται, they cannot — and the thing they cannot do is ἀρέσαι, to please, with God in the dative (θεῷ). Within this set, that is the last word on the flesh side: not a command, but a stated cannot.
- **The close (settled):** (1) At these verses "flesh" names one of the two sides of 8:5, and it has a way of thinking of its own — it is not mere body-stuff: the dictionary files σάρξ at every occurrence here under [26.7], the aspect of human nature that contrasts with the spiritual, and the passage's own predicates for that side are thinking, hostility, non-submission, and inability. (2) The flesh's way of thinking does not submit, and it cannot: the text says it does not submit itself *and is not even able to* (8:7), and that those who are in the flesh *cannot* please God (8:8). What stands ruled out is stated in the passage's own words — οὐχ ὑποτάσσεται, οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται, οὐ δύνανται: no submission from that mind, and no pleasing God from those in the flesh. (3) The other side is stated with the same noun and the same plainness: the Spirit's way of thinking is life and peace (8:6) — and "the Spirit" at these verses is the Holy Spirit under His title filing [12.18], not the man's own inner being under another name; the contrast is not between two parts of one man but between the two sides of 8:5 — κατὰ σάρκα and κατὰ πνεῦμα.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The stub is retired whole, and its old title, "The Default Posture of the Flesh (Independent Self-Reliance)," is retired with it; the parts this build drops are named in the build record above the entry — the severed-spirit mechanism, the "instincts" sentence, and the ἁμαρτία sentence, whose subject stands closed at KB041 (the personification) and KB042 (the article) and is not re-argued here. Craig's settled anthropology — "We are not dual natured. We are a person in a body that has not yet been redeemed, with a mind that is in the process of being renewed" — is the ruling this re-cut was made under; its own ground is KB049, where Rom 8:13 and Rom 12:2 are spent, and it is pointed to there, not spent as a receipt of this set. The heart's bent (יֵצֶר, Gen 6:5; 8:21) stands closed at KB061 and is likewise pointed to, not re-spent. (2) The pasted context is not spent: Romans 8:1–4 and 8:9–11 stand in the paste bank as this passage's own frame — Romans 8 beyond this set is pointed to, not quoted: 8:9–10 stands in the pasted sets of KB024 and KB090 — and no sentence in this entry rests on a verse outside the set as it now stands, 8:5–8. In particular, δικαίωμα at 8:4 is filed [33.334], a filing of its own, different from both of Romans 5's filings ([56.34] at 5:16; [88.14] at 5:18, KB061); it is not spent here, and nothing in this entry joins the filings. (3) φρονέω at 8:5 is double-filed ([30.20] and [87.12]); the entry labels the fork and takes neither filing as the sense. The chain rests the sorting on the verse's own κατὰ contrast and on the noun φρόνημα that the next two verses take up under its single filing, not on choosing one of the verb's two filings. (4) φρόνημα's whole New Testament set is four occurrences, all under the one filing [26.15]: twice at Rom 8:6, once at 8:7, and once at 8:27. The 8:27 occurrence stands OUTSIDE this entry's set — fenced here, and spent nowhere in this entry: there the word names the Spirit's own way of thinking, whose work in its passage is pleading for the saints (Rom 8:26–27, pasted by Craig 2026-11-01, banked at hidden_files/kb063-paste-bank-supplement-2026-11-01.md) — the same word under the same filing, but a different subject from the man's mind this entry is about, and Craig ruled it out of the set on that ground (2026-11-01). An earlier note said the word occurs nowhere else in the New Testament; that note was wrong, and the correction stands here so the error is not repeated. (5) These four verses name no Christians, no believers, and no church: they name οἱ κατὰ σάρκα ὄντες (8:5) and, at 8:8, οἱ ἐν σαρκὶ ὄντες, set against οἱ κατὰ πνεῦμα (8:5), and this entry does not assign any named group to either side; who stands on the Spirit side, and how a man comes to stand there, these four verses do not say; the context that does address the readers' side is 8:9–11, at KB024 and KB090. (6) The NASB's "in accord with" renders κατὰ with the accusative at 8:5, and its "mind set on" renders the noun φρόνημα; the entry's claims rest on the Greek — the κατὰ phrases, the one noun on both sides, ἔχθρα, ὑποτάσσεται, and the two δύναμαι statements (δύναται at 8:7; δύνανται at 8:8) — and not on either English phrase. The asterisks in Craig's paste are Logos export artifacts and stand in no quotation here. (7) The dictionary's definition of φρόνημα, quoted in the filings, carries its own general note on the word — "often with the implication of being wise and provident." That implication is the dictionary's word-level note, not a statement about the flesh's way of thinking at 8:6–7; what that way of thinking is, the verses state themselves: death, and hostility toward God.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The body-only reading: "the flesh" in Romans 8 is the physical body, and the passage is about bodily life against spiritual life. Answered: at every occurrence in this set the dictionary files σάρξ [26.7], the aspect of human nature contrasting with the spiritual — not under its body senses — and the passage's own predicates settle what the word is doing here: this flesh has a φρόνημα, a way of thinking (8:6–7), is hostile toward God, and does not submit to His law. What the body itself awaits is another text's statement (Rom 8:23, spent at KB049 and KB045), not this set's. (b) The self-improvement reading: the mind set on the flesh is a bad habit of thought that the self can retrain — by effort, discipline, or resolve — into submitting to God and pleasing Him. Answered in the passage's own strongest words: οὐχ ὑποτάσσεται, it does not submit; οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται, it is not even able to (8:7); οὐ δύνανται, those in the flesh cannot please God (8:8). The passage states an inability twice, of the mind and of the people; a program that promises to fix that mind with that mind is answered before it starts. (c) The two-tenants reading: the passage describes two selves living in the believer at once, a flesh-self and a Spirit-self, each with its own mind. Answered: the passage names no selves and no tenants; its subjects are the ones who are according to the flesh and the ones who are according to the Spirit (8:5), and its noun on both sides is one φρόνημα, a way of thinking — a man stands on a side, he is not two men. The one-person anthropology is closed on its own ground at KB049, and the personified agent of Romans 6–7 is ἡ ἁμαρτία at KB041 — neither is re-argued here, and neither is needed to see that this set spends neither word for a second self. (d) The man's-own-spirit reading: "the Spirit" side of the contrast is the believer's own spirit, his better part, thinking better thoughts. Answered by the filing: πνεῦμα at 8:5 and 8:6 is filed [12.18], the title — Spirit, Spirit of God, Holy Spirit — not [26.9], the man's inner being; and the verse's own parallel keeps the sides personal in different ways: the things *of the flesh* against the things *of the Spirit*. The man's own spirit has its own texts and its own entry (KB049); it is not the πνεῦμα of these verses.

### Two Humanities: Two Heads, Death and New Creation

- **ID:** KB064
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In Paul's supplied texts, mankind stands under two heads — Adam, by
  whose trespass the many were constituted sinners and death passed to all, and
  Christ, the last Adam and second man, by whose obedience the many are
  constituted righteous — and belonging to the new head is by co-death and new
  creation, not by repair of the old man: the old man was crucified and stripped
  off, the new man was created and put on, transfer is named as rescue into the
  Son's kingdom, and bearing the heavenly man's image is still future.
- **The chain:**
  - Rom 5:12, 14: Διὰ τοῦτο ὥσπερ διʼ ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου ἡ ἁμαρτία εἰς τὸν κόσμον
    εἰσῆλθεν καὶ διὰ τῆς ἁμαρτίας ὁ θάνατος, καὶ οὕτως εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους ὁ
    θάνατος διῆλθεν — through *one man* sin entered and death passed to *all*;
    and that one is named: Ἀδὰμ … ὅς ἐστιν τύπος τοῦ μέλλοντος, "Adam, who is a
    type of the coming one." The headship structure is the passage's own: one
    man, with consequences for πάντες / οἱ πολλοί.
  - Rom 5:15, 17–19: the parallel run four times — τῷ τοῦ ἑνὸς παραπτώματι οἱ
    πολλοὶ ἀπέθανον (5:15); ὁ θάνατος ἐβασίλευσεν διὰ τοῦ ἑνός … ἐν ζωῇ
    βασιλεύσουσιν διὰ τοῦ ἑνὸς Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (5:17); διʼ ἑνὸς παραπτώματος εἰς
    πάντας ἀνθρώπους εἰς κατάκριμα … διʼ ἑνὸς δικαιώματος εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους
    εἰς δικαίωσιν ζωῆς (5:18); and the summary: ὥσπερ γὰρ διὰ τῆς παρακοῆς τοῦ
    ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου ἁμαρτωλοὶ κατεστάθησαν οἱ πολλοί, οὕτως καὶ διὰ τῆς ὑπακοῆς
    τοῦ ἑνὸς δίκαιοι κατασταθήσονται οἱ πολλοί (5:19) — "constituted" is the
    text's own verb on both sides (aorist κατεστάθησαν; future
    κατασταθήσονται).
  - 1 Cor 15:45–47: ἐγένετο ὁ πρῶτος ἄνθρωπος Ἀδὰμ εἰς ψυχὴν ζῶσαν, ὁ ἔσχατος
    Ἀδὰμ εἰς πνεῦμα ζῳοποιοῦν — the first man Adam, a living soul; the *last*
    Adam, life-giving spirit. Order is stated (15:46): οὐ πρῶτον τὸ πνευματικὸν
    ἀλλὰ τὸ ψυχικόν, ἔπειτα τὸ πνευματικόν. And alongside the *last* Adam title
    stands a second: ὁ πρῶτος ἄνθρωπος ἐκ γῆς χοϊκός, ὁ δεύτερος ἄνθρωπος ἐξ
    οὐρανοῦ — the first man earthly, the *second* man from heaven. The two titles
    are not merged in the text: ἔσχατος Ἀδὰμ terminates the Adam line; δεύτερος
    ἄνθρωπος heads what follows it.
  - 1 Cor 15:48–49: οἷος ὁ χοϊκός, τοιοῦτοι καὶ οἱ χοϊκοί, καὶ οἷος ὁ
    ἐπουράνιος, τοιοῦτοι καὶ οἱ ἐπουράνιοι — each head has his plural kind, "such
    as he is, such are they." Then the tense guard: καὶ καθὼς ἐφορέσαμεν τὴν
    εἰκόνα τοῦ χοϊκοῦ, φορέσομεν καὶ τὴν εἰκόνα τοῦ ἐπουρανίου — we *have borne*
    (aorist) the earthly image; we *will bear* (future) the heavenly one. The
    image named is the ἐπουράνιος — the second man's descriptor — and its bearing
    is not-yet. Paul's own clincher follows (15:50): σὰρξ καὶ αἷμα βασιλείαν θεοῦ
    κληρονομῆσαι οὐ δύναται οὐδὲ ἡ φθορὰ τὴν ἀφθαρσίαν κληρονομεῖ — flesh and
    blood *cannot* inherit the kingdom, nor corruption incorruption: the old
    kind cannot simply be carried across, which is why the future bearing of
    15:49 is required at all.
  - Rom 6:3–6: ὅσοι ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν, εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ
    ἐβαπτίσθημεν — immersed into Christ is immediately glossed by the text
    itself: into *his death*. συνετάφημεν αὐτῷ (6:4, co-buried). The mechanism
    for the old head's subjects: ὁ παλαιὸς ἡμῶν ἄνθρωπος συνεσταυρώθη, ἵνα
    καταργηθῇ τὸ σῶμα τῆς ἁμαρτίας (6:6) — the old man *was co-crucified*
    (aorist passive: done to him, and done), so that the body of sin might be
    rendered inoperative. The same passage holds both sides of the tense line:
    bodily resurrection is future — τῆς ἀναστάσεως ἐσόμεθα (6:5), συζήσομεν
    αὐτῷ (6:8), "we *shall be* of the resurrection," "we *shall live* with him"
    — while resurrection *life* is the present walk, stated as the co-burial's
    very purpose: ἵνα … ἡμεῖς ἐν καινότητι ζωῆς περιπατήσωμεν (6:4, "that we
    might walk in newness of life"), and commanded now: λογίζεσθε ἑαυτοὺς
    νεκροὺς μὲν τῇ ἁμαρτίᾳ ζῶντας δὲ τῷ θεῷ ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ (6:11, "reckon
    yourselves dead to sin and *living to God* in Christ Jesus");
    παραστήσατε ἑαυτοὺς τῷ θεῷ ὡσεὶ ἐκ νεκρῶν ζῶντας (6:13, "present yourselves
    to God *as living from the dead*").
  - Gal 2:19–20: Χριστῷ συνεσταύρωμαι — perfect: "I have been crucified with
    Christ," a completed act with standing result; ζῶ δὲ οὐκέτι ἐγώ, ζῇ δὲ ἐν
    ἐμοὶ Χριστός — "I no longer live, but Christ lives in me." The first-person
    form of Rom 6:6's co-crucifixion.
  - Eph 2:15–16: ἵνα τοὺς δύο κτίσῃ ἐν αὐτῷ εἰς ἕνα καινὸν ἄνθρωπον ποιῶν
    εἰρήνην, καὶ ἀποκαταλλάξῃ τοὺς ἀμφοτέρους ἐν ἑνὶ σώματι τῷ θεῷ διὰ τοῦ
    σταυροῦ — God in Christ *creates* (κτίσῃ) the two into *one new man*,
    reconciles both in *one body* through the cross. (Scoped in the honesty
    clause: Eph 2's "two" are the far and near peoples of 2:13, 17 — this link
    supplies the created corporate form, not the heads.)
  - Eph 4:22–24; Col 3:9–10 (the clothing pair, two letters, one pattern):
    ἀποθέσθαι … τὸν παλαιὸν ἄνθρωπον … ἐνδύσασθαι τὸν καινὸν ἄνθρωπον τὸν κατὰ
    θεὸν κτισθέντα (Eph 4:22, 24) — the new man is "the one *created* according
    to God" (aorist passive participle); ἀπεκδυσάμενοι τὸν παλαιὸν ἄνθρωπον σὺν
    ταῖς πράξεσιν αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐνδυσάμενοι τὸν νέον τὸν ἀνακαινούμενον … κατʼ
    εἰκόνα τοῦ κτίσαντος αὐτόν (Col 3:9–10) — stripped off the old (aorist:
    done), put on the new, who is *being renewed* (present: ongoing) after the
    Creator's image; and in that new sphere (3:11) οὐκ ἔνι Ἕλλην καὶ Ἰουδαῖος …
    ἀλλὰ πάντα καὶ ἐν πᾶσιν Χριστός. Lexical note (the words themselves differ,
    and the difference is on the page): Ephesians names the new man καινός —
    at 2:15 and here at 4:24, paired with created (κτισθέντα) — while
    Colossians names him νέος (τὸν νέον, 3:10), yet builds his renewal verb
    from the καιν- root (ἀνακαινούμενον, present participle); Ephesians' own
    renewal verb runs the other way (ἀνανεοῦσθαι, present infinitive, 4:23).
    The kind/time glosses for καινός / νέος are lexical proposals; the
    distribution — created-new in Ephesians, young-and-being-renewed in
    Colossians, both renewal verbs present-tense — is the datum the close uses.
  - Col 1:13 (the transfer verse, with its actual object): τῷ πατρὶ … ὃς
    ἐρρύσατο ἡμᾶς ἐκ τῆς ἐξουσίας τοῦ σκότους καὶ μετέστησεν εἰς τὴν βασιλείαν
    τοῦ υἱοῦ τῆς ἀγάπης αὐτοῦ — the Father *rescued us out of* the authority of
    darkness and *transferred us into the kingdom of his beloved Son.* The
    verb's stated destination is a kingdom, and the domain left is an ἐξουσία:
    domain to domain, by the Father's act.
  - Col 1:15, 18 (the Son the transfer delivers us to): ὅς ἐστιν εἰκὼν τοῦ θεοῦ
    τοῦ ἀοράτου, πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως (1:15) — and, resurrection-side: καὶ
    αὐτός ἐστιν ἡ κεφαλὴ τοῦ σώματος τῆς ἐκκλησίας· ὅς ἐστιν ἀρχή, πρωτότοκος ἐκ
    τῶν νεκρῶν, ἵνα γένηται ἐν πᾶσιν αὐτὸς πρωτεύων (1:18) — he is *head of the
    body*, the assembly; he is *beginning* (ἀρχή), *firstborn from the dead*,
    so as to be first in all things. The risen Christ heads a body — the
    corporate form again, now with "first" language on the resurrection side:
    not only a head over individuals, but ἀρχή of what comes from the dead
    after him.
  - 1 Cor 6:17; Rom 8:9–11, 15 (the present union and indwelling): ὁ δὲ
    κολλώμενος τῷ κυρίῳ ἓν πνεῦμά ἐστιν — the one joined to the Lord is one
    spirit with him; πνεῦμα θεοῦ οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν … εἰ δὲ Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν (Rom
    8:9–10) — the Spirit of God *dwells in* you, Christ *in* you; ἐλάβετε πνεῦμα
    υἱοθεσίας ἐν ᾧ κράζομεν· αββα ὁ πατήρ (8:15) — the received Spirit of
    sonship, crying "Abba, Father." What the present walk puts to death is
    named precisely: εἰ δὲ πνεύματι τὰς πράξεις τοῦ σώματος θανατοῦτε, ζήσεσθε
    (8:13) — "if by the Spirit you put to death *the practices of the body*,
    you will live" — the practices (matching Col 3:9's σὺν ταῖς πράξεσιν
    αὐτοῦ), not the old man, who was already crucified (Rom 6:6). Held together
    with the same passage's future: ὁ ἐγείρας Χριστὸν ἐκ νεκρῶν ζῳοποιήσει καὶ
    τὰ θνητὰ σώματα ὑμῶν (8:11) — he *will give life* also to your mortal
    bodies; and the heirs' horizon: συγκληρονόμοι δὲ Χριστοῦ, εἴπερ συμπάσχομεν
    ἵνα καὶ συνδοξασθῶμεν (8:17) … πρὸς τὴν μέλλουσαν δόξαν ἀποκαλυφθῆναι εἰς
    ἡμᾶς (8:18) — co-heirs with Christ, to be *co-glorified* with him, a glory
    *about to be revealed*. Already indwelling; not yet embodied or glorified.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction settles four things. (1) The two-heads
  structure is textual, not assembled: Rom 5 runs the one-man / the-many parallel
  four times with the constitution verb on both sides, and 1 Cor 15 names both
  heads with their plural kinds (οἱ χοϊκοί / οἱ ἐπουράνιοι). (2) The passage from
  one head to the other is by death and creation, never by relocation or repair
  of the old man: he was co-crucified (Rom 6:6) and stripped off (Col 3:9); the
  new man was created (Eph 2:15 κτίσῃ; Eph 4:24 κτισθέντα) and put on (Col
  3:10). (3) Where Paul names a transfer, its object is the Son's kingdom and its
  origin an authority of darkness (Col 1:13) — the old KB wording "transferred
  directly into the Messiah" is not what the transfer verse says; the εἰς
  Χριστόν of Rom 6:3 is glossed by Paul himself as εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ.
  (4) The two sides are both inside the same texts, and neither may be dropped.
  Bodily resurrection, bearing the heavenly image, the vivifying of mortal
  bodies, and co-glorification are future: φορέσομεν (1 Cor 15:49), ἐσόμεθα /
  συζήσομεν (Rom 6:5, 8), ζῳοποιήσει τὰ θνητὰ σώματα (Rom 8:11), συνδοξασθῶμεν
  / τὴν μέλλουσαν δόξαν (Rom 8:17–18) — with 15:50 barring the shortcut (σὰρξ
  καὶ αἷμα βασιλείαν θεοῦ κληρονομῆσαι οὐ δύναται). Walking in newness of life,
  being living to God, and presenting mortal members to him are present:
  περιπατήσωμεν ἐν καινότητι ζωῆς (Rom 6:4), ζῶντας δὲ τῷ θεῷ (6:11), ὡσεὶ ἐκ
  νεκρῶν ζῶντας (6:13), ζῇ δὲ ἐν ἐμοὶ Χριστός (Gal 2:20). Any statement of the
  new humanity that drops either the future verbs or the present-walk verbs has
  left the chain. (Rom 5's own futures are deliberately not counted here — see
  the honesty clause.)
- **The proposed close (marked):** Gloss the corporate result as "the second
  mankind": the δεύτερος ἄνθρωπος (1 Cor 15:47) with his plural kind (οἱ
  ἐπουράνιοι, 15:48), created as ἕνα καινὸν ἄνθρωπον / ἓν σῶμα (Eph 2:15–16),
  headed by the risen one as ἀρχή, πρωτότοκος ἐκ τῶν νεκρῶν, κεφαλὴ τοῦ σώματος
  (Col 1:18) — in Craig's formulation, the last Adam is "the end of the first
  race, and the first new man of a new race." The "first" words are the text's
  (ἀρχή, πρωτότοκος, πρωτεύων); "mankind / race" are the proposer's collective
  glosses for the texts' plurals and corporate singulars, not Greek words in the
  chain; and the image believers will bear is the second man's (τοῦ
  ἐπουρανίου), not a transfer into the last Adam, who is the terminus (ἔσχατος)
  of the first line.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) Eph 2's "two" are the peoples of its own context — the
  far (Gentiles) and the near (Jews), 2:13, 17 — not Adam and Christ. The entry
  uses Eph 2 only for the created one-new-man / one-body form; the heads come
  from Rom 5 and 1 Cor 15. Fusing the two pairs would be a stitched program, and
  the entry does not fuse them. (2) Rom 5:12's ἐφʼ ᾧ πάντες ἥμαρτον is not
  load-bearing here; the heads chain rests on 5:15–19's explicit parallels and
  on 5:14's τύπος, which stand without adjudicating that phrase. (3) "His
  history becomes our history" (the old entries' slogan) is dropped: the texts
  state specific co-events — co-crucified, co-buried, shall-live-with — and the
  entry claims those, not the slogan. (4) σύμφυτοι (Rom 6:5) is not used to
  build an "organic union" metaphysics; the union claims rest on the verses that
  state them (1 Cor 6:17; Rom 8:9–10; Gal 2:20). (5) All the texts in the chain
  are Paul's; the close is a Pauline-distribution close (Rule 2.8 across his
  letters), and the entry claims nothing about how other authors frame Adam and
  Christ. (6) Col 1:15's πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως is quoted as the hymn's
  pairing with 1:18, but the entry builds nothing on the creation-side
  πρωτότοκος debate; its load-bearing "first" text is 1:18's πρωτότοκος ἐκ τῶν
  νεκρῶν, whose sense the verse's own resurrection context fixes. (7) Rom 5's
  futures (βασιλεύσουσιν, 5:17; κατασταθήσονται, 5:19) are *not* counted as
  not-yet evidence in the close: read from Adam's standpoint — Adam is τύπος
  τοῦ μέλλοντος (5:14), a type of the *coming* one — they may be sequence
  futures (what follows from the Coming One's obedience) rather than
  eschatological ones, and they sit beside present possessions (νῦν τὴν
  καταλλαγὴν ἐλάβομεν, 5:11; ὁ ἀποθανὼν δεδικαίωται ἀπὸ τῆς ἁμαρτίας, Rom
  6:7, perfect). The fork is genuine and the entry leaves it open; the not-yet
  rests on the unambiguous bodily futures (1 Cor 15:49–50; Rom 6:5, 8; 8:11,
  17–18).
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The individual-pardon reading: salvation as
  forgiveness of the individual's sins plus moral reform of the same self.
  Answered: the old man is not reformed in these texts — he is crucified
  (συνεσταυρώθη) and stripped off (ἀπεκδυσάμενοι), and the new man is created
  (κτισθέντα) — death and creation verbs, not improvement verbs; and the
  subjects are corporate throughout (οἱ πολλοί, οἱ χοϊκοί / οἱ ἐπουράνιοι, ἕνα
  καινὸν ἄνθρωπον), not a private transaction. (b) The fully-realized reading:
  the new humanity is complete now, image already borne. Answered by the chain's
  own futures (15:49 φορέσομεν; Rom 6:5, 8; Rom 8:11) — the same passages that
  give the already (perfect συνεσταύρωμαι, present ἀνακαινούμενον, present
  indwelling) guard the not-yet, and the close keeps both. (c) The old KB's own
  transfer wording ("transferred directly into the Messiah, His history becomes
  our history") as a rival to this re-cut. Answered at Col 1:13: the one transfer
  verb in the chain (μετέστησεν) takes βασιλείαν as its object, and Rom 6:3's
  εἰς Χριστόν is self-glossed εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ. The slogan assembled what no
  verse states; the verses, and-ed together, state something else — death,
  creation, kingdom, and a future image.

### Transferred from Adam into Christ: Union Is Co-Location

- **ID:** KB090
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ, ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ, διʼ ἀνθρώπου, κατεστάθησαν / κατασταθήσονται, δεδεκάτωται, ἐν τῇ ὀσφύϊ, μετέστησεν εἰς, μεταβέβηκεν / μεταβεβήκαμεν ἐκ ... εἰς, προσεληλύθατε, ἐξεκόπης / ἐνεκεντρίσθης, ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς, σύμφυτοι γεγόναμεν, συνεσταυρώθη / συνεσταύρωμαι, ἐθανατώθητε, καινὴ κτίσις, συνεζωοποίησεν, συνήγειρεν, συνεκάθισεν ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις, κέκρυπται σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ, μένω / οἰκέω / κατοικέω ἐν, παραλήμψομαι, ὅπου εἰμὶ ἐγὼ, φανερωθήσεσθε ἐν δόξῃ, τετελείωκεν εἰς τὸ διηνεκὲς
- **Verse keys:** 1 Cor 15:21–22; Rom 5:15, 18–19; Rom 8:9–10; 1 Cor 1:30; Heb 7:9–10; Col 1:13, 27; John 5:24; 1 John 3:14; 1 Pet 2:9; Heb 12:22; Eph 2:5–6, 13, 19; Rom 11:17, 24; Rom 6:3, 5–6; Gal 2:19–20; Rom 7:4; 2 Cor 5:14, 17; Col 2:13; Col 3:3–4, 9–10; Eph 4:24; Gal 3:27; John 14:3, 20; John 15:4; John 6:56; 2 Cor 13:5; Eph 3:17; John 17:24; Heb 10:14
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Across the supplied New Testament texts, the believer's passage from
  Adam to Christ is a real transfer of location and inclusion — from being *in
  Adam*, sharing his death, to being *in Christ*, sharing His death, resurrection
  life, and heavenly seating — where union with a Person is two-way co-location
  (we in Him and He in us), accomplished through co-crucifixion and new creation
  on the ground of His one offering, while the bodily transformation and unveiled
  manifestation remain future.
- **The chain:**
  - 1 Cor 15:21–22 (the two locations vs. mere agency): ἐπειδὴ γὰρ διʼ ἀνθρώπου
    θάνατος, καὶ διʼ ἀνθρώπου ἀνάστασις νεκρῶν. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ πάντες
    ἀποθνῄσκουσιν, οὕτως καὶ ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ πάντες ζῳοποιηθήσονται — in v. 21 Paul
    uses διὰ + genitive twice (διʼ ἀνθρώπου) to state *agency*; in v. 22 he shifts
    to ἐν + dative with the personal names (ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ ... ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ) to state
    *location / inclusion*, with two corresponding outcomes: dying in the one
    (ἀποθνῄσκουσιν, present), being made alive in the other (ζῳοποιηθήσονται,
    future). The shift from διὰ to ἐν is the text's own, barring the reduction of
    ἐν in 15:22 to mere instrumentality.
  - Rom 5:19 (the representative headship behind the two locations): ὥσπερ γὰρ
    διὰ τῆς παρακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς ἀνθρώπου ἁμαρτωλοὶ κατεστάθησαν οἱ πολλοί, οὕτως
    καὶ διὰ τῆς ὑπακοῆς τοῦ ἑνὸς δίκαιοι κατασταθήσονται οἱ πολλοί — "the many"
    are constituted (κατεστάθησαν, aorist; κατασταθήσονται, future) in their
    standing by the one man's act (διὰ). 1 Cor 15:22 and 1 Cor 1:30 supply the ἐν
    location; Rom 5:19 supplies the representative constitution under each head.
  - Rom 8:9; 1 Cor 1:30 (present location and its source): ὑμεῖς δὲ οὐκ ἐστὲ ἐν
    σαρκὶ ἀλλʼ ἐν πνεύματι, εἴπερ πνεῦμα θεοῦ οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν (Rom 8:9) — locative
    spheres of existence ("not in flesh, but in Spirit"); ἐξ αὐτοῦ δὲ ὑμεῖς ἐστε
    ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ (1 Cor 1:30) — the present state (ἐστε ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ, "you
    are in Christ Jesus") and the actor whose doing put us there (ἐξ αὐτοῦ, "from
    Him," God the Father).
  - Heb 7:9–10; Rom 5:15, 18 (inclusion in a person carries that person's act and
    condition — the Levi receipt): καὶ ὡς ἔπος εἰπεῖν, διʼ Ἀβραὰμ καὶ Λευὶ ὁ
    δεκάτας λαμβάνων δεδεκάτωται· ἔτι γὰρ ἐν τῇ ὀσφύϊ τοῦ πατρὸς ἦν ὅτε
    συνήντησεν αὐτῷ Μελχισέδεκ (Heb 7:9–10) — Levi "has been tithed" (δεδεκάτωται,
    perfect passive) through Abraham because "he was still in the loins of his
    father" (ἐν τῇ ὀσφύϊ τοῦ πατρὸς ἦν) when Melchizedek met him. Being *in* the
    ancestor means participating in his act and state. On the Adamic side, Rom
    5:15 and 5:18 state the same corporate consequence explicitly: τῷ τοῦ ἑνὸς
    παραπτώματι οἱ πολλοὶ ἀπέθανον (5:15, "by the one man's trespass the many
    died") and διʼ ἑνὸς παραπτώματος εἰς πάντας ἀνθρώπους εἰς κατάκριμα (5:18),
    matching 1 Cor 15:22a (ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ πάντες ἀποθνῄσκουσιν).
  - Col 1:13; John 5:24; 1 John 3:14; 1 Pet 2:9; Heb 12:22 (the transfer and
    passage verbs): ὃς ἐρρύσατο ἡμᾶς ἐκ τῆς ἐξουσίας τοῦ σκότους καὶ μετέστησεν
    εἰς τὴν βασιλείαν τοῦ υἱοῦ τῆς ἀγάπης αὐτοῦ (Col 1:13) — the Father rescued
    *us* (ἡμᾶς) *out of* (ἐκ) darkness's authority and *transferred* (μετέστησεν,
    aorist active) us *into* (εἰς) the kingdom of His beloved Son; ἀλλὰ
    μεταβέβηκεν ἐκ τοῦ θανάτου εἰς τὴν ζωήν (John 5:24) and μεταβεβήκαμεν ἐκ τοῦ
    θανάτου εἰς τὴν ζωήν (1 John 3:14) — perfect active of μεταβαίνω: *has passed
    over* out of death into life and stands across; τοῦ ἐκ σκότους ὑμᾶς
    καλέσαντος εἰς τὸ θαυμαστὸν αὐτοῦ φῶς (1 Pet 2:9, called *out of* darkness
    *into* His marvelous light); ἀλλὰ προσεληλύθατε Σιὼν ὄρει καὶ πόλει θεοῦ
    ζῶντος, Ἰερουσαλὴμ ἐπουρανίῳ (Heb 12:22, perfect active: "you *have come* to
    Mount Zion... the heavenly Jerusalem").
  - Eph 2:13, 19; Rom 11:17, 24 (the movement grammar in Gentile-inclusion
    texts): νυνὶ δὲ ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ ὑμεῖς οἵ ποτε ὄντες μακρὰν ἐγενήθητε ἐγγὺς ἐν
    τῷ αἵματι τοῦ Χριστοῦ (Eph 2:13) — distance abolished (μακρὰν ἐγενήθητε
    ἐγγὺς) inside the sphere (ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ) by the blood, so that οὐκέτι ἐστὲ
    ξένοι καὶ πάροικοι ἀλλʼ ἐστὲ συμπολῖται τῶν ἁγίων καὶ οἰκεῖοι τοῦ θεοῦ (Eph
    2:19); εἰ γὰρ σὺ ἐκ τῆς κατὰ φύσιν ἐξεκόπης ἀγριελαίου καὶ παρὰ φύσιν
    ἐνεκεντρίσθης εἰς καλλιέλαιον (Rom 11:24; cf. 11:17, ἐνεκεντρίσθης ἐν αὐτοῖς
    καὶ συγκοινωνὸς τῆς ῥίζης τῆς πιότητος τῆς ἐλαίας ἐγένου) — transfer from an
    old stock into a living root is by a severing cut (ἐξεκόπης, aorist passive)
    and a graft (ἐνεκεντρίσθης, aorist passive), not by self-improvement.
  - Rom 6:3, 5–6; Gal 2:19–20; Rom 7:4; 2 Cor 5:14, 17; Col 2:13; 3:9–10; Eph
    4:24; Gal 3:27 (the road inside the transfer — death and new creation): ὅσοι
    ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν, εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ ἐβαπτίσθημεν (Rom 6:3)
    — immersion *into Christ* is self-glossed by the text as immersion *into His
    death*; εἰ γὰρ σύμφυτοι γεγόναμεν τῷ ὁμοιώματι τοῦ θανάτου αὐτοῦ, ἀλλὰ καὶ
    τῆς ἀναστάσεως ἐσόμεθα (Rom 6:5 — perfect γεγόναμεν for the death likeness;
    future ἐσόμεθα for the resurrection); ὁ παλαιὸς ἡμῶν ἄνθρωπος συνεσταυρώθη
    (Rom 6:6); Χριστῷ συνεσταύρωμαι· ζῶ δὲ οὐκέτι ἐγώ, ζῇ δὲ ἐν ἐμοὶ Χριστός (Gal
    2:19–20); καὶ ὑμεῖς ἐθανατώθητε τῷ νόμῳ διὰ τοῦ σώματος τοῦ Χριστοῦ, εἰς τὸ
    γενέσθαι ὑμᾶς ἑτέρῳ, τῷ ἐκ νεκρῶν ἐγερθέντι (Rom 7:4 — put to death through
    Christ's body *in order to belong to another*, the risen One); εἷς ὑπὲρ
    πάντων ἀπέθανεν, ἄρα οἱ πάντες ἀπέθανον (2 Cor 5:14) leading directly to
    ὥστε εἴ τις ἐν Χριστῷ, καινὴ κτίσις· τὰ ἀρχαῖα παρῆλθεν, ἰδοὺ γέγονεν καινά
    (2 Cor 5:17 — "if anyone is in Christ, new creation: the old things passed
    away, behold, new things have come to be"); ἀπεκδυσάμενοι τὸν παλαιὸν
    ἄνθρωπον ... καὶ ἐνδυσάμενοι τὸν νέον (Col 3:9–10); τὸν καινὸν ἄνθρωπον τὸν
    κατὰ θεὸν κτισθέντα (Eph 4:24); ὅσοι γὰρ εἰς Χριστὸν ἐβαπτίσθητε, Χριστὸν
    ἐνεδύσασθε (Gal 3:27); καὶ ὑμᾶς νεκροὺς ὄντας ... συνεζωοποίησεν ὑμᾶς σὺν
    αὐτῷ (Col 2:13).
  - Eph 2:5–6; Col 3:3; John 14:20; 15:4; 6:56; Col 1:27; Rom 8:9–10; 2 Cor 13:5;
    Eph 3:17 (the result now — present positional co-location and two-way mutual
    indwelling): καὶ ὄντας ἡμᾶς νεκροὺς τοῖς παραπτώμασιν συνεζωοποίησεν τῷ
    Χριστῷ ... καὶ συνήγειρεν καὶ συνεκάθισεν ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ
    (Eph 2:5–6 — aorist indicatives: made alive *with*, raised *with*, seated
    *with* in the heavenlies *in* Christ Jesus); ἀπεθάνετε γὰρ καὶ ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν
    κέκρυπται σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ (Col 3:3 — perfect passive κέκρυπται:
    hidden *with* Christ *in* God now); and two-way mutual indwelling: ὑμεῖς ἐν
    ἐμοὶ κἀγὼ ἐν ὑμῖν (John 14:20); μείνατε ἐν ἐμοί, κἀγὼ ἐν ὑμῖν (John 15:4); ἐν
    ἐμοὶ μένει κἀγὼ ἐν αὐτῷ (John 6:56); Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν, ἡ ἐλπὶς τῆς δόξης (Col
    1:27); πνεῦμα θεοῦ οἰκεῖ ἐν ὑμῖν ... εἰ δὲ Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν (Rom 8:9–10);
    Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν (2 Cor 13:5); κατοικῆσαι τὸν Χριστὸν διὰ τῆς πίστεως
    ἐν ταῖς καρδίαις ὑμῶν (Eph 3:17).
  - John 14:3; 17:24; Col 3:4 (the result unveiled — future bodily gathering and
    manifestation in the same location): πάλιν ἔρχομαι καὶ παραλήμψομαι ὑμᾶς πρὸς
    ἐμαυτόν, ἵνα ὅπου εἰμὶ ἐγὼ καὶ ὑμεῖς ἦτε (John 14:3 — future παραλήμψομαι at
    His return, "that where I am, you also may be"); θέλω ἵνα ὅπου εἰμὶ ἐγὼ
    κἀκεῖνοι ὦσιν μετʼ ἐμοῦ, ἵνα θεωρῶσιν τὴν δόξαν τὴν ἐμήν (John 17:24); ὅταν ὁ
    Χριστὸς φανερωθῇ, ἡ ζωὴ ὑμῶν, τότε καὶ ὑμεῖς σὺν αὐτῷ φανερωθήσεσθε ἐν δόξῃ
    (Col 3:4 — future passive φανερωθήσεσθε, revealed *with* Him in glory).
  - Heb 10:14 (the ground of the transfer): μιᾷ γὰρ προσφορᾷ τετελείωκεν εἰς τὸ
    διηνεκὲς τοὺς ἁγιαζομένους — by one offering He *has perfected* (τετελείωκεν,
    perfect active) *for all time* (εἰς τὸ διηνεκὲς) those being sanctified.
- **The close (settled):** The conjunction of the supplied verses settles five
  things. (1) "In Adam" (ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ, 1 Cor 15:22a) and "in Christ" (ἐν τῷ
  Χριστῷ, 1 Cor 15:22b; ἐστε ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ, 1 Cor 1:30) are genuine spheres of
  inclusion, explicitly distinguished from instrumental agency (διʼ ἀνθρώπου,
  1 Cor 15:21), and inclusion in the head carries the head's condition and act
  (Heb 7:9–10; Rom 5:15, 18–19; 1 Cor 15:22). (2) The believer has undergone a
  real transfer and passage out of the old domain into the new: rescued *out of*
  (ἐκ) the authority of darkness and *transferred into* (μετέστησεν εἰς) the
  kingdom of the beloved Son (Col 1:13), *passed over out of* death *into* life
  (μεταβέβηκεν / μεταβεβήκαμεν ἐκ τοῦ θανάτου εἰς τὴν ζωήν, John 5:24; 1 John
  3:14), called *out of* darkness *into* light (1 Pet 2:9), and *having come* to
  Mount Zion (προσεληλύθατε, Heb 12:22). (3) That passage never bypasses death or
  relocates an intact old man: immersion *into Christ* is immersion *into His
  death* (Rom 6:3), the old man was co-crucified and stripped off (Rom 6:6; Gal
  2:19; Col 3:9), we were put to death through Christ's body *in order to belong
  to another*, the risen One (Rom 7:4), and in Christ there is a *new creation*
  with the new man created and put on (2 Cor 5:17; Eph 4:24; Col 3:10; Gal 3:27),
  grounded permanently in His one offering (Heb 10:14). (4) The resulting union
  is stated in both directions as spatial and relational inclusion — we *in* Him
  and *with* Him in the heavenlies and in God (1 Cor 1:30; Eph 2:6; Col 3:3; John
  14:20; 15:4; 6:56), and He and the Father's Spirit *in* us (John 14:20; 15:4;
  6:56; Gal 2:20; Rom 8:9–10; Col 1:27; 2 Cor 13:5; Eph 3:17). (5) All four
  guards of the `Two Humanities` entry remain intact: our union is with the risen
  Christ, the second man and Head of the new creation, not with the Last Adam as terminus of the first line (the second-man and firstborn texts are cited from KB064's chain: 1 Cor 15:45–47; Col 1:18); no verse transfers
  "Christ's history" onto us; the old man is executed, not relocated (Rom 6:6; and KB064, with Rom 6:8; 1 Cor 15:42–44); and the bodily transformation and unveiled gathering remain future (Rom 6:5 ἐσόμεθα; Col 3:4 φανερωθήσεσθε; John 14:3 παραλήμψομαι — and 1 Cor 15:49's φορέσομεν, in KB064's chain).
- **The proposed close (marked):** State the combined chain as *"Transferred from
  Adam into Christ: union is co-location."* No single verse says "transferred
  from Adam into Christ" in one breath, and no Greek word in the chain is
  literally "co-location." Both are the proposer's summary labels for what the
  conjunction of links states: the passage from being ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ (1 Cor 15:22a)
  to being ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ (1 Cor 15:22b; 1:30) via the Father's transfer into the
  Son's kingdom (Col 1:13), death-and-life passage (John 5:24; Rom 7:4), and
  immersion into Christ and His death (Rom 6:3); and "co-location" as the
  English summary of the texts' ἐν ("in"), σύν and the συν- compound verbs
  (συνεσταυρώθη, συνεζωοποίησεν, συνήγειρεν, συνεκάθισεν), μετά ("with," John
  17:24), ὅπου ("where," John 14:3; 17:24), and the indwelling verbs μένω and
  οἰκέω / κατοικέω.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The chain is a multi-author New Testament conjunction
  (Paul, John, 1 Peter, Hebrews); each writer's own sentence is quoted for its
  exact grammatical contribution, and the "AND" is the argument of the entry,
  never attributed to a single verse. (2) Eph 2:13, 19 and Rom 11:17, 24 address
  Gentile inclusion (the far-off brought near in Christ's blood; the wild olive
  cut out and grafted into the covenantal root) and are used here strictly for
  their structural grammar of movement (distance abolished *in Christ Jesus*;
  change of stock by a severing cut and a graft rather than self-repair), not as
  Adam-to-Christ texts in themselves. (3) Rom 5:12's ἐφʼ ᾧ πάντες ἥμαρτον is not
  used or rested on in this chain; Adamic fate-inclusion rests on 1 Cor 15:22a,
  Rom 5:15, 18–19, and the pattern of Heb 7:9–10. (4) Rom 5:19's future
  κατασταθήσονται is left open as a genuine fork (sequence future from Adam's
  standpoint vs. eschatological future, per `Two Humanities` Honesty clause 7);
  nothing in this close depends on resolving it. (5) In Col 3:4, NA28 prints σὺν
  αὐτῷ within ⸋ ⸌ apparatus markers; the unmarked σὺν τῷ Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ in Col
  3:3 and συνήγειρεν καὶ συνεκάθισεν ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις ἐν Χριστῷ Ἰησοῦ in Eph
  2:6 independently carry the σὺν / ἐν co-location without relying on the bracketed
  words. (6) In Col 1:27 (`⸋τῆς δόξης⸌`), 2 Cor 13:5 (`⸉Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς⸊`), and
  Col 2:13 (`°[ἐν] τοῖς παραπτώμασιν`), the NA28 apparatus markers do not affect
  the load-bearing clauses (`Χριστὸς ἐν ὑμῖν`, `ἐν ὑμῖν`, `συνεζωοποίησεν ὑμᾶς
  σὺν αὐτῷ`).
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The "instrumental ἐν" and "figure for
  influence" reading: ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ / ἐν τῷ Χριστῷ (1 Cor 15:22) means only "by
  Adam / by Christ," and "in Christ / Christ in you" is a metaphor for moral or
  spiritual influence rather than real location. Answered: 1 Cor 15:21 has just
  used διʼ ἀνθρώπου twice for agency before shifting to ἐν τῷ Ἀδὰμ / ἐν τῷ
  Χριστῷ in 15:22; Heb 7:9–10 grounds shared action in physical/corporate
  location (ἐν τῇ ὀσφύϊ τοῦ πατρὸς ἦν); and the transfer, burial, raising,
  seating, hiding, and dwelling verbs (μετέστησεν εἰς, ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς,
  συνήγειρεν καὶ συνεκάθισεν ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις ἐν Χριστῷ, κέκρυπται σὺν τῷ
  Χριστῷ ἐν τῷ θεῷ, οἰκεῖ / κατοικῆσαι ἐν, ὅπου εἰμὶ ἐγὼ) are uniformly spatial
  and locative across multiple authors. (b) The "seamless relocation of the old
  self" reading: transfer language (Col 1:13; John 5:24) means the existing
  person simply changes sovereigns or spheres without dying. Answered: Rom 6:3
  self-glosses εἰς Χριστόν as εἰς τὸν θάνατον αὐτοῦ, Rom 7:4 makes execution
  through Christ's body (ἐθανατώθητε ... διὰ τοῦ σώματος τοῦ Χριστοῦ) the
  explicit means of coming to belong to the risen One (εἰς τὸ γενέσθαι ὑμᾶς
  ἑτέρῳ), and 2 Cor 5:14–17 ties "all died" directly to "if anyone is in Christ,
  new creation." (c) The "already consummated" reading: present co-seating (Eph
  2:6) and passing from death into life (John 5:24) mean the resurrection and
  glory are wholly present now. Answered: Col 3:3–4 holds both tenses in one
  sentence — our life *is hidden* (κέκρυπται, perfect) with Christ in God now,
  and *when* Christ is manifested, *then* we *will be manifested* (φανερωθήσεσθε,
  future) with Him in glory — matching Rom 6:5 (ἐσόμεθα) and John 14:3 (παραλήμψομαι), and 1 Cor 15:49 (φορέσομεν, cited from KB064's chain).

### Little Faith — and the Faith Called Great

- **ID:** KB066
- **Facet of:** none
- **Greek keys:** ἀγάπη, ἀπιστία, ἄπιστος, δειλός, διαλογίζομαι, διστάζω, κόκκος, μεθίστημι, μεριμνάω, μέτρον, ὀλιγοπιστία, ὀλιγόπιστος, πίστις, πιστεύω, σίναπι
- **Verse keys:** Matt 6:25–30; Matt 8:10; Matt 8:23–26; Matt 14:28–31; Matt 15:28; Matt 16:5–12; Matt 17:14–20; Mark 4:40; Mark 9:24; Luke 8:25; Luke 12:22–28; Luke 17:5–6; Rom 12:3; 1 Cor 13:2
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** Jesus speaks the "little faith" word — ὀλιγόπιστος (oligopistos, 'of little faith') and its noun — to His own disciples at all six of its New Testament places: over anxiety about food and clothing, fear in the storm, doubt on the water, discussion about bread, and a failure to cast out a demon; and at none of them does He prescribe more faith — the apostles' "Increase our faith!" is answered with the mustard seed, the same saying that answers the disciples' "Why could we not cast it out?", while "such great faith" and "your faith is great" are spoken of two outsiders, and the "measure of faith" of Romans 12:3 is God's own allotment to each, given for sober judgment; and Paul, taking up the same mountain at its full measure — "all faith so as to remove mountains" — bounds the faith that moves it: without love, the man who has it is nothing.
- **The chain:**
  - The filings (the dictionary's own judgments): ὀλιγοπιστία carries one sense, [31.95], "the state of having little or inadequate faith" (glosses: to not have enough faith, to have limited faith), and exactly one New Testament reference, Matt 17:20. ὀλιγόπιστος carries one sense, [31.96], "pertaining to having relatively little faith" (glosses: of little faith, of insufficient faith), at exactly five references: Matt 6:30; 8:26; 14:31; 16:8; Luke 12:28. πίστις in this set is filed [31.85], "to believe to the extent of complete trust and reliance," at Matt 17:20; Matt 8:10; Matt 15:28; Mark 4:40; Luke 8:25; Rom 12:3; and 1 Cor 13:2 — and at Luke 17:5 and Luke 17:6 it is filed [31.102], "to believe in the good news about Jesus Christ and to become a follower," the set's one fork, printed at its place below and in the close. πιστεύω at Mark 9:24 is filed [31.85]; ἀπιστία there is filed [31.97]. ἄπιστος at Matt 17:17 is filed [31.98]. διστάζω carries one sense, [31.37], "to think that something may not be true or certain" (glosses: to doubt, to be uncertain about, doubt), at two places in the whole New Testament: Matt 14:31 and Matt 28:17 (fenced — Honesty (2)). δειλός at Matt 8:26 and Mark 4:40 is filed [25.268], "pertaining to being cowardly." μεριμνάω at Matt 6:25 and Luke 12:22 is filed [25.225], "to have an anxious concern, based on apprehension about possible danger or misfortune." διαλογίζομαι at Matt 16:8 is filed [33.158], "to engage in some relatively detailed discussion of a matter" — the discussion sense, not the word's reasoning sense, which the dictionary files at other places. μέτρον at Rom 12:3 is filed [81.1], a unit of measurement, either of length or volume. κόκκος is filed [3.35], "the kernel part of fruit, usually grain," at both mustard-seed sayings; σίναπι is filed [3.20], "mustard plant, a large herb noted for its very small seeds and in some instances growing to a height of three meters (about ten feet)," at both. ἀγάπη at 1 Cor 13:2 is filed [25.43], "to have love for someone or something, based on sincere appreciation and high regard." μεθίστημι at 1 Cor 13:2 is filed [15.9], "to cause something to be moved from one place to another" (gloss: to remove).
  - Matt 6:25–30 with Luke 12:22–28 — anxiety answered by the Father's supply: the passage runs on μεριμνάω (μὴ μεριμνᾶτε, "do not be worried…", v. 25; τί μεριμνᾶτε, "why are you worried…", v. 28) over food, drink, and clothing, and its argument is the Father's own action — He feeds the birds (ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν ὁ οὐράνιος τρέφει αὐτά) and clothes the grass (ὁ θεὸς οὕτως ἀμφιέννυσιν) — landing on the address: οὐ πολλῷ μᾶλλον ὑμᾶς, ὀλιγόπιστοι; "will He not much more clothe you? You of little faith!" The smallness is named at the end of a supply argument, to disciples being taught, with no crisis in the scene. Luke's telling opens with the addressee named — Εἶπεν δὲ πρὸς τοὺς μαθητὰς, "And He said to His disciples" — and runs the same argument in its own words: ravens for Matthew's birds (κατανοήσατε τοὺς κόρακας, "Consider the ravens"), the same lilies, and the same landing: πόσῳ μᾶλλον ὑμᾶς, ὀλιγόπιστοι; "how much more will He clothe you? You of little faith!"
  - The storm, in three tellings (Matt 8:23–26; Mark 4:40; Luke 8:25): in Matthew's telling the disciples come to Him and wake Him — κύριε, σῶσον, ἀπολλύμεθα, "Save us, Lord; we are perishing!" — and the failure is named inside the coming. Matthew: τί δειλοί ἐστε, ὀλιγόπιστοι; "Why are you afraid, you men of little faith?" — fear's word (δειλοί) first, then the address. Mark: τί δειλοί ἐστε; οὔπω ἔχετε πίστιν; "Why are you afraid? Do you still have no faith?" — the same fear word, and faith framed as not-yet-had. Luke: ποῦ ἡ πίστις ὑμῶν; "Where is your faith?" — faith framed as somewhere else at that moment. His answer in every telling is the rebuke of the winds and the sea, not a faith exercise.
  - Matt 14:28–31 — Peter on the water: Peter is already walking (περιεπάτησεν ἐπὶ τὰ ὕδατα) when βλέπων δὲ τὸν ἄνεμον — "seeing the wind" — ἐφοβήθη, "he became frightened," and sinking, he cries κύριε, σῶσόν με, and Jesus' hand takes hold of him before the sentence: ὀλιγόπιστε, εἰς τί ἐδίστασας; "You of little faith, why did you doubt?" This is the one place where the little-faith address and the doubt word (διστάζω [31.37]) stand in a single sentence, and it is spoken to a man who had come to Jesus on the water and had just been caught by His hand.
  - Matt 16:5–12 — the bread discussion: the disciples διελογίζοντο ἐν ἑαυτοῖς, were discussing among themselves that they had brought no bread, and Jesus answers: τί διαλογίζεσθε ἐν ἑαυτοῖς, ὀλιγόπιστοι, ὅτι ἄρτους οὐκ ἔχετε; "You men of little faith, why are you discussing among yourselves the fact that you have no bread?" — and His corrective is memory and understanding, not supply delivered on the spot: οὔπω νοεῖτε, οὐδὲ μνημονεύετε, "Do you not yet understand nor remember the five loaves of the five thousand…?" The passage ends τότε συνῆκαν, "Then they understood…". At this place the dictionary files their verb as discussion [33.158]; what Jesus exposes is a discussion that had forgotten the feedings.
  - Matt 17:14–20 with Mark 9:24 — the boy: the disciples ask privately διὰ τί ἡμεῖς οὐκ ἠδυνήθημεν ἐκβαλεῖν αὐτό; "Why could we not cast it out?" and Matthew's answer names the noun's one occurrence: διὰ τὴν ὀλιγοπιστίαν ὑμῶν, "Because of your meager faith" (NASB) — and in the same breath the seed: ἐὰν ἔχητε πίστιν ὡς κόκκον σινάπεως, "if you have faith the size of a mustard seed, you will say to this mountain, 'Move from here to there,' and it will move; and nothing will be impossible for you." To the generation Jesus says ἄπιστος [31.98], "to be unbelieving" (17:17) — a different word than the one He speaks to His disciples. Mark's telling gives the father's one sentence: πιστεύω· βοήθει μου τῇ ἀπιστίᾳ, "I do believe; help my unbelief!" — believing (πιστεύω [31.85]) and unbelief (ἀπιστία [31.97]) in one breath, from a man who asks for help and receives it.
  - Luke 17:5–6 — the increase request: the apostles say πρόσθες ἡμῖν πίστιν, "Increase our faith!" — the request assumes faith is added by measure — and the answer is the seed again, in the present tense: εἰ ἔχετε πίστιν ὡς κόκκον σινάπεως, "If you had faith the size of a mustard seed, you could say to this mulberry tree, 'Be uprooted and be planted in the sea'; and it would obey you." No increase is administered; the saying replaces the request's frame. The fork stands here: at both of its places here — the request (v. 5) and the saying (v. 6) — the dictionary files πίστις [31.102], the believer sense — not the reliance sense it files at Matthew's saying — and the entry states both filings at their own places and spends neither across the other.
  - The size words at their places (Matt 8:10; 15:28; Rom 12:3): Jesus marvels at the centurion and says to those following: παρʼ οὐδενὶ τοσαύτην πίστιν ἐν τῷ Ἰσραὴλ εὗρον, "I have not found such great faith with anyone in Israel" — and to the Canaanite woman He answers: μεγάλη σου ἡ πίστις, "your faith is great; it shall be done for you as you desire." Both size-praises are spoken of outsiders, never of the disciples. Romans 12:3's μέτρον πίστεως is God's act, not a human grade: ἑκάστῳ ὡς ὁ θεὸς ἐμέρισεν μέτρον πίστεως, "as God has allotted to each a measure of faith" — inside a sentence whose point is that no one think more highly of himself than he ought, but think with sober judgment.
  - 1 Cor 13:2 — the mountain, bounded: Paul's sentence is a string of suppositions ("if I have…"), and its faith clause takes up the seed saying's effect at its maximum: ἐὰν ἔχω πᾶσαν τὴν πίστιν ὥστε ὄρη μεθιστάναι, "if I have all faith so as to remove mountains" — πίστις filed [31.85] here, the reliance sense, as at Jesus' saying. In the supposition the mountains move; the bound falls on the man: ἀγάπην δὲ μὴ ἔχω, οὐθέν εἰμι, "but do not have love, I am nothing." Paul does not qualify the faith's effect; he denies the man standing without love (ἀγάπη [25.43]).
- **The close (settled):** (1) The little-faith word is Jesus' address to His own disciples at every one of its places — the noun once (Matt 17:20), the adjective five times — and each place states its occasion in its own words: anxiety over food and clothing (Matt 6; Luke 12), fear in the storm (Matt 8), fear and doubt on the water (Matt 14), a discussion about bread that had forgotten the feedings (Matt 16), and a failure to cast out a demon (Matt 17). The generation around them He calls ἄπιστος, "to be unbelieving" — a different word, at a different address. (2) At no place does Jesus prescribe an increase of faith. The increase-request and the why-question — the apostles' "Increase our faith!" and the disciples' "Why could we not cast it out?" — both receive the mustard-seed saying; the storm receives His rebuke of wind and sea; Peter receives His hand; the bread discussion receives a remembering. The seed sayings hold out effect, not dosage: a mountain moved, a tree obeying, "nothing will be impossible for you." (3) Size language for faith stands in the texts at its own places and is not blended into a scale: "little" of disciples in the places above; "such great" (Matt 8:10) and "great" (Matt 15:28) of two outsiders; a "measure" (Rom 12:3) that God allots to each for sober judgment. No text shows Jesus adjusting anyone's amount. (4) The fork stands unblended: πίστις in this set is filed [31.85], trust and reliance, at every place except Luke 17:5–6, where it is filed [31.102], the believer sense; each saying is read under the filing its own verse carries. (5) Paul takes up the seed saying's effect at its maximum at his own place (1 Cor 13:2) — "all faith so as to remove mountains," πίστις under its reliance filing [31.85] — and bounds it there: in his supposition the mountains move, and the verdict falls not on the faith's power but on the man: without love, "I am nothing." The bound stands at Paul's place; it is not imported into Jesus' answers, none of which names love.
- **The close (proposed, marked):** Read across the places, these scenes share a direction: at each of these four scenes the men's reckoning has turned to the circumstance in front of them — the food and clothing they must secure (Matt 6; Luke 12), the storm (Matt 8; Mark 4; Luke 8), the wind Peter saw (Matt 14), the bread they did not bring (Matt 16) — and at each of them Jesus turns them back: to the Father who feeds and clothes, to His own presence in the boat and His hand in the water, to the feedings they had already seen. This is stated as the pattern the places make when they are allowed to stand together, not as the little-faith word's definition: the dictionary defines the word as little or inadequate faith, the size words stand in the settled close, and this hearing does not reverse either. Matt 17's scene is not one of these four — its answer is the diagnosis itself, and the seed; both stand in the settled close.
- **Honesty clause:** (1) The sketch is retired as written. Dropped: "independent self-reliance operating in disguise" as the word's meaning (no text in the set names self-reliance; the texts name anxiety, fear, doubt, and a discussion); "double-mindedness (δίψυχος)" (James's word, standing in none of these texts; Peter's text names διστάζω, doubting); the mustard seed that "simply rests completely surrendered to the soil" (neither saying mentions soil or surrender; they speak of a mountain moved and a tree obeying); "keeping your eyes locked on the King, even when the wind is blowing" (the sketch's Peter: "their gaze shifted to the obstacle," and they sank — Peter saw the wind, feared, sank, cried out, and was caught; the sketch drops the cry and the hand). Surviving, narrowed to the proposed close: reliance turning toward the circumstance as the pattern across the places. (2) Matt 28:17 — διστάζω's only other place, "they worshiped Him, but some doubted" — is fenced: named here, unpasted, unspent; Craig's set ruling neither included it nor was asked to exclude it, so it is a fence, not a finding. (3) ἀπιστία's remaining places (Matt 13:58; Mark 6:6; 16:14; Rom 3:3; 4:20; 11:20, 23; Heb 3:12, 19; 1 Tim 1:13) and the διακρίνομαι places under the same doubt filing [31.37] (Matt 21:21; Mark 11:23; Acts 10:20; Rom 4:20; 14:23; Jas 1:6; Jude 22) are outside the pasted set and unspent; this entry closes nothing about unbelief in general. (4) The mustard-seed kingdom parables (Matt 13:31–32; Mark 4:30–32; Luke 13:18–19) are excluded under the set ruling; the seed enters this entry only in the two faith sayings. (5) Romans 12:3 is spent for its one clause inside its own sentence; nothing about Romans 12's teaching on the body is closed here. (6) 1 Cor 13:2 enters as Paul's supposition ("if I have…"), not as a case of a disciple's faith, and only its faith clause is spent here; the prophecy, mysteries, and knowledge clauses of the same sentence are quoted nowhere and closed nowhere. 1 Cor 13:13 (faith, hope, and love abiding) was not pasted and is not in the set.
- **Rival chain (stated fairly):** (a) The quantity reading as the whole account: little faith is a shortage of belief-volume, and the cure is a larger volume. Answered: the size words and the dictionary's "little or inadequate" definition stand in this entry un-denied — but volume-as-remedy is the apostles' assumption (Luke 17:5), not Jesus' prescription: to the increase-request and to the why-question He gives the seed saying, to the storm His rebuke, to Peter His hand, to the discussion a remembering; no text in the set shows Him supplying the missing amount. (b) The sketch's own reverse crown: little faith is not quantity at all, but self-reliance in disguise. Answered: an over-correction — it must erase the dictionary's definition and Matthew's "great faith" verses to stand; it names a failure (self-reliance) that none of the texts names, where the texts name anxiety, fear, doubt, and discussion; and it imports δίψυχος from James to seal a diagnosis the passages themselves do not make. What is true in it stands, narrowed, in the proposed close. (c) The tiny-dose reading of the seed: "even a very little faith is enough." Answered from the verse itself: at Matt 17:20 the seed saying is the second half of the same answer whose first half diagnoses the little-faith state — if the seed were simply the low end of the same quantity scale, the answer would commend the condition it had just named as the failure; the comparison is ὡς κόκκον σινάπεως, literally "as a mustard seed," and its outcomes are a mountain moved and "nothing will be impossible for you," not a minimum effective dose. (d) The measure as a spiritual grade (Rom 12:3): each believer carries an allotted faith-capacity that explains success and failure. Answered at its place: Paul's sentence assigns the allotting to God and aims the measure at sober self-judgment inside the body; it is spent there, and this entry does not carry it into storms, water, bread, or exorcisms, where the texts give it no work.

### The Commissioned Household (ἀποστέλλω)

- **ID:** KB068
- **Kind:** closure
- **Claim:** In John, the disciples are sent on the same pattern as the Son's
  own sending — καθὼς, "as" — and in both cases the sent one does not act from
  himself: the Son acts only as he sees and hears the Father who sent him, and
  the disciples bear fruit only abiding in the Son, apart from whom they can do
  nothing.
- **The chain:**
  - John 17:18 (the sending parallel, stated): καθὼς ἐμὲ ἀπέστειλας εἰς τὸν
    κόσμον, κἀγὼ ἀπέστειλα αὐτοὺς εἰς τὸν κόσμον — "as you sent me into the
    world, I also sent them into the world." Both clauses use ἀποστέλλω,
    both aorist (ἀπέστειλας / ἀπέστειλα) — the tense reporting is kept
    symmetrical with 20:21 below, where the forms shift (perfect / present).
    The disciples' sending into the world is patterned on the Father's
    sending of the Son by the text's own καθὼς, not by the reader's analogy.
  - John 20:21 (the sending parallel, repeated at the commissioning itself):
    καθὼς ἀπέσταλκέν με ὁ πατήρ, κἀγὼ πέμπω ὑμᾶς — "as the Father has sent
    (ἀπέσταλκέν, perfect) me, I also send (πέμπω, present) you." The parallel
    is repeated, not incidental; the second clause's verb is πέμπω, so the
    pattern in John runs across both sending verbs (see Honesty clause).
  - John 5:19 (the Son's mode — seeing, not self-originating): οὐ δύναται ὁ
    υἱὸς ποιεῖν ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ οὐδὲν ἐὰν μή τι βλέπῃ τὸν πατέρα ποιοῦντα· ἃ γὰρ
    ἂν ἐκεῖνος ποιῇ, ταῦτα καὶ ὁ υἱὸς ὁμοίως ποιεῖ — the Son "can do nothing
    from himself" (ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ) except what he sees the Father doing, and what
    the Father does, the Son does ὁμοίως, "likewise." Verse 20 gives the
    ground: ὁ πατὴρ φιλεῖ τὸν υἱὸν καὶ πάντα δείκνυσιν αὐτῷ ἃ αὐτὸς ποιεῖ —
    the Father shows him all that he himself does.
  - John 5:30 (the Son's mode — hearing and the sender's will): Οὐ δύναμαι
    ἐγὼ ποιεῖν ἀπʼ ἐμαυτοῦ οὐδέν· καθὼς ἀκούω κρίνω, καὶ ἡ κρίσις ἡ ἐμὴ
    δικαία ἐστίν, ὅτι οὐ ζητῶ τὸ θέλημα τὸ ἐμὸν ἀλλὰ τὸ θέλημα τοῦ
    πέμψαντός με — "I can do nothing from myself; as I hear, I judge... because
    I do not seek my own will but the will of him who sent me." Judgment,
    the very function given to the Son (5:22, 27), is exercised by hearing,
    not by independent initiative.
  - John 12:44–45, 47–48 (the Sent One does not terminate trust, sight, or
    judgment on himself): ὁ πιστεύων εἰς ἐμὲ οὐ πιστεύει εἰς ἐμὲ ἀλλʼ εἰς
    τὸν πέμψαντά με, καὶ ὁ θεωρῶν ἐμὲ θεωρεῖ τὸν πέμψαντά με — "the one who
    believes in me does not believe in me but in the one who sent me, and the
    one who sees me sees the one who sent me" (12:44–45). The supplied set
    states the same passing-to-the-sender for sight at 14:9 (ὁ ἑωρακὼς ἐμὲ
    ἑώρακεν τὸν πατέρα) and for honor, withheld, at 5:23 (ὁ μὴ τιμῶν τὸν
    υἱὸν οὐ τιμᾷ τὸν πατέρα τὸν πέμψαντα αὐτόν). And judgment: ἐγὼ οὐ κρίνω
    αὐτόν· οὐ γὰρ ἦλθον ἵνα κρίνω τὸν κόσμον, ἀλλʼ ἵνα σώσω τὸν κόσμον
    (12:47); the one who rejects him ἔχει τὸν κρίνοντα αὐτόν· ὁ λόγος ὃν
    ἐλάλησα ἐκεῖνος κρινεῖ αὐτὸν ἐν τῇ ἐσχάτῃ ἡμέρᾳ — "has the one who judges
    him: the word that I spoke, that will judge him on the last day" (12:48).
    The judging word is the spoken word — the word the next verses say was
    commanded, not self-originated (12:49–50).
  - John 12:49–50 (the Son's mode — speech under commandment): ἐγὼ ἐξ
    ἐμαυτοῦ οὐκ ἐλάλησα, ἀλλʼ ὁ πέμψας με πατὴρ αὐτός μοι ἐντολὴν δέδωκεν
    τί εἴπω καὶ τί λαλήσω — "I did not speak from myself, but the Father who
    sent me himself gave me a commandment, what to say and what to speak";
    ἃ οὖν ἐγὼ λαλῶ, καθὼς εἴρηκέν μοι ὁ πατήρ, οὕτως λαλῶ — "what I speak,
    as the Father has said to me, so I speak." Self-originated speech is
    explicitly denied, in the same καθὼς pattern.
  - John 14:10 (the Son's mode — the abiding Father does the works): ἐγὼ ἐν
    τῷ πατρὶ καὶ ὁ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοί ἐστιν ... τὰ ῥήματα ἃ ἐγὼ λέγω ὑμῖν ἀπʼ
    ἐμαυτοῦ οὐ λαλῶ, ὁ δὲ πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοὶ μένων ποιεῖ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ — "I am in
    the Father and the Father is in me... the words I speak to you I do not
    speak from myself, but the Father, abiding (μένων) in me, does his works."
    The mutual indwelling is the setting; the works are the abiding Father's.
  - John 15:4–5 (the disciples' mode — the same inability, the same
    ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ): καθὼς τὸ κλῆμα οὐ δύναται καρπὸν φέρειν ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ ἐὰν μὴ
    μένῃ ἐν τῇ ἀμπέλῳ, οὕτως οὐδὲ ὑμεῖς ἐὰν μὴ ἐν ἐμοὶ μένητε — "as the branch
    cannot bear fruit from itself if it does not abide in the vine, so neither
    can you if you do not abide in me"; ὁ μένων ἐν ἐμοὶ κἀγὼ ἐν αὐτῷ οὗτος
    φέρει καρπὸν πολύν, ὅτι χωρὶς ἐμοῦ οὐ δύνασθε ποιεῖν οὐδέν — "the one
    abiding in me and I in him, this one bears much fruit, because apart from
    me you can do nothing." John applies to the disciples, in relation to the
    Son, the same construction he applied to the Son in relation to the Father:
    οὐ δύναται / οὐ δύνασθε ... οὐδέν, and action ἀφʼ ἑαυτοῦ denied (5:19;
    15:4–5).
  - John 6:56–57 (abiding and living-through — the pattern stated for life,
    before it is stated for fruit): ὁ τρώγων μου τὴν σάρκα καὶ πίνων μου τὸ
    αἷμα ἐν ἐμοὶ μένει κἀγὼ ἐν αὐτῷ — "the one who eats my flesh and drinks
    my blood abides in me and I in him" (6:56, present μένει: the same mutual
    abiding as 15:4–5); καθὼς ἀπέστειλέν με ὁ ζῶν πατὴρ κἀγὼ ζῶ διὰ τὸν
    πατέρα, καὶ ὁ τρώγων με κἀκεῖνος ζήσει διʼ ἐμέ — "as the living Father
    sent me and I live because of (διὰ + accusative) the Father, so also the
    one who eats me — that one will live because of me" (6:57). The καθὼς
    here analogizes *living through another*: the Son's life is διὰ τὸν
    πατέρα, the eater's life (ζήσει, future) is διʼ ἐμέ. Two structural
    notes. First, the correlation is carried by κἀγὼ ... κἀκεῖνος — "and I"
    ... "and that one also" — the same κἀγὼ correlation that carries the
    sending parallels themselves (17:18 κἀγὼ ἀπέστειλα; 20:21 κἀγὼ πέμπω).
    Second, the sending verb is not incidental to the analogy: ἀπέστειλέν
    stands inside the καθὼς premise, joined in one clause with the Son's
    living διὰ τὸν πατέρα — the Father's sending of the Son and the Son's
    living because of the Father are stated together as the premise the
    eater's living διʼ ἐμέ then parallels. What 6:57 does not do is send
    the eater: within this passage ἀποστέλλω is used only of the Father
    sending the Son; no one is sent in 6:54–58 (see Honesty clause 7).
  - John 14:12–14 (the disciples' works — real, and not self-originated):
    ὁ πιστεύων εἰς ἐμὲ τὰ ἔργα ἃ ἐγὼ ποιῶ κἀκεῖνος ποιήσει καὶ μείζονα
    τούτων ποιήσει, ὅτι ἐγὼ πρὸς τὸν πατέρα πορεύομαι — the believer "will do
    the works I do, and greater than these will he do, because I go to the
    Father." The very next verses assign the doing: ὅ τι ἂν αἰτήσητε ἐν τῷ
    ὀνόματί μου τοῦτο ποιήσω, ἵνα δοξασθῇ ὁ πατὴρ ἐν τῷ υἱῷ — "whatever you
    ask in my name, this *I* will do, so that the Father may be glorified in
    the Son" (14:13; repeated 14:14, ἐγὼ ποιήσω). The greater works are
    promised; their stated mechanism is asking in his name and his doing.
  - John 15:26–27 (witness — shared, not solo): Ὅταν ἔλθῃ ὁ παράκλητος ὃν
    ἐγὼ πέμψω ὑμῖν παρὰ τοῦ πατρός, τὸ πνεῦμα τῆς ἀληθείας ὃ παρὰ τοῦ πατρὸς
    ἐκπορεύεται, ἐκεῖνος μαρτυρήσει περὶ ἐμοῦ· καὶ ὑμεῖς δὲ μαρτυρεῖτε, ὅτι
    ἀπʼ ἀρχῆς μετʼ ἐμοῦ ἐστε — the Paraclete, sent by the Son from the Father,
    "will bear witness about me; and you also bear witness, because you have
    been with me from the beginning." The disciples' witness stands second,
    alongside the Spirit's, grounded in their having been with him. The
    supplied set states the same non-solo witness for the Son himself:
    Ἐὰν ἐγὼ μαρτυρῶ περὶ ἐμαυτοῦ, ἡ μαρτυρία μου οὐκ ἔστιν ἀληθής· ἄλλος
    ἐστὶν ὁ μαρτυρῶν περὶ ἐμοῦ (5:31–32) — "if I bear witness about myself,
